from the winter term 2020/2021.
Chapters 5–8 are the lecture notes of its continuation “Real Algebraic Geometry II”
from the summer term 2021.
Chapters 9–10 are the lecture notes of its further continuation “Geometry of Linear Matrix Inequalities”
from the winter term 2021/2022.

\manualmark

Chapter 0
Introduction

The study of polynomial equations is a canonical subject in mathematics education, as is illustrated by the following examples: Quadratic equations in one variable (high school), systems of linear equations (linear algebra), polynomial equations in one variable and their symmetries (algebra, Galois theory), diophantine equations (number theory) and systems of polynomial equations (algebraic geometry, commutative algebra).

In contrast to this, the study of polynomial inequalities (in the sense of “greater than” or “greater or equal than”) is mostly neglected even though it is much more important for applications: Indeed, in applications one often searches for a real solution rather than a complex one (as in classical algebraic geometry) and this solution must not necessarily be exact but only approximate.

In a course about linear algebra there is frequently no time for linear optimization. An introductory course about algebra usually treats groups, rings and fields but disregards ordered and real closed fields as well as preorders or prime cones of rings. In a first course on algebraic geometry there is often no special attention paid to the real part of a variety and in commutative algebra quadratic modules are practically never treated.

Most algebraists do not even know the notion of a preorder although it is as important for the study of systems of polynomial inequalities as the notion of an ideal is for the study of systems of polynomial equations. People from more applied areas such as numerical analysis, mathematical optimization or functional analysis know often more about real algebraic geometry than some algebraists, but often do not even recognize that polynomials play a decisive role in what they are doing. There are for example countless articles from functional analysis which are full of equations with binomial coefficients which turn out to be just disguised simple polynomial identities.

In the same way as the study of polynomial systems of equations leads to the study of rings and their generalizations (such as modules), the study of systems of polynomial inequalities leads to the study of rings which are endowed with something that resembles an order. This additional structure raises many new questions that have to be clarified. These questions arise already at a very basic level so that we need as prerequisites only basic linear algebra, algebra and analysis. In particular, at least the first half of this
course is really extremely well suited to students heading for students enrolled in programs for mathematics education. It includes several topics which are directly relevant for high school teaching.

To arouse the reader’s curiosity, we present the following table.
It contains on the left column notions we assume the reader is familiar with. On the right column we name what could be seen
more or less as their real counterparts mostly introduced in this course.

Algebra

Real Algebra

Algebraic Geometry

Real Algebraic Geometry

systems of polynomial equations

systems of polynomial inequalities

“
=
”

“
\geq
”

complex solutions

real solutions

\mathbb{C}

\mathbb{R}

algebraically closed fields

real closed fields

fields

ordered fields

ideals

preorders

prime ideals

prime cones

spectrum

real spectrum

Noetherian

quasi-compact

radical

real radical

fundamental theorem of algebra

fundamental theorem of algebra

Aachen, Aalborg, Aarhus, …

Dortmund, Dresden, Dublin, Innsbruck, …

…, Zagreb, Zürich

…, Konstanz, Leipzig, Ljubljana, Rennes

It is intended that the fundamental theorem of algebra appears on both sides of the table. In its usual form, it says
that each non-constant univariate complex polynomial has a complex root. In Section
4
, we will formulate it in a
“real” way. The difficulties one has to deal with in the “real world” become already apparent when one asks the corresponding
“real question”: When does a univariate complex polynomial have a real root? The answer to this will be given in Section

6
and requires already quite a bit of thoughts.

Traditionally, Real Algebraic Geometry has many ties with fields like Model Theory, Valuation Theory, Quadratic Form Theory
and Algebraic Topology. In this lecture, we mainly emphasize however connections to fields like Optimization, Functional Analysis and Convexity
that came up during the recent years and are now fully established.

Throughout the lecture,
\mathbb{N}:=\{1,2,3,\dots\}
and
\mathbb{N}_{0}:=\{0\}\cup\mathbb{N}
denote the set of positive and nonnegative integers, respectively.

Chapter 1
Ordered fields

1
Orders of fields

Reminder 1.1
.

Let
M
be a set. An
order
on
M
is a relation
\leq
on
M
such that for all
a,b,c\in M
:

\begin{array}[]{rcl}&a\leq a&\text{(reflexivity)}\\
&(a\leq b\mathbin{\&}b\leq c)\implies a\leq c&\text{(transitivity)}\\
&(a\leq b\mathbin{\&}b\leq a)\implies a=b&\text{(antisymmetry)}\\
\text{and}&a\leq b\text{ or }b\leq a&\text{(linearity)}\end{array}

In this case,
(M,\leq)
(or simply
M
if
\leq
is clear from the context) is called an
ordered set
. For
a,b\in M
, one defines

\displaystyle a<b

\displaystyle:\iff a\leq b\mathbin{\&}a\neq b,

\displaystyle a\geq b

\displaystyle:\iff b\leq a

and so on.

Definition 1.2
.

Let
(M,\leq_{1})
and
(N,\leq_{2})
be ordered sets and
\varphi\colon M\to N
be a map.
Then
\varphi
is called a
homomorphism
(of ordered sets)
or
monotonic
if

a\leq_{1}b\implies\varphi(a)\leq_{2}\varphi(b)

for all
a,b\in M
. If
\varphi
is

\begin{Bmatrix}\text{injective}\\
\text{bijective}\end{Bmatrix}
and if

a\leq_{1}b\iff\varphi(a)\leq_{2}\varphi(b)

for all
a,b\in M
, then
\varphi
is called an
\begin{Bmatrix}\text{\emph{embedding}}\\
\text{\emph{isomorphism}}\end{Bmatrix}
(of ordered sets).

Proposition 1.3
.

Let
(M,\leq_{1})
and
(N,\leq_{2})
be ordered sets and
\varphi\colon M\to N
a homomorphism. Then the following are equivalent:

(a)

\varphi
is an embedding

(b)

\varphi
is injective

(c)

\forall a,b\in M:(\varphi(a)\leq_{2}\varphi(b)\implies a\leq_{1}b)

Proof.

(c)
\implies
(b)
Suppose (c) holds and let
a,b\in M
such that
\varphi(a)=\varphi(b)
. Then

\varphi(a)\leq_{2}\varphi(b)
and
\varphi(a)\geq_{2}\varphi(b)
. Now (c) implies
a\leq_{1}b
and
a\geq_{1}b
. Hence
a=b
.

(b)
\implies
(c)
Suppose (b) holds and let
a,b\in M
with
a\not\leq_{1}b
. To show:
\varphi(a)\not\leq_{2}\varphi(b)
. We have

a>_{1}b
and it suffices to show
\varphi(a)>_{2}\varphi(b)
. From
a\geq_{1}b
it follows by the monotonicity of
\varphi
that
\varphi(a)\geq_{2}\varphi(b)
.
From
a\neq b
and the injectivity of
\varphi
we get
\varphi(a)\neq\varphi(b)
.

From (b)
\iff
(c) and (a)
\iff
((b)
\mathbin{\&}
(c)) [
\to

1.2
] the claim now follows.
∎

Definition 1.4
.

Let
K
be a field. An
order
of
K
is an order
\leq
on
K
such that for all
a,b,c\in K
we have:

\begin{array}[]{rcl}&a\leq b\implies a+c\leq b+c&\text{(monotonicity of addition)}\\
\text{and}&(a\leq b\mathbin{\&}c\geq 0)\implies ac\leq bc&\text{(monotonicity of multiplication).}\\
\end{array}

In this case,
(K,\leq)
(or simply
K
when
\leq
is clear from the context) is called an
ordered field
.

Definition 1.5
.

Let
(K,\leq_{1})
and
(L,\leq_{2})
be ordered fields.

A field homomorphism (or equivalently, field embedding!)
\varphi\colon K\to L

is called a
homomorphism
or
embedding
of ordered fields if
\varphi
is monotonic
(pay attention to
1.3
together with the fact that field homomorphisms are injective).
If
\varphi
is moreover surjective, then
\varphi
is called an
isomorphism
of ordered fields.

If there exists an embedding of ordered fields
from
(K,\leq_{1})
into
(L,\leq_{2})
, then
(K,\leq_{1})
is called
embeddable
in
(L,\leq_{2})
and one denotes

(K,\leq_{1})\hookrightarrow(L,\leq_{2})
.
If there is an isomorphism of ordered fields from
(K,\leq_{1})
to
(L,\leq_{2})
, then
(K,\leq_{1})
and
(L,\leq_{2})
are called

isomorphic
. This is denoted by
(K,\leq_{1})\cong(L,\leq_{2})
.

(K,\leq_{1})
is called an
ordered subfield
of
(L,\leq_{2})
, or equivalently
(L,\leq_{2})
an
ordered extension field
of
(K,\leq_{1})
,
if
(K,\leq_{1})\to(L,\leq_{2}),\ a\mapsto a
is an embedding, that is if
K
is a subfield of
L
and
(\leq_{1})=(\leq_{2})\cap(K\times K)
. For every
subfield of
L
there is obviously a unique order making it into an ordered subfield of
(L,\leq_{2})
. This order is called the order
induced

by
(L,\leq_{2})
.

Proposition 1.6
.

Let
(K,\leq)
be an ordered field. Then
a^{2}\geq 0
for all
a\in K
.

Proof.

Let
a\in K
.
When
a\geq 0
this follows immediately from the monotonicity of multiplication [
\to

1.4
]. When
a\leq 0
the monotonicity of
addition [
\to

1.4
] yields
0=a-a\leq-a
, whence
-a\geq 0
and therefore
a^{2}=(-a)^{2}\geq 0
.
∎

Proposition 1.7
.

Let
(K,\leq)
be an ordered field. Then
K
is of characteristic
0
and the uniquely determined field homomorphism

\mathbb{Q}\to K
is an embedding of ordered fields
{(\mathbb{Q},\leq_{\mathbb{Q}})}\hookrightarrow(K,\leq)
. Hence
(K,\leq)
can be seen as an ordered
extension field of
(\mathbb{Q},\leq_{\mathbb{Q}})
. In particular, for
K=\mathbb{Q}
it follows that
(\leq_{\mathbb{Q}})=(\leq)
, i.e.,
\mathbb{Q}
can only be ordered in the familiar way.

Proof.

From
1.6
we have
0\leq 1^{2}=1
in
(K,\leq)
. Using the monotonicity of the addition, we deduce

(
*
)

0\leq 1\leq 1+1\leq 1+1+1\leq\dots

If we had
\chara K\neq 0
, then
(*)
would give
0\leq 1\leq 0
by the transitivity of
\leq
which would imply

0=1
in
K
by the antisymmetry of
\leq
, contradicting the definition of a field. Let
\varphi
denote the field homomorphism

\mathbb{Q}\to K
and let
a,b\in\mathbb{Q}
with
a\leq_{\mathbb{Q}}b
. To show:
\varphi(a)\leq\varphi(b)
. Write
a=\frac{k}{n}
and
b=\frac{\ell}{n}
with

k,\ell\in\mathbb{Z}
and
n\in\mathbb{N}
. Then

\varphi(n)=\underbrace{1+\dots+1}_{\text{$n$ times}}\underset{\chara K=0}{\overset{(*)}{>}}0

and, by the monotonicity of multiplication
and Proposition
1.6
, also

\frac{1}{\varphi(n)}=\left(\frac{1}{\varphi(n)}\right)^{2}\varphi(n)\geq 0.

Hence it suffices to show that
\varphi(a)\varphi(n)\leq\varphi(b)\varphi(n)
.
This reduces to
\varphi(an)\leq\varphi(bn)
, that is
\varphi(k)\leq\varphi(\ell)
, or equivalently
\varphi(\ell-k)\geq 0
.
But due to
\ell-k\geq_{\mathbb{Q}}0
this follows from
(*)
.
∎

Proposition and Notation 1.8
.

Let
(K,\leq)
be an ordered field. Then for every
a\in K^{\times}
there are uniquely determined

\sgn a\in\{-1,1\}
(“sign” of
a
) and
|a|\in K_{\geq 0}:=\{x\in K\mid x\geq 0\}
(“absolute value” of
a
) such that

a=(\sgn a)|a|.

One declares
\sgn 0:=|0|:=0
. It follows that
|ab|=|a||b|
,
\sgn(ab)=(\sgn a)(\sgn b)
and

|a+b|\leq|a|+|b|
for all
a,b\in K
.

Proof.

The first part is very easy. Let now
a,b\in K
. Then
ab=(\sgn a)(\sgn b)|a||b|
, implying
|ab|=|a||b|
as well as

\sgn(ab)=(\sgn a)(\sgn b)
. For the claimed triangle inequality, we can suppose
a+b\geq 0
(otherwise replace
a
by
-a
and
b
by
-b
).
Then
|a+b|=a+b\leq a+|b|\leq|a|+|b|
.
∎

Definition 1.9
.

Let
(K,\leq)
be an ordered field.

(a)

(K,\leq)
is called
Archimedean
if
\forall a\in K:\exists N\in\mathbb{N}:a\leq N

(or equivalently,
\forall a\in K:\exists N\in\mathbb{N}:-N\leq a
).

(b)

A sequence
(a_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
in
K
is called

•

a
Cauchy sequence
if
\forall\varepsilon\in K_{>0}:\exists N\in\mathbb{N}:\forall m,n\geq N:|a_{m}-a_{n}|<\varepsilon
,

•

convergent to
a\in K
if
\forall\varepsilon\in K_{>0}:\exists N\in\mathbb{N}:\forall n\geq N:|a_{n}-a|<\varepsilon

(one easily shows that
a
is then uniquely determined and writes
\lim_{n\to\infty}a_{n}=a
),

•

convergent
if there is some
a\in K
such that
\lim_{n\to\infty}a_{n}=a
.

We call
(K,\leq)

Cauchy complete
if every Cauchy sequence converges in
K
(by the way it is immediate that every convergent
sequence is a Cauchy sequence).

(c)

We call a subset
A\subseteq K

bounded from above
if
K
contains an upper bound for
A
(meaning some

b\in K
such
that
\forall a\in A:a\leq b
). We call
(K,\leq)

complete
if every nonempty subset of
K
bounded from above possesses a least
upper bound, i.e., a supremum.

Proposition 1.10
.

Let
(K,\leq)
be an ordered field. Then the following are equivalent:

(a)

(K,\leq)
is Archimedean

(b)

\forall a,b\in K:(a<b\implies\exists c\in\mathbb{Q}:a<c<b)

Proof.

(b)
\implies
(a)
Suppose (b) holds and let
a\in K
. To show:
\exists N\in\mathbb{N}:a\leq N
. WLOG
a>0
. To show:
\exists N\in\mathbb{N}:\frac{1}{N}\leq\frac{1}{a}
. Choose
c\in\mathbb{Q}
such that
0<c<\frac{1}{a}
. Write
c=\frac{m}{N}
for certain
m,N\in\mathbb{N}
. Then

\frac{1}{N}\leq\frac{m}{N}=c<\frac{1}{a}
.

(a)
\implies
(b)
Suppose (a) holds and let
a,b\in K
such that
a<b
. Choose
N\in\mathbb{N}
such that

\frac{1}{b-a}<N
. Then
\frac{1}{N}<b-a
and hence
a+\frac{1}{N}<b
. Now choose the smallest
m\in\mathbb{Z}
such that

a<\frac{m}{N}
. If we had
\frac{m}{N}\geq b
, then
a+\frac{1}{N}<\frac{m}{N}
and therefore
a<\frac{m-1}{N}
, contradicting our choice of
m
.
Therefore
a<\frac{m}{N}<b
.
∎

Lemma 1.11
.

Let
(K,\leq)
be an Archimedean ordered field. Then

K=\left\{\lim_{n\to\infty}a_{n}\mid\text{$(a_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}$ sequence in $\mathbb{Q}$ that converges in $K$}\right\}.

Proof.

Let
a\in K
. We have to show that there is a sequence
(a_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
in
\mathbb{Q}
that converges in
K
to
a
. Choose
for every
n\in\mathbb{N}
according to
1.10
some
a_{n}\in\mathbb{Q}
such that
a\leq a_{n}<a+\frac{1}{n}
. Let
\varepsilon\in K_{>0}
. Choose

N\in\mathbb{N}
such that
\frac{1}{\varepsilon}<N
. For
n\geq N
we now have

|a_{n}-a|=a_{n}-a<\frac{1}{n}\leq\frac{1}{N}<\varepsilon
.
∎

Lemma 1.12
.

Suppose
(K,\leq)
is an Archimedean ordered field and
(a_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
is a sequence in
\mathbb{Q}
. Then the following are
equivalent:

(a)

(a_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
is a Cauchy sequence in
(\mathbb{Q},\leq_{\mathbb{Q}})

(b)

(a_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
is a Cauchy sequence in
(K,\leq)

Proof.

This follows easily from
1.10
.
∎

Exercise 1.13
.

Suppose
(K,\leq)
is an ordered field and
(a_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
,
(b_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
are convergent sequences in
K
. Then

\lim_{n\to\infty}(a_{n}+b_{n})=\left(\lim_{n\to\infty}a_{n}\right)+\left(\lim_{n\to\infty}b_{n}\right)\qquad\text{and}\qquad\lim_{n\to\infty}(a_{n}b_{n})=\left(\lim_{n\to\infty}a_{n}\right)\left(\lim_{n\to\infty}b_{n}\right).

Theorem 1.14
.

Let
(K,\leq)
be an ordered field. Then the following are equivalent:

(a)

(K,\leq)
is Archimedean and Cauchy complete

(b)

(K,\leq)
is complete

Proof.

(a)
\implies
(b)
Suppose (a) holds and let
A\subseteq K
be a nonempty subset bounded from above. Choose for
every
n\in\mathbb{N}
the smallest
k_{n}\in\mathbb{Z}
such that
\forall a\in A:a\leq\frac{k_{n}}{n}
and set
a_{n}:=\frac{k_{n}}{n}\in\mathbb{Q}
(use the Archimedean property!). Using again the Archimedean property, one can show easily
that
(a_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
is a Cauchy sequence and therefore convergent by hypothesis. We leave it as an exercise to the reader to show that

a:=\lim_{n\to\infty}a_{n}
is the least upper bound of
A
in
(K,\leq)
.

(b)
\implies
(a)
We prove the contraposition.

First, suppose that
(K,\leq)
is not Archimedean, i.e., the set

A:=\{a\in K\mid\forall N\in\mathbb{N}:a\leq-N\}

is not empty.
We claim that
A
does not have a least upper bound: Indeed, if
a\in K
is an upper bound of
A
, then so is
a-1<a
since

A=\{a\in K\mid\forall N\in\mathbb{Z}:a\leq N\}={\{a\in K\mid\forall N\in\mathbb{Z}:a+1\leq N\}}=\{a-1\mid a\in K,\forall N\in\mathbb{N}:a\leq N\}=A-1
.

Finally, suppose that
(K,\leq)
is not Cauchy complete, say
(a_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
is a Cauchy sequence in
K
that does not converge.
We claim that

A:=\{a\in K\mid\exists N\in\mathbb{N}:\forall n\geq N:a\leq a_{n}\}

is nonempty and bounded from above but does not possess a
least upper bound. We leave this as an exercise to the reader.
∎

Lemma 1.15
.

Suppose
(K,\leq)
is an Archimedean ordered field and
(R,\leq_{R})
a complete ordered field. Then there is exactly one
embedding
(K,\leq)\hookrightarrow(R,\leq_{R})
. This embedding is an isomorphism if and only if
(K,\leq)
is complete.

Proof.

Exercise.
∎

Theorem 1.16
.

There is a complete ordered field
(\mathbb{R},\leq)
. It is essentially unique, for if
{(K,\leq_{K})}
is another complete
ordered field, then there is exactly one
isomorphism from
(K,\leq_{K})
to
(\mathbb{R},\leq)
.

Proof.

The uniqueness is clear from
1.15
together with
1.14
. We only sketch the proof of existence and leave the details
as an exercise to the reader: Show that the Cauchy sequences in
\mathbb{Q}
form a subring
C
of
\mathbb{Q}^{\mathbb{N}}
and that

I:=\left\{(a_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\in C\mid\lim_{n\to\infty}a_{n}=0\right\}

is a maximal ideal of
C
. Set
\mathbb{R}:=C/I
. Show that

a\leq b:\iff\exists(a_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}},(b_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\text{ in }C:(a=\overline{(a_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}}^{\begin{subarray}{c}\vskip-4.09723pt{\scriptscriptstyle I}\end{subarray}}~\&~b=\overline{(b_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}}^{\begin{subarray}{c}\vskip-4.09723pt{\scriptscriptstyle I}\end{subarray}}~\&~\forall n\in\mathbb{N}:a_{n}\leq b_{n})

(
a,b\in\mathbb{R}
) defines an order
\leq
on
\mathbb{R}
. It is clear that
(\mathbb{R},\leq)
is Archimedean. By Theorem
1.14
it suffices to show that

(\mathbb{R},\leq)
is Cauchy complete. To this end, let
(a_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
be a Cauchy sequence in
(\mathbb{R},\leq)
. By
1.10
,
there exists a sequence
(q_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
in
\mathbb{Q}
such that

|a_{n}-q_{n}|<\frac{1}{n}
for
n\in\mathbb{N}
. Now deduce from the fact that
(a_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
is a Cauchy sequence in
(\mathbb{R},\leq)

that
(q_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
is such in
(\mathbb{R},\leq)
and hence also in
(\mathbb{Q},\leq)
. Now
(q_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\in C
. Set

a:=\overline{(q_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}}^{\begin{subarray}{c}\vskip-4.09723pt{\scriptscriptstyle\scriptstyle I}\end{subarray}}
. It is enough to show
\lim_{n\to\infty}a_{n}=a
. Finally show that this is equivalent to

\lim_{n\to\infty}q_{n}=a
in
(K,\leq)
and prove the latter.
∎

Corollary 1.17
.

(\mathbb{R},\leq)
is an Archimedean ordered field into which every Archimedean ordered field can be embedded. Up to isomorphy it is the only such ordered field.

Proof.

The first statement is clear from
1.14
,
1.15
and
1.16
. Uniqueness: Let
(K,\leq_{K})
be another such
ordered field. Then

(\mathbb{R},\leq)\overset{\varphi}{\hookrightarrow}(K,\leq_{K})\overset{\psi}{\hookrightarrow}(\mathbb{R},\leq)

and
\psi\circ\varphi
is the by
1.15

unique embedding
(\mathbb{R},\leq)\hookrightarrow(\mathbb{R},\leq)
, i.e.,
\psi\circ\varphi=\id
. This implies that
\psi
is surjective. Hence
(K,\leq_{K})\cong(\mathbb{R},\leq)
.
∎

Notation 1.18
.

Let
A
be a ring. Then we often use suggestive notation to describe certain subsets of
A
such as the following:

•

A^{2}=\{a^{2}\mid a\in A\}
(“squares”)

•

\sum A^{2}=\{\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}a_{i}^{2}\mid\ell\in\mathbb{N}_{0},a_{i}\in A\}
(“sums of squares”)

•

\sum A^{2}T=\left\{\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}a_{i}^{2}t_{i}\mid\ell\in\mathbb{N}_{0},a_{i}\in A,t_{i}\in T\right\}\qquad(T\subseteq A)

(“sums of elements of
T
weighted by squares”)

•

T+T=\{s+t\mid s,t\in T\}\qquad(T\subseteq A)

•

TT=\{st\mid s,t\in T\}\qquad(T\subseteq A)

•

-T=\{-t\mid t\in T\}\qquad(T\subseteq A)

•

T+aT=\{s+at\mid s,t\in T\}\qquad(T\subseteq A,a\in A)

Proposition 1.19
.

Let
K
be a field.

(a)

If
\leq
is an order of
K

[
\to

1.4
]
, then
P:=K_{\geq 0}=\{a\in K\mid a\geq 0\}
has the following properties:

(
*
)

P+P\subseteq P,\quad PP\subseteq P,\quad P\cup-P=K\quad\text{ and }\quad P\cap-P=\{0\}.

(b)

If
P
is a subset of
K
satisfying
(*)
, then the relation
\leq_{P}
on
K
defined by

a\leq_{P}b:\iff b-a\in P\qquad(a,b\in K)

is an order of
K
.

(c)

The correspondence

\displaystyle(\leq)

\displaystyle\mapsto K_{\geq 0}

\displaystyle(\leq_{P})

\displaystyle\mapsfrom P

defines a bijection between the set of all orders on
K
and the set of all subsets of
K
satisfying
(*)
.

Proof.

(a) We get
P\mathbin{\smash{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{$\underset{\smash{\cdot}}{\smash{+}}$}}}P\subseteq P
from the monotonicity of
\begin{Bmatrix}\text{addition}\\
\text{multiplication}\end{Bmatrix}
[
\to

1.4
],

P\cup-P=K
from the linearity [
\to

1.1
] and
P\cap-P=\{0\}
from the antisymmetry [
\to

1.1
].

(b) We get reflexivity from
0\in P
, transitivity from
P+P\subseteq P
, antisymmetry from
P\cap-P=\{0\}
, linearity from

P\cup-P=K
, monotonicity of addition from the definition of
\leq_{P}
and monotonicity of multiplication

PP\subseteq P
.

(c) Suppose first that
\leq
is an order of
K
and set
P:=K_{\geq 0}
. Then
(\leq)=(\leq_{P})
since

a\leq b\iff b-a\geq 0\iff b-a\in P\iff a\leq_{P}b
for all
a,b\in K
.
Conversely, let
P\subseteq K
be given such that
P
satisfies
(*)
. We show
K_{\geq_{P}\,0}=P
.
Indeed,

K_{\geq_{P}\,0}=\{a\in K\mid 0\leq_{P}a\}=\{a\in K\mid a\in P\}=P.

∎

Remark 1.20
.

1.19
(c) allows us to view orders of fields
K
as subsets of
K
. We reformulate some of the preceding notions and results in this new language:

(a)

Definition
1.4
: Let
K
be a field. An order of
K
is a subset
P
of
K
satisfying

P+P\subseteq P,\quad PP\subseteq P,\quad P\cup-P=K\quad\text{ and }\quad P\cap-P=\{0\}.

(b)

Definition
1.5
: Let
(K,P)
and
(L,Q)
be ordered fields. A field homomorphism
\varphi\colon K\to L
is called a
homomorphism or an embedding of ordered fields if
\varphi(P)\subseteq Q
. One calls
(K,P)
an ordered subfield of
(L,Q)
if

K
is a subfield of
L
and
P=Q\cap K
(or equivalently
P\subseteq Q
).

(c)

Proposition
1.6
: Let
(K,P)
be an ordered field. Then
K^{2}\subseteq P
.

(d)

Definition
1.9
: An ordered field
(K,P)
is called
Archimedean
if

\forall a\in K:\exists N\in\mathbb{N}:N+a\in P,

(
\iff P-\mathbb{N}=K\iff P+\mathbb{Z}=K\iff P+\mathbb{Q}=K
).

2
Preorders

Definition 2.1
.

Let
A
be a commutative ring and
T\subseteq A
. Then
T
is called a
preorder
of
A
if

A^{2}\subseteq T
,
T+T\subseteq T
and
TT\subseteq T
. If moreover
-1\notin T
, then
T
is called a
proper
preorder of
A
.

Example 2.2
.

(a)

If
A
is a commutative ring, then
\sum A^{2}
is the smallest preorder of
A
.

(b)

Every order of a field is a proper preorder.

Proposition 2.3
.

Let
A
be a commutative ring with
\frac{1}{2}\in A
(i.e.,
2\in A^{\times}
). Then

a=\left(\frac{a+1}{2}\right)^{2}-\left(\frac{a-1}{2}\right)^{2}

for all
a\in A
. In particular,
A=A^{2}-A^{2}
.

Definition and Proposition 2.4
.

Let
A
be a commutative ring with
\frac{1}{2}\in A
and
T\subseteq A
a preorder. Then the
support

T\cap-T
of

T
is an ideal of
A
.

Proof.

T\cap-T
is obviously a subgroup of (the additive group of)
A
and we have

\begin{array}[]{rcl}A(T\cap-T)&\overset{\ref{diffsquare}}{=}&(A^{2}-A^{2})(T\cap-T)\\
&\subseteq&(T-T)(T\cap-T)\\
&\subseteq&(T(T\cap-T))-(T(T\cap-T))\\
&\subseteq&((TT)\cap(-TT))+((-TT)\cap TT)\\
&\subseteq&(T\cap-T)+((-T)\cap T)\subseteq T\cap-T.\end{array}

∎

Corollary 2.5
.

Suppose
A
is a commutative ring with
\frac{1}{2}\in A
and
T\subseteq A
is a preorder. Then

\text{T is proper}\iff T\neq A.

Proof.

“
\Longrightarrow
” trivial

“
\Longleftarrow
” Suppose
T\neq A
. Then of course also
T\cap-T\neq A
. Since
T\cap-T
is an ideal, we have
1\notin T\cap-T
.
Since
1=1^{2}\in T
, it follows that
1\notin-T
, i.e.,
-1\notin T
.
∎

Example 2.6
.

In
2.3
,
2.4
and
2.5
, it is essential to require
\frac{1}{2}\in A
. Take for example

A=\mathbb{F}_{2}(X)
. Then
A^{2}=\mathbb{F}_{2}(X^{2})
since
\mathbb{F}_{2}(X)\to\mathbb{F}_{2}(X),\ p\mapsto p^{2}
is a homomorphism (Frobenius). Therefore
A^{2}-A^{2}=\mathbb{F}_{2}(X^{2})\neq\mathbb{F}_{2}(X)
. Moreover
T:=\mathbb{F}_{2}(X^{2})=\sum A^{2}
is a preorder of
A
but
T\cap-T=\mathbb{F}_{2}(X^{2})
is not an ideal of
A

(since
1\in T\cap-T\neq\mathbb{F}_{2}(X)
). Also
T
is not proper although
T\neq A
. The same is true for
\mathbb{F}_{2}[X]
instead of
\mathbb{F}_{2}(X)
and
from this one can get similar examples in the ring
\mathbb{Z}[X]
(exercise).

Proposition 2.7
.

Let
K
be a field and
T\subseteq K
a preorder. Then

\text{T is proper}\iff T\cap-T=\{0\}.

Proof.

If
\chara K=2
, then
-1=1\in T\cap-T
. Therefore suppose now
\chara K\neq 2
. Then

-1\notin T\overset{1\in T}{\iff}1\notin T\cap-T\overset{\ref{supportideal}}{\underset{\begin{subarray}{c}\text{$K$ field}\\
\chara K\neq 2\end{subarray}}{\iff}}T\cap-T=\{0\}.

∎

Lemma 2.8
.

Suppose
A
is a commutative ring,
T\subseteq A
a preorder and
a\in A
. Then
T+aT
is again a preorder.

Proof.

A^{2}\subseteq T\subseteq T+aT
,
(T+aT)+(T+aT)\subseteq(T+T)+a(T+T)\subseteq T+aT
and

(T+aT)(T+aT)\subseteq TT+aTT+aTT+a^{2}TT\subseteq T+aT+aT+A^{2}T\subseteq T+a(T+T)+TT\subseteq T+aT+T\subseteq T+aT

∎

Theorem 2.9
.

Let
K
be a field and
P\subseteq K
. Then the following are equivalent:

(a)

P
is an order of
K

[
\to

1.20
]
.

(b)

P
is a proper preorder of
K

[
\to

2.1
]
such that
P\cup-P=K
.

(c)

P
is a maximal proper preorder of
K
.

Proof.

(a)
\implies
(b)

2.2
(b)

(b)
\implies
(c)
Suppose (b) holds and let
T
be a proper preorder of
K
with
P\subseteq T
. To show:
T\subseteq P
.
To this end, let
a\in T
. If
a
was not in
P
, then
-a\in P\subseteq T
(since
P\cup-P=K
) and therefore

a\in T\cap-T\overset{\ref{propfield}}{=}\{0\}
in contradiction to
0=0^{2}\in P
.

(c)
\implies
(a)
Suppose (c) holds. Because of
2.7
, we have to show only
P\cup-P=K
. Assume
P\cup-P\neq K
.
Choose then
a\in K
such that
a\notin P
and
-a\notin P
. Then
P+aP
and
P-aP
are preorders according to Lemma
2.8

and both contain
P
properly (note that
0,1\in P
). Because of the maximality of
P
none of
P+aP
and
P-aP
is proper, i.e.,

-1\in P+aP
and
-1\in P-aP
. Write
-1=s+as^{\prime}
and
-1=t-at^{\prime}
for certain
s,s^{\prime},t,t^{\prime}\in P
. Then

-as^{\prime}=1+s
and
at^{\prime}=1+t
. It follows that
-a^{2}s^{\prime}t^{\prime}=1+s+t+st
and therefore

-1=s+t+st+a^{2}s^{\prime}t^{\prime}\in P+P+PP+A^{2}PP\subseteq P\ \lightning
.
∎

Theorem 2.10
.

Let
K
be a field and
T\subseteq K
a proper preorder. Then there is an order
P
of
K
such that
T\subseteq P
and we have

T=\bigcap\{P\mid\text{$P$ order of $K$},T\subseteq P\}
.

Proof.

Consider the partially ordered set of all proper preorders of
K
containing
T
. In this partially ordered set, every chain
has an upper bound (the empty chain has
T
as an upper bound and every nonempty chain possesses its union as an upper bound).
By Zorn’s lemma, the partially ordered set has a maximal element. Every such element is obviously a maximal proper preorder and therefore by

2.9
an order. Now we turn to the second statement:

“
\subseteq
” is clear.

“
\supseteq
” Let
a\in K\setminus T
. To show: There is an order
P
of
K
with
T\subseteq P
and
a\notin P
.
By
2.8
,
T-aT
is a preorder. It is proper for otherwise there would be
s,t\in T
with
-1=s-at
and it would follow that

t\neq 0
,
at=1+s
and
a=\left(\frac{1}{t}\right)^{2}t(1+s)\in K^{2}TT\subseteq T
. By the already proved, there is an order
P
of
K

with
T-aT\subseteq P
. If
a
lied in
P
, then
a\in P\cap-P=\{0\}
in contradiction to
a\notin T
.
∎

Definition 2.11
.

A field is called
real
(in older literature mostly
formally real
) if it admits an order.

Theorem 2.12
.

Let
K
be a field. Then the following are equivalent:

(a)

K
is real.

(b)

-1\notin\sum K^{2}

(c)

\forall n\in\mathbb{N}:\forall a_{1},\dots,a_{n}\in K:(a_{1}^{2}+\dots+a_{n}^{2}=0\implies a_{1}=0)

Proof.

(a)
\implies
(b)
follows from
1.6
.

(b)
\implies
(a)
By
2.2
,
\sum K^{2}
is a preorder. If it is proper, then it is contained
in an order by
2.10
.

(b)
\iff
(c)

\displaystyle-1\in\sum K^{2}

\displaystyle\iff\exists n\in\mathbb{N}:\exists a_{2},\dots,a_{n}\in K:-1=a_{2}^{2}+\dots+a_{n}^{2}

\displaystyle\iff\exists n\in\mathbb{N}:\exists a_{2},\dots,a_{n}\in K:1^{2}+a_{2}^{2}+\dots+a_{n}^{2}=0

\displaystyle\iff\exists n\in\mathbb{N}:\exists a_{1}\in K^{\times}:\exists a_{2},\dots,a_{n}\in K:a_{1}^{2}+a_{2}^{2}+\dots+a_{n}^{2}=0

∎

Example 2.13
.

Because of
-1=\mathbbm{i}^{2}\in\sum\mathbb{C}^{2}
, the field
\mathbb{C}:=\mathbb{R}(\mathbbm{i})
does not admit an order.

3
Extensions of orders

Definition 3.1
.

Let
(K,P)
be an ordered field and
L
an extension field of
K
(or in other words: let
L|K
be
a field extension and
P
be an order of
K
). We call
Q
an
extension
of the order
P
to
L
if
the following equivalent conditions are fulfilled [
\to

1.20
(b)]:

(a)

(L,Q)
is an ordered extension field of
(K,P)
.

(b)

Q
is an order of
L
such that
P\subseteq Q
.

(c)

Q
is an order of
L
such that
Q\cap K=P
.

Theorem 3.2
.

Let
(K,P)
be an ordered field and
L
an extension field of
K
. Then the order
P
of
K
can be extended
to
L
if and only if
-1\notin\sum L^{2}P
.

Proof.

Since every order is a preorder [
\to

2.2
], an order of
L
contains
P
if and only if it contains
the preorder generated in
L
by
P
(i.e., the smallest preorder of
L
containing
P
, or in other words, the
intersection of all preorders of
L
containing
P
), namely
\sum L^{2}P
. If
\sum L^{2}P
is not proper,
then there is of course no order of
L
containing it. On the contrary, if
\sum L^{2}P
is proper, then there is
such an order by Theorem
2.10
.
∎

Reminder 3.3
.

Let
L|K
be a field extension with
\chara K\neq 2
. Then

[L:K]\leq 2\iff\exists d\in K:L=K(\sqrt{d})

since for
x\in L
and
a,b,c\in K
with
a\neq 0
and

ax^{2}+bx+c=0
we have
(2ax+b)^{2}=4a(ax^{2}+bx)+b^{2}=b^{2}-4ac=:d
and therefore

K(x)=K(2ax+b)=K(\sqrt{d})
.

Theorem 3.4
.

Let
(K,P)
be an ordered field and
d\in K
. The order
P
can be extended to
K(\sqrt{d})
if and only if

d\in P
.

Proof.

If
\sqrt{d}\in K
, then
d=(\sqrt{d})^{2}\in P
. Suppose now that
\sqrt{d}\notin K
. Set
L:=K(\sqrt{d})
.
Because of
P+dP\subseteq\sum L^{2}P\subseteq P+dP+K\sqrt{d}
, we have

-1\notin\sum L^{2}P\iff-1\notin P+dP
. Since
P
is a maximal proper preorder by

2.9
and
P+dP
is a preorder by
2.8
, we obtain

-1\notin P+dP\iff P=P+dP\iff d\in P
. Combining, we get
-1\notin\sum L^{2}P\iff d\in P
and we conclude
by Theorem
3.2
.
∎

Example 3.5
.

In
3.4
, the extension is in general not unique:
\mathbb{Q}(\sqrt{2})
admits exactly two
orders, namely the ones induced by the two field embeddings
\mathbb{Q}(\sqrt{2})\hookrightarrow\mathbb{R}

(in the one
\sqrt{2}
is positive, in the other negative). That it does not admit a third one, follows from the
fact that for every order
P
of
\mathbb{Q}(\sqrt{2})
we have by
1.17

(\mathbb{Q}(\sqrt{2}),P)\hookrightarrow(\mathbb{R},\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0})
because
P
is Archimedean since

\mathbb{Q}(\sqrt{2})=\mathbb{Q}+\mathbb{Q}\sqrt{2}
and

|\sqrt{2}|_{P}-1\overset{\ref{diffsquare}}{=}\left(\frac{|\sqrt{2}|_{P}}{2}\right)^{2}-\left(\frac{|\sqrt{2}|_{P}-2}{2}\right)^{2}\overset{\ref{squares}}{\leq}_{P}\left(\frac{|\sqrt{2}|_{P}}{2}\right)^{2}=\frac{1}{2}

[
\to

1.9
(a)].

Theorem 3.6
.

If
L|K
is a field extension of odd degree, then each order of
K
can be extended to
L
.

Proof.

Assume the claim does not hold. Then there is a counterexample
L|K
with
[L:K]=2n+1
for some

n\in\mathbb{N}
. We choose the counterexample in a way such that
n
is as small as possible.
We will now produce another counterexample
L^{\prime}|K
with
[L^{\prime}:K]\leq 2n-1
which will contradict
the minimality of
n
. Due to
\chara K=0
, we have that
L|K
is separable. By the primitive element theorem,
there is some
a\in L
with
L=K(a)=K[a]
. The condition
-1\in\sum L^{2}P
which is satisfied by

3.2
translates via the isomorphism

K[X]/(f)\to L,\ \overline{g}\mapsto g(a)
in

(
*
)

1+\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}a_{i}g_{i}^{2}=hf

with
\ell\in\mathbb{N}
,
a_{i}\in P
,
g_{i},h\in K[X]
, where
f
denotes the minimal
polynomial of
a
over
K
(in particular
\deg f=[K(a):K]=[L:K]=2n+1
) and the
g_{i}
are chosen in such a way
that
\deg g_{i}\leq 2n
. Each of the
\ell+1
terms in the sum on the left hand side of
(*)
has an
even

degree
\leq 4n
and a leading coefficient from
PK^{2}\subseteq P
(except those terms that are zero of course).
Since
P
is an order, the monomials of highest degree appearing on the left hand side of
(*)
cannot cancel
out. So the left hand side and therefore also the right hand side of
(*)
has an
even
degree
\leq 4n
.
It follows that
h
has an
odd
degree
\leq 2n-1
. Choose an irreducible divisor
h_{1}
of
h
in

K[X]
of
odd
degree and a root
b
of
h_{1}
in an extension field of
K
(e.g., in the
splitting field of
h_{1}
over
K
or in the algebraic closure of
K
). Set
L^{\prime}:=K(b)
.
Substituting
b
in
(*)
yields
-1=\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}a_{i}g_{i}(b)^{2}\in\sum PL^{\prime 2}
.
By
3.2
,
P
cannot be extended to
L^{\prime}
. Since

[L^{\prime}:K]=[K(b):K]=\deg\irr_{K}b=\deg h_{1}\leq 2n-1
is
odd
, we gain the desired still smaller counterexample.
∎

Theorem 3.7
.

Let
K
be a field. Then every order of
K
can be extended to
K(X)
.

Proof.

Let
P
be an order of
K
. Assume that
P
cannot be extended to
K(X)
. By
3.2

we then have
-1\in\sum K(X)^{2}P
. Because of
\#K=\infty
[
\to

1.7
] we can plug in a suitable
point from
K
(“avoid finitely many poles”) and get
-1\in\sum K^{2}P=P\ \lightning
.
∎

Example 3.8
.

Due to
3.7
there is an order on
\mathbb{R}(X)
. If
P
is such an order, then by the completeness
of
(\mathbb{R},\leq)
[
\to

1.16
], the set
\mathbb{R}_{\leq_{P}X}=\{a\in\mathbb{R}\mid a\leq_{P}X\}
is either empty or
not bounded from above (in which case it is
\mathbb{R}
) or it has a supremum
t
in
\mathbb{R}
(in which case it equals

(-\infty,t)
if
t>_{P}X
or
(-\infty,t]
if
t<_{P}X
). Hence

\mathbb{R}_{\leq_{P}X}=\{a\in\mathbb{R}\mid a\leq_{P}X\}\in\{\emptyset\}\cup\{(-\infty,t)\mid t\in\mathbb{R}\}\cup\{(-\infty,t]\mid t\in\mathbb{R}\}\cup\{\mathbb{R}\}=:C.

We claim now that the map

\displaystyle\Phi\colon\{P\mid P\text{ order of $\mathbb{R}(X)$}\}

\displaystyle\to C

\displaystyle P

\displaystyle\mapsto\mathbb{R}_{\leq_{P}X}

is a bijection. It is easy to see that for all
I,J\in C
there is a ring automorphism
\varphi_{I,J}
of
\mathbb{R}(X)
such that
for all orders
P
of
\mathbb{R}(X)
, we have

\Phi(P)=I\iff\Phi(\varphi_{I,J}(P))=J:

•

I=\mathbb{R}\mathbin{\&}J=(-\infty,0]\leadsto\varphi_{I,J}\colon X\mapsto\frac{1}{X}

•

I=\emptyset\mathbin{\&}J=(-\infty,0)\leadsto\varphi_{I,J}\colon X\mapsto\frac{1}{X}

•

I=(-\infty,t)\mathbin{\&}J=(-\infty,0)\leadsto\varphi_{I,J}\colon X\mapsto X+t

•

I=(-\infty,t]\mathbin{\&}J=(-\infty,0]\leadsto\varphi_{I,J}\colon X\mapsto X+t

•

I=(-\infty,0)\mathbin{\&}J=(-\infty,0]\leadsto\varphi_{I,J}\colon X\mapsto-X

•

other
I
and
J

\leadsto
composition of the above automorphisms

From this we get the
surjectivity
of
\Phi
, since as already mentioned there is an order
P
of
\mathbb{R}(X)

and if we set
I:=\Phi(P)
, then
\Phi(\varphi_{I,J}(P))=J
for all
J\in C
. For the
injectivity
of
\Phi
, it suffices
to show that
there is
some
I\in C
having only one preimage under
\Phi
since then

\displaystyle\#\{P\mid\Phi(P)=J\}

\displaystyle=\#\{P\mid\Phi(\varphi_{J,I}(P))=I\}

\displaystyle=\#\{\varphi_{J,I}(P)\mid\Phi(\varphi_{J,I}(P))=I\}=\#\{P\mid\Phi(P)=I\}=1

for all
J\in C
. We therefore fix
I:=\mathbb{R}\in C
and show that there at most (and therefore exactly) one order

P
of
\mathbb{R}(X)
such that
\Phi(P)=I
. To this end, suppose
\Phi(P)=I
. If
f,g\in\mathbb{R}[X]\setminus\{0\}
, then
one easily verifies that

\frac{f}{g}\in P\overset{\mathbb{R}(X)^{2}\subseteq P}{\iff}fg\in P\iff\text{the leading coefficient of $fg$ is positive.}

This uniquely determines
P
. Consequently,
\Phi
is a bijection. We fix the following notation:

\displaystyle P_{-\infty}

\displaystyle:=\Phi^{-1}(\emptyset)

\displaystyle P_{t-}

\displaystyle:=\Phi^{-1}((-\infty,t))\text{ for $t\in\mathbb{R}$}

\displaystyle P_{t+}

\displaystyle:=\Phi^{-1}((-\infty,t])\text{ for $t\in\mathbb{R}$}

\displaystyle P_{\infty}

\displaystyle:=\Phi^{-1}(\mathbb{R})

Now
\{P\mid P\text{ order of }\mathbb{R}(X)\}=\{P_{-\infty}\}\cup\{P_{t-},P_{t+}\mid t\in\mathbb{R}\}\cup\{P_{\infty}\}
.
By easy considerations one obtains,

\displaystyle P_{-\infty}

\displaystyle=\{r\in\mathbb{R}(X)\mid\exists c\in\mathbb{R}:\forall x\in(-\infty,c):r(x)\geq 0\},

\displaystyle P_{t-}

\displaystyle=\{r\in\mathbb{R}(X)\mid\exists\varepsilon\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}:\forall x\in(t-\varepsilon,t):r(x)\geq 0\}\qquad(t\in\mathbb{R}),

\displaystyle P_{t+}

\displaystyle=\{r\in\mathbb{R}(X)\mid\exists\varepsilon\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}:\forall x\in(t,t+\varepsilon):r(x)\geq 0\}\qquad(t\in\mathbb{R}),

\displaystyle P_{\infty}

\displaystyle=\{r\in\mathbb{R}(X)\mid\exists c\in\mathbb{R}:\forall x\in(c,\infty):r(x)\geq 0\}.

None of these orders is Archimedean.

4
Real closed fields

Proposition 4.1
.

Let
K
be a field. Then the following are equivalent:

(a)

K
admits exactly one order.

(b)

\sum K^{2}
is an order of
K
.

(c)

(\sum K^{2})\cup(-\sum K^{2})=K
and
-1\notin\sum K^{2}

Proof.

(a)
\implies
(b)
Suppose
P
is the unique order of
K
. By
2.2
and
2.10
, we then get
\sum K^{2}=P
.

(b)
\implies
(c)
is trivial.

(c)
\implies
(a)
Suppose (c) holds. Using
1.20
(a) and
2.7
, we see that
\sum K^{2}
is an order of
K
, and it is the only one by

2.2
and
2.9
(b).
∎

Example 4.2
.

\mathbb{Q}
and
\mathbb{R}
possess exactly one order.

Convention 4.3
.

If
K
is a field admitting exactly one order, then we will often understand
K
as an ordered field, that is we speak of
K
but mean
(K,\sum K^{2})
.

Definition 4.4
.

A field
K
is called
Euclidean
if
K^{2}
is an order of
K
.

Remark 4.5
.

If
K
is Euclidean, then
K^{2}
is the unique order of
K
.

Example 4.6
.

\mathbb{R}
is Euclidean but not
\mathbb{Q}
.

Notation and Remark 4.7
.

(a)

Let
(K,\leq)
be an ordered field. If
a,b\in K
such that
a=b^{2}
, then we write

\sqrt{a}:=|b|\in K_{\geq 0}
[
\to

1.8
] (this is obviously well-defined). If
a\in K\setminus K^{2}
, we continue to denote by
\sqrt{a}\in\overline{K}\setminus K
an
arbitrary but fixed square root of
a
in the algebraic closure
\overline{K}
of
K
. One shows easily that
a\leq b\iff\sqrt{a}\leq\sqrt{b}
for all
a,b\in K^{2}
.

(b)

If
K
is a Euclidean field (with order
\leq
[
\to

4.3
,
4.5
]), then in particular
\sqrt{a}\in K_{\geq 0}
and
(\sqrt{a})^{2}=a
for

all

a\in K_{\geq 0}=K^{2}=\sum K^{2}
.

(c)

We write
\mathbbm{i}:=\sqrt{-1}
. If
K
is a real field, then
K(\mathbbm{i})=K\oplus K\mathbbm{i}
as a
K
-vector space

Proposition 4.8
.

Let
K
be a real field. Then the following are equivalent:

(a)

K
is Euclidean.

(b)

K=-K^{2}\cup K^{2}

(c)

K(\mathbbm{i})=K(\mathbbm{i})^{2}

(d)

Every polynomial of degree
2
in
K(\mathbbm{i})[X]
has a root in
K(\mathbbm{i})
.

Proof.

(d)
\implies
(c)
is trivial.

(c)
\implies
(b)
Suppose (c) holds and let
a\in K
. Write
a=(b+\mathbbm{i}c)^{2}
for some
b,c\in K
. Then
a=b^{2}-c^{2}
and
bc=0

[
\to

4.7
(c)]. Therefore
a=b^{2}
or
a=-c^{2}
.

(b)
\implies
(a)
Suppose (b) holds. It suffices to show
K^{2}+K^{2}\subseteq K^{2}
. For this purpose, let
a,b\in K
. To show:
a^{2}+b^{2}\in K^{2}
.
If we had
a^{2}+b^{2}\notin K^{2}
, then
a^{2}+b^{2}\in-K^{2}
, say
a^{2}+b^{2}+c^{2}=0
for some
c\in K
and
2.12
(c) would imply
c=0\ \lightning
.

(a)
\implies
(c)
Suppose (a) holds and let
a,b\in K
. By
4.7
(c), we have to show
a+b\mathbbm{i}\in K(\mathbbm{i})^{2}
. Set
r:=\sqrt{a^{2}+b^{2}}\in K_{\geq 0}

according to
4.7
(b). Then
r^{2}=a^{2}+b^{2}\geq a^{2}=|a|^{2}
and therefore
r\geq|a|
by
4.7
(a), i.e.,
r\pm a\geq 0
. It follows that

\sqrt{\frac{r+a}{2}},\sqrt{\frac{r-a}{2}}\in K_{\geq 0}
and the calculation

\left(\sqrt{\frac{r+a}{2}}\pm\sqrt{\frac{r-a}{2}}\mathbbm{i}\right)^{2}=\frac{r+a}{2}\pm 2\sqrt{\frac{r^{2}-a^{2}}{4}}\mathbbm{i}-\frac{r-a}{2}=a\pm 2\left|\frac{b}{2}\right|\mathbbm{i}=a\pm|b|\mathbbm{i}

shows
a+b\mathbbm{i}\in K(\mathbbm{i})^{2}
.

(c)
\implies
(d)
follows from
X^{2}+bX+c=(X+\frac{b}{2})^{2}+(c-\frac{b^{2}}{4})
for
b,c\in K(\mathbbm{i})
.
∎

Definition 4.9
.

Let
R
be a field. Then
R
is called
real closed
if
R
is Euclidean [
\to

4.4
,
4.8
] and every polynomial of
odd
degree from
R[X]

has a root in
R
.

Example 4.10
.

\mathbb{R}
is real closed by the intermediate value theorem from calculus and by
4.4
.

Remark 4.11
.

We now generalize the fundamental theorem of algebra from
\mathbb{C}=\mathbb{R}(\mathbbm{i})
to
R(\mathbbm{i})
for any real closed field
R
. The usual Galois theoretic proof
goes through as we will
see immediately.

Theorem 4.12

(“generalized fundamental theorem of algebra”)
.

Let
R
be a real closed field. Then
C:=R(\mathbbm{i})
is algebraically closed.

Proof.

Let
z\in\overline{C}
. To show:
z\in C
. Choose an intermediate field
L
of
\overline{C}|C
with
z\in L
such that

L|R
is a finite Galois extension (e.g., the splitting field of
(X^{2}+1)\irr_{R}z
over
R
). We show
L=C
.
Choose a
2
-Sylow subgroup
H
of the Galois group
G:=\Aut(L|R)
. From Galois theory we know that
[L^{H}:R]=[G:H]
is odd. Hence
L^{H}=R
since
every element of
L^{H}
has over
R
a minimal polynomial of odd degree which has a root in
R
and therefore must be linear. Galois theory then implies
G=H
.
Hence
G
is a
2
-group. Therefore the subgroup
I:=\Aut(L|C)
of
G
is also a
2
-group. By Galois theory, it is enough to show
I=\{1\}
. If we had
I\neq\{1\}
, then
there would exist, as one knows from algebra, a subgroup
J
of
I
with
[I:J]=2
. From this we get with Galois theory

[L^{J}:C]=[L^{J}:L^{I}]=[I:J]=2
, contradicting
4.8
(d).
∎

Theorem 4.13
.

Let
R
be a field. Then the following are equivalent:

(a)

R
is real closed.

(b)

R\neq R(\mathbbm{i})
and
R(\mathbbm{i})
is algebraically closed.

(c)

R
is real but there is no real extension field
L\neq R
of
R
such that
L|R
is algebraic.

Proof.

(a)
\implies
(b)
follows from
4.12
.

(b)
\implies
(c)
Suppose (b) holds. In order to show that
R
is real, it is enough to show by Theorem
2.12
that
\sum R^{2}=R^{2}
since

-1\notin R^{2}
because
R\neq R(\mathbbm{i})
. To this end, let
a,b\in R
. To show:
a^{2}+b^{2}\in R^{2}
. Since
R(\mathbbm{i})
is algebraically closed, we have
a+b\mathbbm{i}\in R(\mathbbm{i})^{2}
, that is
there are
c,d\in R
such that
a+b\mathbbm{i}=(c+d\mathbbm{i})^{2}
and it follows that

a^{2}+b^{2}=(a+b\mathbbm{i})(a-b\mathbbm{i})=(c+d\mathbbm{i})^{2}(c-d\mathbbm{i})^{2}=((c+d\mathbbm{i})(c-d\mathbbm{i}))^{2}=(c^{2}+d^{2})^{2}\in R^{2}
. Now let
L|R
be an algebraic field extension and suppose
L
is real.
To show:
L=R
. Since
L(\mathbbm{i})|R(\mathbbm{i})
is again algebraic and
R(\mathbbm{i})
is algebraically closed, we obtain
L(\mathbbm{i})=R(\mathbbm{i})
. For this reason
L
is a real intermediate field of

R(\mathbbm{i})|R
and it follows that
L=R
.

(c)
\implies
(a)
Suppose (c) holds. Choose an order
P
of
R
according to Definition
2.11
. For all
d\in P
,
R(\sqrt{d})
is real
by
3.4
and therefore
R(\sqrt{d})=R
. It follows that
P\subseteq R^{2}\subseteq P
and hence
P=R^{2}
, i.e.,
R
is Euclidean. According to Definition

4.9
it remains to show that each polynomial
f\in R[X]
of odd degree has a root in
R
. Let
f\in R[X]
be of odd degree. Choose an irreducible divisor

g
of
f
in
R[X]
of odd degree. Choose a root
a
of
g
in an extension field of
R
. Since
[R(a):R]=\deg g
is odd,
R(a)
is real by
3.6
and therefore

R(a)=R
. Thus
a\in R
satisfies
g(a)=0
and hence
f(a)=0
.
∎

Theorem 4.14

(“real version of the generalized fundamental theorem of algebra”)
.

Let
R
be a field. Then the following are equivalent:

(a)

R
is real closed.

(b)

\{f\in R[X]\mid\text{$f$ is irreducible and monic}\}=\\
\{X-a\mid a\in R\}\cup\{(X-a)^{2}+b^{2}\mid a,b\in R,b\neq 0\}

Proof.

(a)
\implies
(b)
Suppose (a) holds.

“
\supseteq
” is clear since
R
is real.

“
\subseteq
” Let
f\in R[X]
be irreducible and monic of degree
\geq 2
. Since
R(\mathbbm{i})
is algebraically closed by
4.12
, there are
a,b\in R
such that
f(a+b\mathbbm{i})=0
.
Due to
R\neq R(\mathbbm{i})
we can apply the automorphism of the field extension
R(\mathbbm{i})|R
given by
\mathbbm{i}\mapsto-\mathbbm{i}
in order to obtain
f(a-b\mathbbm{i})=0
.
By observing
a+b\mathbbm{i}\neq a-b\mathbbm{i}
(since
b\neq 0
because
f\in R[X]
is irreducible of degree
\geq 2
), we get

f=\underbrace{(X-(a+b\mathbbm{i}))(X-(a-b\mathbbm{i}))}_{(X-a)^{2}+b^{2}\in R[X]}g

for some
g\in R(\mathbbm{i})[X]
. But then
g\in R[X]
and hence even
g=1
.

(b)
\implies
(a)
Suppose (b) holds. We will show
4.13
(b), i.e., that
R\neq R(\mathbbm{i})
and
R(\mathbbm{i})
is algebraically closed. The first claim
R\neq R(\mathbbm{i})
follows
from the irreducibility of
X^{2}+1=(X-0)^{2}+1^{2}\in R[X]
. Now suppose
f\in R(\mathbbm{i})[X]
is of degree
\geq 1
.
Consider the ring automorphism

R(\mathbbm{i})[X]\to R(\mathbbm{i})[X],\ p\mapsto p^{*}

given by
a^{*}=a
for
a\in R
,
\mathbbm{i}^{*}=-\mathbbm{i}
and
X^{*}=X
. Then
f^{*}f\in R[X]
. If
f^{*}f
has a root
a\in R
,
then
f(a)=0
or
f^{*}(a)=0
and then again
f(a)=0
. Suppose therefore that
f^{*}f
has no root in
R
. Then there exist
a,b\in R
with
b\neq 0

such that
(X-a)^{2}+b^{2}
divides
f^{*}f
in
R[X]
. Because of
(X-a)^{2}+b^{2}=(X-(a+b\mathbbm{i}))(X-(a-b\mathbbm{i}))
,
a+b\mathbbm{i}
is a root of
f
or of
f^{*}
.
If
f^{*}(a+b\mathbbm{i})=0
, then
f(a-\mathbbm{i}b)=f^{**}((a+\mathbbm{i}b)^{*})=(f^{*}(a+\mathbbm{i}b))^{*}=0^{*}=0
. Therefore
a+\mathbbm{i}b
or
a-\mathbbm{i}b
is a root of
f
in
R(\mathbbm{i})
.
∎

Notation and Terminology 4.15
.

Let
(K,\leq)
be an ordered field.

(a)

We extend the order
\leq
in the obvious way to the set
\{-\infty\}\cup K\cup\{\infty\}
by declaring
-\infty<a<\infty
for all
a\in K
.

(b)

We adopt the usual notation for intervals

\displaystyle(a,b)

\displaystyle:=(a,b)_{K}:=\{x\in K\cup\{\pm\infty\}\mid a<x<b\}\qquad(a,b\in K\cup\{\pm\infty\})

(“interval from
a
to
b
without endpoints”)

\displaystyle[a,b)

\displaystyle:=[a,b)_{K}:=\{x\in K\cup\{\pm\infty\}\mid a\leq x<b\}\qquad(a,b\in K\cup\{\pm\infty\})

(“interval from
a
to
b
with
a
and without
b
”)

and so forth.

(c)

We use terminology like

f\geq 0
on
S

\displaystyle:\iff\forall x\in S:f(x)\geq 0\qquad(f\in K[X_{1},\dots,X_{n}],S\subseteq K^{n})

(“
f
is nonnegative on
S
”)

f>0
on
S

\displaystyle:\iff\forall x\in S:f(x)>0\qquad(f\in K[X_{1},\dots,X_{n}],S\subseteq K^{n})

(“
f
is positive on
S
”).

Corollary 4.16

(“intermediate value theorem for polynomials”)
.

Let
R
be a real closed field,
f\in R[X]
and
a,b\in R
such that
a\leq b
and
\sgn(f(a))\neq\sgn(f(b))
. Then there is
c\in[a,b]_{R}
with
f(c)=0
.

Proof.

WLOG
f
is monic. By
4.14
, all nonlinear monic irreducible polynomials in
R[X]
are positive on
R
. Hence

f=g\prod_{i=1}^{k}(X-a_{i})^{\alpha_{i}}
with
k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
,
a_{i}\in R
,
\alpha_{i}\in\mathbb{N}
,
a_{1}<\dots<a_{k}
and some
g\in R[X]
that is positive on
R
. On the sets

(-\infty,a_{1})
,
(a_{1},a_{2})
, …,
(a_{k-1},a_{k})
and
(a_{k},\infty)
each
X-a_{i}
and therefore
f
has constant sign. Hence
a
and
b
cannot lie
both in the same such set. Consequently, there is an
i\in\{1,\dots,k\}
such that
a_{i}\in[a,b]
. Set
c:=a_{i}
.
∎

Corollary 4.17

(“Rolle’s theorem for polynomials”)
.

Suppose
R
is a real closed field,
f\in R[X]
and
a,b\in R
with
a<b
and
f(a)=f(b)
.
Then there exists a
c\in(a,b)_{R}
such that
f^{\prime}(c)=0
.

Proof.

WLOG
f\neq 0
,
f(a)=0=f(b)
and
\nexists x\in(a,b):f(x)=0
. Write

f=(X-a)^{\alpha}(X-b)^{\beta}g

for some
\alpha,\beta\in\mathbb{N}
and
g\in R[X]
with

\forall x\in[a,b]:g(x)\neq 0
. We find

\displaystyle f^{\prime}

\displaystyle=(X-a)^{\alpha}\beta(X-b)^{\beta-1}g+\alpha(X-a)^{\alpha-1}(X-b)^{\beta}g+(X-a)^{\alpha}(X-b)^{\beta}g^{\prime}

\displaystyle=(X-a)^{\alpha-1}(X-b)^{\beta-1}h

where
h:=\beta(X-a)g+\alpha(X-b)g+(X-a)(X-b)g^{\prime}
. Hence it suffices to find
c\in(a,b)
such that
h(c)=0
. We can apply the intermediate value theorem
4.16
because

h(a)=\alpha(a-b)g(a)\quad\text{and}\quad h(b)=\beta(b-a)g(b)

and again by
4.16
we have
\sgn(g(a))=\sgn(g(b))
.
∎

Corollary 4.18

(“mean value theorem for polynomials”)
.

Let
R
be a real closed field,
f\in R[X]
and
a,b\in R
with
a<b
. Then there is some
c\in(a,b)_{R}
satisfying
f^{\prime}(c)=\frac{f(b)-f(a)}{b-a}
.

Proof.

Setting
g:=(X-a)(f(b)-f(a))-(b-a)(f-f(a))
, we get
g(a)=0=g(b)
and
g^{\prime}=f(b)-f(a)-(b-a)f^{\prime}
.
Rolle’s theorem
4.17
yields
c\in(a,b)
such that
g^{\prime}(c)=0
.
∎

Definition 4.19
.

(a)

Let
(M,\leq_{1})
and
(N,\leq_{2})
be ordered sets. A map
\varphi\colon M\to N
is called
anti-monotonic
[
\to

1.2
] if

a\leq_{1}b\implies\varphi(a)\geq_{2}\varphi(b)

for all
a,b\in M
.

(b)

If
(K,\leq)
is an ordered field,
f\in K[X]
and
I\subseteq K
, then we say that

f
is
\begin{Bmatrix}\text{monotonic}\\
\text{injective}\\
\text{anti-monotonic}\end{Bmatrix}
on
I
if
I\to K,\ x\mapsto f(x)
is
\begin{Bmatrix}\text{monotonic}\\
\text{injective}\\
\text{anti-monotonic}\end{Bmatrix}
.

Corollary 4.20
.

Let
R
be a real closed field,
f\in R[X]
and
a,b\in R
.
If
\begin{Bmatrix}f^{\prime}\geq 0\\
f^{\prime}\leq 0\end{Bmatrix}
on
(a,b)

[
\to

4.15
(c)]
, then
f
is
\begin{Bmatrix}\text{}\\
\text{anti-}\end{Bmatrix}
monotonic on
[a,b]
.
If
f^{\prime}
has no root on
(a,b)
, then
f
is injective on
[a,b]
.

Proof.

The statement is empty in case
a>b
, trivial in the case
a=b
and it follows from the mean value theorem
4.18
in case
a<b
.
∎

5
Descartes’ rule of signs

Notation 5.1
.

Let
A
be a commutative ring with
0\neq 1
and
d\in\mathbb{R}
. We denote

A[X_{1},\dots,X_{n}]_{d}:=\{f\in A[X_{1},\dots,X_{n}]\mid\deg f\leq d\}

(where
\deg 0:=-\infty
).

Proposition 5.2

(“Taylor formula for polynomials”)
.

Suppose
K
is a field of characteristic
0
,
d\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
,
f\in K[X]_{d}
and
a\in K
. Then

f=\sum_{k=0}^{d}\frac{f^{(k)}(a)}{k!}(X-a)^{k}.

Proof.

Since
K[X]\to K[X],\ p\mapsto p^{\prime}
commutes with the ring automorphism
K[X]\to K[X],\ p\mapsto p(X+a)
, we can WLOG suppose

a=0
. But then the claim follows from the definition of the (formal) derivative.
∎

Lemma 5.3
.

Suppose
(K,\leq)
is an ordered field,
k\in\mathbb{N}
,
c_{1},\dots,c_{k}\in K^{\times}
,
\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{k}\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
,

\alpha_{1}<\ldots<\alpha_{k}
and
f=\sum_{i=1}^{k}c_{i}X^{\alpha_{i}}
.

(a)

\sgn(f(x))=(\sgn x)^{\alpha_{k}}\sgn(c_{k})
for all
x\in K
satisfying
|x|>\max\left\{1,\frac{|c_{1}|+\ldots+|c_{k-1}|}{|c_{k}|}\right\}

(b)

\sgn(f(x))=(\sgn x)^{\alpha_{1}}\sgn(c_{1})
for all
x\in K^{\times}
satisfying
\frac{1}{|x|}>\max\left\{1,\frac{|c_{2}|+\ldots+|c_{k}|}{|c_{1}|}\right\}

Proof.

(a)

For all
x\in K
with
|x|>\max\left\{1,\frac{|c_{1}|+\ldots+|c_{k-1}|}{|c_{k}|}\right\}
, we have

\left|\sum_{i=1}^{k-1}c_{i}x^{\alpha_{i}}\right|\overset{\ref{introabssgn}}{\leq}\sum_{i=1}^{k-1}|c_{i}||x|^{\alpha_{i}}\overset{1\leq|x|}{\leq}\sum_{i=1}^{k-1}|c_{i}||x|^{\alpha_{k}-1}=|c_{k}|\left(\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{k-1}|c_{i}|}{|c_{k}|}\right)|x|^{\alpha_{k}-1}<|c_{k}x^{\alpha_{k}}|.

(b)

For all
x\in K^{\times}
with
\frac{1}{|x|}>\max\left\{1,\frac{|c_{2}|+\ldots+|c_{k}|}{|c_{1}|}\right\}
, we have

\left|\sum_{i=2}^{k}c_{i}x^{\alpha_{i}}\right|\overset{\ref{introabssgn}}{\leq}\sum_{i=2}^{k}|c_{i}||x|^{\alpha_{i}}\overset{|x|\leq 1}{\leq}\sum_{i=2}^{k}|c_{i}||x|^{\alpha_{1}+1}=|c_{1}|\left(\frac{\sum_{i=2}^{k}|c_{i}|}{|c_{1}|}\right)|x|^{\alpha_{1}+1}<|c_{1}x^{\alpha_{1}}|.

∎

Reminder 5.4
.

Let
K
be a field,
f\in K[X]
and
a\in K
. Then

\mu(a,f):=\sup\{k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}\mid\text{$(X-a)^{k}$ divides $f$ in $K[X]$}\}\in\mathbb{N}_{0}\cup\{\infty\}

is called the
multiplicity
of
a
in
f
. We have

\mu(a,f)=\infty\iff f=0

and

\mu(a,f)\geq 1\iff f(a)=0.

We call
a
a
multiple
root of
f
if
\mu(a,f)\geq 2
and we call it a
k
-fold root of
f
(
k\in\mathbb{N}
) if

\mu(a,f)=k
. In case
\chara K=0
, one has

\mu(a,f)=\sup\{k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}\mid f^{(0)}(a)=\ldots=f^{(k-1)}(a)=0\}

as one can see easily.

Definition 5.5
.

Let
(K,\leq)
be an ordered field and
0\neq f\in K[X]
.

(a)

The
number of positive roots counted with multiplicity
of
f
is

\mu(f):=\sum_{a\in K_{>0}}\mu(a,f)\in\mathbb{N}_{0}.

Writing
f=g\prod_{i=1}^{m}(X-a_{i})
with
a_{1},\dots,a_{m}\in K_{>0}
and
g\in K[X]
with
g(x)\neq 0
for all
x\in K_{>0}
, we
therefore have
\mu(f)=m
.

(b)

Writing
f=\sum_{i=1}^{k}c_{i}X^{\alpha_{i}}
with
c_{1},\dots,c_{k}\in K^{\times}
and
\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{k}\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
such that

\alpha_{1}<\ldots<\alpha_{k},

we define the
number of sign changes in the coefficients
of
f

\sigma(f):=\#\{i\in\{1,\dots,k-1\}\mid\sgn(c_{i})\neq\sgn(c_{i+1})\}\in\mathbb{N}_{0}.

Proposition 5.6
.

Let
R
be a real closed field and
f\in R[X]\setminus\{0\}
. Then
\mu(f)
and
\sigma(f)
have the same parity.

Proof.

Write

f=\sum_{i=1}^{k}c_{i}X^{\alpha_{i}}=g\prod_{i=1}^{m}(X-a_{i})
with
c_{1},\dots,c_{k}\in R^{\times}
,
\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{k}\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
,

a_{1},\dots,a_{m}\in R_{>0}
and
g\in R[X]
such that
\alpha_{1}<\ldots<\alpha_{m}
and
g(x)\neq 0
for all
x\in R_{>0}
.
Since
R
is real closed, WLOG
g(x)>0
for all
x\in R_{>0}
by the intermediate value theorem
4.16
.
But then by Lemma
5.3
, both the lowest and highest coefficient of
g
is positive.
Now the claim follows from

\mu(f)=m
,
\sgn(c_{1})=(-1)^{m}
and
\sgn(c_{k})=1
.
∎

Lemma 5.7
.

Let
R
be a real closed field and
f\in R[X]\setminus R
. Then

\mu(f)\leq\mu(f^{\prime})+1
and
\sigma(f)\leq\sigma(f^{\prime})+1
.

Proof.

The second statement is easy to prove. For the first statement, suppose
a_{1},\dots,a_{m}\in R
are the positive roots of
f
and

a_{1}<\ldots<a_{m}
. Since
R
is real closed, there exist roots
b_{1},\dots,b_{m-1}\in R
of
f^{\prime}
such that

a_{1}<b_{1}<a_{2}<\ldots<b_{m-1}<a_{m}
by Rolle’s Theorem
4.17
. Now
\mu(f^{\prime})=\sum_{a\in K_{>0}}\mu(a,f^{\prime})\geq\sum_{i=1}^{m}\mu(a_{i},f^{\prime})+\sum_{i=1}^{m-1}\mu(b_{i},f^{\prime})\geq\sum_{i=1}^{m}\mu(a_{i},f^{\prime})+m-1=\sum_{i=1}^{m}(\mu(a_{i},f)-1)+m-1=\sum_{i=1}^{m}\mu(a_{i},f)-1=\mu(f)-1
.
∎

Remark 5.8
.

In the situation of Lemma
5.7
,
\sigma(f^{\prime})\leq\sigma(f)
holds trivially but

\mu(f^{\prime})\leq\mu(f)
fails in general as the example
f=(X-1)^{2}+1
shows.

Theorem 5.9

(Descartes’ rule of signs)
.

Let
R
be a real closed field. Then
\mu(f)\leq\sigma(f)
for all
f\in R[X]\setminus\{0\}
.

Proof.

Induction on
d:=\deg f\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
.

d=0

\mu(f)=0=\sigma(f)

d-1\to d

(d\in\mathbb{N})

\mu(f)\overset{\ref{loose1}}{\leq}\mu(f^{\prime})+1\overset{\text{induction}}{\underset{\text{hypothesis}}{\leq}}\sigma(f^{\prime})+1\overset{\ref{loo}}{\leq}\sigma(f)+1

and therefore
\mu(f)\leq\sigma(f)
by Proposition
5.6
.
∎

Example 5.10
.

Let
R
be a real closed field and
f:=X^{4}-5X^{3}-21X^{2}+115X-150\in R[X]
. Then
\sigma(f)=3
and therefore

\mu(f)\in\{1,3\}
by
5.9
and
5.6
. For
f(-X)=X^{4}+5X^{3}-21X^{2}-115X-150
, we have

\sigma(f(-X))=1
and therefore
\mu(f(-X))=1
. One can verify that
\sigma((1+X)^{22}f)=1
from which we get

\mu(f)=\mu((1+X)^{22}f)=1
. Hence
f
has exactly two roots in
R
, namely two simple (i.e.,
1
-fold
[
\to

5.4
]) ones,
one positive and one negative.

Definition 5.11
.

Let
R
be a real closed field. We call a polynomial
f\in R[X]

real-rooted
if it has no root in
R(\mathbbm{i})\setminus R

[
\to

4.12
].

Proposition 5.12
.

Let
R
be real closed field and
f\in R[X]
. Then the following are equivalent:

(a)

f
is real-rooted.

(b)

There are
d\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
,
c\in R^{\times}
and
a_{1},\dots,a_{d}\in R
such that
f=c\prod_{i=1}^{d}(X-a_{i})
.

Proof.

For (a)
\implies
(b) use the fundamental theorem
4.12
or
4.14
.
∎

Theorem 5.13
.

[
\to

5.8
]

Suppose
R
is a real closed field and
f\in R[X]\setminus R
is real-rooted. Then
f^{\prime}
is real-rooted and
\mu(f^{\prime})\leq\mu(f)
.

Proof.

Using
5.12
, write
f=c\prod_{i=1}^{n}(X-a_{i})^{\alpha_{i}}
with
c,a_{1},\dots,a_{n}\in R
and
\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{n}\in\mathbb{N}
such that
c\neq 0
and

a_{1}<\ldots<a_{n}.

Since
R
is real closed, there exist roots
b_{1},\dots,b_{n-1}\in R
of
f^{\prime}
such that

a_{1}<b_{1}<a_{2}<\ldots<b_{n-1}<a_{n}

by Rolle’s Theorem
4.17
. We have
\mu(a_{i},f)=\alpha_{i}
and therefore

\mu(a_{i},f^{\prime})=\alpha_{i}-1

for all
i\in\{1,\dots,n\}
. It follows that

\displaystyle\deg(f^{\prime})

\displaystyle\geq\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mu(a_{i},f^{\prime})+\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}\mu(b_{i},f^{\prime})\geq\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mu(a_{i},f^{\prime})+n-1

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{n}(\alpha_{i}-1)+n-1=\deg(f)-1=\deg(f^{\prime}),

whence

\deg(f^{\prime})=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mu(a_{i},f^{\prime})+\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}\mu(b_{i},f^{\prime})

and

\mu(b_{i},f^{\prime})=1

for all
i\in\{1,\dots,n-1\}
.
It follows that

\{x\in R(\mathbbm{i})\mid f^{\prime}(x)=0\}\subseteq\{a_{1},b_{1},a_{2},\dots,b_{n-1},a_{n}\}\subseteq R,

in particular
f^{\prime}
is real-rooted.
Choose
k\in\{1,\dots,n+1\}
such that
a_{k},\dots,a_{n}
are the positive roots of
f
. Then

\{x\in R\mid f^{\prime}(x)=0,x>0\}\subseteq\begin{cases}\{b_{k-1},a_{k},\dots,b_{n-1},a_{n}\}&\text{if }k\geq 2\\
\{a_{1},b_{1},\dots,b_{n-1},a_{n}\}&\text{if }k=1\end{cases}.

If
k\geq 2
, then

\mu(f^{\prime})\leq\sum_{i=k}^{n}(\underbrace{\mu(b_{i-1},f^{\prime})}_{=1}+\underbrace{\mu(a_{i},f^{\prime})}_{=\mu(a_{i},f)-1})=\mu(f).

If
k=1
, then one sees similarly that
\mu(f^{\prime})=\mu(f)-1\leq\mu(f)
.
∎

Theorem 5.14

(Descartes’ rule of signs for real-rooted polynomials)
.

Let
R
be a real closed field. Then
\mu(f)=\sigma(f)
for all real-rooted
f\in R[X]
.

Proof.

By Theorem
5.9
, it is enough to show
\mu(f)\geq\sigma(f)
for all real-rooted
f\in R[X]
by
induction on
d:=\deg f\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
.

d=0

\mu(f)=0=\sigma(f)

d-1\to d

(d\in\mathbb{N})

\mu(f)\overset{\ref{derrr}}{\geq}\mu(f^{\prime})\overset{\begin{subarray}{c}\text{induction}\\
{\text{hypothesis}}\end{subarray}}{\underset{\ref{derrr}}{\geq}}\sigma(f^{\prime})\overset{\ref{loose1}}{\geq}\sigma(f)-1

and therefore
\mu(f)\geq\sigma(f)
by Proposition
5.6
.
∎

Example 5.15
.

\det\begin{pmatrix}1-X&0&1\\
0&-2-X&1\\
1&1&-X\end{pmatrix}=(1-X)(2+X)X+2+X+X-1\\
=(2+X-2X-X^{2})X+2X+1=-X^{3}-X^{2}+4X+1\in\mathbb{R}[X]

is real-rooted since it is the characteristic polynomial
of a symmetric matrix. By Descartes’ rule
5.14
, it has exactly one positive root.

6
Counting real zeros with Hermite’s method

Reminder 6.1
.

(a)

Let
A
be a commutative ring with
0\neq 1

and
f\in A[X_{1},\dots,X_{n}]
. Then
f
is called
homogeneous
if
f
is a an

A
-linear combination of
monomials of the same degree. Moreover,
f
is called a
k
-form
(
k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
) if
f
is an
A
-linear combination
of monomials of degree

k
(i.e., if
f=0
or
f
is homogeneous of degree
k
). One often says
linear form
instead of
1
-form and

quadratic form
instead of
2
-form.

(b)

If
K
is a field, one can identify the
K
-vector subspace of
K[X_{1},\dots,X_{n}]
consisting of the

\begin{Bmatrix}\text{linear}\\
\text{quadratic}\end{Bmatrix}
forms introduced in (a) via the isomorphism
f\mapsto(x\mapsto f(x))
with the
K
-vector space

\begin{Bmatrix}(K^{n})^{*}\\
Q(K^{n})\end{Bmatrix}
introduced in linear algebra. Hence the notion of a linear or quadratic form introduced in (a) differs only
insignificantly from the corresponding notion from linear algebra.

(c)

Let
A
be a set and
M=(a_{ij})_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq i\leq m\\
1\leq j\leq n\end{subarray}}\in A^{m\times n}
a matrix. Then

M^{T}:=(a_{ij})_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq j\leq n\\
1\leq i\leq m\end{subarray}}\in A^{n\times m}
is called the
transpose
of
M
. The elements of

SA^{n\times n}:=\{M\in A^{n\times n}\mid M=M^{T}\}
are called
symmetric
matrices.

(d)

Let
K
be a field. Then
(a_{1},\dots,a_{n})\mapsto a_{1}X_{1}+\ldots+a_{n}X_{n}
(
a_{i}\in K
) defines an isomorphism between

K^{1\times n}\cong K^{n}
and the
K
-vector space of the linear forms in
K[X_{1},\dots,X_{n}]
. If
\chara K\neq 2
, then

(a_{ij})_{1\leq i,j\leq n}\mapsto\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}a_{ij}X_{i}X_{j}
(
a_{ij}\in K
) defines an isomorphism between
SK^{n\times n}

and the
K
-vector space of the quadratic forms in
K[X_{1},\dots,X_{n}]
. If
f\in K[X_{1},\dots,X_{n}]
is a linear or quadratic form,
then we call the preimage
M(f)
of
f
under the respective isomorphism the
representing matrix
of
f
. This is the
representing matrix of
f
in the sense of linear algebra with respect to the canonical bases.

(e)

Suppose
K
is a field satisfying
\chara K\neq 2
,
q\in K[X_{1},\dots,X_{n}]
a quadratic form,

\ell_{1},\dots,\ell_{m}\in K[X_{1},\dots,X_{n}]
linear forms and
\lambda_{1},\dots,\lambda_{m}\in K
. Then

q=\sum_{k=1}^{m}\lambda_{k}\ell_{k}^{2}\iff M(q)=P^{T}\begin{pmatrix}~\hbox to61.47pt{\vbox to55.55pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 5.15974pt\lower-51.32356pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}

\lxSVG@closescope }}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

\lxSVG@closescope }}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{3.2}{0.0}{0.0}{3.2}{28.98868pt}{-21.69199pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{{}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{3.2}{0.0}{0.0}{3.2}{-2.30952pt}{-44.45428pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}

{

{{{{{}}{

\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\end{pmatrix}P

where

P:=\begin{pmatrix}M(\ell_{1})\\
\vdots\\
M(\ell_{m})\end{pmatrix}\in K^{m\times n}.

Here
P
is invertible if and only if
m=n
and
\ell_{1},\dots,\ell_{m}
are linearly independent.

(f)

Let
K
be a field satisfying
\chara K\neq 2
and
q\in K[X_{1},\dots,X_{n}]
a quadratic form. One can
easily
calculate
linearly independent linear forms
\ell_{1},\dots,\ell_{m}\in K[X_{1},\dots,X_{n}]
and
\lambda_{1},\dots,\lambda_{m}\in K
such that

q=\sum_{k=1}^{m}\lambda_{k}\ell_{k}^{2}
. Indeed, one can write

X_{1}^{2}+a_{2}X_{1}X_{2}+\dots+a_{n}X_{1}X_{n}\qquad(a_{i}\in K)

as

\Big(\underbrace{X_{1}+\frac{a_{2}}{2}X_{2}+\dots+\frac{a_{n}}{2}X_{n}}_{\ell_{1}}\Big)^{2}-\underbrace{\left(\frac{a_{2}}{2}X_{2}+\dots+\frac{a_{n}}{2}X_{n}\right)^{2}}_{\in K[X_{2},\dots,X_{n}]}
and

X_{1}X_{2}+a_{3}X_{1}X_{3}+\ldots+a_{n}X_{1}X_{n}+b_{3}X_{2}X_{3}+\ldots+b_{n}X_{2}X_{n}

as

\big(\underbrace{X_{1}+b_{3}X_{3}+\ldots+b_{n}X_{n}}_{h_{1}}\big)\big(\underbrace{X_{2}+a_{3}X_{3}+\ldots+a_{n}X_{n}}_{h_{2}}\big)\\
-\underbrace{\left(a_{3}X_{3}+\ldots+a_{n}X_{n}\right)\left(b_{3}X_{3}+\ldots+b_{n}X_{n}\right)}_{\in K[X_{3},\dots,X_{n}]}

where

h_{1}h_{2}=\Big(\underbrace{\frac{h_{1}+h_{2}}{2}}_{\ell_{1}}\Big)^{2}-\Big(\underbrace{\frac{h_{1}-h_{2}}{2}}_{\ell_{2}}\Big)^{2}

[
\to

2.3
]. In this way one can in each step place one or two variables in one or two squares and the arising linear forms
are obviously linearly independent. Consider
q:=2X_{1}X_{2}+2X_{1}X_{3}+2X_{2}X_{3}+2X_{3}X_{4}
as an example:

\displaystyle q

\displaystyle:=2(X_{1}X_{2}+X_{1}X_{3}+X_{2}X_{3})+2X_{3}X_{4}

\displaystyle=2((\underbrace{X_{1}+X_{3}}_{h_{1}})(\underbrace{X_{2}+X_{3}}_{h_{2}}))-2X_{3}^{2}+2X_{3}X_{4}

\displaystyle=\underbrace{\frac{1}{2}}_{\lambda_{1}=-\lambda_{2}}((\underbrace{h_{1}+h_{2}}_{\ell_{1}})^{2}-(\underbrace{h_{1}-h_{2}}_{\ell_{2}})^{2})\underbrace{-2}_{\lambda_{3}}\Big(\underbrace{X_{3}-\frac{1}{2}X_{4}}_{\ell_{3}}\Big)^{2}+\underbrace{\frac{2}{4}}_{\lambda_{4}}{\underbrace{X_{4}}_{\ell_{4}}}^{2}.

Hence
q=\sum_{k=1}^{4}\lambda_{k}\ell_{k}^{2}=\frac{1}{2}(X_{1}+X_{2}+2X_{3})^{2}-\frac{1}{2}(X_{1}-X_{2})^{2}-2(X_{3}-\frac{1}{2}X_{4})^{2}+\frac{1}{2}X_{4}^{2}

and by (e)

\begin{pmatrix}0&1&1&0\\
1&0&1&0\\
1&1&0&1\\
0&0&1&0\end{pmatrix}=P^{T}DP

where

P:=\begin{pmatrix}1&1&2&0\\
1&-1&0&0\\
0&0&1&-\frac{1}{2}\\
0&0&0&1\end{pmatrix}\qquad\text{and}\qquad D:=\begin{pmatrix}\frac{1}{2}&0&0&0\\
0&-\frac{1}{2}&0&0\\
0&0&-2&0\\
0&0&0&\frac{1}{2}\end{pmatrix}.

(g)

Translating (f) into the language of matrices, one obtains for each field
K
with
\chara K\neq 2
and each
M\in SK^{n\times n}

the following:
One can
easily
find a
P\in\GL_{n}(K)=(K^{n\times n})^{\times}
and a diagonal matrix
D\in K^{n\times n}
such that

M=\underline{\underline{P^{T}}}DP
. This is the diagonalization of
M
as a quadratic form which is much simpler than the
diagonalization of
M
as an endomorphism where one wants to reach
M=\underline{\underline{P^{-1}}}DP

(in case
K=\mathbb{R}
perhaps even with
P^{-1}=P^{T}
).

(h)

Let
K
be a Euclidean field [
\to

4.4
] and
q\in K[X_{1},\dots,X_{n}]
a quadratic form. According to (f),
one can then
easily
compute
linearly independent
linear forms

\ell_{1},\dots,\ell_{s},\ell_{s+1},\dots,\ell_{s+t}\in K[X_{1},\dots,X_{n}]

satisfying
q=\sum_{i=1}^{s}\ell_{i}^{2}-\sum_{j=1}^{t}\ell_{s+j}^{2}
. By completing
\ell_{1},\dots,\ell_{s+t}
to a
basis
\ell_{1},\dots,\ell_{n}
of the vector space of all linear forms in
K[X_{1},\dots,X_{n}]
and by writing

q=1\cdot\sum_{i=1}^{s}\ell_{i}^{2}+(-1)\sum_{j=1}^{t}\ell_{s+j}^{2}+0\cdot\sum_{k=t+1}^{n}\ell_{k}^{2}
, one sees for the
rank

\rk(q):=\rk M(q)
of
q
that

\rk(q)\overset{(e)}{=}s+t
.
We define the
signature
of
q
as
\sg(q):=s-t
. This is well-defined by
Sylvester’s law of inertia
:
If
\ell_{1}^{\prime},\dots,\ell_{s^{\prime}}^{\prime},\ell_{s^{\prime}+1}^{\prime},\dots,\ell_{s^{\prime}+t^{\prime}}^{\prime}\in K[X_{1},\dots,X_{n}]
are other linearly independent linear forms satisfying

q=\sum_{i=1}^{s^{\prime}}\ell_{i}^{\prime 2}-\sum_{j=1}^{t^{\prime}}\ell_{s^{\prime}+j}^{\prime 2}
, then
s^{\prime}+t^{\prime}=\rk(q)=s+t
and one sees again by completing to a basis and (e)
that there are subspaces
U
,
W
,
U^{\prime}
,
W^{\prime}
of
K^{n}
such that

q(U)\subseteq K_{\geq 0}
,
\dim U=n-t
,
q(W\setminus\{0\})\subseteq K_{<0}
,
\dim W=t
,

q(U^{\prime})\subseteq K_{\geq 0}
,
\dim U^{\prime}=n-t^{\prime}
,
q(W^{\prime}\setminus\{0\})\subseteq K_{<0}
,
\dim W^{\prime}=t^{\prime}
.
One deduces
U\cap W^{\prime}=\{0\}
and
U^{\prime}\cap W=\{0\}
, whence

(n-t)+t^{\prime}\leq n
and
(n-t^{\prime})+t\leq n
. Therefore
t=t^{\prime}
and thus
s=s^{\prime}
.

(i)

Let
K
be a field and
f=X^{d}+a_{d-1}X^{d-1}+\ldots+a_{0}\in K[X]
with
d\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
and
a_{i}\in K
. The

companion matrix

C_{f}
of
f
is the representing matrix of the
K
-vector space endomorphism

K[X]/(f)\to K[X]/(f),\ \overline{p}\mapsto\overline{Xp}\qquad(p\in K[X])

with respect to the basis
\overline{1},\dots,\overline{X^{d-1}}
,
i.e.,

C_{f}=\hbox to239.65pt{\vbox to98.8pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 119.82317pt\lower-49.39734pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setdash{0.8pt,4.0pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray,stroke-dashoffset} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{}{}{{}}{
{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{

{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

{

{

{}{}}{}{}{{}{}}}}}{{}}{}{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -77.06 7.36 L 53.28 -45.72}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{
{}{}{}}{}{

{{{{{}}{

{}{}}{}{}{{}{}}}}}{{}}{}{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -77.06 -12.54 L 9.47 -46.03}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{

{
{}{}{}}{}{
{}{}{}}
{{{{{}}{
{}{}}{}{}{{}{}}}}}{}{{{{{}}{
{}{}}{}{}{{}{}}}}}{{}}{}{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 124.53 1.98 L 124.53 -45.38}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{
{}{}{}}{}{

{{{{{}}{

{
{}{}{}}{}{
{}{}{}}
{{{{{}}{

{}{}}{}{}{{}{}}}}}{{}}{}{}{}{
{}{}{}}
{{{{{}}{
{}{}}{}{}{{}{}}}}}{}{{{{{}}{
{}{}}{}{}{{}{}}}}}{{}}{}{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -92.6 -16.16 L -92.6 -42.42 M -77.06 -52.04 L 9.47 -52.04}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\in K^{d\times d}.

One sees easily that
f
is the minimal polynomial and therefore for degree reasons, up to a sign, also the characteristic polynomial of
C_{f}
.
Now suppose furthermore that
f
splits into linear factors, i.e.,

f=\prod_{k=1}^{m}(X-x_{k})^{\alpha_{k}}

for some
m\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
,
\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{m}\in\mathbb{N}
and
x_{1},\dots,x_{m}\in K

(here the
x_{i}
do not yet have to be pairwise distinct so that one could take
\alpha_{k}=1
but to avoid a confusing change of notation in view of the proof of
Theorem
6.5
, we allow here and in Proposition
6.4
that
\alpha_{k}\in\mathbb{N}
).
Then
C_{f}
is similar to a triangular matrix with diagonal entries

\underbrace{x_{1},\dots,x_{1}}_{\alpha_{1}},\quad\underbrace{x_{2},\dots,x_{2}}_{\alpha_{2}},\quad\dots\quad,\quad\underbrace{x_{m},\dots,x_{m}}_{\alpha_{m}}.

Then
C_{f}^{i}
is for every
i\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
similar to a triangular matrix whose diagonal entries are

\underbrace{x_{1}^{i},\dots,x_{1}^{i}}_{\alpha_{1}},\quad\underbrace{x_{2}^{i},\dots,x_{2}^{i}}_{\alpha_{2}},\quad\dots\quad,\quad\underbrace{x_{m}^{i},\dots,x_{m}^{i}}_{\alpha_{m}}.

In particular, we have
\tr(C_{f}^{i})=\sum_{k=1}^{m}\alpha_{k}x_{k}^{i}
for all
i\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
and consequently

\tr(g(C_{f}))=\sum_{k=1}^{m}\alpha_{k}g(x_{k})

for all
g\in K[X]
.

(j)

If
K
is a field and
x_{1},\dots,x_{m}\in K
are pairwise distinct, then the
Vandermonde
matrix

\begin{pmatrix}1&x_{1}&\dots&x_{1}^{m-1}\\
\vdots&\vdots&&\vdots\\
1&x_{m}&\dots&x_{m}^{m-1}\end{pmatrix}\in K^{m\times m}

is invertible since it is the representing matrix of the injective and therefore bijective linear map

K[X]_{m-1}\to K^{m},\ p\mapsto\begin{pmatrix}p(x_{1})\\
\vdots\\
p(x_{m})\end{pmatrix}

[
\to

5.1
] with respect to the canonical bases.

(k)

Let
K
be a field and let
x_{1},\dots,x_{m}\in K
be pairwise distinct. Furthermore, let
d\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
with
m\leq d
.
Consider for
k\in\{1,\dots,m\}
the linear forms
\ell_{k}:=\sum_{i=1}^{d}x_{k}^{i-1}T_{i}\in K[T_{1},\dots,T_{d}]
. Then

\ell_{1},\dots,\ell_{m}
are linearly independent. Indeed, because of (d) this is equivalent to the linear independence of
the vectors
(x_{k}^{0},\dots,x_{k}^{d-1})
(
k\in\{1,\dots,m\}
) in
K^{d}
. But already the truncated vectors
(x_{k}^{0},\dots,x_{k}^{m-1})

(
k\in\{1,\dots,m\}
) are linearly independent by (j).

Definition 6.2
.

Let
K
be a field and
f,g\in K[X]
where
f
is monic of degree
d
. Then the quadratic form

H(f,g):=\sum_{i,j=1}^{d}\tr(g(C_{f})C_{f}^{i+j-2})T_{i}T_{j}\in K[T_{1},\dots,T_{d}]

is called
the
Hermite form
of
f
with respect to
g
.
The quadratic form
H(f):=H(f,1)
is simply called the Hermite form of
f
.

Remark 6.3
.

Let
K
be a field with
\chara K\neq 2
and let
f,g\in K[X]
where
f
is monic of degree
d
. Then
M(H(f,g))

[
\to

6.1
(d)] is called the
Hermite matrix of
f
with respect to
g
. This is a Hankel matrix, i.e., of the form

\begin{pmatrix}\hbox to35.51pt{\vbox to35.51pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.68452pt\lower 5.00592pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{
{}{{}}{}
{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 47.64 8.27 L 46.85 7.48}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{}{{}}{}
{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 47.64 16.14 L 38.98 7.48}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{}{{}}{}
{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 47.64 24.02 L 31.1 7.48}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{}{{}}{}
{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 47.64 31.89 L 23.23 7.48}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{}{{}}{}
{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 47.64 39.76 L 15.35 7.48}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{}{{}}{}
{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 31.89 55.51 L -0.39 23.23}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{}{{}}{}
{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 24.02 55.51 L -0.39 31.1}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{}{{}}{}
{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 16.14 55.51 L -0.39 38.98}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
