\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\end{pmatrix}.

Furthermore,
M(H(f))
is called the
Hermite matrix of
f
.

Proposition 6.4
.

Let
K
be a field and
f,g\in K[X]
. Suppose
x_{1},\dots,x_{m}\in K
and
\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{m}\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
such that

f=\prod_{k=1}^{m}(X-x_{k})^{\alpha_{k}}
and
d:=\deg f
. Then

H(f,g)=\sum_{i,j=1}^{d}\left(\sum_{k=1}^{m}\alpha_{k}g(x_{k})x_{k}^{i+j-2}\right)T_{i}T_{j}=\sum_{k=1}^{m}\alpha_{k}g(x_{k})\left(\sum_{i=1}^{d}x_{k}^{i-1}T_{i}\right)^{2}.

Proof.

6.2
and
6.1
(i).
∎

Theorem 6.5

(Counting roots with one side condition)
.

Let
R
be a real closed field,
C:=R(\mathbbm{i})
,

f,g\in R[X]
and
f
monic. Then

\displaystyle\rk H(f,g)

\displaystyle=\#\{x\in C\mid f(x)=0,g(x)\neq 0\}\qquad\text{and}

\displaystyle\sg H(f,g)

\displaystyle=\#\{x\in R\mid f(x)=0,g(x)>0\}

\displaystyle\,-\#\{x\in R\mid f(x)=0,g(x)<0\}.

Proof.

Denote by
p\mapsto p^{*}
again the ring automorphism of
C[X]
with
x^{*}=x
for all
x\in R
,

\mathbbm{i}^{*}=-\mathbbm{i}
and
X^{*}=X
.
Using the fundamental theorem of algebra
4.14
and this automorphism, we can write

f=\prod_{k=1}^{m}(X-x_{k})^{\alpha_{k}}\prod_{t=1}^{n}(X-z_{t})^{\beta_{t}}\prod_{t=1}^{n}(X-z_{t}^{*})^{\beta_{t}}

for some
m,n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}

\alpha_{k},\beta_{t}\in\mathbb{N}
,
x_{k}\in R
,
z_{t}\in C\setminus R
and

x_{1},\dots,x_{m},z_{1},\dots,z_{n},z_{1}^{*},\dots,z_{n}^{*}
pairwise distinct. By renumbering the
z_{t}
, we can find
r\in\{0,\dots,n\}
such
that
g(z_{1})\neq 0,\dots,g(z_{r})\neq 0
and
g(z_{r+1})=0,\dots,g(z_{n})=0
.
By
6.4
,
6.1
(k) and
4.8
(c), we obtain linear forms

\ell_{1},\dots,\ell_{m},g_{1},\dots,g_{r},h_{1},\dots h_{r}\in R[T_{1},\dots,T_{d}]
where
d:=\deg f
such that

\displaystyle H(f,g)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{m}\alpha_{k}g(x_{k})\ell_{k}^{2}+\sum_{t=1}^{r}(g_{t}+\mathbbm{i}h_{t})^{2}+\sum_{t=1}^{r}(g_{t}-\mathbbm{i}h_{t})^{2}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{m}\alpha_{k}g(x_{k})\ell_{k}^{2}+2\sum_{t=1}^{r}g_{t}^{2}-2\sum_{t=1}^{r}h_{t}^{2}

where
\ell_{1},\dots,\ell_{m},g_{1}+\mathbbm{i}h_{1},g_{1}-\mathbbm{i}h_{1},\dots,g_{r}+\mathbbm{i}h_{r},g_{r}-\mathbbm{i}h_{r}\in C[T_{1},\dots,T_{d}]
are linearly independent.
Due to
C(g_{i}+\mathbbm{i}h_{i})+C(g_{i}-\mathbbm{i}h_{i})=Cg_{i}+Ch_{i}
, we have that

\ell_{1},\dots,\ell_{m},g_{1},\dots,g_{r},h_{1},\dots,h_{r}

are also
linearly independent in
C[T_{1},\dots,T_{d}]
and therefore also in
R[T_{1},\dots,T_{d}]
. It follows that

\displaystyle\rk H(f,g)

\displaystyle=\#\{k\in\{1,\dots,m\}\mid g(x_{k})\neq 0\}+2r

\displaystyle=\#\{k\in\{1,\dots,m\}\mid g(x_{k})\neq 0\}+2\#\{t\in\{1,\dots,n\}\mid g(z_{t})\neq 0\}

\displaystyle=\#\{x\in C\mid f(x)=0,g(x)\neq 0\}\qquad\text{and}

\displaystyle\sg H(f,g)

\displaystyle=\#\{k\in\{1,\dots,m\}\mid g(x_{k})>0\}-\#\{k\in\{1,\dots,m\}\mid g(x_{k})<0\}+r-r

\displaystyle=\#\{x\in R\mid f(x)=0,g(x)>0\}-\#\{x\in R\mid f(x)=0,g(x)<0\}.

∎

Corollary 6.6

(Counting roots without side conditions)
.

Let
R
be a real closed field,
C:=R(\mathbbm{i})
and suppose

f\in R[X]
is monic. Then

\displaystyle\rk H(f)

\displaystyle=\#\{x\in C\mid f(x)=0\}\qquad\text{and}

\displaystyle\sg H(f)

\displaystyle=\#\{x\in R\mid f(x)=0\}.

Corollary 6.7

(Counting roots with several side conditions)
.

Let
R
be a real closed field,
m\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
,
f,g_{1},\dots,g_{m}\in R[X]
and
f
monic. Then

\frac{1}{2^{m}}\sum_{\alpha\in\{1,2\}^{m}}\sg H(f,g_{1}^{\alpha_{1}}\dots g_{m}^{\alpha_{m}})=\#\{x\in R\mid f(x)=0,g_{1}(x)>0,\dots,g_{m}(x)>0\}

Proof.

The left hand side equals

\frac{1}{2^{m}}\sum_{\alpha\in\{1,2\}^{m}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}x\in R\\
f(x)=0\end{subarray}}\sgn((g_{1}^{\alpha_{1}}\dots g_{m}^{\alpha_{m}})(x))\\
=\frac{1}{2^{m}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}x\in R\\
f(x)=0\end{subarray}}\prod_{k=1}^{m}(\underbrace{\sgn(g_{k}(x))+(\sgn(g_{k}(x)))^{2}}_{\textstyle=\begin{cases}0&\text{if }g_{k}(x)\leq 0\\
2&\text{if }g_{k}(x)>0\end{cases}}).

∎

7
The real closure

Definition 7.1
.

Let
(K,P)
be an ordered field. An extension field
R
of
K
is called a
real closure
of
(K,P)
if

R
is real closed,
R|K
is algebraic and the order of
R
[
\to

4.3
,
4.4
] is an extension of
P

[
\to

3.1
].

Proposition 7.2
.

Let
(R,P)
be an ordered field. Then
R
is real closed if and only if there is no ordered extension field
(L,Q)
of
(R,P)
such that

L\neq R
and
L|R
is algebraic.

Proof.

One direction follows from
4.13
(c). Conversely, suppose that every ordered extension field
(L,Q)
of
(R,P)

with
L|R
algebraic satisfies
L=R
. To show:

(a)

R
is Euclidean.

(b)

Every polynomial of odd degree from
R[X]
has a root in
R
.

For (a), we show
P=R^{2}
. To this end, let
a\in P
. By
3.4
, we can extend
P
to
R(\sqrt{a})
. Due to the hypothesis, this
implies
R(\sqrt{a})=R
and therefore
a=(\sqrt{a})^{2}\in R^{2}
.

To show (b), let
f\in R[X]
be of odd degree. Choose in
R[X]
an irreducible divisor
g
of
f
of odd degree. Choose a root
x
of

g
in some extension field of
R
. Then
R(x)
is an extension field of
R
with odd
[R(x):R]
so that

P
can be extended to
R(x)

by
3.6
. By hypothesis, this gives
R(x)=R
. In particular,
g
and therefore
f
has a root in
R
.
∎

Theorem 7.3
.

Every ordered field has a real closure.

Proof.

Let
(K,P)
be an ordered field. Consider the algebraic closure
\overline{K}
of
K
and the set

M:=\{(L,Q)\mid L\text{ subfield of }\overline{K},Q\text{ order of }L,(K,P)\text{ is an ordered subfield of }(L,Q)\}

which is partially ordered by declaring

\displaystyle(L,Q)\preceq(L^{\prime},Q^{\prime})

\displaystyle:\iff(L,Q)\text{ is an ordered subfield of }(L^{\prime},Q^{\prime})

\displaystyle\overset{\text{\ref{unaryrem}(b)}}{\iff}(L\subseteq L^{\prime}\mathbin{\&}Q\subseteq Q^{\prime})

for all
(L,Q),(L^{\prime},Q^{\prime})\in M
. In
M
every chain possesses an upper bound: The empty chain has
(K,P)
as an upper bound.
A nonempty chain
C\subseteq M
has

\left(\bigcup\{L\mid(L,Q)\in C\},\bigcup\{Q\mid(L,Q)\in C\}\right)\in M

as an upper bound. By Zorn’s lemma,
M
possesses a maximal element
(R,Q)
. Of course,
\overline{K}
is also the algebraic
closure of
R
and therefore each algebraic extension of
R
is (up to
R
-isomorphy) an intermediate field of
\overline{K}|R
.
The maximality of
(R,Q)
in
M
signifies by
7.2
just that
R
is real closed. Because of
(R,Q)\in M
, the field
extension
R|K
is algebraic and the order
Q
is an extension of
P
.
∎

Lemma 7.4
.

Let
(K,P)
be an
ordered
subfield of the real closed fields
R
and
R^{\prime}
[
\to

4.3
] and

f\in K[X]
. Then
f
has the same number of roots in both
R
and
R^{\prime}
.

Proof.

WLOG
f
is monic. The number in question is by
6.6
equal to the signature of
H(f)
that can be calculated already over

(K,P)
[
\to

6.1
(f)(h)].
∎

Theorem 7.5
.

Let
(K,P)
be an ordered subfield of
(L,Q)
such that
L|K
is algebraic. Let
\varphi
be a homomorphism of ordered fields
from
(K,P)
into a real closed field
R
.
Then there is exactly one homomorphism
\psi
of ordered fields from
(L,Q)
to
R
with
\psi|_{K}=\varphi
.

Proof.

Choose a real closure
R^{\prime}
of
(L,Q)
according to
7.3
.

Existence: Using Zorn’s lemma, one reduces easily to the case where
L|K
is finite.
Since
\varphi\colon K\to\varphi(K)\subseteq R
is an isomorphism of ordered fields,
we can suppose WLOG that
(K,P)
is an ordered subfield of
R
and
\varphi=\id_{K}
.
We denote the different
K
-homomorphisms
from
L
to
R
by
\psi_{1},\dots,\psi_{m}
(
m\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
). Assume that none of these is a homomorphism of ordered
fields from
(L,Q)
to
R
(for example if
m=0
). Then there are
b_{1},\dots,b_{m}\in Q
such that
\psi_{1}(b_{1})\notin R^{2},\dots,\psi_{m}(b_{m})\notin R^{2}
. By the primitive element theorem there exists

a\in L^{\prime}:=L(\sqrt{b_{1}},\dots,\sqrt{b_{m}})\overset{b_{i}\in Q\subseteq R^{\prime 2}}{\subseteq}R^{\prime}

such that
L^{\prime}=K(a)
. The minimal polynomial of
a
over
K
has by
7.4
the same number of roots in
R^{\prime}
and
R

and therefore in particular a root in
R
. Hence there is a
K
-homomorphism
\psi\colon L^{\prime}\to R
.
Choose
i\in\{1,\dots,m\}
with
\psi|_{L}=\psi_{i}
(in particular
m>0
). Then
\psi_{i}(b_{i})=\psi(b_{i})=(\psi(\sqrt{b_{i}}))^{2}\in R^{2}

\lightning
.

Unicity: Let
a\in L
. Choose
f\in K[X]\setminus\{0\}
with
f(a)=0
. Choose
a_{1},\dots,a_{m}\in R^{\prime}
with
a_{1}<\ldots<a_{m}

such that
\{x\in R^{\prime}\mid f(x)=0\}=\{a_{1},\dots,a_{m}\}
. Again WLOG
\varphi|_{K}=\id_{K}

and hence
(K,P)
is an ordered subfield of
R
. By
7.4
there are
b_{1},\dots,b_{m}\in R

such that
b_{1}<\ldots<b_{m}
and

\{x\in R\mid f(x)=0\}=\{b_{1},\dots,b_{m}\}
. Choose now
i\in\{1,\dots,m\}
such that
a=a_{i}
. We show that each
homomorphism
\psi
of ordered fields from
(L,Q)
to
R
with
\psi|_{K}=\id
satisfies
\psi(a)=b_{i}
. To this end, fix such a
\psi
.
By the already proved existence statement, there is a homomorphism of ordered fields
\varrho\colon R^{\prime}\to R
such that

\varrho|_{L}=\psi
. Since
\varrho
is an embedding, we have
\{\varrho(a_{1}),\dots,\varrho(a_{m})\}=\{b_{1},\dots,b_{m}\}
and by the monotonicity
we even get
\varrho(a_{j})=b_{j}
for all
j\in\{1,\dots,m\}
. We deduce
\psi(a)=\psi(a_{i})=\varrho(a_{i})=b_{i}
.
∎

Corollary 7.6
.

Let
R
and
R^{\prime}
be real closures of the ordered field
(K,P)
. Then there is exactly one
K
-isomorphism from
R
to
R^{\prime}
.

Proof.

The
K
-isomorphisms from
R
to
R^{\prime}
are obviously exactly the isomorphisms of ordered fields from
R
to
R^{\prime}
whose
restriction to
K
is the identity. For this reason, the claim follows easily from
7.5
(for the surjectivity in the existence part
use either
4.13
(c) or the unicity of
K
-automorphisms of
R
and of
R^{\prime}
[
\to

7.5
]).
∎

Notation and Terminology 7.7
.

Because of
7.6
, we speak of
the
real closure
\overline{(K,P)}
of

(K,P)
. It contains by
7.5
(up to
K
-isomorphy)
every ordered field extension
(L,Q)
of
(K,P)
with
L|K
algebraic.

Theorem 7.8
.

Suppose
(K,P)
is an ordered field,
L|K
an algebraic extension,
R
a real closed field and
\varphi
a homomorphism of ordered
fields from
(K,P)
to
R
. Then

\displaystyle\{\psi\mid\psi\colon L\to R\text{ homomorphism},\psi|_{K}=\varphi\}

\displaystyle\to\{Q\mid\text{$Q$ is an extension of $P$ to $L$}\}

\displaystyle\psi

\displaystyle\mapsto\psi^{-1}(R^{2})

is a bijection.

Proof.

The well-definedness is easy to see. To verify the bijectivity, let
Q
be an extension of
P
to
L
. We have to show that
there is exactly one homomorphism
\psi\colon L\to R
with
\psi|_{K}=\varphi
fulfilling the condition
\psi^{-1}(R^{2})=Q
that is equivalent
to
\psi
being a homomorphism of ordered fields from
(L,Q)
to
R
since

\displaystyle\psi^{-1}(R^{2})=Q

\displaystyle\iff\psi^{-1}(R^{2}\cap\psi(L))=Q\overset{\psi\colon L\to\psi(L)}{\underset{\text{bijective}}{\iff}}R^{2}\cap\psi(L)=\psi(Q)

\displaystyle\overset{R^{2}\cap\psi(L)}{\underset{\text{order of $\psi(L)$}}{\iff}}\psi(Q)\subseteq R^{2}\cap\psi(L)\iff\psi(Q)\subseteq R^{2}.

Hence we get the unicity and existence of
\psi
from
7.5
.
∎

Corollary 7.9
.

Suppose
(K,P)
is an ordered field,
R:=\overline{(K,P)}
and
L|K
a finite extension.
Let
a\in L
with
L=K(a)
and
f
be the minimal polynomial of
a
over
K
. Then

\displaystyle\{x\in R\mid f(x)=0\}

\displaystyle\to\{Q\mid\text{$Q$ is an extension of $P$ to $L$}\}

\displaystyle x

\displaystyle\mapsto\{g(a)\mid g\in K[X],g(x)\in R^{2}\}

is a bijection.

Proof.

By
7.8
it is enough to see that

\displaystyle\{x\in R\mid f(x)=0\}

\displaystyle\to\{\psi\mid\text{$\psi\colon L\to R$ is a $K$-homomorphism}\}

\displaystyle x

\displaystyle\mapsto(g(a)\mapsto g(x))\qquad(g\in K[X])

is a bijection. This is easy to see.
∎

Example 7.10
.

Let
(K,P)
be an ordered field with
2\notin K^{2}
. Denote by
\sqrt{2}
one of the two
square roots of
2
in the algebraic closure
\overline{K}
of
K
[
\to

4.7
(a)].
Then there are exactly
2
orders of

K(\sqrt{2})
that extend
P
, namely the two induced by the field embeddings
K(\sqrt{2})\hookrightarrow\overline{(K,P)}

(in one of which
\sqrt{2}
is positive and in one of which it is negative). In particular, this is true if
(K,P)
is
not Archimedean [
\to

1.20
(d)] and in this case we cannot argue with
\mathbb{R}
instead of
\overline{(K,P)}

as we did in
3.5
.

Proposition 7.11
.

Let
R
be a real closed field and
K
a subfield of
R
that is (relatively) algebraically closed in
R
(i.e., no element of

R\setminus K
is algebraic over
K
). Then
K
is real closed.

Proof.

Apply the criterion from
7.2
: Every ordered extension field
(L,Q)
of
(K,R^{2}\cap K)

such that
L|K
is algebraic is contained
in
R
up to
K
-isomorphy [
\to

7.5
,
7.7
] and therefore equal to
K
.
∎

Example 7.12
.

The field
\mathbb{R}_{\text{alg}}:=\{x\in\mathbb{R}\mid\text{$x$ algebraic over $\mathbb{Q}$}\}
of
real algebraic numbers

is the algebraic closure of
\mathbb{Q}
in
\mathbb{R}
. By
7.11
,
\mathbb{R}_{\text{alg}}
is real closed and therefore the real closure
of
\mathbb{Q}
[
\to

4.3
]. Hence
\mathbb{R}_{\text{alg}}
is uniquely embeddable in every real closed field by
7.5
.
In this sense,
\mathbb{R}_{\text{alg}}
is the smallest real closed field.

8
Real quantifier elimination

Remark 8.1
.

Let
M
,
I
and
J_{i}
for each
i\in I
be sets and suppose
A_{ij}\subseteq M
for all
i\in I
and
j\in J_{i}
. Defining
the empty intersection as
M
(that is
\bigcap_{i\in\emptyset}\ldots:=\bigcap\emptyset:=M
), one has

\displaystyle\bigcup_{i\in I}\bigcap_{j\in J_{i}}A_{ij}

\displaystyle=\bigcap_{(j_{i})_{i\in I}\in\prod_{i\in I}J_{i}}\bigcup_{i\in I}A_{ij_{i}},

\displaystyle\bigcap_{i\in I}\bigcup_{j\in J_{i}}A_{ij}

\displaystyle=\bigcup_{(j_{i})_{i\in I}\in\prod_{i\in I}J_{i}}\bigcap_{i\in I}A_{ij_{i}},

\displaystyle\complement\bigcup_{i\in I}\bigcap_{j\in J_{i}}A_{ij}

\displaystyle=\bigcap_{i\in I}\bigcup_{j\in J_{i}}\complement A_{ij}\qquad\text{and}

\displaystyle\complement\bigcap_{i\in I}\bigcup_{j\in J_{i}}A_{ij}

\displaystyle=\bigcup_{i\in I}\bigcap_{j\in J_{i}}\complement A_{ij}

where the
complement
of
A\subseteq M
is given by

\complement A:=\complement_{M}A:=M\setminus A
.

Definition and Proposition 8.2
.

Let
M
be a set and
\mathscr{P}(M)
its power set.

(a)

We call
\mathscr{S}\subseteq\mathscr{P}(M)
a
Boolean algebra
on
M
if

•

\emptyset\in\mathscr{S}
,

•

\forall S\in\mathscr{S}:\complement S\in\mathscr{S}
,

•

\forall S_{1},S_{2}\in\mathscr{S}:S_{1}\cap S_{2}\in\mathscr{S}
and

•

\forall S_{1},S_{2}\in\mathscr{S}:S_{1}\cup S_{2}\in\mathscr{S}
.

(b)

Let
\mathscr{G}\subseteq\mathscr{P}(M)
. Then the set of all finite
\begin{Bmatrix}\text{unions}\\
\text{intersections}\end{Bmatrix}
of finite
\begin{Bmatrix}\text{intersections}\\
\text{unions}\end{Bmatrix}
of
elements of
\mathscr{G}
and their complements (with
\bigcap\emptyset:=M
) is obviously the smallest Boolean algebra
\mathscr{S}

on
M
with
\mathscr{G}\subseteq\mathscr{S}
. It is called the Boolean algebra
generated
by
\mathscr{G}
(on
M
). Its
elements are called the
Boolean combinations
of elements of
\mathscr{G}
.

Definition and Remark 8.3
.

In the sequel, we let
(K,P)
always be an ordered field, for example
(K,P)=(\mathbb{Q},\mathbb{Q}_{\geq 0})
unless otherwise stated.
Moreover, we let
\mathscr{R}
be a set of real closed fields containing
(K,P)
as an ordered subfield. For
n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
,
we set

\mathscr{R}_{n}:=\{(R,x)\mid R\in\mathscr{R},x\in R^{n}\}.

Thereby we have
R^{0}=\{\emptyset\}=\{0\}
and
we identify
\mathscr{R}_{0}
with
\mathscr{R}
. A Boolean combination of sets of the form

\{(R,x)\in\mathscr{R}_{n}\mid p(x)\geq 0\text{ (in $R$)}\}\qquad(p\in K[X_{1},\dots,X_{n}])

is called a

•

K
-semialgebraic set in
R^{n}
if
\mathscr{R}=\{R\}
, and

•

an
n
-ary
(K,P)
-semialgebraic class

if
\mathscr{R}
is “potentially very big” (in any case big enough to contain all real closed ordered extension fields of
(K,P)
that
are currently in the game).

We identify
K
-semialgebraic sets in
R^{n}
with subsets of
R^{n}
. Thus these are simply the subsets
of
R^{n}
that can be defined by combining finitely many polynomial inequalities with coefficients in
K
by the logical connectives
“not”, “and” and “or”.
A
semialgebraic set
in
R^{n}
is an
R
-semialgebraic set in
R^{n}
. A
semialgebraic class
is a

\mathbb{Q}
-semialgebraic class.

Remark 8.4
.

(a)

On the first reading, the reader might want to think of
\mathscr{R}=\{R\}
or even of
\mathscr{R}=\{\mathbb{R}\}
in order to have
a good geometric perception. Initially one can therefore think of
(K,P)
-semialgebraic classes as
K
-semialgebraic sets.

(b)

One can conceive
\mathscr{R}
as the “set” of all real closed ordered extension fields of
(K,P)
. Unfortunately, this is not a set
(otherwise Zorn’s lemma would yield real closed fields having no proper real closed extension field in contradiction to

3.7
combined with
7.3
) but a proper
class
. But we do not want to get into the formal notion of a class
and instead adopt a naïve point of view from which sets and classes are synonymous where “big” sets often
tend to be called classes.

(c)

Whoever gets vertiginous from (b), has several ways out: Our resort here is that
\mathscr{R}
is a honest set that is
at any one time sufficiently big (often
\#\mathscr{R}=1
is enough and almost always
\#\mathscr{R}=2
is enough).
Alternatively, one could learn the subtle non-naïve handling of sets and classes. As a third option, one could work,
instead of with
(K,P)
-semialgebraic classes, with formulas of first-order logic in the language of ordered fields with
additional constants for the elements of
K
. The last two options are technically very involved.

Remark 8.5
.

Obviously,
\emptyset
and
\mathscr{R}
are the only
0
-ary
(K,P)
-semialgebraic classes. Note that this uses heavily that
(K,P)
is an ordered subfield of every
R\in\mathscr{R}
.

Proposition 8.6
.

Every
(K,P)
-semialgebraic class is of the form

\bigcup_{i=1}^{k}\left\{(R,x)\in\mathscr{R}_{n}\mid f_{i}(x)=0,g_{i1}(x)>0,\dots,g_{im}(x)>0\right\}

for some
n,k,m\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
,
f_{i},g_{ij}\in K[X_{1},\dots,X_{n}]
.

Proof.

By
8.3
and
8.2
(b) such a class is a finite union of classes of the form

\left\{(R,x)\in\mathscr{R}_{n}\mid h_{1}(x)\geq 0,\dots,h_{s}(x)\geq 0,h_{s+1}(x)<0,\dots,h_{s+t}(x)<0\right\}\\
=\bigcup_{\delta\in\{0,1\}^{s}}\left\{(R,x)\in\mathscr{R}_{n}\mid\begin{aligned} \sgn(h_{1}(x))=\delta_{1},\dots,\sgn(h_{s}(x))=\delta_{s},\\
-h_{s+1}(x)>0,\dots,-h_{s+t}(x)>0\end{aligned}\right\}\\
=\bigcup_{\delta\in\{0,1\}^{s}}\left\{(R,x)\in\mathscr{R}_{n}\mid\begin{aligned} \left(\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i=1\\
\delta_{i}=0\end{subarray}}^{s}h_{i}^{2}\right)(x)=0,\mathop{\vphantom{\sum}\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\huge$\mathrm{\&}$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\Large$\mathrm{\&}$}}}{\mathrm{\&}}{\mathrm{\&}}}\displaylimits_{\begin{subarray}{c}i=1\\
\delta_{i}=1\end{subarray}}^{s}h_{i}(x)>0,\\
-h_{s+1}(x)>0,\dots,-h_{s+t}(x)>0\end{aligned}\right\}

for some
s,t\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
and
h_{i}\in K[X_{1},\dots,X_{n}]

∎

Proposition 8.7
.

Let
m,n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
,
h_{1},\dots,h_{m}\in K[X_{1},\dots,X_{n}]
and
S\subseteq\mathscr{R}_{m}
a
(K,P)
-semialgebraic
class. Then

\{(R,x)\in\mathscr{R}_{n}\mid(R,(h_{1}(x),\dots,h_{m}(x)))\in S\}
is a
(K,P)
-semialgebraic class.

Proof.

If
S=\bigcup_{i=1}^{k}\{(R,y)\in\mathscr{R}_{m}\mid f_{i}(y)=0,g_{i1}(y)>0,\dots,g_{i\ell}(y)>0\}
with
m,k,\ell\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
,

f_{i},g_{ij}\in K[Y_{1},\dots,Y_{m}]
so that

\{(R,x)\in\mathscr{R}_{n}\mid(h_{1}(x),\dots,h_{m}(x))\in S\}\\
=\bigcup_{i=1}^{k}\left\{(R,x)\in\mathscr{R}_{n}\mid(f_{i}(h_{1},\dots,h_{m}))(x)=0,\right.\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\ \;\\
\left.(g_{i1}(h_{1},\dots,h_{m}))(x)>0,\dots,(g_{i\ell}(h_{1},\dots,h_{m}))(x)>0\right\}.

∎

Corollary 8.8
.

Let
R
be a real closed field. Preimages of semialgebraic subsets of
R^{m}
under polynomial maps
R^{n}\to R^{m}
are again
semialgebraic in
R^{n}
.

Lemma 8.9
.

For every
s\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
,

\left\{(R,x)\in\mathscr{R}_{d+1}\mid\sigma\left(\sum_{i=0}^{d}x_{i}T^{i}\right)=s\text{ with respect to $R[T]$}\right\}

is a semialgebraic class.

Proof.

The class in question equals

\bigcup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\delta\in\{-1,0,1\}^{d+1}\\
\sigma\left(\sum_{i=0}^{d}\delta_{i}T^{i}\right)=s\text{ with respect to $\mathbb{R}[T]$}\end{subarray}}\left\{(R,x)\in\mathscr{R}_{d+1}\mid\sgn_{R}(x_{0})=\delta_{0},\dots,\sgn_{R}(x_{d})=\delta_{d}\right\}.

∎

Remark 8.10
.

We will now need the
simultaneous
diagonalization of a symmetric matrix as a quadratic form and as an endomorphism
[
\to

6.1
(g)]. The reader should know this over
\mathbb{R}
from linear algebra but we will now
need it more generally over an arbitrary real closed field. Later in this chapter, we will provide methods from which it becomes
immediately clear that, for each fixed matrix size, the class of all fields
R\in\mathscr{R}
, over which the corresponding
statement is true, is a
0
-ary semialgebraic class. Since the statement is true over
\mathbb{R}
, it must then by
8.5
also
hold true over every real closed field. In a similar way, we will soon be able to carry over
a great many statements from
\mathbb{R}
to all real closed
fields. Unfortunately, we are not that far yet and therefore we have to check if the proof from linear algebra goes through over
an arbitrary real closed field. Some of the proofs of the diagonalization in question use however proper analysis instead of
just the fundamental theorem of algebra. Since the whole analysis is built on the completeness of
\mathbb{R}

[
\to

1.16
], those proofs do not generalize without further ado. Thus we give a compact ad-hoc-proof.

Theorem 8.11
.

Let
R
be a real closed field and
M\in SR^{n\times n}
. Then there is some
P\in\GL_{n}(R)
satisfying
P^{T}P=I_{n}
such that

P^{T}MP
is a diagonal matrix.

Proof.

Call a symmetric bilinear form
V\times V\to R,\ (v,w)\mapsto\langle v,w\rangle
on an
R
-vector space
V

positive definite if
\langle v,v\rangle>0
for all
v\in V\setminus\{0\}
. Call an
R
-vector space together with a positive definite
symmetric bilinear form a Euclidean
R
-vector space. Call an endomorphism
f
of a Euclidean
R
-vector space
V

self-adjoint if
\langle f(v),w\rangle=\langle v,f(w)\rangle
for all
v,w\in V
.

Claim 1:
Let
V
be a Euclidean
R
-vector space,
f\in\End(V)
self-adjoint and
v
an eigenvector of
f
. Then

U:=\{u\in V\mid\langle u,v\rangle=0\}
is a subspace of
V
with
v\notin U
and
f(U)\subseteq U
.

Explanation.
Choose
\lambda\in R
with
f(v)=\lambda v
and let
u\in U
. Then

\langle f(u),v\rangle=\langle u,f(v)\rangle=\langle u,\lambda v\rangle=\lambda\langle u,v\rangle=\lambda 0=0
.

Claim 2:

Let
V\neq\{0\}
be a finite-dimensional Euclidean
R
-vector space and
f\in\End(V)
self-adjoint. Then
f
possesses an eigenvalue
in
R
.

Explanation.
Assume
f
has no eigenvalue. By Cayley-Hamilton and the fundamental theorem
4.14
, it is easy to show that there are

a,b\in R
with
b\neq 0
such that

(f-a\id_{V})^{2}+b^{2}\id_{V}

has a non-trivial kernel. Since
f
is self-adjoint,
g:=f-a\id_{V}
is so.
Choose
v\in V
with
g^{2}(v)=-b^{2}v
. Then
0\leq\langle g(v),g(v)\rangle=\langle g^{2}(v),v\rangle=\langle-b^{2}v,v\rangle=-b^{2}\langle v,v\rangle<0
.
\lightning

Claim 3:

Let
V
be a finite-dimensional Euclidean
R
-vector space and
f\in\End(V)
self-adjoint. Then there is an eigenbasis

v_{1},\dots,v_{n}
for
f
with
(\langle v_{i},v_{j}\rangle)_{1\leq i,j\leq n}=I_{n}
.

Explanation.
Use Claim 1, Claim 2 and induction over the dimension
V
.

In virtue of
\langle x,y\rangle:=\sum_{i=1}^{n}x_{i}y_{i}
(
x,y\in R^{n}
),
R^{n}
is a Euclidean
R
-vector space and

f\colon R^{n}\to R^{n},\ x\mapsto Mx
is self-adjoint. By Claim 3, there is an eigenbasis
v_{1},\dots,v_{n}
for
f
such that

(\langle v_{i},v_{j}\rangle)_{1\leq i,j\leq n}=I_{n}
. Set
P:=(v_{1}\ldots v_{n})\in\GL_{n}(R)
. Then

P^{T}P=\begin{pmatrix}v_{1}^{T}\\
\vdots\\
v_{n}^{T}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}v_{1}&\ldots&v_{n}\end{pmatrix}=I_{n}

and
P
is the change-of-basis matrix from
(v_{1},\dots,v_{n})
to the standard basis. It follows that

P^{T}MP=P^{-1}MP
is the representing matrix of
f
with respect to
(v_{1},\dots,v_{n})
.
∎

Corollary 8.12

(Determination of the signature using Descartes’ rule of signs)
.

Let
R
be a real closed field,
q\in R[T_{1},\dots,T_{d}]
a quadratic form and
h:=\det(M(q)-XI_{d})\in R[X]
the characteristic
polynomial of the representing matrix
[
\to

6.1
(d)]
of
q
. Then we have:

(a)

h
is real-rooted
[
\to

5.11
]

(b)

\sg q=\mu(h)-\mu(h(-X))

[
\to

6.1
(h),
5.5
(a)]

(c)

\sg q=\sigma(h)-\sigma(h(-X))

[
\to

5.5
(b)]

Proof.

Using
8.11
, choose
P\in\GL_{d}(R)
such that
P^{T}P=I_{d}
and
P^{T}M(q)P
is diagonal, say

P^{T}M(q)P=\begin{pmatrix}~\hbox to58.54pt{\vbox to55.55pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 5.15974pt\lower-51.32356pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}

from which (a) follows immediately. Because of

and

, it follows from
6.1
(e) that

\sg q=\#\{i\in\{1,\dots,d\}\mid\lambda_{i}>0\}-\#\{i\in\{1,\dots,d\}\mid\lambda_{i}<0\}=\mu(h)-\mu(h(-X)),

which proves (b). Finally, (c) follows from (a) and (b) due to the exactness of Descartes’ rule of signs for
real rooted polynomials [
\to

5.14
].
∎

Remark 8.13
.

Combining
8.12
with
6.7
, one can reduce the count of real roots of polynomials without multiplicity
with side conditions by means of the Hermite method from §
6
to the count of roots of real-rooted polynomials with
multiplicity by means of Descartes’ rule from §
5
.

Lemma 8.14
.

Let
m,n,d\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
and
f,g_{1},\dots,g_{m}\in K[X_{1},\dots,X_{n+1}]
. Then

\displaystyle\{(R,x)\in\mathscr{R}_{n}\mid

\displaystyle\deg f(x,X_{n+1})=d\quad\mathbin{\&}

\displaystyle\exists x_{n+1}\in R\colon(f(x,x_{n+1})=0\mathbin{\&}g_{1}(x,x_{n+1})>0\mathbin{\&}\ldots\mathbin{\&}g_{m}(x,x_{n+1})>0)\}

is a
(K,P)
-semialgebraic class.

Proof.

Write
f=\sum_{i=0}^{D}h_{i}X_{n+1}^{i}
for some
D\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
,
D\geq d
and
h_{i}\in K[X_{1},\dots,X_{n}]
. WLOG
h_{d}\neq 0
.
Then

f_{0}:=\sum_{i=0}^{d}\frac{h_{i}}{h_{d}}X_{n+1}^{i}\in K(X_{1},\dots,X_{n})[X_{n+1}]

is monic of degree
d
. For every
\alpha\in\{1,2\}^{m}
,
we consider also
g_{1}^{\alpha_{1}}\dotsm g_{m}^{\alpha_{m}}
as a polynomial in
X_{n+1}
with coefficients from the field

K(X_{1},\dots,X_{n})
and set

h_{\alpha}:=\det(M(H(f_{0},g_{1}^{\alpha_{1}}\dotsm g_{m}^{\alpha_{m}}))-XI_{d})\in K(X_{1},\dots,X_{n})[X].

By construction [
\to

6.1
(i),
6.2
,
6.3
], there is some
N\in\mathbb{N}
such that

h_{d}^{N}h_{\alpha}\in K[X_{1},\dots,X_{n},X]

for all
\alpha\in\{1,2\}^{m}
. Now the class from the claim can be written by

6.7
as

\displaystyle\Bigg\{(R,x)\in\mathscr{R}_{n}\mid

\displaystyle h_{D}(x)=\ldots=h_{d+1}(x)=0\neq h_{d}(x)\quad\mathbin{\&}

\displaystyle\sum_{\alpha\in\{1,2\}^{m}}\sg H(f_{0}(x,X_{n+1}),(g_{1}^{\alpha_{1}}\dotsm g_{m}^{\alpha_{m}})(x,X_{n+1}))>0\Bigg\}.

But

\left\{(R,x)\in\mathscr{R}_{n}\mid h_{d}(x)\neq 0\mathbin{\&}\sum_{\alpha\in\{1,2\}^{m}}\sg H(f_{0}(x,X_{n+1}),(g_{1}^{\alpha_{1}}\dotsm g_{m}^{\alpha_{m}})(x,X_{n+1}))>0\right\}\\
\overset{\ref{combining}}{\underset{\hbox to10.33pt{\vbox to9.05pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 5.16286pt\lower-3.14983pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}{{}{}}{{}{}}{}{{}{}}{}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 7.61 L -6.59 -3.8 L 6.59 -3.8 Z}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{0.8}{0.0}{0.0}{0.8}{-1.11221pt}{-1.94443pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}{=}}\left\{(R,x)\in\mathscr{R}_{n}\mid h_{d}(x)\neq 0\mathbin{\&}\sum_{\alpha\in\{1,2\}^{m}}(\sigma(h_{\alpha}(x,X))-\sigma(h_{\alpha}(x,-X)))>0\right\}\\
=\bigcup_{\begin{subarray}{c}(s_{\alpha})_{\alpha\in\{1,2\}^{m}},(t_{\alpha})_{\alpha\in\{1,2\}^{m}}\in\{0,\dots,d\}^{\{1,2\}^{m}}\\
\sum_{\alpha\in\{1,2\}^{m}}(s_{\alpha}-t_{\alpha})>0\end{subarray}}\bigcap_{\alpha\in\{1,2\}^{m}}\left\{(R,x)\in\mathscr{R}_{n}\mid\begin{aligned} &h_{d}(x)\neq 0,\\
&\sigma((h_{d}^{N}h_{\alpha})(x,X))=s_{\alpha},\\
&\sigma((h_{d}^{N}h_{\alpha})(x,-X))=t_{\alpha}\end{aligned}\right\}

is
(K,P)
-semialgebraic by
8.9
and
8.7
.
Here the warning sign
!
indicates where
an important argument flows in:

h_{\alpha}(x,X)=\det(M(H(f_{0}(x,X_{n+1}),(g_{1}^{\alpha_{1}}\dotsm g_{m}^{\alpha_{m}})(x,X_{n+1})))-XI_{d})

since evaluating in
x
commutes with building companion matrices, Hermite forms and with taking determinants
[
\to

6.2
,
6.1
(i)].
∎

Lemma 8.15
.

Let
R
be a real closed field,
m\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
and
g_{1},\dots,g_{m}\in R[X]
. Setting
g:=g_{1}\dotsm g_{m}
and
f:=(1-g^{2})g^{\prime}
, we have

(a)

There is an
x\in R
satisfying
g_{1}(x)>0,\dots,g_{m}(x)>0
if and only if there is such an
x\in R
satisfying in addition

f(x)=0
.

(b)

If
f=0
and
g_{1}\neq 0,\dots,g_{m}\neq 0
, then
g_{1},\dots,g_{m}\in R
.

Proof.

(b) Suppose
f=0
. Then
g^{2}=1
or
g^{\prime}=0
. In both cases it follows
g\in R
and thus
g_{1},\dots,g_{m}\in R
provided that

g_{1}\neq 0,\dots,g_{m}\neq 0
.

(a) Let
x\in R
such that
g_{1}(x)>0,\dots,g_{m}(x)>0
. Denote by
a_{1},\dots,a_{r}
where
r\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
and
a_{1}<\ldots<a_{r}
the
roots of
g
in
R
.

First consider the case where
r=0
. By the intermediate value theorem
4.16
each of the
g_{i}
is positive on
R
. It suffices therefore to show that
f
has a root in
R
. By Definition
4.9
,
g
has even degree.
If
g
has degree
0
, then
g^{\prime}=0
and we are done. So suppose now
\deg g\geq 2
. Then the degree of
g^{\prime}
is odd so that
g^{\prime}

and in particular
f
has a root in
R
by Definition
4.9
.

From now on suppose that
r>0
.
By the intermediate value theorem
4.16
each of the
g_{i}
has constant sign on each of the
intervals
(-\infty,a_{1}),(a_{1},a_{2}),\dots,(a_{r-1},a_{r}),(a_{r},\infty)
. It is therefore enough to show that
f
possesses
in each of these sets a root.
By Rolle’s theorem
4.17
,
g^{\prime}
and therefore
f
has on each of the sets
(a_{i},a_{i+1})

(
1\leq i\leq r-1
) a root. WLOG
f\neq 0
. Then
g^{\prime}\neq 0
and
g
has degree
\geq 1
. Consequently,
1-g^{2}
has a leading
monomial of even degree with a negative leading coefficient. By Lemma
5.3
(a),
(1-g^{2})(y)<0
for all
y\in R

with
|y|
sufficiently big. On the other hand,
(1-g^{2})(a_{1})=1=(1-g^{2})(a_{r})
. By the intermediate value theorem
4.16
,

1-g^{2}
and therefore
f
has a root on each of the sets
(-\infty,a_{1})
and
(a_{r},\infty)
.
∎

Lemma 8.16
.

Let
m,n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
and
g_{1},\dots,g_{m}\in K[X_{1},\dots,X_{n+1}]
. Then

\{(R,x)\in\mathscr{R}_{n}\mid\exists x_{n+1}\in R:(g_{1}(x,x_{n+1})>0\mathbin{\&}\dots\mathbin{\&}g_{m}(x,x_{n+1})>0)\}

is a
(K,P)
-semialgebraic
class.

Proof.

Set
g:=g_{1}\dotsm g_{m}
and
f:=(1-g^{2})\frac{\partial g}{\partial X_{n+1}}
. Denote by
D:=\deg_{X_{n+1}}f\in\{-\infty\}\cup\mathbb{N}_{0}

the degree of
f
considered as a polynomial in
X_{n+1}
with coefficients from
K[X_{1},\dots,X_{n}]
. The class in question equals
because of
8.15

\bigcup_{d=0}^{D}\left\{(R,x)\in\mathscr{R}_{n}\mid\deg f(x,X_{n+1})=d\mathbin{\&}\exists x_{n+1}\in R:\left(\begin{aligned} f(x,x_{n+1})&=0\ \mathbin{\&}\\
g_{1}(x,x_{n+1})&>0\ \mathbin{\&}\\
&\ \ \vdots\\
g_{m}(x,x_{n+1})&>0\end{aligned}\right)\right\}\\
\cup\{(R,x)\in\mathscr{R}_{n}\mid f(x,X_{n+1})=0\mathbin{\&}g_{1}(x,0)>0\mathbin{\&}\dots\mathbin{\&}g_{m}(x,0)>0\}

and therefore is
(K,P)
-semialgebraic by
8.14
.
∎

Theorem 8.17

(Real quantifier elimination)
.

Suppose
n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
and
S
is an
(n+1)
-ary
(K,P)
-semialgebraic class. Then

\{(R,x)\in\mathscr{R}_{n}\mid\exists x_{n+1}\in R:(R,(x,x_{n+1}))\in S\}
and

\{(R,x)\in\mathscr{R}_{n}\mid\forall x_{n+1}\in R:(R,(x,x_{n+1}))\in S\}
are
n
-ary
(K,P)
-semialgebraic classes.

Proof.

Because the second class is the complement of

\{(R,x)\in\mathscr{R}_{n}\mid\exists x_{n+1}\in R:(R,(x,x_{n+1}))\in\complement S\},

it is enough to consider the first class.
By means of
8.6
, one can assume WLOG that
S
is of the form

S=\{(R,(x,x_{n+1})\in\mathscr{R}_{n+1}\mid f(x,x_{n+1})=0,g_{1}(x,x_{n+1})>0,\dots,g_{m}(x,x_{n+1})>0\}

for some
f,g_{i}\in K[X_{1},\dots,X_{n+1}]
. Setting
D:=\deg_{X_{n+1}}f
, we obtain

\{(R,x)\in\mathscr{R}_{n}\mid\exists x_{n+1}\in R:(R,(x,x_{n+1}))\in S\}\\
=\bigcup_{d=0}^{D}\left\{(R,x)\in\mathscr{R}_{n}\mid\deg f(x,X_{n+1})=d\mathbin{\&}\exists x_{n+1}\in R:\left(\begin{aligned} f(x,x_{n+1})&=0\ \mathbin{\&}\\
g_{1}(x,x_{n+1})&>0\ \mathbin{\&}\\
&\ \ \vdots\\
g_{m}(x,x_{n+1})&>0\end{aligned}\right)\right\}\\
\cup\left(\begin{aligned} &\{(R,x)\in\mathscr{R}_{n}\mid f(x,X_{n+1})=0\}\cap\\
&\{(R,x)\in\mathscr{R}_{n}\mid\exists x_{n+1}\in R:(g_{1}(x,x_{n+1})>0\mathbin{\&}\dots\mathbin{\&}g_{m}(x,x_{n+1})>0)\}\end{aligned}\right)

which is
(K,P)
-semialgebraic by
8.14
and
8.16
.
∎

Theorem 8.18
.

[
\to

8.8
]

Let
R
be a real closed field. Images of semialgebraic subsets of
R^{n}
under polynomial maps
R^{n}\to R^{m}
are again
semialgebraic in
R^{m}
.

Proof.

Let
S\subseteq R^{n}
be semialgebraic and let
h_{1},\dots,h_{m}\in R[X_{1},\dots,X_{n}]
.
We have to show that
\{y\in\mathbb{R}^{m}\mid\exists x\in R^{n}:(x\in S\mathbin{\&}y_{1}=h_{1}(x)\mathbin{\&}\ldots\mathbin{\&}y_{m}=h_{m}(x))\}

is again semialgebraic. But this follows by applying
n
times the quantifier elimination
8.17
.
∎

Example 8.19

(Tarski principle)
.

The real quantifier elimination
8.17
can be used together with
8.5
to generalize many statements from
\mathbb{R}
to
other real closed fields. This has already been advertised in
8.10
. To give the reader a sense of the type of statements
admitting such a generalization, we give several examples.

(a)

(“intermediate value theorem for rational functions”) [
\to

4.16
] From analysis, we know for
R=\mathbb{R}
:
If
f,g\in R[X]
,
a,b\in R
with
a\leq b
,
g(c)\neq 0
for all
c\in[a,b]
and

\sgn\left(\frac{f(a)}{g(a)}\right)\neq\sgn\left(\frac{f(b)}{g(b)}\right)
, then there is a
c\in[a,b]
with
f(c)=0
.
We claim that this is valid even for all real closed fields
R
. To this end, it is enough to show that for each
d\in\mathbb{N}

S_{d}:=\left\{R\in\mathscr{R}\mid\begin{aligned} &\forall x_{0},\dots,x_{d},y_{0},\dots,y_{d},a,b\in R:\\
&\left.\left(\begin{aligned} &\left.\left(\begin{aligned} &\scriptstyle(a\leq b~\mathbin{\&}~\left(\forall c\in[a,b]:\sum_{i=0}^{d}y_{i}c^{i}\neq 0\right)~\mathbin{\&}\\
&\scriptstyle\sgn\left(\left(\sum_{i=0}^{d}x_{i}a^{i}\right)\left(\sum_{i=0}^{d}y_{i}b^{i}\right)\right)\neq\sgn\left(\left(\sum_{i=0}^{d}x_{i}b^{i}\right)\left(\sum_{i=0}^{d}y_{i}a^{i}\right)\right)\end{aligned}\right)\right\}\scriptstyle(*)\\
&\implies\left.\exists c\in[a,b]:\sum_{i=0}^{d}x_{i}c^{i}=0\right\}\scriptstyle(**)\end{aligned}\right)\right\}\scriptstyle(***)\end{aligned}\right\}

is a semialgebraic class because then
\mathbb{R}\in S_{d}
implies by
8.5

S_{d}=\mathscr{R}
. Fix
d\in\mathbb{N}
.
Applying the quantifier elimination
8.17

2d+4
times, it is enough to show that the following class is
semialgebraic:

\{(R,(x_{0},\dots,x_{d},y_{0},\dots,y_{d},a,b))\in\mathscr{R}^{2d+4}\mid(***)\}=\\
\complement\underbrace{\{(R,(x_{0},\dots,x_{d},y_{0},\dots,y_{d},a,b))\in\mathscr{R}^{2d+4}\mid(*)\}}_{S^{\prime}}\\
\cup\underbrace{\{(R,(x_{0},\dots,x_{d},y_{0},\dots,y_{d},a,b))\in\mathscr{R}^{2d+4}\mid(**)\}}_{S^{\prime\prime}}

It is thus enough to show that
S^{\prime}
and
S^{\prime\prime}
are semialgebraic. We accomplish this in each case by applying the
quantifier elimination
8.17
. We explicate this only for
S^{\prime}
since it is analogous and even simpler for
S^{\prime\prime}
:

S^{\prime}=\{(R,(x_{0},\dots,x_{d},y_{0},\dots,y_{d},a,b))\mid b-a\geq 0\}\cap\\
\{(R,(x_{0},\dots,x_{d},y_{0},\dots,y_{d},a,b))\mid\forall c\in R:(\overbrace{c\in[a,b]\implies\sum_{i=0}^{d}y_{i}c^{i}\neq 0}^{(****)})\}\cap\\
\bigcup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\delta,\varepsilon\in\{-1,0,1\}\\
\delta\neq\varepsilon\end{subarray}}\left\{(R,(x_{0},\dots,x_{d},y_{0},\dots,y_{d},a,b))\mid\begin{aligned} \scriptstyle\sgn\left(\left(\sum_{i=0}^{d}x_{i}a^{i}\right)\left(\sum_{i=0}^{d}y_{i}b^{i}\right)\right)=\delta,\\
\scriptstyle\sgn\left(\left(\sum_{i=0}^{d}x_{i}b^{i}\right)\left(\sum_{i=0}^{d}y_{i}a^{i}\right)\right)=\varepsilon\end{aligned}\right\}.

By quantifier elimination it is enough to show that

\{(R,(x_{0},\dots,x_{d},y_{0},\dots,y_{d},a,b))\mid({*}{*}{*}{*})\}

is semialgebraic. But this class equals

\{(R,(x_{0},\dots,x_{d},y_{0},\dots,y_{d},a,b))\mid c<a\text{ or }b<c\}~\cup\\
\left\{(R,(x_{0},\dots,x_{d},y_{0},\dots,y_{d},a,b,c))\mid\sum_{i=0}^{d}y_{i}c^{i}\neq 0\right\}

(b)

Let
R
be a real closed field and
f\in R[X]
with
f\geq 0
on
R
. We claim that the sum
g:=f+f^{\prime}+f^{\prime\prime}+\dots
of all
derivatives of
f
satisfies again
g\geq 0
on
R
. We show this first for
R=\mathbb{R}
: In this case, we have for all
x\in\mathbb{R}

\frac{dg(x)e^{-x}}{dx}=g^{\prime}(x)e^{-x}-g(x)e^{-x}=(g^{\prime}(x)-g(x))e^{-x}=-f(x)e^{-x}\leq 0,

from which it follows that
h\colon\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R},\ x\mapsto g(x)e^{-x}
is
anti-monotonic [
\to

4.19
].
From this and the fact that
\lim_{x\to\infty}h(x)=\lim_{x\to\infty}(g(x)e^{-x})=0
, we deduce that
h(x)\geq 0
and therefore

g(x)\geq 0
for all
x\in\mathbb{R}
. Thus the claim is proved for
R=\mathbb{R}
. To show it for all real closed fields
R
, it is now enough to
show that for all
d\in\mathbb{N}

\displaystyle S_{d}:=\Bigg\{R\in\mathscr{R}\mid~

\displaystyle\forall a_{0},\dots,a_{d}\in R:\Bigg(\left(\forall x\in R:\sum_{i=0}^{d}a_{i}x^{i}\geq 0\right)\implies

\displaystyle\forall x\in R:\sum_{k=0}^{d}\sum_{i=k}^{d}i(i-1)\dotsm(i-k+1)a_{i}x^{i-k}\geq 0\Bigg)\Bigg\}

is semialgebraic since then by
8.5

\mathbb{R}\in S_{d}
implies
S_{d}=\mathscr{R}
. This can be shown for each

d\in\mathbb{N}
by applying the quantifier elimination
d+3
times.

(c)

We can reprove
8.11
since for
R=\mathbb{R}
it is already known from linear algebra and it suffices to show for
fixed
n\in\mathbb{N}
that

S_{n}:=\left\{R\in\mathscr{R}\mid\begin{aligned} &\forall a_{11},a_{12},\dots,a_{nn}\in R:\\
&\left(\begin{aligned} &(\forall i,j\in\{1,\dots,n\}:a_{ij}=a_{ji})\implies\\
&\left(\begin{aligned} &\exists b_{11},b_{12},\dots,b_{nn}\in R:\\
&\left(\begin{aligned} \begin{aligned} &\left(\forall i,k\in\{1,\dots,n\}:\sum_{j=1}^{n}b_{ji}b_{jk}=\delta_{ik}\right)\mathbin{\&}\\
&\forall i,\ell\in\{1,\dots,n\}:\left(i\neq\ell\implies\sum_{j,k=1}^{n}b_{ji}a_{jk}b_{k\ell}=0\right)\end{aligned}\end{aligned}\right)\end{aligned}\right)\end{aligned}\right)\end{aligned}\right\}

is semialgebraic. We manage to do so by implementing the quantifications over
i,j,k,\ell
as finite intersections of semialgebraic
classes and by eliminating the quantification over
a_{11},\dots,b_{nn}
by applying
2n^{2}
times
8.17
.

(d)

By
8.5
,
\{R\in\mathscr{R}\mid R\text{ archimedean}\}
[
\to

1.9
(a)] is not a semialgebraic class
(if
\mathscr{R}
is big enough) since it contains
\mathbb{R}
but not
\overline{(\mathbb{R}(X),P)}
where
P
is an arbitrary order of
\mathbb{R}(X)
.

9
Canonical isomorphisms of Boolean algebras of semialgebraic sets and classes

In this section, we fix again an ordered field
(K,P)
and a set
\mathscr{R}
of real closed extensions of
(K,P)

[
\to

8.3
].

Definition 9.1
.

Let
M_{1}
and
M_{2}
be sets,
\mathscr{S}_{1}
a Boolean algebra on
M_{1}
and
\mathscr{S}_{2}
a Boolean algebra on

M_{2}
. Then
\Phi\colon\mathscr{S}_{1}\to\mathscr{S}_{2}
is called a
homomorphism of Boolean algebras
if

\Phi(\emptyset)=\emptyset
,
\Phi\left(\complement S\right)=\complement\Phi(S)
,

\Phi(S\cap T)=\Phi(S)\cap\Phi(T)
and
\Phi(S\cup T)=\Phi(S)\cup\Phi(T)
for all
S,T\in\mathscr{S}_{1}
. If
\Phi
is in addition

\begin{Bmatrix}\text{injective}\\
\text{surjective}\\
\text{bijective}\end{Bmatrix}
, then
\Phi
is called an
\begin{Bmatrix}\text{embedding}\\
\text{epimorphismus}\\
\text{isomophism}\end{Bmatrix}
of Boolean
algebras.

Lemma 9.2
.

Suppose
\mathscr{S}_{1}
and
\mathscr{S}_{2}
are Boolean algebras and

\Phi\colon\mathscr{S}_{1}\to\mathscr{S}_{2}
is a homomorphism. Then the following are equivalent:

(a)

\Phi
is an embedding.

(b)

\forall S\in\mathscr{S}_{1}:(\Phi(S)=\emptyset\implies S=\emptyset)

Proof.

(a)
\implies
(b)
Suppose (a) holds and consider
S\in\mathscr{S}_{1}
such that
\Phi(S)=\emptyset
. Then

\Phi(S)=\emptyset=\Phi(\emptyset)
and hence
S=\emptyset
by the injectivity of
\Phi
.

(b)
\implies
(a)
Suppose (b) holds and let
S,T\in\mathscr{S}_{1}
such that
\Phi(S)=\Phi(T)
. Then

\Phi(S\setminus T)=\Phi\left(S\cap\complement T\right)=\Phi(S)\cap\complement\Phi(T)=\emptyset
and
therefore
S\setminus T=\emptyset
. Analogously, we obtain
T\setminus S=\emptyset
. Then
S=T
.
∎

Notation 9.3
.

Let
n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
. From now on, we denote by
\mathscr{S}_{n}
the Boolean algebra of all
n
-ary

(K,P)
-semialgebraic classes.
For every
R\in\mathscr{R}
, we let furthermore
\mathscr{S}_{n,R}
denote the Boolean algebra of all

K
-semialgebraic subsets of
R^{n}

(i.e.,
\mathscr{S}_{n,R}=\mathscr{S}_{n}
for
\mathscr{R}=\{R\}
). We call the
map
\set_{R}\colon\mathscr{S}_{n}\to\mathscr{S}_{n,R},\ S\mapsto\{x\in R^{n}\mid(R,x)\in S\}
the
setification
to
R
for every
R\in\mathscr{R}
.

Theorem and Definition 9.4
.

Let
n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
and
R\in\mathscr{R}
. The setification

\set_{R}\colon\mathscr{S}_{n}\to\mathscr{S}_{n,R}

is an isomorphism of
Boolean algebras. We call its inverse map

\class_{R}:=\set_{R}^{-1}\colon\mathscr{S}_{n,R}\to\mathscr{S}_{n}

the

classification
.

Proof.

It is clear that
\set_{R}
is an epimorphism. Suppose
\emptyset\neq S\in\mathscr{S}_{n}
. By Lemma
9.2
, it suffices to show

\set_{R}S\neq\emptyset
. By the quantifier elimination
8.17
,

T:=\{R^{\prime}\in\mathscr{R}\mid\exists x\in R^{\prime n}:(R^{\prime},x)\in S\}

is

(K,P)
-semialgebraic and hence by
8.5
either empty or
\mathscr{R}
. From
S\neq\emptyset
, we have of course

T\neq\emptyset
. Therefore
R\in\mathscr{R}=T
, i.e., there is some
x\in R^{n}
with
(R,x)\in S
. Then
x\in\set_{R}S
and thus

\set_{R}S\neq\emptyset
.
∎

Corollary and Definition 9.5
.

Let
n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
and
R,R^{\prime}\in\mathscr{R}
. Then there is exactly one isomorphism of Boolean algebras

\transfer_{R,R^{\prime}}\colon\mathscr{S}_{n,R}\to\mathscr{S}_{n,R^{\prime}}
satisfying

\transfer_{R,R^{\prime}}(\{x\in R^{n}\mid p(x)\geq 0\})=\{x\in R^{\prime n}\mid p(x)\geq 0\}

for all
p\in K[X_{1},\dots,X_{n}]
. We call
\transfer_{R,R^{\prime}}
the
transfer
from
R
to
R^{\prime}
.

Proof.

The uniqueness is clear since
\mathscr{S}_{n,R}
is generated by

\{\{x\in R^{n}\mid p(x)\geq 0\}\mid p\in K[X_{1},\dots,X_{n}]\}

[
\to

8.2
(b)]. Existence is established by setting
\transfer_{R,R^{\prime}}:=\set_{R^{\prime}}\circ\class_{R}
.
Indeed, let
p\in K[X_{1},\dots,X_{n}]
and set
S:=\{(R^{\prime\prime},x)\in\mathscr{R}_{n}\mid p(x)\geq 0\text{ in $R^{\prime\prime}$}\}
. Then the claim is
that
\transfer_{R,R^{\prime}}(\set_{R}S)=\set_{R^{\prime}}(S)
which is clear since

\transfer_{R,R^{\prime}}(\set_{R}S)=(\set_{R^{\prime}}\circ\class_{R})(\set_{R}S)=\set_{R^{\prime}}((\underbrace{\class_{R}\circ\set_{R}}_{\id_{\mathscr{S}_{n}}})(S))
.
∎

Chapter 2
Hilbert’s 17th problem

1
Nonnegative polynomials in one variable

Theorem 1.1
.

Suppose
R
is a real closed field and
f\in R[X]
. Then the following are equivalent:

(a)

f\geq 0
on
R

[
\to

4.15
]

(b)

f
is a sum of two squares in
R[X]
.

(c)

f\in\sum R[X]^{2}

[
\to

1.18
]

Proof.

(b)
\implies
(c)
\implies
(a) is trivial. In order to show (a)
\implies
(b), we
set
C:=R(\mathbbm{i})
and consider the ring automorphism

C[X]\to C[X],\ p\mapsto p^{*}

given by
a^{*}=a
for
a\in R
,
\mathbbm{i}^{*}=-\mathbbm{i}
and
X^{*}=X
. WLOG
f\neq 0
. By
the fundamental theorem of algebra
4.14
, there exist

k,\ell\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
,
c\in R^{\times}
,
a_{1},\dots,a_{k}\in R
,
b_{1},\dots,b_{k}\in R^{\times}
,
\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{\ell}\in\mathbb{N}
and
pairwise different
d_{1},\dots,d_{\ell}\in R
such that

\displaystyle f

\displaystyle=c\left(\prod_{i=1}^{k}((X-a_{i})^{2}+b_{i}^{2})\right)\prod_{j=1}^{\ell}(X-d_{j})^{\alpha_{j}}

\displaystyle=c\left(\prod_{i=1}^{k}(X-(a_{i}+b_{i}\mathbbm{i}))\right)\left(\prod_{i=1}^{k}(X-(a_{i}-b_{i}\mathbbm{i}))\right)\prod_{j=1}^{\ell}(X-d_{j})^{\alpha_{j}}.

Suppose now
f\geq 0
on
R
. Then we have
0\leq\sgn(f(x))=(\sgn c)\prod_{j=1}^{\ell}(\sgn(x-d_{j}))^{\alpha_{j}}
for all
x\in R
.
From this, we deduce easily
\alpha_{j}\in 2\mathbb{N}
and
c\in R^{2}
. Setting

g:=\sqrt{c}\left(\prod_{i=1}^{k}(X-(a_{i}+b_{i}\mathbbm{i}))\right)\prod_{j=1}^{\ell}(X-d_{j})^{\frac{\alpha_{j}}{2}}\in C[X],

we have now
f=g^{*}g
. Writing
g=p+\mathbbm{i}q
with
p,q\in R[X]
, this amounts to

f=(p-\mathbbm{i}q)(p+\mathbbm{i}q)=p^{2}+q^{2}
.
∎

Theorem 1.2

(Cassels)
.

Let
(K,\leq)
be an ordered field. Suppose
\ell\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
,
f_{1},\dots,f_{\ell}\in K[X]
,
g_{1},\dots,g_{\ell}\in K[X]\setminus\{0\}
and

a_{1},\dots,a_{\ell}\in K_{\geq 0}
with
\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}a_{i}\left(\frac{f_{i}}{g_{i}}\right)^{2}\in K[X]
. Then there are
p_{1},\dots,p_{\ell}\in K[X]
such
that

\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}a_{i}\left(\frac{f_{i}}{g_{i}}\right)^{2}=\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}a_{i}p_{i}^{2}.

Proof.

WLOG
a_{i}>0
for all
i\in\{1,\dots,\ell\}
and
g_{1}=\ldots=g_{\ell}
. It suffices to show:
Let
h\in K[X]
a polynomial for which there exists some
g\in K[X]
of degree
\geq 1
and

f_{1},\dots,f_{\ell}\in K[X]
satisfying
hg^{2}=\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}a_{i}f_{i}^{2}
. Then there is some
G\in K[X]\setminus\{0\}

with a degree that is smaller than that of
g
and
F_{1},\dots,F_{\ell}\in K[X]
satisfying

hG^{2}=\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}a_{i}F_{i}^{2}
.
We prove this: Write
f_{i}=q_{i}g+r_{i}
with
q_{i},r_{i}\in K[X]
and
\deg r_{i}<\deg g

for all
i\in\{1,\dots,\ell\}
. If
r_{i}=0
for all
i\in\{1,\dots,\ell\}
, then we set
G:=1
and
F_{i}:=q_{i}
for all

i\in\{1,\dots,\ell\}
and have

hG^{2}=h=\frac{1}{g^{2}}(hg^{2})=\frac{1}{g^{2}}\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}a_{i}f_{i}^{2}=\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}a_{i}\left(\frac{f_{i}}{g}\right)^{2}=\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}a_{i}q_{i}^{2}=\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}a_{i}F_{i}^{2}.

In the sequel, we suppose that the set
I:=\{i\in\{1,\dots,\ell\}\mid r_{i}\neq 0\}
is nonempty.
Now we set
s:=\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}a_{i}q_{i}^{2}-h
,
t:=\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}a_{i}f_{i}q_{i}-gh
,

F_{i}:=sf_{i}-2tq_{i}
for
i\in\{1,\dots,\ell\}
and
G:=sg-2t
. Then we obtain

\displaystyle hG^{2}

\displaystyle=s^{2}hg^{2}-4stgh+4t^{2}h

\displaystyle=s^{2}\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}a_{i}f_{i}^{2}-4st(t+gh)+4t^{2}(s+h)

\displaystyle=s^{2}\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}a_{i}f_{i}^{2}-4st\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}a_{i}f_{i}q_{i}+4t^{2}\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}a_{i}q_{i}^{2}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}a_{i}(sf_{i}-2tq_{i})^{2}=\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}a_{i}F_{i}^{2}.

It remains to show that
G\neq 0
and
\deg G<\deg g
. To this end, we calculate

\displaystyle G

\displaystyle=g\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}a_{i}q_{i}^{2}-gh-2\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}a_{i}f_{i}q_{i}+2gh

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{g}\left(g^{2}\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}a_{i}q_{i}^{2}+g^{2}h-2g\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}a_{i}f_{i}q_{i}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{g}\left(g^{2}\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}a_{i}q_{i}^{2}+\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}a_{i}f_{i}^{2}-2g\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}a_{i}f_{i}q_{i}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{g}\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}a_{i}(g^{2}q_{i}^{2}-2(gq_{i})f_{i}+f_{i}^{2})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{g}\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}a_{i}(gq_{i}-f_{i})^{2}=\frac{1}{g}\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}a_{i}r_{i}^{2}=\frac{1}{g}\sum_{i\in I}a_{i}r_{i}^{2}.

If we had
G=0
, then this would mean
\sum_{i\in I}a_{i}r_{i}^{2}=0
. Since the leading coefficient of
a_{i}r_{i}^{2}

is positive for all
i\in I\neq\emptyset
, this is impossible. Hence
G\neq 0
. Because of

\deg r_{i}<\deg g
for all
i\in I
, we have
\deg G<2\deg g-\deg g=\deg g
.
∎

2
Homogenization and dehomogenization

Definition 2.1
.

Let
A
be commutative ring with
0\neq 1
.

(a)

If
k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
and
f\in A[X_{1},\dots,X_{n}]
, then the sum of all terms (i.e.,
monomials with their coefficients)
of degree
k
of
f
is called the
k
-th homogeneous part
of
f
. This is a
k
-form
[
\to

6.1
(a)].

(b)

If
f\in A[X_{1},\dots,X_{n}]\setminus\{0\}
and
d:=\deg f
, then the
d
-th homogeneous part of
f

is called the
leading form

\lf(f)
of
f
. We set
\lf(0):=0
.

(c)

If
f\in A[X_{1},\dots,X_{n}]
,
d:=\deg f\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
and
f=\sum_{k=0}^{d}f_{k}
with a
k
-form
f_{k}
for
all
k\in\{0,\dots,d\}
, then the
homogenization

f^{*}\in A[X_{0},\dots,X_{n}]
of
f
(with respect to

X_{0}
) is
given by

f^{*}:=\sum_{k=0}^{d}X_{0}^{d-k}f_{k}

which equals
X_{0}^{d}f\left(\frac{X_{1}}{X_{0}},\dots,\frac{X_{n}}{X_{0}}\right)
in case
A
is a field
(since then the field of rational functions
A(X_{0},X_{1},\ldots,X_{n})
exists).
We set
0^{*}:=0
.

(d)

For homogeneous
f\in A[X_{0},\dots,X_{n}]
, we call
\widetilde{f}:=f(1,X_{1},\dots,X_{n})

the
dehomogenization
of
f
(with respect to
X_{0}
).

Remark 2.2
.

Let
A
be a commutative ring with
0\neq 1
.

(a)

\lf(f)=f^{*}(0,X_{1},\dots,X_{n})
for all
f\in A[X_{1},\dots,X_{n}]
.

(b)

For
f,g\in A[X_{1},\dots,X_{n}]
, we have

(f+g)^{*}=f^{*}+g^{*}

in case
\deg f=\deg g=\deg(f+g)
and

(fg)^{*}=f^{*}g^{*}

if
A
is an integral domain.

(c)

A[X_{0},\dots,X_{n}]\to A[X_{1},\dots,X_{n}],\ f\mapsto\widetilde{f}
is a ring homomorphism.

(d)

For all
f,g\in A[X_{1},\dots,X_{n}]
, we have

\lf(f+g)=\lf(f)+\lf(g)

in case
\deg f=\deg g=\deg(f+g)
and

\lf(fg)=\lf(f)\lf(g)

if
A
is an integral domain.

(e)

For all
f\in A[X_{1},\dots,X_{n}]
, we have
\widetilde{f^{*}\,}=f
.

(f)

If
f\in A[X_{0},\dots,X_{n}]\setminus\{0\}
is homogeneous and
m:=\max\{k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}\mid X_{0}^{k}\mid f\}
,
then
X_{0}^{m}{\widetilde{f}\;}^{*}=f
.

Lemma 2.3
.

Suppose
K
is a field,
n,d\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
,
f\in K[X_{1},\dots,X_{n}]_{d}
and let
I_{1},\dots,I_{n}\subseteq K

be sets of cardinality at least
d+1
each
such that
f(x)=0
for all
x\in I_{1}\times\ldots\times I_{n}
. Then
f=0
.

Proof.

Induction by
n
.

n=0
✓

n-1\to n\quad(n\in\mathbb{N})
Write
f=\sum_{k=0}^{d}f_{k}X_{n}^{k}
with
f_{k}\in K[X_{1},\dots,X_{n-1}]_{d}
.
For all

(x_{1},\dots,x_{n-1})\in I_{1}\times\ldots\times I_{n-1},

the polynomial

f(x_{1},\dots,x_{n-1},X_{n})=\sum_{k=0}^{d}f_{k}(x_{1},\dots,x_{n-1})X_{n}^{k}\in K[X_{n}]_{d}
is
a polynomial with at
d+1
roots. Thus
f_{k}(x_{1},\dots,x_{n-1})=0
for all
k\in\{0,\dots,d\}
and

(x_{1},\dots,x_{n-1})\in I_{1}\times\ldots\times I_{n-1}
. By induction hypothesis,
f_{k}=0
for all

k\in\{0,\dots,d\}
.
∎

Remark 2.4
.

Let
K
be a real field,
\ell,n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
,
p_{1},\dots,p_{\ell}\in K[X_{1},\dots,X_{n}]
and

f:=\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}p_{i}^{2}.

(a)

If
f=0
, then
p_{1}=\ldots=p_{\ell}=0
. This follows from
2.3
together with
2.12
(c).
Instead of
2.3
, one can alternatively employ the fact that
K(X_{1},\dots,X_{n})
is real which is clear
by applying
3.7

n
times.

(b)

If
f\neq 0
, then
\deg f=2d
with
d:=\max\{\deg(p_{i})\mid i\in\{1,\dots,\ell\}\}
since otherwise

\sum_{i=1,\deg(p_{i})=d}^{\ell}\lf(p_{i})^{2}=0
, contradicting (a).

(c)

If
d\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
and
f
is a
2d
-form, then every
p_{i}
is a
d
-form. This can be seen similarly to
(b) by considering the homogeneous parts of the
p_{i}
of smallest (instead of largest) degree.

(d)

We have
f^{*}\in\sum K[X_{0},\dots,X_{n}]^{2}
. More precisely,
f^{*}
is a
2d
-form for some
d\in\mathbb{N}_{0}

that is a sum of
\ell
squares of
d
-forms since

f^{*}=X_{0}^{2d}f\left(\frac{X_{1}}{X_{0}},\dots,\frac{X_{n}}{X_{0}}\right)=\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}\left(X_{0}^{d}p_{i}\left(\frac{X_{1}}{X_{0}},\dots,\frac{X_{n}}{X_{0}}\right)\right)^{2}

and
X_{0}^{d}p_{i}\left(\frac{X_{1}}{X_{0}},\dots,\frac{X_{n}}{X_{0}}\right)=X_{0}^{d-\deg p_{i}}p_{i}^{*}\in K[X_{0},\dots,X_{n}]

for all
i\in\{1,\dots,\ell\}
with
p_{i}\neq 0
(note that
\deg p_{i}\leq d
by (b)).

Proposition 2.5
.

Let
(K,\leq)
be an ordered field and
f\in K[X_{1},\dots,X_{n}]
with
f\geq 0
on
K^{n}
. Then
f
has
an even degree except if
f=0
, and we have
\lf(f)\geq 0
on
K^{n}
.

Proof.

WLOG
f\neq 0
. Then
g:=\lf(f)\neq 0
. Set
d:=\deg g
. For all
x\in K^{n}
,
f_{x}:=f(Tx)\in K[T]
is a polynomial
in one variable with
f_{x}\geq 0
on
K
whose leading coefficient is
g(x)
in case that
g(x)\neq 0
.
Choose
x_{0}\in K^{n}
with
g(x_{0})\neq 0
[
\to

2.3
]. Then
f_{x_{0}}
has degree
d
and because
of
f_{x_{0}}\geq 0
on
K
, it follows that
d\in 2\mathbb{N}_{0}
by
5.3
(a).
Now let
x\in K^{n}
be arbitrary such that

g(x)\neq 0
. Again by
5.3
(a), it follows from
f_{x}\geq 0
on
K
that
g(x)\geq 0
.
∎

Proposition 2.6
.

Let
(K,\leq)
be an ordered field and
f\in K[X_{1},\dots,X_{n}]
.

(a)

f\geq 0
on
K^{n}\iff f^{*}\geq 0\text{ on }K^{n+1}

(b)

f\in\sum K[X_{1},\dots,X_{n}]^{2}\iff f^{*}\in\sum K[X_{0},\dots,X_{n}]^{2}

Proof.

(a) “
\Longleftarrow
” If
f^{*}
is nonnegative on
K^{n+1}
, then also on
\{1\}\times K^{n}
.

“
\Longrightarrow
” Suppose
f\geq 0
on
K^{n}
. WLOG
f\neq 0
. By
2.5
, we can write
\deg f=2d

with
d\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
.
Due to
f^{*}\overset{\ref{introhom}(c)}{=}X_{0}^{2d}f\left(\frac{X_{1}}{X_{0}},\dots,\frac{X_{n}}{X_{0}}\right)
,
we deduce
f^{*}\geq 0
on
K^{\times}\times K^{n}
. It remains to show
f^{*}\geq 0
on
\{0\}\times K^{n}

which is equivalent by
2.2
(a) to
\lf(f)\geq 0
on
K^{n}
. The latter holds by
2.5
.

(b) “
\Longrightarrow
” has been shown in
2.4
(d).

“
\Longleftarrow
” follows from
2.2
(c).
∎

3
Nonnegative quadratic polynomials

Definition 3.1
.

Let
(K,\leq)
be an ordered field.

(a)

If
f\in K[X_{1},\dots,X_{n}]
is homogeneous [
\to

6.1
(a)], then
f
is called

\begin{Bmatrix}\text{\emph{positive semidefinite (psd)}}\\
\text{\emph{positive definite (pd)}}\end{Bmatrix}
(over
K
) if

f\begin{Bmatrix}\geq 0\text{ on }K^{n}\\
>0\text{ on }K^{n}\setminus\{0\}\end{Bmatrix}
.

(b)

If
M\in SK^{n\times n}
, then
M
is called
\begin{Bmatrix}\text{psd}\\
\text{pd}\end{Bmatrix}
(over K) if the quadratic form
represented by
M
[
\to

6.1
(d)] is
\begin{Bmatrix}\text{psd}\\
\text{pd}\end{Bmatrix}
, i.e.,
x^{T}Mx\begin{Bmatrix}\geq 0\text{ for all }x\in K^{n}\\
>0\text{ for all }x\in K^{n}\setminus\{0\}\end{Bmatrix}
.

Proposition 3.2
.

Let
K
be a Euclidean field and
q\in K[X_{1},\dots,X_{n}]
a quadratic form. Then the
following are equivalent:

(a)

q
is psd
[
\to

3.1
(a)]

(b)

q\in\sum K[X_{1},\dots,X_{n}]^{2}

[
\to

1.18
]

(c)

q
is a sum of
n
squares of linear forms
[
\to

6.1
(a)]
.

(d)

\sg q=\rk q

[
\to

6.1
(h)]
.

Proof.

(d)
\implies
(c)
\implies
(b)
\implies
(a) is trivial. Now suppose that (d) does not hold. We show that
then (a) also fails. Write
q=\sum_{i=1}^{s}\ell_{i}^{2}-\sum_{j=1}^{t}\ell_{s+j}^{2}
with
s,t\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
and
linearly independent linear forms
\ell_{1},\dots,\ell_{s},\ell_{s+1},\dots,\ell_{s+t}\in K[X_{1},\dots,X_{n}]
.
Since
s-t=\sg q\neq\rk q=s+t
, we have
t\geq 1
. By linear algebra,

\varphi\colon K^{n}\to K^{s+t},\ x\mapsto\begin{pmatrix}\ell_{1}(x)\\
\vdots\\
\ell_{s+t}(x)\end{pmatrix}

is surjective. Choose
x\in K^{n}
with
\varphi(x)=\begin{pmatrix}0\\
\vdots\\
0\\
1\end{pmatrix}
. Then

q(x)=-1<0
.
∎

Proposition 3.3
.

Let
K
be a Euclidean field and
M\in SK^{n\times n}
. Then the following are equivalent:

(a)

M
is psd
[
\to

3.1
(b)]
.

(b)

\exists s\in\mathbb{N}_{0}:\exists A\in K^{s\times n}:M=A^{T}A

(c)

\exists A\in K^{n\times n}:M=A^{T}A

(d)

All eigenvalues of
M
in the real closure
\overline{(K,K^{2})}
are nonnegative.

(e)

All coefficients of
\det(M+XI_{n})\in K[X]
are nonnegative.

(f)

If
M=(a_{ij})_{1\leq i,j\leq n}
, then for all
I\subseteq\{1,\dots,n\}
, we have

\det((a_{ij})_{(i,j)\in I\times I})\geq 0
.

Proof.

Using
6.1
(e) and
2.4
(c), one sees that (a), (b) and (c) are nothing else than the corresponding statements in
3.2
.

(a)
\implies
(f)
follows from applying (a)
\implies
(c) to the submatrices of
M
in question.

(f)
\implies
(e)
Each coefficients of
\det(M+XI_{n})
is a sum of certain determinants
appearing in (f).

(e)
\implies
(d)
is trivial.

(d)
\implies
(a)
follows easily from
8.11
.
∎

Terminology 3.4
.

[
\to

5.1
,
6.1
(a)]
Let
A
be a commutative ring with
0\neq 1
.
Polynomials from
A[X_{1},\dots,X_{n}]_{d}
[
\to

5.1
] are called

constant
for
d=0
,
linear
for
d=1
,
quadratic
for
d=2
,
cubic
for
d=3
,

quartic
for
d=4
,
quintic
for
d=5
, …

Proposition 3.5
.

Let
K
be a Euclidean field and
q\in K[X_{1},\dots,X_{n}]_{2}
.
The following are equivalent:

(a)

q\geq 0
on
K^{n}

(b)

q\in\sum K[X_{1},\dots,X_{n}]^{2}

(c)

q
is a sum of
n+1
squares of linear polynomials.

Proof.

(a)
\overset{\text{\ref{psdpsdhom}(a)}}{\implies}q^{*}\geq 0\text{ on $K^{n+1}$}\overset{\ref{sospsd2}}{\implies}
(c)
\implies
(b)

\implies
(a)
∎

4
The Newton polytope

Definition and Proposition 4.1
.

Let
(K,\leq)
be an ordered field,
V
a
K
-vector space and
A\subseteq V
. Then
A
is called

convex
if
\forall x,y\in A:\forall\lambda\in[0,1]_{K}:\lambda x+(1-\lambda)y\in A
.
The smallest convex superset of
A
is obviously

\conv A:=\left\{\sum_{i=1}^{m}\lambda_{i}x_{i}\mid m\in\mathbb{N},\lambda_{i}\in K_{\geq 0},x_{i}\in A,\sum_{i=1}^{m}\lambda_{i}=1\right\},

called the convex set
generated
by
A
or the
convex hull
of
A
.
We call finitely generated convex sets, i.e., convex hulls of finite sets,
polytopes
.
A polytope is thus of the form

\conv\{x_{1},\dots,x_{m}\}=\left\{\sum_{i=1}^{m}\lambda_{i}x_{i}\mid\lambda_{i}\in K_{\geq 0},\sum_{i=1}^{m}\lambda_{i}=1\right\}

for some
m\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
and
x_{1},\dots,x_{m}\in V
. If
A
is a convex set, then a point
x\in A
is called
an
extreme point
of
A
if there are no
y,z\in A
such that
y\neq z
and
x=\frac{y+z}{2}
.
Extreme points of polytopes are also called
vertices
of the polytope.

Exercise 4.2
.

Suppose
(K,\leq)
is an ordered field,
V
a
K
-vector space,
A\subseteq V
convex,

x\in A
and
\lambda\in(0,1)_{K}
.
Then the following are equivalent:

(a)

x
is an extreme point of
A
.

(b)

There are no
y,z\in A
such that
y\neq z
and
x=\lambda y+(1-\lambda)z
.

Lemma 4.3
.

Let
(K,\leq)
be an ordered field,
V
a
K
-vector space,
m\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
,

x_{1},\dots,x_{m}\in V
,
P:=\conv\{x_{1},\dots,x_{m}\}
and suppose

P\neq\conv(\{x_{1},\dots,x_{m}\}\setminus\{x_{i}\})
for all
i\in\{1,\dots,m\}
. Then
P
is a polytope
and
x_{1},\dots,x_{m}
are its vertices.

Proof.

To show:

(a)

Every vertex of
P
equals one of the
x_{i}
.

(b)

Every
x_{i}
is a vertex of
P
.

For (a), let
x
be a vertex of
P
. Write
x=\sum_{i=1}^{m}\lambda_{i}x_{i}
with
\lambda_{i}\in K_{\geq 0}
and

\sum_{i=1}^{m}\lambda_{i}=1
. WLOG
\lambda_{1}\neq 0
. Then
\lambda_{1}=1
for otherwise

\mu:=\sum_{i=2}^{m}\lambda_{i}=1-\lambda_{1}>0
and

x=\lambda_{1}x_{1}+\mu\Bigg(\underbrace{\sum_{i=2}^{m}\frac{\lambda_{i}}{\mu}x_{i}}_{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptstyle\in\conv\{x_{2},\dots,x_{m}\}$\hss}}\Bigg)
,
contradicting
4.2
(b).

To prove (b), we let
y,z\in P
with
x_{1}=\frac{y+z}{2}
. To show:
y=z
. Write
y=\sum_{i=1}^{m}\lambda_{i}x_{i}

and
z=\sum_{i=1}^{m}\mu_{i}x_{i}
with
\lambda_{i},\mu_{i}\in K_{\geq 0}
and

\sum_{i=1}^{m}\lambda_{i}=1=\sum_{i=1}^{m}\mu_{i}
. We show that
\lambda_{1}=1=\mu_{1}
. It is enough to show

\frac{\lambda_{1}+\mu_{1}}{2}=1
. If we had
\frac{\lambda_{1}+\mu_{1}}{2}<1
, then it would follow from

(1-\frac{\lambda_{1}+\mu_{1}}{2})x_{1}=\sum_{i=2}^{m}\frac{\lambda_{i}+\mu_{i}}{2}x_{i}
that
x_{1}\in\conv\{x_{2},\dots,x_{m}\}

and therefore
P=\conv\{x_{1},\dots,x_{m}\}=\conv\{x_{2},\dots,x_{m}\}\ \lightning
.
∎

Corollary 4.4
.

Every polytope is the convex hull of its finitely many vertices.

Definition and Proposition 4.5
.

Suppose
(K,\leq)
is an ordered field,
V
is a
K
-vector space and let
A
and
B
be subsets of

V
. Then
A+B:=\{x+y\mid x\in A,y\in B\}
is called the
Minkowski sum
of
A
and
B
. We
have
(\conv A)+(\conv B)=\conv(A+B)
. Let now
A
and
B
be convex. Then
A+B
is also convex.
If
z
is an extreme point of
A+B
, then there are uniquely determined
x\in A
and
y\in B
such that

z=x+y
, and
x
is an extreme point of
A
and
y
is one of
B
.

Proof.

“
\subseteq
” Let
x_{1},\dots,x_{m}\in A
,
y_{1},\dots,y_{n}\in B
,
\lambda_{1},\dots,\lambda_{m}\in K_{\geq 0}
,

\mu_{1},\dots,\mu_{n}\in K_{\geq 0}
and
\sum_{i=1}^{m}\lambda_{i}=1=\sum_{j=1}^{n}\mu_{j}
. Then

\sum_{i=1}^{m}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\lambda_{i}\mu_{j}=\left(\sum_{i=1}^{m}\lambda_{i}\right)(\sum_{j=1}^{n}\mu_{j})=1\cdot 1=1

and

\sum_{i=1}^{m}\lambda_{i}x_{i}+\sum_{j=1}^{n}\mu_{j}y_{j}=\left(\sum_{j=1}^{n}\mu_{j}\right)\sum_{i=1}^{m}\lambda_{i}x_{i}+\left(\sum_{i=1}^{m}\lambda_{i}\right)\sum_{j=1}^{n}\mu_{j}y_{j}=\sum_{i=1}^{m}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\lambda_{i}\mu_{j}(x_{i}+y_{j}).

“
\supseteq
” is trivial.

Let now
A
and
B
be convex. Then
A+B=(\conv A)+(\conv B)=\conv(A+B)
is convex. Finally,
let
z
be
an extreme point of
A+B
and let
x\in A
and
y\in B
with
z=x+y
. Then
x
is an extreme point of

A
since if we had
x=\frac{x_{1}+x_{2}}{2}
with different
x_{1},x_{2}\in A
, then it would follow
that
z=\frac{(x_{1}+y)+(x_{2}+y)}{2}
and
x_{1}+y\neq x_{2}+y\ \lightning
. In the same way,
y
is an extreme point
of
B
. Suppose now that
x^{\prime}\in A
and
y^{\prime}\in B
such that
z=x^{\prime}+y^{\prime}
. Then

z=\frac{x+x^{\prime}}{2}+\frac{y+y^{\prime}}{2}
and
\frac{x+x^{\prime}}{2}
is also an extreme point of
A
which is possible only for

x=x^{\prime}
. Analogously,
y=y^{\prime}
.
∎

Notation 4.6
.

Suppressing
n
in the notation, we denote by
\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.6817pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.7584pt{}}}$\hss}}}X:=(X_{1},\dots,X_{n})
a tuple of variables and set

A[\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.6817pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.7584pt{}}}$\hss}}}X]:=A[X_{1},\dots,X_{n}]
for every commutative ring
A
with
0\neq 1
in
A
.
For
\alpha\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{n}
, we write
|\alpha|:=\alpha_{1}+\dots+\alpha_{n}
and
\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.6817pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.7584pt{}}}$\hss}}}X^{\alpha}:=X_{1}^{\alpha_{1}}\dotsm X_{n}^{\alpha_{n}}
.

Definition 4.7
.

Let
K
be a field and
f\in K[\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.6817pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.7584pt{}}}$\hss}}}X]
. Write
f=\sum_{\alpha\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{n}}a_{\alpha}\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.6817pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.7584pt{}}}$\hss}}}X^{\alpha}
with
a_{\alpha}\in K
. Then the finite set
\supp(f):=\{\alpha\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{n}\mid a_{\alpha}\neq 0\}
is called
the
support
of
f
and its convex hull
N(f):=\conv(\supp(f))\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}
the

Newton polytope
of
f
.

Definition 4.8
.

Let
K
be a field,
f\in K[\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.6817pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.7584pt{}}}$\hss}}}X]
and
a\in K
. We say that
a
is a

vertex coefficient
of
f
if there is a vertex
\alpha
of
N(f)
such that

a\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.6817pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.7584pt{}}}$\hss}}}X^{\alpha}
is a term of
f
.

Remark 4.9
.

Since every vertex of the Newton polytope of a polynomial lies by

4.3
in the support of the polynomial, vertex coefficients are always
\neq 0
.

Theorem 4.10
.

Let
K
be a field and
f,g\in K[\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.6817pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.7584pt{}}}$\hss}}}X]
. Then
N(fg)=N(f)+N(g)
and every vertex coefficient of
fg

is the product of a vertex coefficient of
f
with a vertex coefficient of
g
.

Proof.

“
\subseteq
”
\supp(fg)\subseteq\supp(f)+\supp(g)\subseteq N(f)+N(g)
and therefore

N(fg)=\conv(\supp(fg))\subseteq N(f)+N(g)
since
N(f)+N(g)
is convex by
4.5
.

“
\supseteq
” By
4.5
,
N(f)+N(g)
is a polytope. By virtue of
4.4
, it suffices
to show that its vertices lie in
N(fg)
. Consider therefore a vertex
\gamma
of
N(f)+N(g)
. We even show that

\gamma\in\supp(fg)
. By
4.5
, there are uniquely determined
\alpha\in N(f)
and
\beta\in N(g)
such that

\gamma=\alpha+\beta
, and
\alpha
is a vertex of
N(f)
and
\beta
a vertex of
N(g)
. By
4.9
, we have

\alpha\in\supp(f)
and
\beta\in\supp(g)
. Because of unicity of
\alpha
and
\beta
, the coefficient of

\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.6817pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.7584pt{}}}$\hss}}}X^{\gamma}
in
fg
equals the product of the respective coefficients of

\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.6817pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.7584pt{}}}$\hss}}}X^{\alpha}
and
\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.6817pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.7584pt{}}}$\hss}}}X^{\beta}
in
f
and
g
, respectively, and hence is in particular
\neq 0
.
Thus
N(fg)=N(f)+N(g)
is shown. Also the extra claim follows from the above.
∎

Proposition 4.11
.

Let
K
be a field and
f,g\in K[\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.6817pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.7584pt{}}}$\hss}}}X]
. Then
N(f+g)\subseteq\conv(N(f)\cup N(g))
.

Proof.

\supp(f+g)\subseteq\supp(f)\cup\supp(g)\subseteq N(f)\cup N(g)
implies

N(f+g)=\conv(\supp(f+g))\subseteq\conv(N(f)\cup N(g)).

∎

Theorem 4.12
.

Let
(K,\leq)
be an ordered field and
f,g\in K[\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.6817pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.7584pt{}}}$\hss}}}X]
such that all vertex coefficients of
f
and
g

have the same sign. Then
N(f+g)=\conv(N(f)\cup N(g))
and all vertex coefficients of
f+g
also have this
sign.

Proof.

“
\subseteq
” is
4.11

“
\supseteq
” We have that
\conv(N(f)\cup N(g))=\conv(\supp(f)\cup\supp(g))
is a polytope.
Let
\alpha
be one of its vertices. By
4.4
, it is enough to show that
\alpha\in N(f+g)
.
We even show that
\alpha\in\supp(f+g)
. By
4.3
,
\alpha
lies in at least one of the sets

\supp(f)
and
\supp(g)
. If
\alpha
lies only in one of these two, then the claim is clear. If on the other hand

\alpha
lies in both, then
\alpha
is a vertex of both
\conv(\supp(f))=N(f)
and
\conv(\supp(g))=N(g)

and the coefficients of
\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.6817pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.7584pt{}}}$\hss}}}X^{\alpha}
in
f
and in
g
and hence also in
f+g
have the same sign, from which
it follows again that
\alpha\in\supp(f+g)
. Thus
N(f+g)=\conv(N(f)\cup N(g))
is proven. The extra
claim follows from what was shown.
∎

Lemma 4.13
.

Let
(K,\leq)
be an ordered field,
V
a
K
-vector space and
A
a convex subset of
V
.
Then
A+A=2A:=\{2x\mid x\in A\}
.

Proof.

“
\supseteq
” trivial

“
\subseteq
” Let
x,y\in A
. Then
x+y=2\frac{x+y}{2}\in 2A
.
∎

Theorem 4.14
.

Let
(K,\leq)
be an ordered field and
f\in K[\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.6817pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.7584pt{}}}$\hss}}}X]
. Then
N(f^{2})=2N(f)
and all vertex
coefficients of
f^{2}
are squares of vertex coefficients of
f
and therefore positive.

Proof.

N(f^{2})=2N(f)
follows from
4.10
and
4.13
.
Suppose
\gamma
is a vertex of
N(f^{2})\overset{\ref{newtontimes}}{=}N(f)+N(f)
.
By
4.5
, there are uniquely determined
\alpha,\beta\in N(f)
with
\gamma=\alpha+\beta
.
Due to
\gamma=\beta+\alpha
, it follows that
\alpha=\beta
. But then the coefficient of
\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.6817pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.7584pt{}}}$\hss}}}X^{\gamma}
in
f^{2}

is just the coefficient belonging to
\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.6817pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.7584pt{}}}$\hss}}}X^{\alpha}
in
f
squared.
∎

Theorem 4.15
.

Let
(K,\leq)
be an ordered field,
\ell\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
,
p_{1},\dots,p_{\ell}\in K[\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.6817pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.7584pt{}}}$\hss}}}X]
and
f:=\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}p_{i}^{2}
.
Then
N(f)=2\conv(N(p_{1})\cup\ldots\cup N(p_{\ell}))
and all vertex coefficients of
f
are positive.

Proof.

For each
i\in\{1,\dots,\ell\}
, we have by
4.14
that
N(p_{i}^{2})=2N(p_{i})
and that all
vertex coefficients of
p_{i}^{2}
are positive. By
4.12
,

\displaystyle N(f)

\displaystyle=\conv(N(p_{1}^{2})\cup\ldots\cup N(p_{\ell}^{2}))=\conv(2N(p_{1})\cup\ldots\cup 2N(p_{\ell}))

\displaystyle=2\conv(N(p_{1})\cup\ldots\cup N(p_{\ell}))

and all vertex coefficients of
f
are positive.
∎

Example 4.16
.

For the
Motzkin polynomial

f:=X^{4}Y^{2}+X^{2}Y^{4}-3X^{2}Y^{2}+1\in\mathbb{R}[X,Y]
, we have

f\geq 0
on
\mathbb{R}^{2}
but
f\notin\sum\mathbb{R}[X,Y]^{2}
. At first we show
f\geq 0
on
\mathbb{R}^{2}
in three different ways:

(1)

From the inequality of arithmetic and geometric means known from analysis, it follows that

\sqrt[3]{abc}\leq\frac{1}{3}(a+b+c)
for all
a,b,c\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
. Setting here
a:=x^{4}y^{2}
,
b:=x^{2}y^{4}

and
c:=1
for arbitrary
x,y\in\mathbb{R}
, we deduce
x^{2}y^{2}\leq\frac{1}{3}(x^{4}y^{2}+x^{2}y^{4}+1)
.

(2)

\displaystyle(1+X^{2})f

\displaystyle=X^{4}Y^{2}+X^{2}Y^{4}-3X^{2}Y^{2}+1+X^{6}Y^{2}+X^{4}Y^{4}-3X^{4}Y^{2}+X^{2}

\displaystyle=1-2X^{2}Y^{2}+X^{4}Y^{4}+X^{2}-2X^{2}Y^{2}+X^{2}Y^{4}+X^{2}Y^{2}-2X^{4}Y^{2}+X^{6}Y^{2}

\displaystyle=(1-X^{2}Y^{2})^{2}+X^{2}(1-Y^{2})^{2}+X^{2}Y^{2}(1-X^{2})^{2}\in\sum\mathbb{R}[X,Y]^{2}

(3)

\displaystyle f(X^{3},Y^{3})

\displaystyle=X^{12}Y^{6}+X^{6}Y^{12}-3X^{6}Y^{6}+1

\displaystyle=X^{4}Y^{2}-X^{8}Y^{4}-X^{6}Y^{6}+\frac{1}{4}X^{12}Y^{6}+\frac{1}{2}X^{10}Y^{8}+\frac{1}{4}X^{8}Y^{10}

\displaystyle\quad+X^{2}Y^{4}-X^{6}Y^{6}-X^{4}Y^{8}+\frac{1}{4}X^{10}Y^{8}+\frac{1}{2}X^{8}Y^{10}+\frac{1}{4}X^{6}Y^{12}

\displaystyle\quad+1-X^{4}Y^{2}-X^{2}Y^{4}+\frac{1}{4}X^{8}Y^{4}+\frac{1}{2}X^{6}Y^{6}+\frac{1}{4}X^{4}Y^{8}

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{3}{4}X^{8}Y^{4}-\frac{3}{2}X^{6}Y^{6}+\frac{3}{4}X^{4}Y^{8}

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{3}{4}X^{10}Y^{8}-\frac{3}{2}X^{8}Y^{10}+\frac{3}{4}X^{6}Y^{12}

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{3}{4}X^{12}Y^{6}-\frac{3}{2}X^{10}Y^{8}+\frac{3}{4}X^{8}Y^{10}

\displaystyle=\left(X^{2}Y-\frac{1}{2}X^{4}Y^{5}-\frac{1}{2}X^{6}Y^{3}\right)^{2}

\displaystyle\quad+\left(XY^{2}-\frac{1}{2}X^{3}Y^{6}-\frac{1}{2}X^{5}Y^{4}\right)^{2}

\displaystyle\quad+\left(1-\frac{1}{2}X^{2}Y^{4}-\frac{1}{2}X^{4}Y^{2}\right)^{2}

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{3}{4}\left(X^{2}Y^{4}-X^{4}Y^{2}\right)^{2}

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{3}{4}(X^{3}Y^{6}-X^{5}Y^{4})^{2}

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{3}{4}(X^{4}Y^{5}-X^{6}Y^{3})^{2}

Now we show
f\notin\sum\mathbb{R}[X,Y]^{2}
:

\displaystyle N(f)

\displaystyle=\conv(\supp(f))=\conv\{(4,2),(2,4),(2,2),(0,0)\}

\displaystyle=\conv\{(4,2),(2,4),(0,0)\}.

Assume
f=\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}p_{i}^{2}
with
\ell\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
and
p_{1},\dots,p_{\ell}\in\sum\mathbb{R}[X,Y]
. Then

N(p_{i})\subseteq\conv(N(p_{1})\cup\ldots\cup N(p_{\ell}))=\frac{1}{2}N(f)=\conv\{(2,1),(1,2),(0,0)\}

by
4.15
and hence

\supp(p_{i})\subseteq\mathbb{N}_{0}^{2}\cap N(p_{i})\subseteq\mathbb{N}_{0}^{2}\cap\conv\{(2,1),(1,2),(0,0)\}=\{(0,0),(1,1),(2,1),(1,2)\}
for all
i\in\{1,\dots,\ell\}
.
The coefficient of
X^{2}Y^{2}
in
p_{i}^{2}
is therefore the coefficient of
XY
in
p_{i}
squared and therefore
nonnegative. Then the coefficient of
X^{2}Y^{2}
in
f
is also nonnegative
\lightning
.
This shows
f\notin\sum\mathbb{R}[X,Y]^{2}
. Thus one can neither generalize
1.1
(a)
\implies
(c)
to polynomials in several variables nor
3.5
(a)
\implies
(b) to polynomials of arbitrary degree.
Note also that exactly the same proof shows even
f+c\notin\sum\mathbb{R}[X,Y]^{2}
for all
c\in\mathbb{R}
. By

2.6
, the
Motzkin form

f^{*}:=X^{4}Y^{2}+X^{2}Y^{4}-3X^{2}Y^{2}Z^{2}+Z^{6}
is psd
[
\to

3.1
] but is likewise no sum of squares of polynomials. Again by
2.6
,
the dehomogenizations
f^{*}(1,Y,Z)=Y^{2}+Y^{4}-3Y^{2}Z^{2}+Z^{6}
and

f^{*}(X,1,Z)=X^{4}+X^{2}-3X^{2}Z^{2}+Z^{6}
are also polynomials that are
\geq 0
on
\mathbb{R}^{2}
but that are no
sums of squares of polynomials.

5
Artin’s solution to Hilbert’s 17th problem

Lemma 5.1
.

Let
R
be a real closed field and

f,p,q\in R[\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.6817pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.7584pt{}}}$\hss}}}X]
.
Suppose
q\neq 0
,
f=\frac{p}{q}
,
p\geq 0
on
R^{n}
and
q\geq 0
on
R^{n}
.
Then
f\geq 0
on
R^{n}
.

Proof.

Using the Tarski principle
8.19
, one can reduce to the case
R=\mathbb{R}
.
But then the subset
\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\mid f(x)<0\}
of
\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\mid q(x)=0\}
is open in
\mathbb{R}^{n}

and therefore empty since otherwise
q=0
would follow from
2.3
.
∎

In the year 1900, Hilbert presented his famous list of 23 seminal problems
at the International Congress of Mathematicians in Paris.
In 1927, Artin gave a positive solution to the 17th of these problems.
This corresponds to the case
K=\mathbb{R}
in the following theorem.

Theorem 5.2

(Artin)
.

Suppose
R
is a real closed field and
(K,\leq)
an ordered subfield of
R
. Let
f\in K[\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.6817pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.7584pt{}}}$\hss}}}X]
.
Then the following are equivalent:

(a)

f\geq 0
on
R^{n}

(b)

f\in\sum K_{\geq 0}K(\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.6817pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.7584pt{}}}$\hss}}}X)^{2}

Proof.

(b)
\implies
(a) follows from Lemma
5.1
. We show (a)
\implies
(b) by contraposition.
Suppose
f\notin\sum K_{\geq 0}K(\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.6817pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.7584pt{}}}$\hss}}}X)^{2}
. To show:
\exists x\in R^{n}:f(x)<0
.
Since
\sum K_{\geq 0}K(\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.6817pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.7584pt{}}}$\hss}}}X)^{2}
is now a proper preorder of
K(\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.6817pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.7584pt{}}}$\hss}}}X)
[
\to

2.1
,
2.5
],
there is by
2.10
an order
P
of
K(\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.6817pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.7584pt{}}}$\hss}}}X)
with
f\notin P
. Set
R^{\prime}:=\overline{(K(\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.6817pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.7584pt{}}}$\hss}}}X),P)}
.
Then there is an
x\in R^{\prime n}
with
f(x)<0
namely
x:=(X_{1},\dots,X_{n})
since

f(x)=f<0
in
R^{\prime}
. Due to
K_{\geq 0}\subseteq P\subseteq R^{\prime 2}
,
(K,\leq)
is an
ordered
subfield
of
R^{\prime}
. Since the
K
-semialgebraic set
\{x\in R^{\prime n}\mid f(x)<0\}
is nonempty, its transfer

\{x\in R^{n}\mid f(x)<0\}
to
R
[
\to

9.5
] is also nonempty.
∎

Corollary 5.3
.

[
\to

1.2
]

Suppose
R
is a real closed field and
(K,\leq)
an ordered subfield of
R
. Let
f\in K[X]
.
Then the following are equivalent:

(a)

f\geq 0
on
R

(b)

f\in\sum K_{\geq 0}K[X]^{2}

Proof.

(b)
\implies
(a) is trivial.

(a)
\implies
(b) follows from
5.2
and
1.2
.
∎

6
The Gram matrix method

Theorem 6.1
.

Let
K
be a Euclidean field,
f\in K[\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.6817pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.7584pt{}}}$\hss}}}X]
and

\frac{1}{2}N(f)\cap\mathbb{N}_{0}^{n}\subseteq\{\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{m}\}\subseteq\mathbb{N}_{0}^{n}

(for instance set
\{\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{m}\}
equal to
\frac{1}{2}N(f)\cap\mathbb{N}_{0}^{n}
or to

\{\alpha\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{n}\mid 2|\alpha|\leq\deg f\}
)
. Set

v:=\begin{pmatrix}\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.6817pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.7584pt{}}}$\hss}}}X^{\alpha_{1}}\\
\vdots\\
\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.6817pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.7584pt{}}}$\hss}}}X^{\alpha_{m}}\end{pmatrix}
. Then the following are equivalent:

(a)

f\in\sum K[\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.6817pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.7584pt{}}}$\hss}}}X]^{2}

(b)

There is a
psd
matrix
[
\to

3.1
(b)]

G\in SK^{m\times m}
(“Gram matrix”)
satisfying
f=v^{T}Gv
.

(c)

f
is a sum of
m
squares in
