K[\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.6817pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.7584pt{}}}$\hss}}}X]
.

Proof.

(a)
\implies
(b)
Let
\ell\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
and
p_{1},\dots,p_{\ell}\in K[\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.6817pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.7584pt{}}}$\hss}}}X]
with

f=\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}p_{i}^{2}
.
By
4.15
, we have
\supp(p_{i})\subseteq\frac{1}{2}N(f)\cap\mathbb{N}_{0}^{n}\subseteq\{\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{m}\}
.
Hence there is an
A\in K^{\ell\times m}
such that

Av=\begin{pmatrix}p_{1}\\
\vdots\\
p_{\ell}\end{pmatrix}.

It follows that
f=\begin{pmatrix}p_{1}&\dots&p_{\ell}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}p_{1}\\
\vdots\\
p_{\ell}\end{pmatrix}=(Av)^{T}Av=v^{T}A^{T}Av=v^{T}Gv
where

G:=A^{T}A\in SK^{m\times m}
. By
3.3
,
G
is psd.

(b)
\implies
(c)
Let
G\in SK^{m\times m}
be psd with
f=v^{T}Gv
. Choose according to
3.3
an
A\in K^{m\times m}

satisfying
G=A^{T}A
. Write

Av=\begin{pmatrix}p_{1}\\
\vdots\\
p_{m}\end{pmatrix}.

Then
p_{1},\dots,p_{m}\in K[\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.6817pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.7584pt{}}}$\hss}}}X]
and

v^{T}Gv=v^{T}A^{T}Av=(Av)^{T}Av=\begin{pmatrix}p_{1}&\dots&p_{m}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}p_{1}\\
\vdots\\
p_{m}\end{pmatrix}=\sum_{i=1}^{m}p_{i}^{2}.

(c)
\implies
(a)
is trivial.
∎

Example 6.2
.

Let
K
be a Euclidean field and
f:=2X_{1}^{4}+5X_{2}^{4}-X_{1}^{2}X_{2}^{2}+2X_{1}^{3}X_{2}\in K[X_{1},X_{2}]
.
Then
N(f)=\conv\{(4,0),(0,4)\}
and therefore

\frac{1}{2}N(f)\cap\mathbb{N}_{0}^{2}=\{(2,0),(1,1),(0,2)\}.

Set
v:=\begin{pmatrix}X_{1}^{2}\\
X_{1}X_{2}\\
X_{2}^{2}\end{pmatrix}
. From

\{G\in SK^{3\times 3}\mid f=v^{T}Gv\}=\left\{\begin{pmatrix}2&1&a\\
1&-2a-1&0\\
a&0&5\end{pmatrix}\mid a\in K\right\}
, we obtain

f\in\sum K[X_{1},X_{2}]^{2}\iff\exists a\in K:\begin{pmatrix}2&1&a\\
1&-2a-1&0\\
a&0&5\end{pmatrix}\text{ psd.}

For all
a\in K
, we have

\displaystyle\det\begin{pmatrix}2+T&1&a\\
1&T-2a-1&0\\
a&0&5+T\end{pmatrix}=(2+T)(T-2a-1)(5+T)-a^{2}(T-2a-1)-5-T

\displaystyle=(T^{2}-2aT+T-4a-2)(5+T)-(1+a^{2})T+2a^{3}+a^{2}-5

\displaystyle=T^{3}-2aT^{2}+T^{2}-4aT-2T+5T^{2}-10aT+5T-20a-10-(1+a^{2})T+2a^{3}+a^{2}-5

\displaystyle=T^{3}+(6-2a)T^{2}+(2-14a-a^{2})T-15-20a+a^{2}+2a^{3}

and by
3.3
(e), we obtain

\begin{pmatrix}2&1&a\\
1&-2a-1&0\\
a&0&5\end{pmatrix}\text{ psd}\iff\begin{array}[c]{rl}2a^{3}+a^{2}-20a-15\geq 0\\
\mathbin{\&}\ -a^{2}-14a+2\geq 0\\
\mathbin{\&}\ -2a+6\geq 0&.\end{array}

Set
a:=-3
. Then
2a^{3}+a^{2}-20a-15=-2\cdot 27+9+60-15=-54+9+60-15=0
,

-a^{2}-14a+2=-9+42+2=35\geq 0
and
-2a+6=12\geq 0
.
For this reason
f\in\sum K[X_{1},X_{2}]^{2}
. The quadratic form

q:=\begin{pmatrix}T_{1}&T_{2}&T_{3}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}2&1&a\\
1&-2a-1&0\\
a&0&5\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}T_{1}\\
T_{2}\\
T_{3}\end{pmatrix}\in K[T_{1},T_{2},T_{3}]

obviously satisfies

q(X_{1}^{2},X_{1}X_{2},X_{2}^{3})=v^{T}\begin{pmatrix}2&1&a\\
1&-2a-1&0\\
a&0&5\end{pmatrix}v=f.

Because of

\sg q\overset{\text{\ref{sospsd2}(d)}}{=}\rk q=\rk\begin{pmatrix}2&1&-3\\
1&5&0\\
-3&0&5\end{pmatrix}=2,

q
is a sum of
2
squares of
linear forms in
K[T_{1},T_{2},T_{3}]
and thus
f
a sum of
2
squares of polynomials. To compute this
representation explicitely, we employ the procedure from
6.1
(f):

\displaystyle q

\displaystyle=2T_{1}^{2}+2T_{1}T_{2}-6T_{1}T_{3}+5T_{2}^{2}+5T_{3}^{2}

\displaystyle=2\Big(\underbrace{T_{1}+\frac{1}{2}T_{2}-\frac{3}{2}T_{3}}_{\ell_{1}}\Big)^{2}-2\Big(\frac{1}{2}T_{2}-\frac{3}{2}T_{3}\Big)^{2}+5T_{2}^{2}+5T_{3}^{2}

\displaystyle=2\ell_{1}^{2}+\frac{9}{2}T_{2}^{2}+3T_{2}T_{3}+\frac{1}{2}T_{3}^{2}

\displaystyle=2\ell_{1}^{2}+\frac{9}{2}\Big(\underbrace{T_{2}+\frac{1}{3}T_{3}}_{\ell_{2}}\Big)^{2}=2\ell_{1}^{2}+\frac{9}{2}\ell_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}(2T_{1}+T_{2}-3T_{3})^{2}+\frac{1}{2}(3T_{2}+T_{3})^{2}.

Hence
f=\frac{1}{2}(2X_{1}^{2}+X_{1}X_{2}-3X_{2}^{2})^{2}+\frac{1}{2}(3X_{1}X_{2}+X_{2}^{2})^{2}
.

Chapter 3
Prime cones and real Stellensätze

1
The real spectrum of a commutative ring

In this section, we let
A
,
B
and
C
always be commutative rings.

Reminder 1.1
.

An ideal
\mathfrak{p}
of
A
is called a prime ideal of
A
if

1\notin\mathfrak{p}\quad\text{ and }\quad\forall a,b\in A:(ab\in\mathfrak{p}\implies(a\in\mathfrak{p}\text{ or }b\in\mathfrak{p})).

We call

\spec A=\{\mathfrak{p}\mid\mathfrak{p}\text{ prime ideal of $A$}\}

the
spectrum
of
A
. If
I
is an ideal of
A
, then

I\in\spec A\iff A/I\text{ is an integral domain.}

Because every integral domain extends to a field (e.g., to its quotient field) and every field to an
algebraically closed field (e.g., to its algebraic closure),
\spec A
consists exactly of the kernels of
ring homomorphisms of
A
in
\begin{Bmatrix}\text{integral domains}\\
\text{fields}\\
\text{algebraically closed fields}\end{Bmatrix}
.
Every ring homomorphism
\varphi\colon A\to B
induces a map

\spec\varphi\colon\spec B\to\spec A,\mathfrak{q}\mapsto\varphi^{-1}(\mathfrak{q}),

for if
\mathfrak{q}\in\spec B
, then
\mathfrak{p}:=\varphi^{-1}(\mathfrak{q})\in\spec A
since
\varphi
induces an
embedding

A/\mathfrak{p}\hookrightarrow B/\mathfrak{q}
by the homomorphism theorem. If
\varphi\colon A\to B
and

\psi\colon B\to C
are ring homomorphisms, then

\spec(\psi\circ\varphi)=(\spec\varphi)\circ(\spec\psi).

Notation 1.2
.

If
A
is an integral domain, then

\qf A:=(A\setminus\{0\})^{-1}A=\left\{\frac{a}{b}\mid a,b\in A,b\neq 0\right\}

denotes its quotient field.

Definition 1.3
.

We call
\sper A:=\{(\mathfrak{p},\leq)\mid\mathfrak{p}\in\spec A,\ \text{$\leq$ order of }\qf(A/\mathfrak{p})\}
the
real spectrum
of
A
.

Remark 1.4
.

Every ring homomorphism
\varphi\colon A\to B
induces a map

\sper\varphi\colon\sper B\to\sper A,\ (\mathfrak{q},\leq)\mapsto(\varphi^{-1}(\mathfrak{q}),\leq^{\prime}),

where
\leq^{\prime}
denotes the order of
\qf(A/\mathfrak{p})
with
\mathfrak{p}:=\varphi^{-1}(\mathfrak{q})
which makes
the canonical embedding
\qf(A/\mathfrak{p})\hookrightarrow\qf(B/\mathfrak{q})
into an embedding

(\qf(A/\mathfrak{p}),\leq^{\prime})\hookrightarrow(\qf(B/\mathfrak{q}),\leq)
of ordered fields.
If
\varphi\colon A\to B
and

\psi\colon B\to C
are ring homomorphisms, then we have again

\sper(\psi\circ\varphi)=(\sper\varphi)\circ(\sper\psi).

Example 1.5
.

Since
\mathbb{R}[X]
is a principal ideal domain, the fundamental theorem
4.14
implies

\spec\mathbb{R}[X]=\{(0)\}\cup\{(X-a)\mid a\in\mathbb{R}\}\cup\{(\underbrace{(X-a)^{2}+b^{2}}_{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptstyle=(X-(a+b\mathbbm{i}))(X-(a-b\mathbbm{i}))$\hss}})\mid a,b\in\mathbb{R},b\neq 0\}

where
((X-a)^{2}+b^{2})=((X-a^{\prime})^{2}+b^{\prime 2})\iff(a=a^{\prime}\mathbin{\&}|b|=|b^{\prime}|)
for all
a,a^{\prime},b,b^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}
. The spectrum of

\mathbb{R}[X]
therefore can be seen as consisting of

•

one “generic point”,

•

the real numbers, and

•

the unordered pairs of two distinct conjugated complex numbers.

Because of
\qf(\mathbb{R}[X]/(0))\cong\qf(\mathbb{R}[X])=\mathbb{R}(X)
,

\qf(\mathbb{R}[X]/(X-a))=\mathbb{R}[X]/(X-a)\cong\mathbb{R}
for all
a\in\mathbb{R}
and

\qf(\mathbb{R}[X]/((X-a)^{2}+b^{2}))\cong\mathbb{R}[X]/((X-a)^{2}+b^{2})\cong\mathbb{C}
for
a,b\in\mathbb{R}
with
b\neq 0
, we obtain
in the notation of
3.8
(and with the identification
\mathbb{R}[X]/(0)=\mathbb{R}[X]
)

\displaystyle\sper\mathbb{R}[X]=\{((0),P_{-\infty}),((0),P_{\infty})\}

\displaystyle\cup\{((0),P_{a-})\mid a\in\mathbb{R}\}\cup\{((0),P_{a+})\mid a\in\mathbb{R}\}

\displaystyle\cup\{((X-a),(\mathbb{R}[X]/(X-a))^{2})\mid a\in\mathbb{R}\}.

The
real
spectrum of
\mathbb{R}[X]
thus corresponds to an accumulation consisting of

•

the two points at infinity,

•

for each real number two points infinitely close, and

•

the real numbers.

Definition 1.6
.

We call
\supp\colon\sper A\to\spec A,\ (\mathfrak{p},\leq)\mapsto\mathfrak{p}
the
support map
.

Definition 1.7
.

[
\to

1.19
(a),
1.1
]
A subset
P
of
A
is called a
prime cone

of
A
if
P+P\subseteq P
,
PP\subseteq P
,
P\cup-P=A
,
-1\notin P
and

\forall a,b\in A:(ab\in P\implies(a\in P\text{ or }-b\in P))
.

Proposition 1.8
.

Every prime cone of
A
is a proper preorder of
A

[
\to

2.1
]
.

Proof.

Suppose
P
is a prime cone of
A
and
a\in A
. To show:
a^{2}\in P
. Due to
a\in A=P\cup-P
, we have

a\in P
or
-a\in P
. In the first case we get
a^{2}=aa\in PP\subseteq P
and in the second

a^{2}=(-a)^{2}=(-a)(-a)\in PP\subseteq P
.
∎

Proposition 1.9
.

Suppose
P\subseteq A
satisfies
P+P\subseteq P
,
PP\subseteq P
and
P\cup-P=A
.
Then the following are equivalent:

(a)

P
is a prime cone of
A
.

(b)

-1\notin P
and
\forall a,b\in A:(ab\in P\implies(a\in P\text{ or }-b\in P))

(c)

P\cap-P
is a prime ideal of
A

Proof.

(a)
\iff
(b)
is Definition
1.7
.

(b)
\implies
(c)
Suppose (b) holds and set
\mathfrak{p}:=P\cap-P
. Then
\mathfrak{p}
is obviously a
subgroup of
A
and we have
A\mathfrak{p}=(P\cup-P)\mathfrak{p}=P\mathfrak{p}\cup-P\mathfrak{p}=P(P\cap-P)\cup-P(P\cap-P)\subseteq(PP\cap-PP)\cup(-PP\cap PP)\subseteq(P\cap-P)\cup(-P\cap P)=P\cap-P=\mathfrak{p}
, i.e.,

\mathfrak{p}
is an ideal of
A
(if
\frac{1}{2}\in A
this follows alternatively from
1.8
and

2.4
). From
-1\notin P
we get
1\notin\mathfrak{p}
. It remains to show

\forall a,b\in A\colon(ab\in\mathfrak{p}\implies(a\in\mathfrak{p}\text{ or }b\in\mathfrak{p}))
. To this end, let
a,b\in A
with
a\notin\mathfrak{p}

and
b\notin\mathfrak{p}
. To show:
ab\notin\mathfrak{p}
. WLOG
a\notin P
and
-b\notin P

(otherwise replace
a
by
-a
and/or
-b
by
b
, taking into account
-\mathfrak{p}=\mathfrak{p}
).
By hypothesis, we obtain then
ab\notin P
and thus
ab\notin\mathfrak{p}
.

(c)
\implies
(b)
Suppose (c) holds. Due to
P\cup-P=A
, we have
1\in P
or

-1\in P
. If
-1\in P
, then again
1=(-1)(-1)\in PP\subseteq P
. Hence
1\in P
. If we had

-1\in P
, then
1\in\mathfrak{p}:=P\cap-P\in\spec A

\lightning
. Thus
-1\notin P
. Let now
a,b\in A
such that

a\notin P
and
-b\notin P
. To show:
ab\notin P
. Because of
P\cup-P=A
, we have
a\in-P
and

b\in P
from which
-ab=(-a)b\in PP\subseteq P
. If we had in addition
ab\in P
, then

ab\in\mathfrak{p}
and thus
a\in\mathfrak{p}\subseteq P
or
b\in\mathfrak{p}\subseteq-P

\lightning
.
Hence
ab\notin P
.
∎

Remark 1.10
.

If
K
is a field, then
1.9
signifies because of
\spec K=\{(0)\}
just that the
prime cones of
K
are exactly the orders of
K
[
\to

1.20
].

Lemma 1.11
.

Let
P
be a prime cone of
A
and
\mathfrak{p}:=P\cap-P
[
\to

1.9
(c)]. Then

P_{\mathfrak{p}}:=\left\{\frac{\overline{a}^{\begin{subarray}{c}\vskip-4.09723pt{\scriptscriptstyle\mathfrak{p}}\end{subarray}}}{\overline{s}^{\begin{subarray}{c}\vskip-4.09723pt{\scriptscriptstyle\mathfrak{p}}\end{subarray}}}\mid a\in A,s\in A\setminus\mathfrak{p},as\in P\right\}

is an order (i.e., a prime cone
[
\to

1.10
]) of
\qf(A/\mathfrak{p})
.

Proof.

To show [
\to

1.20
(a)]:

(a)

P_{\mathfrak{p}}+P_{\mathfrak{p}}\subseteq P_{\mathfrak{p}}
,

(b)

P_{\mathfrak{p}}P_{\mathfrak{p}}\subseteq P_{\mathfrak{p}}
,

(c)

P_{\mathfrak{p}}\cup-P_{\mathfrak{p}}=\qf(A/\mathfrak{p})
, and

(d)

P_{\mathfrak{p}}\cap-P_{\mathfrak{p}}=(0)
.

(a) Suppose that
a,b\in A
and
s,t\in A\setminus\mathfrak{p}
with
as,bt\in P
define arbitrary elements

\frac{\overline{a}}{\overline{s}},\frac{\overline{b}}{\overline{t}}\in P_{\mathfrak{p}}
.
Then

\frac{\overline{a}^{\begin{subarray}{c}\vskip-4.09723pt{\scriptscriptstyle\mathfrak{p}}\end{subarray}}}{\overline{s}^{\begin{subarray}{c}\vskip-4.09723pt{\scriptscriptstyle\mathfrak{p}}\end{subarray}}}+\frac{\overline{b}^{\begin{subarray}{c}\vskip-4.09723pt{\scriptscriptstyle\mathfrak{p}}\end{subarray}}}{\overline{t}^{\begin{subarray}{c}\vskip-4.09723pt{\scriptscriptstyle\mathfrak{p}}\end{subarray}}}=\frac{\overline{at}^{\begin{subarray}{c}\vskip-4.09723pt{\scriptscriptstyle\mathfrak{p}}\end{subarray}}}{\overline{st}^{\begin{subarray}{c}\vskip-4.09723pt{\scriptscriptstyle\mathfrak{p}}\end{subarray}}}+\frac{\overline{bs}^{\begin{subarray}{c}\vskip-4.09723pt{\scriptscriptstyle\mathfrak{p}}\end{subarray}}}{\overline{st}^{\begin{subarray}{c}\vskip-4.09723pt{\scriptscriptstyle\mathfrak{p}}\end{subarray}}}=\frac{\overline{at+bs}^{\begin{subarray}{c}\vskip-4.09723pt{\scriptscriptstyle\mathfrak{p}}\end{subarray}}}{\overline{st}^{\begin{subarray}{c}\vskip-4.09723pt{\scriptscriptstyle\mathfrak{p}}\end{subarray}}}\in P_{\mathfrak{p}},

since
at+bs\in A
,
st\in A\setminus\mathfrak{p}
and
(at+bs)st=ast^{2}+bts^{2}\in PA^{2}+PA^{2}\subseteq PP+PP\subseteq P+P\subseteq P
.

(b) Let again
a,b\in A
and
s,t\in A\setminus\mathfrak{p}
satisfy
as,bt\in P
. Then

\frac{\overline{a}^{\begin{subarray}{c}\vskip-4.09723pt{\scriptscriptstyle\mathfrak{p}}\end{subarray}}}{\overline{s}^{\begin{subarray}{c}\vskip-4.09723pt{\scriptscriptstyle\mathfrak{p}}\end{subarray}}}\frac{\overline{b}^{\begin{subarray}{c}\vskip-4.09723pt{\scriptscriptstyle\mathfrak{p}}\end{subarray}}}{\overline{t}^{\begin{subarray}{c}\vskip-4.09723pt{\scriptscriptstyle\mathfrak{p}}\end{subarray}}}=\frac{\overline{ab}^{\begin{subarray}{c}\vskip-4.09723pt{\scriptscriptstyle\mathfrak{p}}\end{subarray}}}{\overline{st}^{\begin{subarray}{c}\vskip-4.09723pt{\scriptscriptstyle\mathfrak{p}}\end{subarray}}}\in P_{\mathfrak{p}}

since
ab\in A
,
st\in A\setminus\mathfrak{p}
and
abst=(as)(bt)\in PP\subseteq P
.

(c) Let
a\in A
and
s\in A\setminus\mathfrak{p}
define an arbitrary element

\frac{\overline{a}}{\overline{s}}\in\qf(A/\mathfrak{p})
. Because of
P\cup-P=A
, we have

as\in P
or
-as\in P
, i.e.,

-\frac{\overline{a}}{\overline{s}}=\frac{\overline{-a}}{\overline{s}}\in P_{\mathfrak{p}}
or

\frac{\overline{a}}{\overline{s}}\in P_{\mathfrak{p}}
.

(d) Suppose
a,b\in A
and
s,t\in A\setminus\mathfrak{p}
with
as,bt\in P
satisfy

\frac{\overline{a}^{\begin{subarray}{c}\vskip-4.09723pt{\scriptscriptstyle\mathfrak{p}}\end{subarray}}}{\overline{s}^{\begin{subarray}{c}\vskip-4.09723pt{\scriptscriptstyle\mathfrak{p}}\end{subarray}}}=-\frac{\overline{b}^{\begin{subarray}{c}\vskip-4.09723pt{\scriptscriptstyle\mathfrak{p}}\end{subarray}}}{\overline{t}^{\begin{subarray}{c}\vskip-4.09723pt{\scriptscriptstyle\mathfrak{p}}\end{subarray}}}.

Then
at+bs\in\mathfrak{p}
and therefore
ast^{2}+bts^{2}=st(at+bs)\in\mathfrak{p}\subseteq-P
, i.e.,

-ast^{2}-bts^{2}\in P
. From
ast^{2}=(as)t^{2}\in PA^{2}\subseteq P
and
bts^{2}=(bt)s^{2}\in PA^{2}\subseteq P
we
deduce
-ast^{2},-bts^{2}\in P
. Consequently,
ast^{2},bts^{2}\in\mathfrak{p}
and thus
a,b\in\mathfrak{p}
. We obtain

\frac{\overline{a}^{\begin{subarray}{c}\vskip-4.09723pt{\scriptscriptstyle\mathfrak{p}}\end{subarray}}}{\overline{s}^{\begin{subarray}{c}\vskip-4.09723pt{\scriptscriptstyle\mathfrak{p}}\end{subarray}}}=0=\frac{\overline{b}^{\begin{subarray}{c}\vskip-4.09723pt{\scriptscriptstyle\mathfrak{p}}\end{subarray}}}{\overline{t}^{\begin{subarray}{c}\vskip-4.09723pt{\scriptscriptstyle\mathfrak{p}}\end{subarray}}}

as desired.
∎

Lemma 1.12
.

[
\to

1.19
]
Let
(\mathfrak{p},\leq)\in\sper A
. Then
\{a\in A\mid\overline{a}^{\begin{subarray}{c}\vskip-4.09723pt{\scriptscriptstyle\mathfrak{p}}\end{subarray}}\geq 0\}
is a prime cone
of
A
.

Proof.

Set
P:=\{a\in A\mid\overline{a}^{\begin{subarray}{c}\vskip-4.09723pt{\scriptscriptstyle\mathfrak{p}}\end{subarray}}\geq 0\}
. Then
P+P\subseteq P
,
PP\subseteq P
,
P\cup-P=A
and

P\cap-P=\mathfrak{p}\in\spec A
. Now
P
is a prime cone of
A
by
1.9
(c).
∎

Proposition 1.13
.

[
\to

1.19
(c)]

The correspondence

\displaystyle(\mathfrak{p},\leq)

\displaystyle\mapsto\{a\in A\mid\overline{a}^{\begin{subarray}{c}\vskip-4.09723pt{\scriptscriptstyle\mathfrak{p}}\end{subarray}}\geq 0\}

\displaystyle(P\cap-P,P_{P\cap-P})

\displaystyle\mapsfrom P

defines a bijection between
\sper A
and the set of all prime cones of
A
.

Proof.

The well-definedness of both maps follows from Lemmata
1.11
and
1.12
.
Now first let
(\mathfrak{p},\leq)\in\sper A
and
P:=\{a\in A\mid\overline{a}^{\begin{subarray}{c}\vskip-4.09723pt{\scriptscriptstyle\mathfrak{p}}\end{subarray}}\geq 0\}
. We show

(\mathfrak{p},\leq)=(P\cap-P,P_{P\cap-P})
. It is clear that
\mathfrak{p}=P\cap-P
. Finally,

\displaystyle P_{P\cap-P}=P_{\mathfrak{p}}

\displaystyle=\left\{\frac{\overline{a}^{\begin{subarray}{c}\vskip-4.09723pt{\scriptscriptstyle\mathfrak{p}}\end{subarray}}}{\overline{s}^{\begin{subarray}{c}\vskip-4.09723pt{\scriptscriptstyle\mathfrak{p}}\end{subarray}}}\mid a\in A,s\in A\setminus\mathfrak{p},as\in P\right\}

\displaystyle=\left\{\frac{\overline{a}^{\begin{subarray}{c}\vskip-4.09723pt{\scriptscriptstyle\mathfrak{p}}\end{subarray}}}{\overline{s}^{\begin{subarray}{c}\vskip-4.09723pt{\scriptscriptstyle\mathfrak{p}}\end{subarray}}}\mid a\in A,s\in A\setminus\mathfrak{p},\overline{as}^{\begin{subarray}{c}\vskip-4.09723pt{\scriptscriptstyle\mathfrak{p}}\end{subarray}}\geq 0\right\}

\displaystyle=\left\{\frac{\overline{a}^{\begin{subarray}{c}\vskip-4.09723pt{\scriptscriptstyle\mathfrak{p}}\end{subarray}}}{\overline{s}^{\begin{subarray}{c}\vskip-4.09723pt{\scriptscriptstyle\mathfrak{p}}\end{subarray}}}\mid a\in A,s\in A\setminus\mathfrak{p},\frac{\overline{a}^{\begin{subarray}{c}\vskip-4.09723pt{\scriptscriptstyle\mathfrak{p}}\end{subarray}}}{\overline{s}^{\begin{subarray}{c}\vskip-4.09723pt{\scriptscriptstyle\mathfrak{p}}\end{subarray}}}\geq 0\right\}=\{x\in\qf(A/\mathfrak{p})\mid x\geq 0\}.

Conversely, suppose that
P
is a prime cone of
A
and
\mathfrak{p}:=P\cap-P
. We show

P=\{a\in A\mid\overline{a}^{\begin{subarray}{c}\vskip-4.09723pt{\scriptscriptstyle\mathfrak{p}}\end{subarray}}\in P_{\mathfrak{p}}\}.

Here “
\subseteq
” is trivial. To show “
\supseteq
”, let
a\in A

such that
\overline{a}^{\begin{subarray}{c}\vskip-4.09723pt{\scriptscriptstyle\mathfrak{p}}\end{subarray}}\in P_{\mathfrak{p}}
. Then there are
b\in A
and
s\in A\setminus\mathfrak{p}
such that
bs\in P
and

\overline{a}=\frac{\overline{b}}{\overline{s}}
. It follows that
\overline{as^{2}}^{\begin{subarray}{c}\vskip-4.09723pt{\scriptscriptstyle\mathfrak{p}}\end{subarray}}=\overline{bs}^{\begin{subarray}{c}\vskip-4.09723pt{\scriptscriptstyle\mathfrak{p}}\end{subarray}}
and thus

as^{2}\in bs+\mathfrak{p}\subseteq P+\mathfrak{p}\subseteq P+P\subseteq P
. Since
P
is a prime cone, we deduce
a\in P

or
-s^{2}\in P
. If we had
-s^{2}\in P
, then
s^{2}\in P\cap-P=\mathfrak{p}
(since
s^{2}\in A^{2}\subseteq P
) and therefore

s\in\mathfrak{p}\ \lightning
.
∎

Remark 1.14
.

[
\to

1.20
]
As a result of
1.13
, we can see elements of the real spectrum as prime cones. We
reformulate some of the above in this new language:

(a)

Remark
1.4
: Let
\varphi\colon A\to B
be a ring homomorphism. Then
\varphi
induces the map

\sper\varphi\colon\sper B\to\sper A,\ Q\mapsto\varphi^{-1}(Q)
. Suppose namely that
Q\in\sper B
,

\mathfrak{q}:=Q\cap-Q
,
P:=\varphi^{-1}(Q)
and
\mathfrak{p}:=P\cap-P
. Then
\varphi^{-1}(\mathfrak{q})=\varphi^{-1}(Q)\cap-\varphi^{-1}(Q)=P\cap-P=\mathfrak{p}
and the embedding
\qf(A/\mathfrak{p})\hookrightarrow\qf(B/\mathfrak{q})
induced by
\varphi
is an embedding of
ordered fields
(\qf(A/\mathfrak{p}),P_{\mathfrak{p}})\hookrightarrow(\qf(B/\mathfrak{q}),Q_{\mathfrak{q}})
because for
a\in A
and
s\in A\setminus\mathfrak{p}

with
as\in P
we have
\varphi(a)\in B
,
\varphi(s)\in B\setminus\mathfrak{q}
,
\varphi(a)\varphi(s)=\varphi(as)\in\varphi(P)\subseteq Q
.

(b)

Definition
1.6
: The support map is
\supp\colon\sper A\to\spec A,\ P\mapsto P\cap-P

[
\to

1.13
]. In particular, the Definitions
1.6
and
2.4
are
compatible.

Definition 1.15
.

For every
(\mathfrak{p},\leq)\in\sper A
, we call the real closed field

R_{(\mathfrak{p},\leq)}:=\overline{(\qf(A/\mathfrak{p}),\leq)}

the
representation field
of
(\mathfrak{p},\leq)
and the ring homomorphism

\varrho_{(\mathfrak{p},\leq)}\colon A\to R_{(\mathfrak{p},\leq)},\ a\mapsto\overline{a}^{\begin{subarray}{c}\vskip-4.09723pt{\scriptscriptstyle\mathfrak{p}}\end{subarray}}

the
representation
of
(\mathfrak{p},\leq)
.

Proposition 1.16
.

Let
P\in\sper A
. Then
P=\varrho_{P}^{-1}(R_{P}^{2})
and
\supp P=\ker\varrho_{P}
.

Proof.

\varrho_{P}^{-1}(R_{P}^{2})=\{a\in A\mid\varrho_{P}(a)\geq 0\text{ in }R_{P}\}=\{a\in A\mid\overline{a}^{\begin{subarray}{c}\vskip-4.09723pt{\scriptscriptstyle\supp P}\end{subarray}}\in P_{\supp P}\}\overset{\ref{sperprimecones}}{=}P
and therefore

\supp P=P\cap-P=\varrho_{P}^{-1}(R_{P}^{2})\cap-\varrho_{P}^{-1}(R_{P}^{2})=\varrho_{P}^{-1}(R_{P}^{2}\cap-R_{P}^{2})=\varrho_{P}^{-1}(\{0\})=\ker\varrho_{P}.

∎

Proposition 1.17
.

[
\to

1.1
]

Let
P
be a set. Then the following are equivalent:

(a)

P\in\sper A

(b)

There is an ordered field
(K,\leq)
and a ring homomorphism
\varphi\colon A\to K
such that

P=\varphi^{-1}(K_{\geq 0})
.

(c)

There exists a real closed field
R
and a ring homomorphism
\varphi\colon A\to R
such that

P=\varphi^{-1}(R^{2})
.

Proof.

(a)
\overset{\ref{kernelrep}}{\implies}
(c)
\overset{\text{trivial}}{\implies}
(b)
\overset{\text{\ref{newlang}(a)}}{\implies}
(a)
∎

2
Preorders and maximal prime cones

Throughout this section, let
A
be a commutative ring.

Proposition 2.1
.

Let
T
be a proper preorder of
A

[
\to

2.1
]
.
Then the following are equivalent:

(a)

T
is a prime cone of
A
.

(b)

\forall a,b\in A:(ab\in T\implies(a\in T\text{ or }-b\in T))

Proof.

(a)
\implies
(b)
is trivial by Definition
1.7
.

(b)
\implies
(a)
Suppose (b) holds. By Definition
1.7
,
it suffices to show
T\cup-T=A
. But for all
a\in A
it follows from (b) that

a\in T
or
-a\in T
because of
aa=a^{2}\in T
.
∎

Theorem 2.2
.

[
\to

2.9
]

Suppose
T
is a maximal proper preorder

[
\to

2.1
]
of
A
. Then
T
is a prime cone of
A
.

Proof.

We show
2.1
(b). For this purpose let
a,b\in A
satisfy

a\notin T
and
-b\notin T
. Then
T+aT
and
T-bT
are preorders of
A
[
\to

2.8
] that
properly contain
T
. Due to the maximality of
T
, therefore neither
T+aT
nor
T-bT
is proper as a
preorder, i.e.,
-1\in T+aT
and
-1\in T-bT
. Choose
s,t\in T
such that
-as\in 1+T
and
bt\in 1+T
.
Then
-abst\in(1+T)(1+T)\subseteq 1+T
and thus
-1\in T+abst\subseteq T+abT
. Since
T
is proper,
we conclude that
ab\notin T
as desired.
∎

Corollary 2.3
.

Every proper preorder of
A
is contained in a maximal prime cone of
A
.

Proof.

Use
2.2
and Zorn’s lemma.
∎

Proposition 2.4
.

Let
P,Q\in\sper A
such that
P\subseteq Q
and set
\mathfrak{q}:=\supp Q
. Then
Q=P\cup\mathfrak{q}
.

Proof.

“
\supseteq
” is trivial.

“
\subseteq
” Let
a\in Q\setminus P
. To show:
a\in\mathfrak{q}
. From
-a\in P\subseteq Q
we get

a\in Q\cap-Q=\mathfrak{q}
.
∎

Proposition and Terminology 2.5
.

Let
P\in\sper A
. Then “the spear”

\{Q\in\sper A\mid P\subseteq Q\}

is a chain
in the partially ordered set
\sper A
that possesses a largest element (“a spearhead”).

Proof.

Let
Q_{1},Q_{2}\in\sper A
with
P\subseteq Q_{1}
and
P\subseteq Q_{2}
. Suppose

Q_{1}\not\subseteq Q_{2}
. To show:
Q_{2}\subseteq Q_{1}
. Choose
a\in Q_{1}\setminus Q_{2}
. Let
b\in Q_{2}
.
To show
b\in Q_{1}
. We have
a-b\notin Q_{2}
(or else
a\in Q_{2}\ \lightning
) and thus
a-b\notin P
because of

P\subseteq Q_{2}
. Then
b-a\in P\subseteq Q_{1}
and thus

b\in Q_{1}
. The existence
of the “spearhead” follows now from
2.3
.
∎

3
Quotients and localization

Throughout this section, we let
A
be a commutative ring.

Proposition 3.1
.

\begin{Bmatrix}\text{Preimages}\\
\text{Images}\end{Bmatrix}
of preorders
[
\to

2.1
]

under
\begin{Bmatrix}\text{homomorphisms}\\
\text{epimorphisms}\end{Bmatrix}
of
commutative rings are again preorders.

Proof.

Exercise.
∎

Proposition 3.2
.

Let
I
be an ideal of
A
. The correspondence

\displaystyle T

\displaystyle\mapsto\overline{T}^{\begin{subarray}{c}\vskip-4.09723pt{\scriptscriptstyle I}\end{subarray}}:=\{\overline{a}^{\begin{subarray}{c}\vskip-4.09723pt{\scriptscriptstyle I}\end{subarray}}\mid a\in T\}

\displaystyle\{a\in A\mid\overline{a}^{\begin{subarray}{c}\vskip-4.09723pt{\scriptscriptstyle I}\end{subarray}}\in P\}

\displaystyle\mapsfrom P

defines a bijection between the set of

\begin{Bmatrix}\text{preorders \emph{[$\to$ \ref{defpreorder}]}}\\
\text{prime cones \emph{[$\to$ \ref{dfprimecone}]}}\end{Bmatrix}

T
of
A
with

I\subseteq T
and the set of
\begin{Bmatrix}\text{preorders}\\
\text{prime cones}\end{Bmatrix}
of
A/I
.

Proof.

Exercise.
∎

Lemma 3.3
.

Let
S\subseteq A
be multiplicative and
T\subseteq A
a preorder.
Let

\iota\colon A\to S^{-1}A,\ a\mapsto\frac{a}{1}

denote the canonical homomorphism. Then the preorder
generated by
\iota(T)
in
S^{-1}A
equals
S^{-2}T=\left\{\frac{a}{s^{2}}\mid a\in T,s\in S\right\}
.
This preorder is proper if and only if
T\cap-S^{2}=\emptyset
.

Proof.

Exercise.
∎

Proposition 3.4
.

Let
S\subseteq A
be multiplicative. The correspondence

\displaystyle P

\displaystyle\mapsto S^{-2}P

\displaystyle\left\{a\in A\mid\frac{a}{1}\in Q\right\}

\displaystyle\mapsfrom Q

gives rise to a bijection between
\{P\in\sper A\mid(\supp P)\cap S=\emptyset\}
and
\sper(S^{-1}A)
.

Proof.

Let
P\in\sper A
with
(\supp P)\cap S=\emptyset
. By
3.3
,
S^{-2}P
is a proper
preorder of
S^{-1}A
since
P\cap-S^{2}\subseteq(P\cap-A^{2})\cap(-S)\subseteq(P\cap-P)\cap(-S)=(\supp P)\cap-S=-((\supp P)\cap S)=-\emptyset=\emptyset
. To show that
S^{-2}P
is a prime cone of

S^{-1}A
, we verify the condition from
2.1
(b) where we use that for any two
fractions in
S^{-1}A
, one can find a common denominator from
S^{2}
. Let
a,b\in A
and
s\in S
with

\frac{a}{s^{2}}\cdot\frac{b}{s^{2}}\in S^{-2}P
. To show:
\frac{a}{s^{2}}\in S^{-2}P
or
-\frac{b}{s^{2}}\in S^{-2}P
.
Choose
c\in P
and
u\in S
with
\frac{ab}{s^{4}}=\frac{c}{u^{2}}
. Then there is
v\in S
such that

abu^{2}v=cs^{4}v
and therefore
(au^{2})(bv^{2})=abu^{2}v^{2}=cs^{4}v^{2}\in P
. Since
P
is a prime cone,
it follows that
au^{2}\in P
or
-bv^{2}\in P
. Hence
\frac{a}{s^{2}}=\frac{au^{2}}{(su)^{2}}\in S^{-2}P
or

-\frac{b}{s^{2}}=\frac{-bv^{2}}{(sv)^{2}}\in S^{-2}P
.

Conversely, let
Q\in\sper(S^{-1}A)
. For

\iota\colon A\to S^{-1}A,\ a\mapsto\frac{a}{1}
, we have [
\to

1.14
(a)]

\left\{a\in A\mid\frac{a}{1}\in Q\right\}=(\sper\iota)(Q)\in\sper A.

If we had
s\in S
with
\frac{s}{1}\in Q\cap-Q
, then

1=\frac{s}{s}=\frac{1}{s}\cdot\frac{s}{1}\in S^{-1}A(\supp Q)\subseteq\supp Q\ \lightning
.

It remains to show that the maps are inverse to each other:

(a)

If
P\in\sper A
with
(\supp P)\cap S=\emptyset
, then
P=\left\{a\in A\mid\frac{a}{1}\in S^{-2}P\right\}
.

(b)

If
Q\in\sper(S^{-1}A)
, then
Q=\left\{\frac{a}{s^{2}}\mid a\in A,\frac{a}{1}\in Q,s\in S\right\}
.

To show (a), let
P\in\sper A
with
(\supp P)\cap S=\emptyset
.

“
\subseteq
” is trivial.

“
\supseteq
” Let
a\in A
with
\frac{a}{1}\in S^{-2}P
. Choose
b\in P
and
s\in S
with

\frac{a}{1}=\frac{b}{s^{2}}
.
Then there is
t\in S
such that
as^{2}t=bt
and thus
as^{2}t^{2}=bt^{2}\in P
. It follows that
a\in P
or

-s^{2}t^{2}\in P
. The latter would lead to
s^{2}t^{2}\in(\supp P)\cap S\lightning
. Hence
a\in P
.

To show (b), consider an arbitrary
Q\in\sper(S^{-1}A)
.

“
\supseteq
” is trivial.

“
\subseteq
” Let
b\in A
and
s\in S
with
\frac{b}{s}\in Q
. Then for
a:=sb\in A
, we have

\frac{b}{s}=\frac{sb}{s^{2}}=\frac{a}{s^{2}}
and
\frac{a}{1}=\frac{sb}{1}=\left(\frac{s}{1}\right)^{2}\frac{b}{s}\in Q
.
∎

4
Abstract real Stellensätze

Definition 4.1
.

Let
A
be a commutative ring. We call the ring homomorphism

A\to\prod_{(\mathfrak{p},\leq)\in\sper A}R_{(\mathfrak{p},\leq)},\ a\mapsto\left(\widehat{a}\colon(\mathfrak{p},\leq)\mapsto\overline{a}^{\begin{subarray}{c}\vskip-4.09723pt{\scriptscriptstyle\mathfrak{p}}\end{subarray}}\right)

the
real representation
of
A
. For
a\in A
, we say that
\widehat{a}
is the
function represented

by
a
on the real spectrum of
A
.

Theorem 4.2

(abstract real Stellensatz
[
Kri
,
Ste
,
Pre
]
)
.

Suppose
A
is a commutative ring,
I\subseteq A
an ideal,
S\subseteq A
a multiplicative set and

T\subseteq A
a preorder. Then the following conditions are equivalent:

(a)

There does
not
exist any
P\in\sper A
satisfying

\displaystyle\forall a\in I

\displaystyle:\widehat{a}(P)=0,

\displaystyle\forall s\in S

\displaystyle:\widehat{s}(P)\neq 0\quad\text{and}

\displaystyle\forall t\in T

\displaystyle:\widehat{t}(P)\geq 0.

(b)

There are
a\in I
,
s\in S
and
t\in T
such that
a+s^{2}+t=0
.

Proof.

(b)
\implies
(a)
is trivial.

(a)
\implies
(b)
Replacing
T
by the preorder
T+I
, we can suppose WLOG
I=(0)
.
Suppose (b) does not hold. By
3.3
,
S^{-2}T
is then a proper preorder of
S^{-1}A
.
Consequently,
S^{-2}T
is contained in a prime cone
Q
of
S^{-1}A
by
2.3
. Now

3.4
yields
P:=\left\{a\in A\mid\frac{a}{1}\in Q\right\}\in\sper A
and
(\supp P)\cap S=\emptyset
.
For all
s\in S
, we have
\widehat{s}(P)=\overline{s}^{\begin{subarray}{c}\vskip-4.09723pt{\scriptscriptstyle\supp P}\end{subarray}}\neq 0
in
R_{P}

[
\to

1.15
,
1.13
] since
s\notin\supp P
. For all
t\in T
, we have
\widehat{t}(P)\geq 0

because
t\in P
.
∎

Terminology and Notation 4.3
.

(a)

We call a pair
(A,T)
consisting of a commutative ring
A
and a preorder
T
of
A
a

preordered ring
.

(b)

If
(A,T)
is a preordered ring, then we define its
real spectrum

\sper(A,T):=\{P\in\sper A\mid T\subseteq P\}.

(c)

[
\to

4.15
(c)] If
A
is a commutative ring,
a\in A
and
S\subseteq\sper A
, then we write

\displaystyle\widehat{a}\geq 0\text{ on $S$}

\displaystyle:\iff\forall P\in S:\widehat{a}(P)\geq 0,

\displaystyle\widehat{a}>0\text{ on $S$}

\displaystyle:\iff\forall P\in S:\widehat{a}(P)>0,

and so forth.

Corollary 4.4

(abstract Positivstellensatz)
.

Let
(A,T)
be a preordered ring and
a\in A
. Then the following are equivalent:

(a)

\widehat{a}>0
on
\sper(A,T)

(b)

\exists t\in T:ta\in 1+T

(c)

\exists t\in T:(1+t)a\in 1+T
.

Proof.

(b)
\implies
(c)
If
t,t^{\prime}\in T
satisfy
ta=1+t^{\prime}
, then

(1+t+t^{\prime})a=ta+(1+t^{\prime})a=1+t^{\prime}+ta^{2}\in 1+T.

(c)
\implies
(a)
is trivial.

(a)
\implies
(b)
follows by applying
4.2
on the ideal
(0)
, the
multiplicative set
\{1\}
and the preorder
T-aT
.
∎

Corollary 4.5

(abstract Nichtnegativstellensatz)
.

Let
(A,T)
be a preordered ring and
a\in A
. Then the following are equivalent:

(a)

\widehat{a}\geq 0
on
\sper(A,T)

(b)

\exists t\in T:\exists k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}:ta\in a^{2k}+T

Proof.

(b)
\implies
(a)
is trivial.

(a)
\implies
(b)
follows by applying
4.2
on the ideal
(0)
, the
multiplicative set
\{1,a,a^{2},\dots\}
and the preorder
T-aT
.
∎

Corollary 4.6

(abstract real Nullstellensatz
[
Kri
,
Du2
,
Ris
,
Efr
]
)
.

Let
A
be a commutative ring,
I\subseteq A
an ideal and
a\in A
. Then the following are equivalent:

(a)

\widehat{a}=0
on
\{P\in\sper A\mid I\subseteq\supp P\}

(b)

\exists k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}:\exists s\in\sum A^{2}:a^{2k}+s\in I

Proof.

(b)
\implies
(a)
is trivial.

(a)
\implies
(b)
follows by applying
4.2
on the ideal
I
, the
multiplicative set
\{1,a,a^{2},\dots\}
and the preorder
\sum A^{2}
.
∎

5
The real radical ideal

Throughout this section, we let
A
be a commutative ring.

Definition 5.1
.

[
\to

2.12
(c)]
A
is called
real
(or
real reduced
)
if

\forall n\in\mathbb{N}:\forall a_{1},\dots,a_{n}\in A:(a_{1}^{2}+\dots+a_{n}^{2}=0\implies a_{1}=0).

Remark 5.2
.

We have

A\neq\{0\}\text{ real}\implies-1\notin\sum A^{2}\overset{\ref{inmaxprimecone}}{\underset{\text{\ref{sqsm}(a)}}{\iff}}\sper A\neq\emptyset.

Here “
\Longrightarrow
” cannot be replaced by “
\iff
” (in contrast to the case where

A
is a field [
\to

2.12
]) as the example of
A=\mathbb{R}[X]/(X^{2})
shows.

Definition 5.3
.

An ideal
I\subseteq A
is called
real
(or
real radical ideal
) if
A/I
is real, i.e.,

\forall n\in\mathbb{N}:\forall a_{1},\dots,a_{n}\in A:(a_{1}^{2}+\ldots+a_{n}^{2}\in I\implies a_{1}\in I)
.

Proposition 5.4
.

Let
\mathfrak{p}\in\spec A
. Then the following are equivalent:

(a)

\mathfrak{p}
is real
[
\to

5.3
]

(b)

\qf(A/\mathfrak{p})
is real
[
\to

2.11
]

(c)

\exists P\in\sper A:\mathfrak{p}=\supp P

[
\to

1.14
(b)]

Proof.

(a)
\implies
(b)
Suppose (a) holds and let
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
a_{1},\dots,a_{n},s\in A/\mathfrak{p}

with
s\neq 0
such that
\left(\frac{a_{1}}{s}\right)^{2}+\ldots+\left(\frac{a_{n}}{s}\right)^{2}=0
. Then

a_{1}^{2}+\ldots+a_{n}^{2}=0
. Since
A/\mathfrak{p}
is real, it follows that
a_{1}=0
and therefore
\frac{a_{1}}{s}=0
.

(b)
\implies
(c)
Suppose (b) holds. Then
\qf(A/\mathfrak{p})
possesses an order
\leq
.
According to Definition
1.3
, we have
(\mathfrak{p},\leq)\in\sper A
and of course

\mathfrak{p}=\supp(\mathfrak{p},\leq)
by Definition
1.6
.

(c)
\implies
(a)
Suppose
\mathfrak{p}=\supp P
for some
P\in\sper A
. Let
n\in\mathbb{N}
and

a_{1},\dots,a_{n}\in A
satisfy
a_{1}^{2}+\ldots+a_{n}^{2}\in\mathfrak{p}
. Then

\widehat{a}_{1}(P)^{2}+\ldots+\widehat{a}_{n}(P)^{2}=0
and thus
\widehat{a}_{1}(P)=0
, i.e.,
a_{1}\in\mathfrak{p}
.
∎

Definition 5.5
.

The
real radical

\rrad I
of an ideal
I\subseteq A
is defined by

\rrad I:=\bigcap\left\{\mathfrak{p}\in\rspec A\mid I\subseteq\mathfrak{p}\right\}

where

\rspec A:=\{\mathfrak{p}\in\spec A\mid\mathfrak{p}\text{ is real}\}
and
\bigcap\emptyset:=A
.

Remark 5.6
.

Since every intersection of real ideals of
A
is obviously again a real ideal of
A
, for every
ideal
I\subseteq A
, the set
\rrad I
is a real ideal of
I
.

Theorem 5.7
.

[
\to

4.6
]

For every ideal
I
of
A
,

\rrad I=\left\{a\in A\mid\exists k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}:\exists s\in\sum A^{2}:a^{2k}+s\in I\right\}.

Proof.

5.4
shows that this is just a reformulation of
4.6
.
∎

Remark 5.8
.

Let
I\subseteq A
be an ideal. Then by
5.6
,
\rrad I
is the smallest real
ideal of
A
containing
I
.

Definition 5.9
.

We call
\rnil A:=\bigcap\rspec A=\rrad(0)
the
real nilradical
of
A
.

Corollary 5.10
.

We have

\{a\in A\mid\widehat{a}=0\}=\rnil A=\{a\in A\mid\exists k\in\mathbb{N}:\exists s\in\sum A^{2}:a^{2k}+s=0\}.

6
Constructible sets

In this section, we let
(A,T)
always be a preordered ring [
\to

4.3
(a)]. At the moment it
is a general one but after Proposition
6.2
,
we will further specialize
(A,T)
.

Definition 6.1
.

[
\to

8.3
]
A Boolean combination [
\to

8.2
(b)] of sets of the form

\{P\in\sper(A,T)\mid a\in P\}\qquad(a\in A)

is called a
constructible subset
of the real spectrum of
(A,T)
. We denote
the Boolean algebra of all constructible sets of
\sper(A,T)
by
\mathscr{C}_{(A,T)}
. The analogous
definition remains in force for a commutative ring instead of a preordered ring
(A,T)
.

Proposition 6.2
.

[
\to

8.6
]

Every constructible subset of
\sper(A,T)
is of the form

\bigcup_{i=1}^{k}\left\{P\in\sper(A,T)\mid\widehat{a}_{i}(P)=0,\widehat{b}_{i1}(P)>0,\ldots,\widehat{b}_{im}(P)>0\right\}

for some
k,m\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
,
a_{i},b_{ij}\in A
.

Proof.

Completely analogous to
8.6
using that

a\in P\overset{\ref{sperprimecones}}{\iff}\widehat{a}(P)\geq 0
for all
a\in A
and
P\in\sper A
.
∎

For the rest of this section, we fix an ordered field
(K,\leq)
, denote by
R:=\overline{(K,\leq)}
its
real closure, we let
n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
and set
A:=K[\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.6817pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.7584pt{}}}$\hss}}}X]
and
T:=\sum K_{\geq 0}A^{2}
.
Then
(A,T)
is a preordered ring and for all
P\in\sper(A,T)
there is by
7.5
exactly
one homomorphism from
R
to the representation field
R_{P}
of
P
extending
\varrho_{P}|_{K}

[
\to

1.15
]. In virtue of this homomorphism, which is of course an embedding of ordered
fields, we interpret
R
as an (ordered) subfield of
R_{P}
. In particular, we write
R=R_{P}
if
it is an isomorphism.

Proposition and Notation 6.3
.

The correspondence

\displaystyle P

\displaystyle\mapsto x_{P}:=(\varrho_{P}(X_{1}),\ldots,\varrho_{P}(X_{n}))

\displaystyle\{f\in A\mid f(x)\geq 0\}=:P_{x}

\displaystyle\mapsfrom x

defines a bijection between
\{P\in\sper(A,T)\mid R_{P}=R\}
and
R^{n}
.

Proof.

We first show that both maps are well-defined.
For every
P\in\sper(A,T)
with
R_{P}=R
, we have
x_{P}\in R^{n}
under the identification
of
R_{P}
and
R
. Conversely, let
x\in R^{n}
. Consider the ring homomorphism

\varphi\colon A\to R,\ f\mapsto f(x).

Then
P_{x}=\varphi^{-1}(R^{2})=(\sper\varphi)(R_{\geq 0})\in\sper A
[
\to

1.4
,
1.14
(a)].
Obviously,
K_{\geq 0}\subseteq P_{x}
and therefore
P_{x}\in\sper(A,T)
. In order to show

R_{P_{x}}=R
, we set
\mathfrak{p}:=\supp P_{x}
and consider the homomorphism of ordered fields

(\qf(A/\mathfrak{p}),(P_{x})_{\mathfrak{p}})\to(R,R^{2}),\ \frac{\overline{a}^{\begin{subarray}{c}\vskip-4.09723pt{\scriptscriptstyle\mathfrak{p}}\end{subarray}}}{\overline{s}^{\begin{subarray}{c}\vskip-4.09723pt{\scriptscriptstyle\mathfrak{p}}\end{subarray}}}\mapsto\frac{a(x)}{s(x)}\qquad(a\in A,s\in A\setminus\mathfrak{p})

induced by
\varphi
according to
1.4
taking into account
1.14
. Since
R
is real closed,
this homomorphism extends (uniquely) to a homomorphism of (ordered) fields

\psi\colon R_{P_{x}}=\overline{(\qf(A/\mathfrak{p}),(P_{x})_{\mathfrak{p}})}\to R.

We obviously have
\psi|_{K}=\id
and therefore
\psi|_{R}
is a
K
-endomorphism of the real closure
R
of

(K,\leq)
which can only be the identity by
7.5
. The injectivity of
\psi
now implies

R_{P_{x}}=R
as desired. For later use we note that
\psi=\id_{R}
implies

(
*
)

\overline{f}^{\begin{subarray}{c}\vskip-4.09723pt{\scriptscriptstyle\mathfrak{p}}\end{subarray}}=\psi^{-1}(\psi(\overline{f}^{\begin{subarray}{c}\vskip-4.09723pt{\scriptscriptstyle\mathfrak{p}}\end{subarray}}))=\psi^{-1}(f(x))=f(x)

for all
f\in A
.

It remains to show that both maps are inverse to each other. This means:

(a)

P=P_{x_{P}}
for all
P\in\sper(A,T)
with
R_{P}=R

(b)

x=x_{P_{x}}
for all
x\in R^{n}

To show (a), let
P\in\sper(A,T)
such that
R_{P}=R
. Then

\displaystyle P_{x_{P}}

\displaystyle=\{f\in A\mid f(\varrho_{P}(X_{1}),\dots,\varrho_{P}(X_{n}))\geq 0\text{ in $R$}\}

\displaystyle=\{f\in A\mid\varrho_{P}(f)\geq 0\text{ in $R_{P}$}\}=\{f\in A\mid\widehat{f}(P)\geq 0\}=P.

To show (b), we let
x\in R^{n}
. Then
\overline{X_{i}}^{\begin{subarray}{c}\vskip-4.09723pt{\scriptscriptstyle\supp P_{x}}\end{subarray}}\overset{(*)}{=}x_{i}\in R
for all
i\in\{1,\dots,n\}
.
Consequently,
x_{P_{x}}=(\varrho_{P_{x}}(X_{1}),\dots,\varrho_{P_{x}}(X_{n}))=(\overline{X_{1}}^{\begin{subarray}{c}\vskip-4.09723pt{\scriptscriptstyle\supp P_{x}}\end{subarray}},\dots,\overline{X_{n}}^{\begin{subarray}{c}\vskip-4.09723pt{\scriptscriptstyle\supp P_{x}}\end{subarray}})=(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})=x
.
∎

Theorem and Definition 6.4
.

[
\to

9.3
,
9.4
]

Let
n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
and denote again
by
\mathscr{S}_{n,R}
the Boolean algebra of all
K
-semialgebraic subsets of

R^{n}
. Then

\slim\colon\mathscr{C}_{(A,T)}\to\mathscr{S}_{n,R},\ C\mapsto\{x\in R^{n}\mid P_{x}\in C\}

is an isomorphism of Boolean algebras. We call
\slim
the
despectrification
or
slimming

(in German:
Entspeckung
)
and

\fatten:=\slim^{-1}

the
spectrification
or
fattening

(in German:
Verspeckung
). For all
f\in A
, one has

\slim(\{P\in\sper(A,T)\mid f\in P\})=\{x\in R^{n}\mid f(x)\geq 0\}.

Proof.

It is obvious that
\slim
is a homomorphism of Boolean algebras [
\to

9.1
]
satisfying
\slim(\{P\in\sper(A,T)\mid f\in P\})=\{x\in R^{n}\mid f\in P_{x}\}=\{x\in R^{n}\mid f(x)\geq 0\}
for
all
f\in A
. Let
\mathscr{R}\supseteq\{R_{P}\mid P\in\sper(A,T)\}
be a set of real closed fields
that are ordered extension fields of
(K,\leq)
[
\to

8.4
(b)]. Let
\mathscr{S}_{n}
again denote the
Boolean algebra of all
(K,\leq)
-semialgebraic classes [
\to

9.3
] and consider

\Phi\colon\mathscr{S}_{n}\to\mathscr{C}_{(A,T)},\ S\mapsto\{P\in\sper(A,T)\mid(R_{P},(\varrho_{P}(X_{1}),\dots,\varrho_{P}(X_{n})))\in S\}.

It is obvious that
\Phi
is a homomorphism of Boolean algebras satisfying

\Phi(\{(R^{\prime},x)\mid R^{\prime}\in\mathscr{R},x\in R^{\prime n},f(x)\geq 0\text{ in }R^{\prime}\})\\
=\{P\in\sper(A,T)\mid f(\varrho_{P}(X_{1}),\dots,\varrho_{P}(X_{n}))\geq 0\text{ in }R_{P}\}\\
=\{P\in\sper(A,T)\mid\varrho_{P}(f)\geq 0\text{ in }R_{P}\}\\
=\{P\in\sper(A,T)\mid\widehat{f}(P)\geq 0\}=\{P\in\sper(A,T)\mid f\in P\}

for all
f\in A
. From this one sees, in the first place, that
\Phi
is surjective and, secondly,
that
\slim\circ\,\Phi=\set_{R}
[
\to

9.3
]
which is an isomorphism of Boolean algebras by
9.4
.
Along with
\set_{R}
,
\Phi
is also injective. We conclude that
\Phi
is an isomorphism and
with it
\slim=(\slim\circ\,\Phi)\circ\;\Phi^{-1}
.
∎

Example 6.5
.

In
1.5
, we have already described
\sper\mathbb{R}[X]
. Now we describe
\sper\mathbb{R}[X]
as a set of
prime cones [
\to

1.14
] while using
3.8
: For
t\in\mathbb{R}
, we set

\displaystyle P_{t-}

\displaystyle:=\{f\in\mathbb{R}[X]\mid\exists\varepsilon\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}:\forall x\in(t-\varepsilon,t):f(x)\geq 0\},

\displaystyle P_{t}

\displaystyle:=\{f\in\mathbb{R}[X]\mid f(t)\geq 0\}\quad\text{and}

\displaystyle P_{t+}

\displaystyle:=\{f\in\mathbb{R}[X]\mid\exists\varepsilon\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}:\forall x\in(t,t+\varepsilon):f(x)\geq 0\}

Finally, we set

\displaystyle P_{-\infty}

\displaystyle:=\{f\in\mathbb{R}[X]\mid\exists c\in\mathbb{R}:\forall x\in(-\infty,c):f(x)\geq 0\}\quad\text{and}

\displaystyle P_{\infty}

\displaystyle:=\{f\in\mathbb{R}[X]\mid\exists c\in\mathbb{R}:\forall x\in(c,\infty):f(x)\geq 0\}.

Then

\sper\mathbb{R}[X]=\{P_{-\infty},P_{\infty}\}\cup\{P_{t-}\mid t\in\mathbb{R}\}\cup\{P_{t}\mid t\in\mathbb{R}\}\cup\{P_{t+}\mid t\in\mathbb{R}\}.

The fattening of the semialgebraic set
[0,1)\subseteq\mathbb{R}
is the set

\displaystyle C:=\{P_{0},P_{0+}\}

\displaystyle\cup\{P_{t-}\mid t\in(0,1)\}\cup\{P_{t}\mid t\in(0,1)\}

\displaystyle\cup\{P_{t+}\mid t\in(0,1)\}\cup\{P_{1-}\}\subseteq\sper\mathbb{R}[X].

In particular,
C
is constructible. In contrast to this,
C^{\prime}:=C\setminus\{P_{1-}\}
is
not constructible for otherwise
C
and
C^{\prime}
would have the same slimming in contradiction to

6.4
.

7
Real Stellensätze

Remark 7.1
.

Let
A
be a commutative ring.

(a)

Since every intersection of
\begin{Bmatrix}\text{ideals}\\
\text{multiplicative sets}\\
\text{preorders}\end{Bmatrix}
of
A
is again

\begin{Bmatrix}\text{an ideal}\\
\text{a multiplicative set}\\
\text{a preorder}\end{Bmatrix}
of
A
, there exists for every subset
E\subseteq A

\begin{Bmatrix}\text{a smallest ideal}\\
\text{a smallest multiplicative set}\\
\text{a smallest preorder}\end{Bmatrix}
of
A
containing
E
.
It is called the
\begin{Bmatrix}\text{ideal}\\
\text{multiplicative set}\\
\text{preorder}\end{Bmatrix}

generated
by
E
.

(b)

\begin{Bmatrix}\text{An ideal}\\
\text{A multiplicative set}\\
\text{A preorder}\end{Bmatrix}
of
A
is called
finitely generated

if it is generated by a finite subset of
A
.

(c)

The
\begin{Bmatrix}\text{ideal}\\
\text{multiplicative set}\\
\text{preorder}\end{Bmatrix}
generated by
a_{1},\dots,a_{m}\in A

(i.e., by
\{a_{1},\dots,a_{m}\}\subseteq A
) is
\begin{Bmatrix}Aa_{1}+\ldots+Aa_{m}\\
\{a_{1}^{\alpha_{1}}\dotsm a_{m}^{\alpha_{m}}\mid\alpha\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{m}\}\\
\sum_{\delta\in\{0,1\}^{m}}\sum A^{2}a_{1}^{\delta_{1}}\dotsm a_{m}^{\delta_{m}}\end{Bmatrix}
.

(d)

If
\begin{Bmatrix}\text{an ideal}\\
\text{a multiplicative set}\\
\text{a preorder}\end{Bmatrix}
of
A
is generated by
E\subseteq A
, then it is the union
over all
\begin{Bmatrix}\text{ideals}\\
\text{multiplicative sets}\\
\text{preorders}\end{Bmatrix}
of
A
generated by a finite subset of
E
.

(e)

If
\begin{Bmatrix}\text{an ideal $I$}\\
\text{a multiplicative set $S$}\\
\text{a preorder $T$}\end{Bmatrix}
\,\subseteq A
is generated by

E\subseteq A
and if
P\in\sper A
, then

\begin{Bmatrix}\forall a\in I:\widehat{a}(P)=0\\
\forall s\in S:\widehat{s}(P)\neq 0\\
\forall t\in T:\widehat{t}(P)\geq 0\end{Bmatrix}\iff\begin{Bmatrix}\forall a\in E:\widehat{a}(P)=0\\
\forall s\in E:\widehat{s}(P)\neq 0\\
\forall t\in E:\widehat{t}(P)\geq 0\end{Bmatrix}
.

Remark 7.2
.

Let
(L,\leq)
be an ordered field and
K
a subfield of
L
. If

\begin{Bmatrix}\text{an ideal $I$}\\
\text{a multiplicative set $S$}\\
\text{a preorder $T$}\end{Bmatrix}
\subseteq K[\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.6817pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.7584pt{}}}$\hss}}}X]
is generated
by
E\subseteq K[\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.6817pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.7584pt{}}}$\hss}}}X]
and if
x\in L^{n}
, then

\begin{Bmatrix}\forall g\in I:g(x)=0\\
\forall h\in S:h(x)\neq 0\\
\forall f\in T:f(x)\geq 0\end{Bmatrix}\iff\begin{Bmatrix}\forall g\in E:g(x)=0\\
\forall h\in E:h(x)\neq 0\\
\forall f\in E:f(x)\geq 0\end{Bmatrix}.

Remark and Terminology 7.3
.

(a)

“over
B
generated by
E
” stands for “generated by
B\cup E
”

(b)

“over
B
finitely generated” stands for “generated by
B\cup E
for some finite set
E
”

(c)

If
(K,\leq)
is an ordered field and
n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
, then the preorder generated by

p_{1},\dots,p_{m}\in K[\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.6817pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.7584pt{}}}$\hss}}}X]
over
K_{\geq 0}
equals

\sum_{\delta\in\{0,1\}^{m}}\sum K_{\geq 0}K[\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.6817pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.7584pt{}}}$\hss}}}X]^{2}p_{1}^{\delta_{1}}\dotsm p_{m}^{\delta_{m}}

[
\to

7.1
(c)].

Proposition 7.4
.

Let
(K,\leq)
be an ordered field,
R:=\overline{(K,\leq)}
and
n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}

[
\to

6.3
]
. Let
I
be an ideal,
S
a
finitely generated

multiplicative set and
T
a preorder of
K[\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.6817pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.7584pt{}}}$\hss}}}X]

finitely generated over
K_{\geq 0}
. Then

\{P\in\sper K[\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.6817pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.7584pt{}}}$\hss}}}X]\mid(\forall g\in I:\widehat{g}(P)=0),(\forall h\in S:\widehat{h}(P)\neq 0),(\forall f\in T:\widehat{f}(P)\geq 0)\}

is a constructible subset of
\sper(K[\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.6817pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.7584pt{}}}$\hss}}}X],\sum K_{\geq 0}K[\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.6817pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.7584pt{}}}$\hss}}}X]^{2})
whose
slimming is the
K
-semialgebraic set

\{x\in R^{n}\mid(\forall g\in I:g(x)=0),(\forall h\in S:h(x)\neq 0),(\forall f\in T:f(x)\geq 0)\}.

Proof.

By Hilbert’s basis theorem,
I
is finitely generated as well. Now use

7.1
,
6.4
and

7.2
.
∎

Theorem 7.5

(real Stellensatz
[
Kri
,
Ste
,
Pre
]
)
.

[
\to

4.2
]

Let
(K,\leq)
be an ordered subfield of the real closed field
R
,
n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
,

I
an ideal of
K[\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.6817pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.7584pt{}}}$\hss}}}X]
,
S
a
finitely generated

multiplicative set of
K[\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.6817pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.7584pt{}}}$\hss}}}X]
and
T
a preorder of
K[\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.6817pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.7584pt{}}}$\hss}}}X]

finitely generated over
K_{\geq 0}
.
Then the following are equivalent:

(a)

There does
not
exist any
x\in R^{n}
satisfying

\displaystyle\forall g\in I

\displaystyle:g(x)=0,

\displaystyle\forall h\in S

\displaystyle:h(x)\neq 0\quad\text{and}

\displaystyle\forall t\in T

\displaystyle:t(x)\geq 0.

(b)

0\in I+S^{2}+T

Proof.

(b)
\implies
(a)
is trivial.

(a)
\implies
(b)
WLOG
R=\overline{(K,\leq)}
[
\to

7.11
].
Because the fattening of the empty set is empty by
6.4
[
\to

9.1
], (a) implies
Condition
4.2
(a) from the abstract real Stellensatz applied to

A:=K[\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.6817pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.7584pt{}}}$\hss}}}X]
.
∎

Corollary 7.6

(Positivstellensatz)
.

[
\to

4.4
]

Let
(K,\leq)
be an ordered subfield of the real closed field
R
,
n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
,

T
a preorder of
K[\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.6817pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.7584pt{}}}$\hss}}}X]

finitely generated over
K_{\geq 0}
,

S:=\{x\in R^{n}\mid\forall p\in T:p(x)\geq 0\}

and
f\in K[\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.6817pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.7584pt{}}}$\hss}}}X]
.
Then the following are equivalent:

(a)

f>0
on
S

(b)

\exists t\in T:tf\in 1+T

(c)

\exists t\in T:(1+t)f\in 1+T

Proof.

Alternatively from
7.5
(as
4.4
from

4.2
) or from
4.4

(as
7.5
from
4.2
using
6.4
).
∎

Corollary 7.7

(Nichtnegativstellensatz)
.

[
\to

4.5
]

Let
(K,\leq)
be an ordered subfield of the real closed field
R
,
n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
,

T
a preorder of
K[\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.6817pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.7584pt{}}}$\hss}}}X]

finitely generated over
K_{\geq 0}
,

S:=\{x\in R^{n}\mid\forall p\in T:p(x)\geq 0\}

and
f\in K[\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.6817pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.7584pt{}}}$\hss}}}X]
.
Then the following are equivalent:

(a)

f\geq 0
on
S

(b)

\exists t\in T:\exists k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}:tf\in f^{2k}+T

Proof.

Alternatively from
7.5
(as
4.5
from

4.2
) or from
4.5

(as
7.5
from
4.2
using
6.4
).
∎

Remark 7.8
.

In the special case
T=\sum K_{\geq 0}K[\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.6817pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.7584pt{}}}$\hss}}}X]^{2}
, the Nichtnegativstellensatz

7.7
is obviously a strengthening of Artin’s solution
5.2

to Hilbert’s 17th problem in which Condition (b) is refined. This refinement has the advantage that
the proof of (b)
\implies
(a) does not require a real argument as it was the case in
5.2
.
The proof of
7.7
requires prime cones of rings instead of just preorders of fields
and therefore is substantially more difficult as the proof of
5.2
.

Corollary 7.9

(real Nullstellensatz
[
Kri
,
Du2
,
Ris
,
Efr
]
)
.

[
\to

4.6
]

Let
K
be a Euclidean subfield of the real closed field
R
,
n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
,

I
an ideal of
K[\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.6817pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.7584pt{}}}$\hss}}}X]
and

V:=\{x\in R^{n}\mid\forall p\in I:p(x)=0\}.

Then
\{f\in K[\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.6817pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.7584pt{}}}$\hss}}}X]\mid f=0\text{ on }V\}=\rrad I
.

Proof.

Using the description of
\rrad I
from
5.7
, this follows alternatively
from
7.5
(as
4.6
from

4.2
) or from
4.6

(as
7.5
from
4.2
using
6.4
).
∎

Definition 7.10
.

[
\to

7.1
] Let
K
be field. An extension field
R
of
K
is called
a

real closure of
K
if
R
is real closed and
R|K
is algebraic.

Remark 7.11
.

For two fields
K
and
R
, the following are equivalent:

(a)

R
is a real closure of
K
.

(b)

There is an order
\leq
of
K
such that
R=\overline{(K,\leq)}
.

Theorem 7.12

(variant of the real Stellensatz)
.

[
\to

7.5
]

Let
K
be a field,
n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
,
I
an ideal of
K[\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.6817pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.7584pt{}}}$\hss}}}X]
,
S
a
finitely generated

multiplicative set of
K[\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.6817pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.7584pt{}}}$\hss}}}X]
and
T
a
finitely generated
preorder of
K[\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.6817pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.7584pt{}}}$\hss}}}X]
.
Then the following are equivalent:

(a)

There does
not
exist a real closure
R
of
K
and an
x\in R^{n}
such that

\displaystyle\forall g\in I

\displaystyle:g(x)=0,

\displaystyle\forall h\in S

\displaystyle:h(x)\neq 0\quad\text{and}

\displaystyle\forall f\in T

\displaystyle:f(x)\geq 0.

(b)

0\in I+S^{2}+T

Proof.

(b)
\implies
(a)
is trivial.

We show
(a)
\implies
(b)
by contraposition. Suppose (b) does not hold. We have to show that
(a) does not hold.
By the abstract real Stellensatz
4.2
, there is some

P\in\sper K[\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.6817pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.7584pt{}}}$\hss}}}X]
such that

\forall g\in I:\widehat{g}(P)=0
,
\forall h\in S:\widehat{h}(P)\neq 0
and
\forall f\in T:\widehat{f}(P)\geq 0

all hold at the same time. Now consider the real closure
R:=\overline{(K,K\cap P)}
of
K

and the preordered ring
(K[\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.6817pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.7584pt{}}}$\hss}}}X],\sum(K\cap P)K[\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.6817pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.7584pt{}}}$\hss}}}X]^{2})
.
The set

U:=\left\{x\in R^{n}\mid(\forall g\in I:g(x)=0),(\forall h\in S:h(x)\neq 0),(\forall f\in T:f(x)\geq 0)\right\}

is
K
-semialgebraic by
7.2
since
I
,
S
and
T
are finitely generated.
We will show that
U\neq\emptyset
. We have chosen

P
to be an element of the constructible subset
of the real spectrum of this preordered ring which is the
fattening of
U
, i.e.,
P\in\fatten(U)
in the notation of
6.4
.
In particular,
\fatten(U)\neq\emptyset
and thus
U\neq\emptyset
by
6.4

∎

Remark 7.13
.

Wherever the hypothesis “finitely generated” appears in this section, it cannot be omitted.
For instance, assume that the Positivstellensatz
7.6
holds with the weaker hypothesis
“
K_{\geq 0}\subseteq T
” instead of “
T
finitely generated over
K_{\geq 0}
”. Consider then
K:=R:=\mathbb{R}
,

n:=1
and the preorder of
\mathbb{R}[X]
generated by

E:=\{X-N\mid N\in\mathbb{N}\}.

Then

S:=\{x\in\mathbb{R}\mid\forall p\in T:p(x)\geq 0\}=\emptyset
and thus
f:=-1>0
on
S
.
It follows that
\exists t\in T:tf\in 1+T
and thus by
7.1
(d) even

\exists t\in T^{\prime}:tf\in 1+T^{\prime}
for a preorder
T^{\prime}\subseteq T
generated by a finite set
E^{\prime}\subseteq E
.
The trivial direction of
7.6
then yields
-1>0
on

S^{\prime}:=\{x\in\mathbb{R}\mid\forall p\in T^{\prime}:p(x)\geq 0\}\overset{\ref{idealmultiplicativesetpreorderpolynomialring}}{=}\{x\in\mathbb{R}\mid\forall p\in E^{\prime}:p(x)\geq 0\}=[N,\infty)
for some
N\in\mathbb{N}
.
\lightning

Remark 7.14
.

[
\to

2.10
] Let
A
be a commutative ring and
T\subseteq A
a proper
preorder. Exactly as in the field case, there exists some
P\in\sper A
such that
T\subseteq P

[
\to

2.3
]. In sharp contrast, to the field case we do in general however not have that

T=\bigcap\sper(A,T)
. As an example, take
A:=\mathbb{R}[X,Y]
,
T:=\sum\mathbb{R}[X,Y]^{2}
and consider the Motzkin
polynomial
f:=X^{4}Y^{2}+X^{2}Y^{4}-3X^{2}Y^{2}+1
. By
4.16
, we have
f\notin T
and

S:=\{(x,y)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}\mid f(x,y)\geq 0\}=\mathbb{R}^{2}
. By
6.4
, the fattening

C:=\{P\in\sper A\mid f\in P\}\subseteq\sper A=\sper(A,T)

of
S
equals the whole of
\sper A
, i.e.,

f\in\bigcap\sper(A,T)
.

Chapter 4
Schmüdgen’s Positivstellensatz

1
The abstract Archimedean Positivstellensatz

Definition 1.1
.

[
\to

1.20
(d)]
A preordered ring
(A,T)
is called
Archimedean
if

\forall a\in A:\exists N\in\mathbb{N}:N+a\in T,

which is equivalent to
T-\mathbb{N}=A
and also to
T+\mathbb{Z}=A
.

Definition 1.2
.

Let
A
be a commutative ring.

(a)

A preorder
T
of
A
is called Archimedean if
(A,T)
is Archimedean.

(b)

A
is called Archimedean if
(A,\sum A^{2})
is Archimedean.

Theorem 1.3

(abstract Archimedean Positivstellensatz
[
Sto
,
Kad
,
Kri
,
Du1
]
)
.

[
\to

4.4
]

Let
(A,T)
be an Archimedean preordered ring and
a\in A
. Then the following are equivalent:

(a)

\widehat{a}>0
on
\sper(A,T)

(b)

\exists N\in\mathbb{N}:Na\in 1+T

Proof.

(b)
\implies
(a)
is trivial

(a)
\implies
(b)
For the multiplicative set
S:=\mathbb{N}\cdot 1\subseteq A
,

(S^{-1}A,S^{-2}T)
is again an Archimedean preordered ring [
\to

3.3
] and we have
[
\to

3.4
]

\widehat{a}>0\text{ on }\sper(A,T)\iff\widehat{\left(\frac{a}{1}\right)}>0\text{ on }\sper(S^{-1}A,S^{-2}T).

We can therefore suppose
\mathbb{N}\cdot 1\subseteq A^{\times}
and therefore have a homomorphism

\mathbb{Q}=\mathbb{N}^{-1}\mathbb{Z}\to A,\ \frac{p}{q}\mapsto\frac{p}{q}\qquad(p\in\mathbb{Z},q\in\mathbb{N}).

Suppose now that (a) holds. By the abstract Positivstellensatz
4.4
, there
is some
t\in T
such that
ta\in 1+T
. Since
T
is Archimedean, there are
N\in\mathbb{N}
with
N-t\in T

and
r\in\mathbb{N}
with
a+r\in T
. Now you can decrease
r\in\frac{1}{N}\mathbb{N}_{0}
a finite number of times by
\frac{1}{N}

until it gets negative since

a+\left(r-\frac{1}{N}\right)=\frac{N}{N^{2}}((\underbrace{N-t}_{\in T})(\underbrace{a+r}_{\in T})+(\underbrace{ta-1}_{\in T})+\underbrace{rt}_{\in T})\in T

as long as
r\geq 0
. It follows
a-\frac{1}{N}\in T
and thus
Na\in 1+T
.
∎

Corollary 1.4
.

Let
A
be a commutative ring and
P\in\sper A
. Then the following are equivalent:

(a)

P
is Archimedean and a maximal prime cone.
[
\to

1.2
(a)]

(b)

(\qf(A/\mathfrak{p}),P_{\mathfrak{p}})
is Archimedean where
\mathfrak{p}:=\supp P
.
[
\to

1.11
]

(c)

R_{P}
is Archimedean.
[
\to

1.15
]

(d)

There exists a homomorphism
\varphi\colon A\to\mathbb{R}
such that
P=(\sper\varphi)(\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0})
.

Proof.

(a)
\implies
(b)
Suppose (a) holds and let
a\in A
and
s\in A\setminus\mathfrak{p}
.
To show:
\exists N\in\mathbb{N}:\frac{\overline{a}^{\begin{subarray}{c}\vskip-4.09723pt{\scriptscriptstyle\mathfrak{p}}\end{subarray}}}{\overline{s}^{\begin{subarray}{c}\vskip-4.09723pt{\scriptscriptstyle\mathfrak{p}}\end{subarray}}}+N\in P_{\mathfrak{p}}
. WLOG
s\in P
. Since
P
is maximal, we have

\sper(A,P)=\{P\}
and thus
\widehat{s}>0
on
\sper(A,P)
. By
1.3
,
there is
N^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N}
such that
N^{\prime}s\in 1+P
. Choose
N^{\prime\prime}\in\mathbb{N}
such that
a+N^{\prime\prime}\in P
and set
N:=N^{\prime}N^{\prime\prime}
. Then

a+Ns=a+N^{\prime}N^{\prime\prime}s\in a+N^{\prime\prime}+P\subseteq P+P\subseteq P
and thus
(a+Ns)s\in PP\subseteq P
. It follows that

\frac{\overline{a}^{\begin{subarray}{c}\vskip-4.09723pt{\scriptscriptstyle\mathfrak{p}}\end{subarray}}}{\overline{s}^{\begin{subarray}{c}\vskip-4.09723pt{\scriptscriptstyle\mathfrak{p}}\end{subarray}}}+N=\frac{\overline{a+Ns}^{\begin{subarray}{c}\vskip-4.09723pt{\scriptscriptstyle\mathfrak{p}}\end{subarray}}}{\overline{s}^{\begin{subarray}{c}\vskip-4.09723pt{\scriptscriptstyle\mathfrak{p}}\end{subarray}}}\in P_{\mathfrak{p}}
.

(b)
\implies
(c)
If (b) holds, then

(\qf(A/\mathfrak{p}),P_{\mathfrak{p}})\hookrightarrow(\mathbb{R},\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0})
by
1.17
[
\to

1.5
] and

R_{P}=\overline{(\qf(A/\mathfrak{p}),P_{\mathfrak{p}})}\hookrightarrow(\mathbb{R},\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0})

by
7.5
.

(c)
\implies
(d)
Suppose that (c) holds. Choose an embedding
\iota\colon R_{P}\hookrightarrow\mathbb{R}

according to
1.17
. We have
\iota^{-1}(\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0})=(R_{P})_{\geq 0}
because

\iota
is an embedding of ordered fields. Now set
\varphi:=\iota\circ\varrho_{P}
. Then

\varphi^{-1}(\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0})=\varrho_{P}^{-1}(\iota^{-1}(\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}))=\varrho_{P}^{-1}((R_{P})_{\geq 0})\overset{\ref{kernelrep}}{=}P
.

(d)
\implies
(a)
Suppose
\varphi\colon A\to\mathbb{R}
is a homomorphism with

P=\varphi^{-1}(\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0})
. Then
P
is Archimedean for if
a\in A
, then one can choose
N\in\mathbb{N}
with

\varphi(a)+N\geq 0
and it follows that
a+N\in\varphi^{-1}(\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0})=P
. In order to show that
P
is maximal,
let
Q\in\sper A
with
P\subseteq Q
. To show:
P=Q
. If we had
a\in Q\setminus P
, then
\varphi(a)<0

and thus
\varphi(Na)\leq-1
for some
N\in\mathbb{N}
from which it would follow that
\varphi(-1-Na)\geq 0
and thus

-1-Na\in P\subseteq Q
and
-1=(-1-Na)+Na\in Q+Q\subseteq Q

\lightning
.
∎

2
The Archimedean Positivstellensatz [
\to
§
7
]

Lemma 2.1
.

Suppose
(K,\leq)
is an ordered subfield of
\mathbb{R}
,
n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
and

K_{\geq 0}\subseteq T\subseteq K[\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.6817pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.7584pt{}}}$\hss}}}X]
. Then the correspondence

\displaystyle x

\displaystyle\mapsto\ev_{x}\colon K[\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.6817pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.7584pt{}}}$\hss}}}X]\to\mathbb{R},\ p\mapsto p(x)

\displaystyle(\varphi(X_{1}),\dots,\varphi(X_{n}))

\displaystyle\mapsfrom\varphi

defines a bijection between
S:=\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\mid\forall p\in T:p(x)\geq 0\}
and the set of all ring homomorphisms

\varphi\colon K[\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.6817pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.7584pt{}}}$\hss}}}X]\to\mathbb{R}
satisfying
\varphi(T)\subseteq\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
.

Proof.

It is obviously enough to show that every ring homomorphism
\varphi\colon K[\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.6817pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.7584pt{}}}$\hss}}}X]\to\mathbb{R}
with

\varphi(T)\subseteq\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
is the identity on
K
. But this is clear by
1.15
since the identity
is the
only
embedding of ordered fields
(K,\leq)\hookrightarrow(\mathbb{R},\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0})
.
∎

Theorem 2.2

(Archimedean Positivstellensatz)
.

[
\to

1.3
,

7.6
]

Suppose
(K,\leq)
is an ordered subfield of
\mathbb{R}
,
n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
,

T\subseteq K[\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.6817pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.7584pt{}}}$\hss}}}X]
is an
Archimedean
preorder containing
K_{\geq 0}
,

S:=\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\mid\forall p\in T:p(x)\geq 0\}
and
f\in K[\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.6817pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.7584pt{}}}$\hss}}}X]
. Then the following are equivalent:

(a)

f>0
on
S

(b)

\exists N\in\mathbb{N}:f\in\frac{1}{N}+T

Proof.

(b)
\implies
(a)
is trivial.

(a)
\implies
(b)
Suppose that (a) holds. It is enough to show that

\widehat{f}>0
on
\sper(K[\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.6817pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.7584pt{}}}$\hss}}}X],T)
due to the abstract Archimedian Positivstellensatz

1.3
using
\frac{1}{N}=N\left(\frac{1}{N}\right)^{2}
.
To this end, let
P\in\sper(K[\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.6817pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.7584pt{}}}$\hss}}}X],T)
. We show that
\widehat{f}(P)>0
, i.e.,
\widehat{f}(P)\not\leq 0
which is equivalent to
f\notin-P
. Choose a maximal prime cone
Q
of
K[\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.6817pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.7584pt{}}}$\hss}}}X]
such that

P\subseteq Q
by
2.3
. We even show that
f\notin-Q
.
By
1.4
(d) and
2.1
, there is some
x\in S
satisfying

Q=\ev_{x}^{-1}(\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0})=\{p\in K[\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.6817pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.7584pt{}}}$\hss}}}X]\mid p(x)\geq 0\}
. From
f(x)>0
, we deduce now
f\notin-Q

as desired.
∎

Remark 2.3
.

If
T
is finitely generated over
K_{\geq 0}
in the situation of
2.2
, then
one can reduce
2.2
alternatively by fattening to

1.3
. This ultimately uses unnecessarily the heavy artillery
of real quantifier elimination
8.17
and is not applicable if
T
is not finitely generated over
K_{\geq 0}
.
The principal reason why the real quantifier elimination is not needed here is
1.17
.

3
Schmüdgen’s characterization of Archimedean preorders of the polynomial ring

Definition and Proposition 3.1
.

Let
(A,T)
be a preordered ring. Then

B_{(A,T)}:=\{a\in A\mid\exists N\in\mathbb{N}:N\pm a\in T\}

is a subring of
A
which we call the ring of with respect to
T

arithmetically bounded
elements of
A
.

Proof.

One sees immediately that
B_{(A,T)}
is a subgroup of the additive group of
A
. It is clear that

1\in B_{(A,T)}
. Finally, we have
B_{(A,T)}B_{(A,T)}\subseteq B_{(A,T)}
as one sees immediately
from the identity

3N^{2}\pm ab=(N+a)(N\pm b)+N(N-a)+N(N\mp b)\qquad(N\in\mathbb{N},a,b\in A).

∎

Lemma 3.2
.

Let
(A,T)
be a preordered ring such that
\frac{1}{2}\in A
. Then

a^{2}\in B_{(A,T)}\implies a\in B_{(A,T)}

for all
a\in A
.

Proof.

Choose
N\in\mathbb{N}
with
(N-1)-a^{2}\in T
. Then

N\pm a=(N-1)-a^{2}+\left(\frac{1}{2}\pm a\right)^{2}+3\left(\frac{1}{2}\right)^{2}\in T.

∎

Remark 3.3
.

If
(A,T)
is a preordered ring, then
T
is Archimedean if and only if
B_{(A,T)}=A
.

Lemma 3.4
.

Suppose
(K,\leq)
is an ordered subfield of
\mathbb{R}
,
n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
and
T\subseteq K[\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.6817pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.7584pt{}}}$\hss}}}X]

is a preorder containing
K_{\geq 0}
. Then the following are equivalent:

(a)

T
is Archimedean.

(b)

\exists N\in\mathbb{N}:N-\sum_{i=1}^{n}X_{i}^{2}\in T

(c)

\exists N\in\mathbb{N}:\forall i\in\{1,\dots,n\}:N\pm X_{i}\in T

Proof.

(a)
\implies
(b)
is trivial.

(b)
\implies
(c)
If (b) holds, then
N-X_{i}^{2}\in T
and thus
X_{i}^{2}\in B_{(A,T)}

for all
i\in\{1,\dots,n\}
. Now apply
3.2
.

(c)
\implies
(a)
Since
(K,\leq)
is Archimedean and
K_{\geq 0}\subseteq T
,
we have
K\subseteq B_{(A,T)}
. If now moreover (c) holds, then
K[\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.6817pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.7584pt{}}}$\hss}}}X]=B_{(A,T)}
.
∎

Theorem 3.5

(Schmüdgen’s Theorem
[
Sch
,
BW
]
)
.

Suppose
(K,\leq)
is an ordered subfield of
\mathbb{R}
,

n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
and
T
a preorder of
K[\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.6817pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.7584pt{}}}$\hss}}}X]
which is finitely generated
over
K_{\geq 0}
. Write

S:=\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\mid\forall p\in T:p(x)\geq 0\}.

Then

\text{$T$ Archimedean}\iff\text{$S$ compact}.

Proof.

[
BW
]
“
\Longrightarrow
” Let
T
be Archimedean. By
3.4
(b), there is some

N\in\mathbb{N}
with
N-\sum_{i=1}^{n}X_{i}^{2}\in T
. Then
S
is contained in the ball of radius
\sqrt{N}

centered at the origin and thus bounded. Anyway
S
is already closed. Consequently,
S
is compact.

“
\Longleftarrow
” Let
S
be compact. Write
r:=\sum_{i=1}^{n}X_{i}^{2}
and choose
N\in\mathbb{N}

such that
N-r>0
on
S
. By the Positivstellensatz
7.6
, we find
t\in T
such that

(1+t)(N-r)\in 1+T\subseteq T
. We know that
T^{\prime}:=T+(N-r)T
is a preorder of
K[\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.6817pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.7584pt{}}}$\hss}}}X]
that is Archimedean
by
3.4
. We have
(1+t)T^{\prime}\subseteq T
and
N-r+Nt=(1+t)(N-r)+tr\in T+T\subseteq T
.
Choose
N^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N}
with
N^{\prime}-t\in T^{\prime}
. Then

(1+N^{\prime})(N^{\prime}-t)=(1+t)(N^{\prime}-t)+(N^{\prime}-t)^{2}\in(1+t)T^{\prime}+T\subseteq T+T\subseteq T

from which
N^{\prime}-t\in T
follows because of
\frac{1}{1+N^{\prime}}=(1+N^{\prime})\left(\frac{1}{1+N^{\prime}}\right)^{2}\in T
.
We conclude that

N(N^{\prime}+1)-r=NN^{\prime}+N-r=(N-r+tN)+N(N^{\prime}-t)\in T+T\subseteq T.

Now
3.4
implies that
T
is Archimedean.
∎

Corollary 3.6

(Schmüdgen’s Positivstellensatz)
.

[
\to

2.2
]

Suppose
(K,\leq)
is an ordered subfield of
\mathbb{R}
,

n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
,
T
a preorder of
K[\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.6817pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.7584pt{}}}$\hss}}}X]
which is finitely generated
over
K_{\geq 0}
. Suppose

S:=\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\mid\forall p\in T:p(x)\geq 0\}
is
compact
and
f\in K[\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.6817pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.7584pt{}}}$\hss}}}X]
. Then the following are equivalent:

(a)

f>0
on
S

(b)

\exists N\in\mathbb{N}:f\in\frac{1}{N}+T

Proof.

By Schmüdgen’s Theorem
3.5
,
T
is Archimedean. But then the Archimedean Positivstellensatz

2.2
proves the equivalence of (a) and (b).
∎

Remark 3.7
.

(a)

Exactly as in
7.13
, one sees that the hypothesis “
T
finitely
generated over
K_{\geq 0}
” cannot be replaced by the weaker hypothesis “
K_{\geq 0}\subseteq T
”.

(b)

If one drops the requirement that
S
is compact, then
3.6

gets wrong as the example
K:=\mathbb{R}
,
n:=1
,
T:=\sum\mathbb{R}[X]^{2}+\sum\mathbb{R}[X]^{2}X^{3}
and
f:=X+1
shows:
We have
f>0
on
S=[0,\infty)
but
f\notin T
for degree reasons as one sees from
2.4
(b).

(c)

In the situation of
3.6
, we unfortunately do not have in general

f\geq 0\text{ on }S\iff f\in T.

For this, consider
K:=\mathbb{R}
,
n:=1
,
T:=\sum\mathbb{R}[X]^{2}+\sum\mathbb{R}[X]^{2}X^{3}(1-X)
and
f:=X
. Then

f\geq 0
on
S=[0,1]
. Assume
f\in T
. Write
f=\sum_{i}p_{i}^{2}+\sum_{j}q_{j}^{2}X^{3}(1-X)
for some

p_{i},q_{j}\in\mathbb{R}[X]
. Evaluating in
0
, yields
0=\sum_{i}p_{i}(0)^{2}
and thus
p_{i}(0)=0
for all
i
.
Write
p_{i}=Xp_{i}^{\prime}
for some
p_{i}^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}[X]
. Then
X=f=X^{2}\left(\sum_{i}p_{i}^{\prime 2}+\sum_{j}q_{j}^{2}X(1-X)\right)\ \lightning
.

Chapter 5
The real spectrum as a topological space

1
Tikhonov’s theorem

Remark 1.1
.

Any finite intersection of unions of certain sets is a union of finite intersections of such sets
[
\to

8.1
].

Reminder 1.2
.

[
\to

8.2
]
Let
M
be a set.

(a)

A set
\mathscr{O}\subseteq\mathscr{P}(M)
is called a
topology
on
M
if

•

M\in\mathscr{O}
,

•

\forall A_{1},A_{2}\in\mathscr{O}:A_{1}\cap A_{2}\in\mathscr{O}
and

•

\forall\mathscr{A}\subseteq\mathscr{O}:\bigcup\mathscr{A}\in\mathscr{O}
.

In this case,
(M,\mathscr{O})
is called a
topological space
and the elements of
\mathscr{O}
are called
its
open sets
.

(b)

Let
\mathscr{G}\subseteq\mathscr{P}(M)
. Then the set of all unions of finite intersections of elements of

\mathscr{G}
(where
\bigcap\emptyset:=M
) is obviously the smallest topology
\mathscr{O}
on
M
such that

\mathscr{G}\subseteq\mathscr{O}
. It is called the topology
generated by
\mathscr{G}
(on
M
).

(c)

If
\mathscr{O}
and
\mathscr{O}^{\prime}
are topologies on
M
, then
\mathscr{O}
is called

\begin{Bmatrix}\text{coarser}\\
\text{finer}\end{Bmatrix}

than
\mathscr{O}^{\prime}
if
\begin{Bmatrix}\mathscr{O}\subseteq\mathscr{O}^{\prime}\\
\mathscr{O}\supseteq\mathscr{O}^{\prime}\end{Bmatrix}
.

(d)

The finest topology on
M
is the
discrete topology

\mathscr{O}:=\mathscr{P}(M)
.

(e)

The coarsest topology on
M
is the
trivial topology
(in German:
Klumpentopologie
)

\mathscr{O}:=\{\emptyset,M\}
.

Reminder 1.3
.

Let
(M,\mathscr{O})
and
(N,\mathscr{P})
be topological spaces and
f\colon M\to N
be a map.
Then
f
is called
continuous
if
f^{-1}(B)\in\mathscr{O}
for all
B\in\mathscr{P}
. If
\mathscr{P}
is generated by
\mathscr{G}
, then
f
is continuous if and only if

f^{-1}(B)\in\mathscr{\mathscr{O}}
for all
B\in\mathscr{G}
.

Reminder 1.4
.

Let
M
be a set,
(N_{i},\mathscr{P}_{i})_{i\in I}
a family of topological spaces and
(f_{i})_{i\in I}
a family
of maps
f_{i}\colon M\to N_{i}
(
i\in I
). Then there exists a coarsest topology
\mathscr{O}
on
M

making all
f_{i}
(
i\in I
) continuous. One calls
\mathscr{O}
the

initial topology

(or
weak topology
) with respect to
(f_{i})_{i\in I}
. If
I=\{1,\dots,n\}
, then
\mathscr{O}
is also called the
initial topology with respect to
f_{1},\dots,f_{n}
. This topology
\mathscr{O}
is generated by

\{f_{i}^{-1}(B_{i})\mid i\in I,B\in\mathscr{P}_{i}\}
. More generally, the following holds: If
\mathscr{P}_{i}
is generated
by
\mathscr{G}_{i}
for
i\in I
, then
\mathscr{O}
is generated by
\{f_{i}^{-1}(B)\mid i\in I,B\in\mathscr{G}_{i}\}
.
It holds that
\mathscr{O}
is the unique topology on
M
having the following property:
For every topological space
(M^{\prime},\mathscr{O}^{\prime})
and every
g\colon M^{\prime}\to M
, the map
g

is continuous if and only if all the maps
f_{i}\circ g
with
i\in I
are continuous.

Example 1.5
.

(a)

Let
(N,\mathscr{P})
be a topological space and
M\subseteq N
. Then one endows
M
with the
initial topology
\mathscr{O}
with respect to
M\to N,\ x\mapsto x
. One calls
\mathscr{O}
the
topology
induced
by
\mathscr{P}
on
M
and
(M,\mathscr{O})
a
subspace

of
(N,\mathscr{P})
. We have

\mathscr{O}=\{M\cap B\mid B\in\mathscr{P}\}.

(b)

Let
(N_{i},\mathscr{P}_{i})_{i\in I}
be a family of topological spaces. Then there exists a coarsest topology

\mathscr{O}
on
N:=\prod_{i\in I}N_{i}
making all projections
\pi_{i}\colon N\to N_{i},\ (x_{j})_{j\in I}\mapsto x_{i}

(
i\in I
) continuous. One calls
\mathscr{O}
the
product topology
of the
\mathscr{P}_{i}
(
i\in I
) on
N

and
(N,\mathscr{O})
the
product space
of the
(N_{i},P_{i})
(
i\in I
).
The elements of
\mathscr{O}
are exactly the unions of sets of the form

\prod_{i\in I}B_{i}
where
B_{i}\in\mathscr{P}_{i}
for
i\in I
and
\#\{i\in I\mid B_{i}\neq N_{i}\}<\infty
.

Remark 1.6
.

The constructions (a) and (b) in Example
1.5
commute
in the following sense:
Let
(N_{i},\mathscr{P}_{i})_{i\in I}
be a family of topological spaces and

(N,\mathscr{O})
its product. Furthermore, let

(M_{i})_{i\in I}
be a family of sets such that
M_{i}\subseteq N_{i}
for each
i\in I

and set
M:=\prod_{i\in I}M_{i}
. Then
\mathscr{O}
induces on
M

the product topology of the topologies induced on the
M_{i}
by the
\mathscr{P}_{i}
.

Definition 1.7
.

Let
M
be a set and
\mathscr{S}
a Boolean algebra on
M
[
\to

8.2
]
(for instance
\mathscr{S}=\mathscr{P}(M)
). A set
\mathscr{F}\subseteq\mathscr{S}
is called a

filter
in
\mathscr{S}
(or filter on
M
in case
\mathscr{S}=\mathscr{P}(M)
) if

•

\emptyset\notin\mathscr{F},M\in\mathscr{F}
,

•

\forall U,V\in\mathscr{F}:U\cap V\in\mathscr{F}
and

•

\forall U\in\mathscr{F}:\forall V\in\mathscr{S}:(U\subseteq V\implies V\in\mathscr{F})
.

If in addition
\forall U\in\mathscr{S}:(U\in\mathscr{F}\text{ or }\complement U\in\mathscr{F})
, then
\mathscr{F}

is called an
ultrafilter
.

Proposition 1.8
.

Let
\mathscr{S}
be a Boolean algebra on the set
M
and
\mathscr{F}
a filter in
\mathscr{S}
.
Then the following are equivalent:

(a)

\mathscr{F}
is an ultrafilter.

(b)

\forall U,V\in\mathscr{S}:(U\cup V\in\mathscr{F}\implies(U\in\mathscr{F}\text{ or }V\in\mathscr{F}))

Proof.

(a)
\implies
(b)
Suppose that (a) holds and let
U,V\in\mathscr{S}
such that

U\cup V\in\mathscr{F}
and
U\notin\mathscr{F}
. To show:
V\in\mathscr{F}
. Since
\mathscr{F}
is an ultrafilter,
we have
\complement U\in\mathscr{F}
and thus
(U\cup V)\cap\complement U\in\mathscr{F}
.
Because of
(U\cup V)\cap\complement U\subseteq V
it then also holds that
V\in\mathscr{F}
.

(b)
\implies
(a)
is trivial.
∎

Example 1.9
.

Let
(M,\mathscr{O})
be a topological space and
x\in M
. Then

\mathscr{U}_{x}:=\{U\in\mathscr{P}(M)\mid\exists A\in\mathscr{O}:x\in A\subseteq U\}

is a filter on
M
.
One calls
\mathscr{U}_{x}
the
neighborhood filter
of
x
and its elements the

neighborhoods
of
x
. In general,
\mathscr{U}_{x}
is not an ultrafilter since

[-1,1]=[-1,0]\cup[0,1]
is a neighborhood of
0
in
\mathbb{R}
as opposed to
[-1,0]
and
[0,1]
.

Definition 1.10
.

Let
(M,\mathscr{O})
be a topological space,
\mathscr{F}
a filter on
M
and
x\in M
. One says that

\mathscr{F}

converges
to
x
and writes
\mathscr{F}\to x
if

\mathscr{U}_{x}\subseteq\mathscr{F}.

If
\mathscr{F}
converges to exactly one point
x
, one calls this the
limit
of
\mathscr{F}
and writes

x=\lim\mathscr{F}.

Definition 1.11
.

Let
(M,\mathscr{O})
be a topological space,
(a_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
a sequence in
M
and
x\in M
. We call

\mathscr{F}:=\{U\in\mathscr{P}(M)\mid\exists N\in\mathbb{N}:\{a_{n}\mid n\geq N\}\subseteq U\}

the filter associated to
(a_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
. It is clearly a filter on
M
.
One says that
