(a_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}

converges
to
x
and writes

a_{n}\overset{n\to\infty}{\longrightarrow}x

if
\mathscr{F}\to x
.
If
\mathscr{F}
converges to exactly one point
x
, one calls this the
limit
of
(a_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
and writes

x=\lim_{n\to\infty}a_{n}.

Definition and Lemma 1.12
.

Suppose
f\colon M\to N
is a map and
\mathscr{F}
a filter on
M
. Then the
image filter

f(\mathscr{F}):=\{V\in\mathscr{P}(N)\mid\exists U\in\mathscr{F}:f(U)\subseteq V\}

is a filter on
N
.
If
\mathscr{F}
is an ultrafilter, then so is
f(\mathscr{F})
.

Proof.

One sees immediately that
f(\mathscr{F})
is a filter. Now let
\mathscr{F}
be an ultrafilter.
Suppose
V\subseteq N
and
V\notin f(\mathscr{F})
.
To show:
\complement V\in f(\mathscr{F})
. For
U:=f^{-1}(V)
, one has
f(U)\subseteq V
and thus

U\notin\mathscr{F}
.
But then
f^{-1}(\complement V)=\complement U\in\mathscr{F}
. From

f(\complement U)\subseteq\complement V
, we obtain thus
\complement V\in f(\mathscr{F})
.
∎

Lemma 1.13
.

Let
M
be a set endowed with the initial topology with respect to a family
(f_{i})_{i\in I}
of maps

f_{i}\colon M\to N_{i}
into topological spaces
N_{i}
(
i\in I
). Let
\mathscr{F}
be a filter on
M
and
x\in M
.
Then

\mathscr{F}\to x\iff\forall i\in I:f_{i}(\mathscr{F})\to f_{i}(x).

Proof.

“
\Longrightarrow
” Suppose
\mathscr{F}\to x
and let
i\in I
. To show:
f_{i}(\mathscr{F})\to f_{i}(x)
.
Let
V\in\mathscr{U}_{f_{i}(x)}
. To show:
V\in f_{i}(\mathscr{F})
. Since
f_{i}
is continuous, we have

U:=f_{i}^{-1}(V)\in\mathscr{U}_{x}
and thus
U\in\mathscr{F}
. From
f_{i}(U)\subseteq V
, we get

V\in f_{i}(\mathscr{F})
.

“
\Longleftarrow
” Suppose
f_{i}(\mathscr{F})\to f_{i}(x)
for all
i\in I
. Let
U\in\mathscr{U}_{x}
.
To show:
U\in\mathscr{F}
. Choose
n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
,
i_{1},\dots,i_{n}\in I
and

V_{k}
open in
N_{i_{k}}
(
k\in\{1,\dots,n\}
) such that

x\in f_{i_{1}}^{-1}(V_{1})\cap\ldots\cap f_{i_{n}}^{-1}(V_{n})\subseteq U.

Since
\mathscr{F}
is a filter, it is enough to
show that
f_{i_{k}}^{-1}(V_{k})\in\mathscr{F}
for all
k\in\{1,\dots,n\}
. Fix therefore
k\in\{1,\dots,n\}
.
Since
V_{k}
is an (open) neighborhood of
f_{i_{k}}(x)
in
N_{i_{k}}
,
the hypothesis yields
V_{k}\in f_{i_{k}}(\mathscr{F})
. Hence there is
U_{0}\in\mathscr{F}
such that

f_{i_{k}}(U_{0})\subseteq V_{k}
. Now everything follows from

U_{0}\subseteq f_{i_{k}}^{-1}(f_{i_{k}}(U_{0}))\subseteq f_{i_{k}}^{-1}(V_{k})
.
∎

Definition 1.14
.

Let
(M,\mathscr{O})
be a topological space. Then
(M,\mathscr{O})
is called a
Hausdorff
space
if every two distinct points of
M
can be separated by disjoint neighborhoods, i.e.,

\forall x,y\in M:(x\neq y\implies\exists U\in\mathscr{U}_{x}:\exists V\in\mathscr{U}_{y}:U\cap V=\emptyset).

We call
(M,\mathscr{O})

quasicompact
if every open cover of
M
possesses a finite subcover, i.e.,

\forall\mathscr{A}\subseteq\mathscr{O}:\left(M=\bigcup\mathscr{A}\implies\exists\mathscr{B}\subseteq\mathscr{A}:\left(\#\mathscr{B}<\infty~\mathbin{\&}~M=\bigcup\mathscr{B}\right)\right).

Furthermore, we call a quasicompact Hausdorff space
compact
.

Proposition 1.15
.

Suppose
M
is a set,
\mathscr{S}
a Boolean algebra on
M
and
\mathscr{U}
a filter in
\mathscr{S}
.
Then the following are equivalent:

(a)

\mathscr{U}
is an ultrafilter in
\mathscr{S}
.

(b)

\mathscr{U}
is a maximal filter in
\mathscr{S}
.

Proof.

(a)
\implies
(b)
Suppose that (a) holds and let
\mathscr{F}
be a filter in
\mathscr{S}

such that
\mathscr{U}\subseteq\mathscr{F}
. In order to show
\mathscr{F}\subseteq\mathscr{U}
, we fix

U\in\mathscr{F}
. If we had
U\notin\mathscr{U}
, we would get

\complement U\in\mathscr{U}\subseteq\mathscr{F}
and thus
\emptyset=U\cap\complement U\in\mathscr{F}

\lightning
.

(b)
\implies
(a)
Suppose that (b) holds and let
U\in\mathscr{S}
satisfy

U\notin\mathscr{U}
. To show:
\complement U\in\mathscr{U}
. It is enough to show that

\mathscr{F}:=\{V\in\mathscr{S}\mid\exists A\in\mathscr{U}:A\cap\complement U\subseteq V\}
is a filter in

\mathscr{S}
because then it follows from
\mathscr{U}\subseteq\mathscr{F}
that

\complement U\in\mathscr{F}=\mathscr{U}
. For this, it suffices to show
\emptyset\notin\mathscr{F}
.
If we had
\emptyset\in\mathscr{F}
, then there would be an
A\in\mathscr{U}
satisfying

A\cap\complement U=\emptyset
and from
A\subseteq U
it would follow that
U\in\mathscr{U}

\lightning
.
∎

Theorem 1.16
.

Let
M
be a set,
\mathscr{S}
a Boolean algebra on
M
and
\mathscr{F}
a filter in
\mathscr{S}
.
Then there is an ultrafilter
\mathscr{U}
in
\mathscr{S}
such that
\mathscr{F}\subseteq\mathscr{U}
.

Proof.

By
1.15
, it suffices to show that the set

\{\mathscr{F}^{\prime}\mid\mathscr{F}^{\prime}\text{ Filter in }\mathscr{S},\mathscr{F}\subseteq\mathscr{F}^{\prime}\}

partially ordered by inclusion has a maximal element. This follows from Zorn’s lemma since the union of
a nonempty chain of filters in
\mathscr{S}
is again a filter in
\mathscr{S}
.
∎

Theorem 1.17
.

A topological space
M
is quasicompact if and only if each ultrafilter on the set
M
converges in
M
.

Proof.

Let
M
be a topological space. We show the equivalence of the following statements:

(a)

M
is not quasicompact.

(b)

There is an ultrafilter on
M
that does not converge.

(a)
\implies
(b)
Suppose that (a) holds. Then for each
x\in M
, there is obviously
an open set
A_{x}\subseteq M
with
x\in A_{x}
in such a way that

\bigcup_{x\in M}A_{x}=M
and
A_{x_{1}}\cup\ldots\cup A_{x_{n}}\neq M
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
and
x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\in M
.
Then

\mathscr{F}:=\left\{U\in\mathscr{P}(M)\mid\exists n\in\mathbb{N}:\exists x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}\in M:\left(\complement A_{x_{1}}\right)\cap\ldots\cap\left(\complement A_{x_{n}}\right)\subseteq U\right\}

is a filter on
M
that can be extended by
1.16
to an ultrafilter
\mathscr{U}
on
M
.
Let
x\in M
. We show that
\mathscr{U}
does not converge to
x
. If we had
\mathscr{U}\to x
,
then
A_{x}\in\mathscr{U}
in contradiction to
\complement A_{x}\in\mathscr{U}
.

(b)
\implies
(a)
Suppose that (b) holds. Choose an ultrafilter
\mathscr{U}
on
M

that does not converge. Then for every
x\in M
there is an
U_{x}\in\mathscr{U}_{x}
such that

U_{x}\notin\mathscr{U}
. WLOG
U_{x}
is open for every
x\in M
. Of course
M=\bigcup_{x\in M}U_{x}
.
If
n\in\mathbb{N}
and
x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\in M
, then

\complement(U_{x_{1}}\cup\ldots\cup U_{x_{n}})=\left(\complement U_{x_{1}}\right)\cap\ldots\cap\left(\complement U_{x_{n}}\right)\in\mathscr{U}
and thus

\emptyset\neq\complement(U_{x_{1}}\cup\ldots\cup U_{x_{n}})
, i.e.,
M\neq U_{x_{1}}\cup\ldots\cup U_{x_{n}}
.
∎

Theorem 1.18

(Tikhonov)
.

Products of quasicompact topological spaces are quasicompact.

Proof.

Let
(N_{i})_{i\in I}
be a family of quasicompact topological spaces and

M:=\prod_{i\in I}N_{i}
the product space [
\to

1.5
(b)]. Consider for each
i\in I

the canonical projection
\pi_{i}\colon M\to N_{i}
. According to
1.17
it suffices to show that
every ultrafilter on
M
converges. For this purpose, let
\mathscr{U}
be an ultrafilter on
M
.
By
1.12
, the image filters
\pi_{i}(\mathscr{U})
(
i\in I
) are again ultrafilters and therefore converge.
Accordingly, we choose
(x_{i})_{i\in I}
satisfying
\pi_{i}(\mathscr{U})\to x_{i}
for each
i\in I
. From

1.13
, we now get
\mathscr{U}\to(x_{i})_{i\in I}
.
∎

Corollary 1.19
.

Products of compact spaces are compact.

Remark 1.20
.

Let
M
be a topological space.
\begin{Bmatrix}\text{In \ref{ultracompact}, we have shown}\\
\text{Using \ref{makeultra}, one shows as
an exercise}\end{Bmatrix}
that
M
is
\begin{Bmatrix}\text{quasicompact}\\
\text{a Hausdorff space}\end{Bmatrix}
if and only if every ultrafilter on
M

converges to
\begin{Bmatrix}\text{at least}\\
\text{at most}\end{Bmatrix}
one point of
M
. Therefrom,
M
is compact if and only if each
ultrafilter on
M
converges to exactly one point of
M
.

Reminder 1.21
.

Let
M
be a topological space and
A\subseteq M
. Then
A
is called
closed
in
M

if
\complement A
is open in
M
. We call
A

\begin{Bmatrix}\text{quasicompact}\\
\text{compact}\end{Bmatrix}
if
A
furnished with
the subspace topology [
\to

1.5
(a)] is a
\begin{Bmatrix}\text{quasicompact}\\
\text{compact}\end{Bmatrix}

topological space. Consequently,
A
is quasicompact if and only if each open cover of
A
in
M

possesses a finite subcover, i.e.,

\forall\mathscr{A}\subseteq\mathscr{O}:\left(A\subseteq\bigcup\mathscr{A}\implies\exists\mathscr{B}\subseteq\mathscr{A}:\left(\#\mathscr{B}<\infty~\mathbin{\&}~A\subseteq\bigcup\mathscr{B}\right)\right).

It follows immediately that closed subsets of
\begin{Bmatrix}\text{quasicompact}\\
\text{compact}\end{Bmatrix}
topological spaces are again

\begin{Bmatrix}\text{quasicompact}\\
\text{compact}\end{Bmatrix}
.

2
Topologies on the real spectrum

Definition 2.1
.

Let
A
be a commutative ring. We call the
topology generated by

\{\{P\in\sper A\mid\widehat{a}(P)>0\}\mid a\in A\}

on
\sper A

the
spectral topology
(or
Harrison-topology
) on
\sper A
.
Moreover, we call the topology generated by

\mathscr{C}_{A}
[
\to

6.1
] or, equivalently [
\to

6.2
], by

\{\{P\in\sper A\mid\widehat{a}(P)=0\}\mid a\in A\}\cup\{\{P\in\sper A\mid\widehat{a}(P)>0\}\mid a\in A\},

the
constructible topology
on
\sper A
. Unless otherwise indicated, we endow
\sper A
always with
the spectral topology. It is coarser than the constructible topology.

Reminder 2.2
.

Let
M
and
N
be topological spaces. A bijection
f\colon M\to N
is called a
homeomorphism

if both
f
and
f^{-1}
are continuous. One calls
M
and
N

homeomorphic
if there exists a
homeomorphism from
M
to
N
.

Theorem 2.3
.

Let
A
be a commutative ring. Then
\sper A
is compact with respect to the constructible topology.

Proof.

We endow the two-element set
\{0,1\}
with the discrete topology [
\to

1.2
(d)]. Then

\{0,1\}
is compact and so is
\{0,1\}^{A}=\prod_{i\in A}\{0,1\}
with respect to the product topology
by Tikhonov’s Theorem
1.18
. For every
B\subseteq A
, we denote by

1_{B}\colon A\to\{0,1\},\ a\mapsto\begin{cases}0&\text{if }a\notin B\\
1&\text{if }a\in B\end{cases}

the corresponding characteristic function. Consider
S:=\{1_{P}\mid P\in\sper A\}\subseteq\{0,1\}^{A}

endowed with the subspace topology of the product topology. Obviously,

\sper A\to S,\ P\mapsto 1_{P}

is a homeomorphism. Since
\{0,1\}^{A}
is compact by
1.19
,
it suffices to show that
S
is closed in
\{0,1\}^{A}
since then
S
and consequently
\sper A
is compact
[
\to

1.21
]. Encoding
1.7
in characteristic functions, we obtain

\displaystyle S=

\displaystyle\bigcap_{a,b\in A}\left\{\chi\in\{0,1\}^{A}\mid\chi(a)=0\text{ or }\chi(b)=0\text{ or }\chi(a+b)=1\right\}\cap

\displaystyle\bigcap_{a,b\in A}\left\{\chi\in\{0,1\}^{A}\mid\chi(a)=0\text{ or }\chi(b)=0\text{ or }\chi(ab)=1\right\}\cap

\displaystyle\bigcap_{a\in A}\left\{\chi\in\{0,1\}^{A}\mid\chi(a)=1\text{ or }\chi(-a)=1\right\}\cap

\displaystyle\left\{\chi\in\{0,1\}^{A}\mid\chi(-1)=0\right\}\cap

\displaystyle\bigcap_{a,b\in A}\left\{\chi\in\{0,1\}^{A}\mid\chi(ab)=0\text{ or }\chi(a)=1\text{ or }\chi(-b)=1\right\}.

Being thus an intersection of closed sets,
S
is itself closed.
∎

Corollary 2.4
.

Let
A
be a commutative ring. Then
\sper A
is quasicompact.

Proof.

Every open cover of
\sper A
is in particular an open cover with respect to the finer
constructible topology. By
2.3
, it possesses therefore a finite subcover.
∎

Reminder 2.5
.

Let
M
be a topological space and
A\subseteq M
.

\begin{Bmatrix}\text{The \emph{interior} $A^{\circ}$}\\
\text{The \emph{closure} $\overline{A}$}\end{Bmatrix}

of
A
(in
M
) is the
\begin{Bmatrix}\text{union}\\
\text{intersection}\end{Bmatrix}
over all
\begin{Bmatrix}\text{open subsets}\\
\text{closed supersets}\end{Bmatrix}
of
A
in
M
,
i.e., the
\begin{Bmatrix}\text{largest open subset}\\
\text{smallest closed superset}\end{Bmatrix}
of
A
in
M
. One shows immediately

\displaystyle A^{\circ}

\displaystyle=\{x\in M\mid\exists U\in\mathscr{U}_{x}:U\subseteq A\}\quad\text{and}

\displaystyle\overline{A}

\displaystyle=\{x\in M\mid\forall U\in\mathscr{U}_{x}:U\cap A\neq\emptyset\}.

Therefore one calls the elements
of
\begin{Bmatrix}A^{\circ}\\
\overline{A}\end{Bmatrix}
also
\begin{Bmatrix}\text{interior}\\
\text{adherent}\end{Bmatrix}
points
of
A
.
One says that
A
is
dense

in
M
if
\overline{A}=M
or, equivalently, if every nonempty open subset of
M
contains an element of
A
.

Remark 2.6
.

Let
A
be a commutative ring and let
P,Q\in\sper A
. Then

\displaystyle P\subseteq Q

\displaystyle\iff\forall a\in A:(\widehat{a}(P)\geq 0\implies\widehat{a}(Q)\geq 0)

\displaystyle\iff\forall a\in A:(\widehat{a}(Q)<0\implies\widehat{a}(P)<0)

\displaystyle\iff\forall a\in A:(\widehat{-a}(Q)<0\implies\widehat{-a}(P)<0)

\displaystyle\iff\forall a\in A:(\widehat{a}(Q)>0\implies\widehat{a}(P)>0)

\displaystyle\iff\forall U\in\mathscr{U}_{Q}:P\in U

\displaystyle\iff\forall U\in\mathscr{U}_{Q}:U\cap\{P\}\neq\emptyset

\displaystyle\iff Q\in\overline{\{P\}}.

Thus if there are
P,Q\in\sper A
with
P\subset Q
,
then
\sper A
ist not a Hausdorff space. For example,
\sper\mathbb{R}[X]
is not a Hausdorff space
[
\to

6.5
].

Remark 2.7
.

Suppose
A
and
B
are commutative rings and
\varphi\colon A\to B
is a ring homomorphism. Then

\sper\varphi\colon\sper B\to\sper A,\ Q\mapsto\varphi^{-1}(Q)

is continuous with respect to the spectral topologies on both sides as well as with respect to the constructible
topologies on both sides because for
a\in A
, we have

\displaystyle(\sper\varphi)^{-1}(\{P\in\sper A\mid\widehat{a}(P)>0\})

\displaystyle=\{Q\in\sper B\mid\widehat{a}((\sper\varphi)(Q))>0\}

\displaystyle=\{Q\in\sper B\mid\widehat{a}(\varphi^{-1}(Q))>0\}

\displaystyle=\{Q\in\sper B\mid a\in\varphi^{-1}(Q)\setminus-\varphi^{-1}(Q)\}

\displaystyle=\{Q\in\sper B\mid a\in\varphi^{-1}(Q\setminus-Q)\}

\displaystyle=\{Q\in\sper B\mid\varphi(a)\in Q\setminus-Q\}

\displaystyle=\{Q\in\sper B\mid\widehat{\varphi(a)}(Q)>0\}

and analogously

(\sper\varphi)^{-1}(\{P\in\sper A\mid\widehat{a}(P)\geq 0\})=\{Q\in\sper B\mid\widehat{\varphi(a)}(Q)\geq 0\}.

Remark 2.8
.

Let
(A,T)
be a preordered ring [
\to

4.3
]. Then

\sper(A,T)=\bigcap_{t\in T}\left\{P\in\sper A\mid\widehat{t}(P)\geq 0\right\},

as an intersection of closed sets, is again closed in
\sper A
, namely with respect to the spectral but also
with respect to the constructible topology on
\sper A
. By
1.21
,
\sper(A,T)
is thus
quasicompact with respect to the spectral and compact with respect to the constructible topology.

3
The real spectrum of polynomial rings

As in §
6
, we fix in this section an ordered field
(K,\leq)
, we denote by

R:=\overline{(K,\leq)}
its real closure, we let
n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
,
A:=K[\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.6817pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.7584pt{}}}$\hss}}}X]=K[X_{1},\dots,X_{n}]
and

T:=\sum K_{\geq 0}A^{2}
. Moreover, we denote by
\mathscr{S}:=\mathscr{S}_{n,R}
the Boolean
algebra of all
K
-semialgebraic subsets of
R^{n}
[
\to

8.3
,
9.3
] and by

\mathscr{C}:=\mathscr{C}_{(A,T)}
the Boolean algebra of all constructible subsets of
\sper(A,T)

[
\to

6.1
]. Consider again the isomorphisms of Boolean algebras

\slim\colon\mathscr{C}\to\mathscr{S},C\mapsto\{x\in R^{n}\mid P_{x}\in C\}

and
\fatten:=\slim^{-1}
[
\to

6.4
].

Theorem 3.1
.

Let
S\in\mathscr{S}
. Then
\fatten(S)
is the closure of
\{P_{x}\mid x\in S\}
in
\sper(A,T)

(or equivalently in
\sper A

[
\to

2.8
]
) with respect to the constructible topology.

Proof (simplified by Jakob Everling).

For the duration of this proof, we endow
\sper(A,T)
with the constructible topology.
Since
\complement\fatten(S)\in\mathscr{C}
is open,
\fatten(S)
is closed. Because of

S=\slim(\fatten(S))\overset{\ref{slimfatten}}{=}\{x\in R^{n}\mid P_{x}\in\fatten(S)\},

we have

\{P_{x}\mid x\in S\}\subseteq\fatten(S)
and thus

\overline{\{P_{x}\mid x\in S\}}\subseteq\fatten(S)
. In order to show
\fatten(S)\subseteq\overline{\{P_{x}\mid x\in S\}}
, we let
P\in\fatten(S)
and
U\in\mathscr{U}_{P}
.
To show:
U\cap\{P_{x}\mid x\in S\}\neq\emptyset
. WLOG
U
is open. WLOG
U\subseteq\fatten(S)

(because
\fatten(S)
is open and
P\in\fatten(S)
, one can otherwise replace
U
by

U\cap\fatten(S)\in\mathscr{U}_{P})
. Since
\slim
is an isomorphism of Boolean algebras by
6.4
, it follows from

\emptyset\neq U\subseteq\fatten(S)
that
\emptyset\neq\slim(U)\subseteq\slim(\fatten(S))=S
.
But since
\slim(U)=\{x\in R^{n}\mid P_{x}\in U\}
, this means that there is an
x\in S
such that
P_{x}\in U
.
∎

Corollary 3.2
.

\{P_{x}\mid x\in R^{n}\}
lies dense in
\sper(A,T)
with respect to the constructible topology and thus also
with respect to the spectral topology.

Lemma 3.3
.

Let
\mathscr{F}
be
\begin{Bmatrix}\text{a filter}\\
\text{an ultrafilter}\end{Bmatrix}
in
\mathscr{S}
. Then

\{\fatten(S)\mid S\in\mathscr{F}\}
is
\begin{Bmatrix}\text{a filter}\\
\text{an ultrafilter}\end{Bmatrix}
in
\mathscr{C}
and

\bigcap\{\fatten(S)\mid S\in\mathscr{F}\}
is
\begin{Bmatrix}\text{nonempty}\\
\text{a singleton}\end{Bmatrix}
.

Proof.

The first part follows immediately from the fact that
\fatten
is according to
6.4
an isomorphism
of Boolean algebras combined with the definition of
\begin{Bmatrix}\text{a filter}\\
\text{an ultrafilter}\end{Bmatrix}

1.7
.
Since
\fatten(S)
is for each
S\in\mathscr{F}
closed with respect to the constructible topology, it would
follow from

\bigcap\{\fatten(S)\mid S\in\mathscr{F}\}=\emptyset
together with the compactness of
\sper(A,T)
with
respect to the constructible topology [
\to

2.8
] that there would be

n\in\mathbb{N}
and
S_{1},\dots,S_{n}\in\mathscr{F}
such that
\fatten(S_{1})\cap\ldots\cap\fatten(S_{n})=\emptyset
, which would imply

\fatten(S_{1}\cap\ldots\cap S_{n})=\emptyset
and thus
\emptyset=S_{1}\cap\ldots\cap S_{n}\in\mathscr{F}\ \lightning
. Hence
\bigcap\{\fatten(S)\mid S\in\mathscr{F}\}\neq\emptyset
.
Finally, let
\mathscr{F}
and thus
\{\fatten(S)\mid S\in\mathscr{F}\}
be an ultrafilter
and fix
P,Q\in\bigcap\{\fatten(S)\mid S\in\mathscr{F}\}
.
Assume
P\neq Q
. Since
\sper(A,T)
is a Hausdorff space with respect to the constructible topology,
there is some
C\in\mathscr{C}
such that
P\in C
but
Q\notin C
.
Since
\{\fatten(S)\mid S\in\mathscr{F}\}
is an ultrafilter in
\mathscr{C}
, we obtain

C=\fatten(S)
or
\complement C=\fatten(S)
for some
S\in\mathscr{F}
.
In the first case, it follows that
Q\notin\fatten(S)\ \lightning
, in the second that
P\notin\fatten(S)\ \lightning
.
∎

Lemma 3.4
.

Let
\mathscr{U}
be an ultrafilter in
\mathscr{S}
. Then

P_{\mathscr{U}}:=\{f\in A\mid\{x\in R^{n}\mid f(x)\geq 0\}\in\mathscr{U}\}\in\sper(A,T)

and
\bigcap\{\fatten(S)\mid S\in\mathscr{U}\}=\{P_{\mathscr{U}}\}
.

Proof.

By Lemma
3.3
, there is some
Q\in\sper(A,T)
satisfying

\bigcap\{\fatten(S)\mid S\in\mathscr{U}\}=\{Q\}.

We show
P_{\mathscr{U}}=Q
. If
f\in P_{\mathscr{U}}
,
then
Q\in\fatten(\{x\in R^{n}\mid f(x)\geq 0\})
, i.e.,
\widehat{f}(Q)\geq 0
and
hence
f\in Q
. If on the other hand
f\in A\setminus P_{\mathscr{U}}
,
then
\{x\in R^{n}\mid f(x)<0\}\in\mathscr{U}
(since
\mathscr{U}
is an ultrafilter)
and thus
Q\in\fatten(\{x\in R^{n}\mid f(x)<0\})
, i.e.,
\widehat{f}(Q)<0
and hence

f\notin Q
.
∎

Lemma 3.5
.

Let
P\in\sper(A,T)
. Then

\displaystyle\mathscr{U}_{P}:=\{S\in\mathscr{S}\mid~

\displaystyle\exists f\in\supp P:\exists m\in\mathbb{N}:\exists g_{1},\dots,g_{m}\in P\setminus-P:

\displaystyle\{x\in R^{n}\mid f(x)=0,g_{1}(x)>0,\dots,g_{m}(x)>0\}\subseteq S\}

is an ultrafilter in
\mathscr{S}
and we have

\{S\in\mathscr{S}\mid P\in\fatten(S)\}=\mathscr{U}_{P}
.

Proof.

Since
\{C\in\mathscr{C}\mid P\in C\}
is an ultrafilter in
\mathscr{C}
and

\slim\colon\mathscr{C}\to\mathscr{S}
is an isomorphism of Boolean algebras,

\{S\in\mathscr{S}\mid P\in\fatten(S)\}
is an ultrafilter in
\mathscr{S}
.
From the description of
K
-semialgebraic subsets of
R^{n}
implied by
Theorem
8.6
, one gets that this ultrafilter equals

\displaystyle\{S\in\mathscr{S}\mid

\displaystyle\exists S^{\prime}\subseteq S:\exists f,g_{1},\dots,g_{m}\in A:

\displaystyle S^{\prime}=\{x\in R^{n}\mid f(x)=0,g_{1}(x)>0,\dots,g_{m}(x)>0\}\mathbin{\&}P\in\fatten(S^{\prime})\}=\mathscr{U}_{P}

since
\fatten
is an isomorphism of Boolean algebras.
∎

Theorem 3.6

(Bröcker’s ultrafilter theorem
[
Brö
]
)
.

The correspondence

\begin{array}[]{rcll}\mathscr{U}&\mapsto&P_{\mathscr{U}}&[\to\ref{ultra2cone}]\\
\mathscr{U}_{P}&\mapsfrom&P&[\to\ref{cone2ultra}]\end{array}

defines a bijection between the set of ultrafilters in
\mathscr{S}
and
\sper(A,T)
.

Proof.

To show: (a) If
\mathscr{U}
is an ultrafilter in
\mathscr{S}
, then

\mathscr{U}=\mathscr{U}_{P_{\mathscr{U}}}
.

(b) If
P\in\sper(A,T)
, then
P=P_{{\mathscr{U}}_{P}}
.

In order to show (a), we let
\mathscr{U}
be an ultrafilter in
\mathscr{S}
. By

3.5
, we have to show that
\{S\in\mathscr{S}\mid P_{\mathscr{U}}\in\fatten(S)\}=\mathscr{U}
. Since
\fatten
is an isomorphism of
Boolean algebras by
6.4
,
\{S\in\mathscr{S}\mid P_{\mathscr{U}}\in\fatten(S)\}
is a filter in
\mathscr{S}
. Since
\mathscr{U}
is a maximal filter in

\mathscr{S}
[
\to

1.15
], it suffices to show that

\mathscr{U}\subseteq\{S\in\mathscr{S}\mid P_{\mathscr{U}}\in\fatten(S)\}
.
To this end, let
S\in\mathscr{U}
. Then
\{P_{\mathscr{U}}\}\subseteq\fatten(S)
by
3.4
and thus
P_{\mathscr{U}}\in\fatten(S)
.

For (b), we let
P\in\sper(A,T)
. By
3.4
,

\bigcap\{\fatten(S)\mid S\in\mathscr{U}_{P}\}
consists of exactly one element, namely

P_{\mathscr{U}_{P}}
. Therefore it is enough to show
P\in\bigcap\{\fatten(S)\mid S\in\mathscr{U}_{P}\}
. Thus fix
S\in\mathscr{U}_{P}
. By

3.5
, we then obtain
P\in\fatten(S)
.
∎

Proposition 3.7
.

Every semialgebraic subset of
R^{n}

[
\to

8.3
]
is even

K
-semialgebraic.

Proof.

To begin with, we show that all one-element subsets of
R
are
K
-semialgebraic.
For this, let
a\in R
. To show:
\{a\}
is
K
-semialgebraic. Since
R|K
is algebraic,
there is
f\in K[X]\setminus\{0\}
with
f(a)=0
. Set
k:=\#\{x\in R\mid f(x)=0\}

and choose
j\in\{1,\dots,k\}
such that
a
is the
j
-th root of
f
when the
roots of
f
in
R
are arranged in increasing order with respect to the order

\leq_{R}
of
R
. By applying the real quantifier elimination
8.17

k
times, we obtain that

\{a\}=\{y\in R\mid\exists x_{1},\dots,x_{k}\in R:(x_{1}<_{R}\ldots<_{R}x_{k}\mathbin{\&}f(x_{1})=\ldots=f(x_{k})=0\mathbin{\&}x_{j}=y)\}

is
K
-semialgebraic.
Now consider an arbitrary
p\in R[\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.6817pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.7584pt{}}}$\hss}}}X]
. It suffices to show that

\{x\in R^{n}\mid p(x)\geq 0\}
is
K
-semialgebraic. Write

p=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{n}\\
|\alpha|\leq d\end{subarray}}a_{\alpha}\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.6817pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.7584pt{}}}$\hss}}}X^{\alpha}

[
\to

4.6
] with

d:=\deg p
and
a_{\alpha}\in R
. Since all
\{a_{\alpha}\}
are
K
-semialgebraic by
what has already been shown, real quantifier elimination yields that

\displaystyle\{x\in R^{n}\mid p(x)\geq 0\}=\Bigg\{x\in R^{n}\mid

\displaystyle\exists\text{ family }(y_{\alpha})_{|\alpha|\leq d}\text{ in $R$}:

\displaystyle\left(\mathop{\text{\LARGE\&}}_{|\alpha|\leq d}y_{\alpha}\in\{a_{\alpha}\}\mathbin{\&}\sum_{|\alpha|\leq d}y_{\alpha}x_{1}^{\alpha_{1}}\dotsm x_{n}^{\alpha_{n}}\geq 0\right)\Bigg\}

is
K
-semialgebraic.
∎

Theorem 3.8
.

\sper R[\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.6817pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.7584pt{}}}$\hss}}}X]\to\sper(A,T),\ P\mapsto P\cap A
is bijective.

Proof.

Because of
3.7
, we obtain from applying
the ultrafilter theorem of Bröcker twice
(once in the special case
K=R
) that

\displaystyle\sper R[\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.6817pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.7584pt{}}}$\hss}}}X]

\displaystyle\to\sper(A,T)

\displaystyle\{f\in R[\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.6817pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.7584pt{}}}$\hss}}}X]\mid\{x\in R^{n}\mid f(x)\geq 0\}\in\mathscr{U}\}

\displaystyle\mapsto\{f\in A\mid\{x\in R^{n}\mid f(x)\geq 0\}\in\mathscr{U}\}

(
\mathscr{U}
an ultrafilter in
\mathscr{S}
)

is a bijection.
∎

Corollary 3.9
.

\sper R(\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.6817pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.7584pt{}}}$\hss}}}X)\to\sper(K(\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.6817pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.7584pt{}}}$\hss}}}X),\sum K_{\geq 0}K(\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.6817pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.7584pt{}}}$\hss}}}X)^{2}),\ P\mapsto P\cap K(\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.6817pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.7584pt{}}}$\hss}}}X)
is
bijective.

Proof.

In the commutative diagram
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sper R(\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.6817pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.7584pt{}}}$\hss}}}X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sper(K(\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.6817pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.7584pt{}}}$\hss}}}X),\sum K_{\geq 0}K(\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.6817pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.7584pt{}}}$\hss}}}X)^{2})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\{P\in\sper R[\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.6817pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.7584pt{}}}$\hss}}}X]\mid\supp P=(0)\}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\{P\in\sper(A,T)\mid\supp P=(0)\}}
P\mapsto P\cap R[\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.6817pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.7584pt{}}}$\hss}}}X]
P\mapsto P\cap K(\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.6817pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.7584pt{}}}$\hss}}}X)
P\mapsto P\cap A
P\mapsto P\cap A

both vertical arrows represent bijections by Proposition

3.4
or by the very definition of the real spectrum
1.3
. It therefore suffices to show that the lower horizontal arrow
represents a bijection. Because of the bijection from
3.8
,
it therefore suffices to show that every
P\in\sper R[\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.6817pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.7584pt{}}}$\hss}}}X]
with

\supp P\neq(0)
satisfies even
\supp(P\cap A)\neq(0)
. Thus fix

P\in\sper R[\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.6817pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.7584pt{}}}$\hss}}}X]
and
f\in\mathfrak{p}:=\supp P
with
f\neq 0
. Since
K
has
characteristic
0
, there exists an extension field
L
of
K
containing
all coefficients of
f
such that
L|K
is a finite Galois extension.
If
C
denotes the algebraic closure of
R
(and therefore of
K
),
then we can of course suppose that
L
is a subfield of
C
.
By extending
the action of the Galois group
\Aut(L|K)
from
L
to
L[\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.6817pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.7584pt{}}}$\hss}}}X]
, we obtain

h:=\prod_{g\in\Aut(L|K)}gf\in A\setminus\{0\}
. Clearly,
f
divides
h
in
L[\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.6817pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.7584pt{}}}$\hss}}}X]

and therefore in
C[X]
. Translating this divisibility into a system of affine linear
equations (whose variables correspond to the coefficients of the corresponding multiplier polynomial),
we see by linear algebra that the same system must have a solution over the field
L\cap R
.
This means that
f
divides
h
in
(L\cap R)[\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.6817pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.7584pt{}}}$\hss}}}X]
and therefore in
R[\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.6817pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.7584pt{}}}$\hss}}}X]
.
Since
f\in\mathfrak{p}
and
\mathfrak{p}
is an ideal in
R[\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.6817pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.7584pt{}}}$\hss}}}X]
, we get now

h\in\mathfrak{p}\cap A=\supp(P\cap A)
.
∎

Theorem 3.10
.

Let
(L,\leq^{\prime})
be an ordered extension field of
(K,\leq)
. Then

\sper\left(L[\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.6817pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.7584pt{}}}$\hss}}}X],\sum L_{\geq^{\prime}0}L[\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.6817pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.7584pt{}}}$\hss}}}X]^{2}\right)\to\sper(A,T),\ P\mapsto P\cap A

is surjective.

Proof.

Let
\mathscr{S}^{\prime\prime}
denote the Boolean algebra of all
L
-semialgebraic subsets
of
R^{\prime n}
where
R^{\prime}:=\overline{(L,L_{\geq^{\prime}0})}
. The Boolean algebra

\mathscr{S}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathscr{S}^{\prime\prime}
of all
K
-semialgebraic subsets of
R^{\prime n}

is isomorphic to
\mathscr{S}
in virtue of the

\transfer_{R,R^{\prime}}\colon\mathscr{S}\to\mathscr{S}^{\prime}
[
\to

9.5
].
Now let
Q\in\sper(A,T)
be given. We show that there is

P\in\sper(L[\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.6817pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.7584pt{}}}$\hss}}}X],\sum L_{\geq^{\prime}0}L[\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.6817pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.7584pt{}}}$\hss}}}X]^{2})
with
Q=P\cap A
.
By
3.5
,
\mathscr{U}_{Q}
is an ultrafilter in

\mathscr{S}
. Since
\mathscr{U}_{Q}
is a filter in
\mathscr{S}
,

\mathscr{F}:=\{S^{\prime\prime}\in\mathscr{S}^{\prime\prime}\mid\exists S\in\mathscr{U}_{Q}:\transfer_{R,R^{\prime}}(S)\subseteq S^{\prime\prime}\}

is a filter in
\mathscr{S}^{\prime\prime}
. Choose by
1.16
an ultrafilter

\mathscr{U}
in
\mathscr{S}^{\prime\prime}
such that
\mathscr{F}\subseteq\mathscr{U}
.
By Bröcker’s ultrafilter theorem
3.6
, there is

P\in\sper(L[\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.6817pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.7584pt{}}}$\hss}}}X],\sum L_{\geq^{\prime}0}L[\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.6817pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.7584pt{}}}$\hss}}}X]^{2})
such that

\mathscr{U}=\mathscr{U}_{P}
. We have

\displaystyle Q\overset{\ref{broecker}}{=}P_{\mathscr{U}_{Q}}

\displaystyle=\{f\in A\mid\{x\in R^{n}\mid f(x)\geq 0\}\in\mathscr{U}_{Q}\}

\displaystyle=\{f\in A\mid\transfer_{R,R^{\prime}}(\{x\in R^{n}\mid f(x)\geq 0\})\in\{\transfer_{R,R^{\prime}}(S)\mid S\in\mathscr{U}_{Q}\}\}

\displaystyle\overset{!}{=}\{f\in A\mid\{x\in R^{\prime n}\mid f(x)\geq^{\prime\prime}0\}\in\mathscr{U}\}=P_{\mathscr{U}}\cap A=P_{\mathscr{U}_{P}}\cap A\overset{\ref{broecker}}{=}P\cap A

where
\leq^{\prime\prime}
denotes the unique order on
R^{\prime}
and
the equality flagged with an exclamation mark follows from the claim

\mathscr{U}\cap\mathscr{S}^{\prime}=\{\transfer_{R,R^{\prime}}(S)\mid S\in\mathscr{U}_{Q}\}.

The inclusion “
\supseteq
” in this claim is trivial. The other inclusion
“
\subseteq
” follows from the fact that

\{\transfer_{R,R^{\prime}}(S)\mid S\in\mathscr{U}_{Q}\}
is an ultrafilter and thus a maximal
filter in
\mathscr{S}^{\prime}
and that
\mathscr{U}\cap\mathscr{S}^{\prime}
is a filter in

\mathscr{S}^{\prime}
.
∎

4
The finiteness theorem for semialgebraic classes

In this section, we fix a real closed field
R_{0}
(in the applications, one
mostly has
R_{0}=\mathbb{R}
or
R_{0}=\mathbb{R}_{\text{alg}}
[
\to

7.12
]).
Moreover, we let
\mathscr{R}
denote the class of all real closed extension fields
of
R_{0}
[
\to

8.4
(b)] (that is the class of all real closed fields
in case
R_{0}=\mathbb{R}_{\text{alg}}
). Whoever gets vertiginous from this
[
\to

8.4
(c)] can take for
\mathscr{R}
a set of real closed extension
fields of
R_{0}
that is sufficiently large to contain all representation fields

R_{P}
of prime cones
P\in\sper R_{0}[\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.6817pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.7584pt{}}}$\hss}}}X]
[
\to

1.15
]
(which we perceive as an extension fields of
R_{0}
in virtue of the
representation
\varrho_{P}
of
P
, confer the discussion before

6.3
).

Theorem 4.1

(Finiteness theorem for semialgebraic classes)
.

Let
n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
and
\mathscr{E}
a set of
n
-ary
R_{0}
-semialgebraic
classes. Then the following are equivalent:

(a)

\bigcup\mathscr{E}=\mathscr{R}_{n}

(b)

\exists k\in\mathbb{N}:\exists S_{1},\dots,S_{k}\in\mathscr{E}:S_{1}\cup\ldots\cup S_{k}=\mathscr{R}_{n}
.

(c)

\exists k\in\mathbb{N}:\exists S_{1},\dots,S_{k}\in\mathscr{E}:\set_{R_{0}}(S_{1})\cup\ldots\cup\set_{R_{0}}(S_{k})=R_{0}^{n}

[
\to

9.3
]
.

Proof.

(b)
\iff
(c)
is clear because the setification

\set_{R_{0}}\colon\mathscr{S}_{n}\to\mathscr{S}_{n,R_{0}}
[
\to

9.3
]
and thus also the classification

\class_{R_{0}}=\set_{R_{0}}^{-1}\colon\mathscr{S}_{n,R_{0}}\to\mathscr{S}_{n}
is an
isomorphism of Boolean algebras [
\to

9.4
].

(b)
\implies
(a)
is trivial.

(a)
\implies
(b)
Suppose that (a) holds. In the proof of
6.4
, we
have shown that

\Phi\colon\mathscr{S}_{n}\to\mathscr{C}_{R_{0}[\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.6817pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.6817pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to3.55522pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to2.53944pt{}}}$\hss}}}X]},\ S\mapsto\{P\in\sper R_{0}[\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.6817pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.7584pt{}}}$\hss}}}X]\mid(R_{P},(\varrho_{P}(X_{1}),\dots,\varrho_{P}(X_{n})))\in S\}

is an isomorphism of Boolean algebras. Moreover, we have

\bigcup\{\Phi(S)\mid S\in\mathscr{E}\}=\sper R_{0}[\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.6817pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.7584pt{}}}$\hss}}}X]

by the definition of

\Phi
.
From
2.3
, we get the existence of
k\in\mathbb{N}
and

S_{1},\dots,S_{k}\in\mathscr{E}
satisfying

\Phi(S_{1})\cup\ldots\cup\Phi(S_{k})=\sper R_{0}[\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.6817pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.7584pt{}}}$\hss}}}X]
. Since

\Phi
is an isomorphism, we deduce

S_{1}\cup\ldots\cup S_{k}=\mathscr{R}_{n}
.
∎

Corollary 4.2
.

Let
n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
and
\mathscr{E}
a set of

n
-ary
R_{0}
-semialgebraic classes satisfying

\forall S_{1},S_{2}\in\mathscr{E}:\exists S_{3}\in\mathscr{E}:S_{1}\cup S_{2}\subseteq S_{3}.

Then the following
are equivalent:

(a)

\bigcup\mathscr{E}=\mathscr{R}_{n}

(b)

S=\mathscr{R}_{n}
for some
S\in\mathscr{E}

(c)

\set_{R_{0}}(S)=R_{0}^{n}
for some
S\in\mathscr{E}

Remark 4.3
.

In practice,
4.2
is mostly applied in the following context:
One has a certain true statement about real numbers (for example that

\mathbb{R}
is Archimedean [
\to

1.9
(a)]).
Now one is interested in one of the
following questions:

(a)

Does the statement hold for all real closed extension fields of
\mathbb{R}
?
(In our example: Is every real closed field extension of
\mathbb{R}
Archimedean?)

(b)

Does the statement hold in a strengthened form
(with certain quantitative additional information, so called “bounds”)
for every real closed extension of
\mathbb{R}
?
(In our example: Is there an
N\in\mathbb{N}
such that we have for all real closed field
extensions
R
of
\mathbb{R}
and all
a\in R
that
|a|\leq N
?)

(c)

Does the statement hold in the strengthened from (that is “with bounds”)
for the real numbers? (In our example: Is there some
N\in\mathbb{N}
such that for all

a\in\mathbb{R}
one has
|a|\leq N
?)

4.2
establishes under certain circumstances a connection between
these three questions. For this aim, one tries to express the statement in such a way
that for
n
numbers a certain “semialgebraic event” occurs where the event is the
existence of a bound. The set of events is
\mathscr{E}
.

Example 4.4
.

For
n:=1
,
R_{0}:=\mathbb{R}
and

\mathscr{E}:=\{\{(R,a)\in\mathscr{R}_{1}\mid-N\leq a\leq N\}\mid N\in\mathbb{N}\}
,

4.2
says that the following are equivalent:

(a)

For every real closed extension field
R
of
\mathbb{R}
and every
a\in R
, there
is some
N\in\mathbb{N}
with
|a|\leq N
, i.e., every real closed extension field
R

of
\mathbb{R}
is Archimedean.

(b)

There is some
N\in\mathbb{N}
such that for every real closed extension field
R

of
\mathbb{R}
and every
a\in R
we have
|a|\leq N
.

(c)

There is some
N\in\mathbb{N}
such that for every
a\in\mathbb{R}
we have
|a|\leq N
.

Since (c) obviously fails, we see that (a) also fails. Thus we see (once more)
that there are non-Archimedean real closed (extension) fields (of
\mathbb{R}
).

Theorem 4.5

(Existence of degree bounds for Hilbert’s 17th problem)
.

For all
n,d\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
, there is some
D\in\mathbb{N}
such that for every real closed field

R
and every
f\in R[\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.6817pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.7584pt{}}}$\hss}}}X]_{d}

[
\to

5.1
]
with
f\geq 0
on
R^{n}
,
there are

p_{1},\dots,p_{D}\in R[\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.6817pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.7584pt{}}}$\hss}}}X]_{D}
and
q\in R[\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.6817pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.7584pt{}}}$\hss}}}X]\setminus\{0\}
with

f=\sum_{i=1}^{D}\left(\frac{p_{i}}{q}\right)^{2}
.

Proof.

Let
n,d\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
. Set
N:=\dim\mathbb{R}[\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.6817pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.7584pt{}}}$\hss}}}X]_{d}
and write

\{\alpha\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{n}\mid|\alpha|\leq d\}=\{\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{N}\}
. Set
R_{0}:=\mathbb{R}_{\text{alg}}

and

S_{D}:=\left\{(R,(a_{1},\dots,a_{N}))\in\mathscr{R}_{N}~\middle|~\begin{aligned} &\left(\forall x\in R^{n}:\sum_{i=1}^{N}a_{i}x_{1}^{\alpha_{i1}}\dotsm x_{n}^{\alpha_{in}}\geq 0\right)\implies\\
&\text{There are families $(b_{i\alpha})_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq i\leq D\\
|\alpha|\leq D\end{subarray}}$
and $(c_{\alpha})_{|\alpha|\leq D}\neq 0$ in $R$}\\
&\text{such that}\\
&\left(\sum_{i=1}^{N}a_{i}\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.6817pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.7584pt{}}}$\hss}}}X^{\alpha_{i}}\right)\left(\sum_{|\alpha|\leq D}c_{\alpha}\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.6817pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.7584pt{}}}$\hss}}}X^{\alpha}\right)^{2}=\sum_{i=1}^{D}\left(\sum_{|\alpha|\leq D}b_{i\alpha}\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.6817pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.7584pt{}}}$\hss}}}X^{\alpha}\right)^{2}\end{aligned}\right\}

for each
D\in\mathbb{N}
. Obviously,
S_{D}
is for each
D\in\mathbb{N}
an

R_{0}
-semialgebraic class
since the polynomial identity in the last part of its specification can for example
be expressed by finitely many polynomial equations in the
a_{i}
,
b_{i\alpha}

and
c_{\alpha}
, the requirement on the existence of the two finite families and the
quantification “
\forall x\in R^{n}
” is allowed because of the real
quantifier elimination
8.17
. Set
\mathscr{E}:=\{S_{D}\mid D\in\mathbb{N}\}
and
observe that
\forall D_{1},D_{2}\in\mathbb{N}:\exists D_{3}\in\mathbb{N}:S_{D_{1}}\cup S_{D_{2}}\subseteq S_{D_{3}}
(take
D_{3}:=\max\{D_{1},D_{2}\}
). By Artin’s solution to Hilbert’s 17th
problem
5.2
, we have
\bigcup\mathscr{E}=\mathscr{R}_{N}
. Now

4.2
yields
S_{D}=\mathscr{R}_{N}
for some
D\in\mathbb{N}
.
∎

Remark 4.6
.

Recently, Lombardi, Perrucci and Roy
[
LPR
]

managed to prove that
one can choose in
4.5

D:=2^{2^{2^{d^{4^{n}}}}}.

We will neither use nor prove this in this lecture.

Definition and Proposition 4.7
.

Let
(K,\leq)
be an ordered extension field of
\mathbb{R}
. Then

\mathscr{O}_{(K,\leq)}:=B_{(K,K_{\geq 0})}=\{a\in K\mid\exists N\in\mathbb{N}:|a|\leq N\}

is a subring of
K

[
\to

3.1
]

with a single maximal ideal

\mathfrak{m}_{(K,\leq)}:=\left\{a\in K\mid\forall N\in\mathbb{N}:|a|\leq\frac{1}{N}\right\}

with group of units

\mathscr{O}_{(K,\leq)}^{\times}=\mathscr{O}_{(K,\leq)}\setminus\mathfrak{m}_{(K,\leq)}=\left\{a\in K\mid\exists N\in\mathbb{N}:\frac{1}{N}\leq|a|\leq N\right\}.

We call the elements of

\begin{Bmatrix}\mathscr{O}_{(K,\leq)}\\
\mathfrak{m}_{(K,\leq)}\\
K\setminus\mathscr{O}_{(K,\leq)}\end{Bmatrix}

the
\begin{Bmatrix}\text{finite}\\
\text{infinitesimal}\\
\text{infinite}\end{Bmatrix}
elements of
(K,\leq)
. For every

a\in\mathscr{O}_{(K,\leq)}
, there is exactly one
\st(a)\in\mathbb{R}
, called
the
standard part
of
a
, such that

a-\st(a)\in\mathfrak{m}_{(K,\leq)}.

The map
\mathscr{O}_{(K,\leq)}\to\mathbb{R},\ a\mapsto\st(a)
is a ring homomorphism with
kernel
\mathfrak{m}_{(K,\leq)}
. If
a,b\in\mathscr{O}_{(K,\leq)}
satisfy
\st(a)<\st(b)
, then

a<b
. The standard part
\st(p)
of a polynomial
p\in\mathscr{O}_{(K,\leq)}[\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.6817pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.7584pt{}}}$\hss}}}X]
arises
by replacing each coefficient of
p
by its standard part. Also

\mathscr{O}_{(K,\leq)}[\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.6817pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.7584pt{}}}$\hss}}}X]\to\mathbb{R}[\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.6817pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.7584pt{}}}$\hss}}}X],\ p\mapsto\st(p)
is a ring homomorphism.

Proof.

The existence of the standard part follows easily from the completeness of
\mathbb{R}

[
\to

1.16
] and its uniqueness is trivial.
The rest is also easy. We show exemplarily:

(a)

\st(ab)=(\st(a))(\st(b))
for all
a,b\in\mathscr{O}_{(K,\leq)}

(b)

\st(a)<\st(b)\implies a<b
for all
a,b\in\mathscr{O}_{(K,\leq)}

To show (a), let
a,b\in\mathscr{O}_{(K,\leq)}
. Because of

a-\st(a),b-\st(b)\in\mathfrak{m}_{(K,\leq)}
, we have

\displaystyle ab-(\st(a))(\st(b))

\displaystyle=(ab-(\st(a))b)+((\st(a))b-(\st(a))(\st(b)))

\displaystyle=(a-\st(a))b+(\st(a))(b-\st(b))\in\mathfrak{m}_{(K,\leq)}+\mathfrak{m}_{(K,\leq)}\subseteq\mathfrak{m}_{(K,\leq)}

For (b), we fix again
a,b\in\mathscr{O}_{(K,\leq)}
with
\st(a)<\st(b)
. Choose

N\in\mathbb{N}
with

\st(b)-\st(a)>\frac{1}{N}.

Then
|a-\st(a)|\leq\frac{1}{2N}
and
|b-\st(b)|\leq\frac{1}{2N}
and thus

\displaystyle a

\displaystyle=a-\st(a)+\st(a)\leq|a-\st(a)|+\st(a)\leq\frac{1}{2N}+\st(a)-\st(b)+\st(b)

\displaystyle<\frac{1}{2N}-\frac{1}{N}+\st(b)=-\frac{1}{2N}+\st(b)-b+b

\displaystyle\leq-\frac{1}{2N}+|b-\st(b)|+b\leq-\frac{1}{2N}+\frac{1}{2N}+b=b

∎

Example 4.8

(Nonexistence of degree bounds for Schmüdgen’s
Positivstellensatz [
\to

3.6
])
.

For every
\varepsilon\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
, we have
X+\varepsilon>0
on
[0,1]
so that
Schmüdgen’s Positivstellensatz

3.6
together with
1.1
(b) yields

p_{1},p_{2},q_{1},q_{2}\in\mathbb{R}[X]
such that

(*)\qquad X+\varepsilon=p_{1}^{2}+p_{2}^{2}+(q_{1}^{2}+q_{2}^{2})X^{3}(1-X).

One can ask the question
if there is in analogy to
4.5
a
D\in\mathbb{N}
such that
for all
\varepsilon\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
there are
p_{1},p_{2},q_{1},q_{2}\in\mathbb{R}[X]_{D}
satisfying

(*)
. To this end, consider for each
D\in\mathbb{N}

S_{D}:=\left\{(R,\varepsilon)\in\mathscr{R}_{1}~\middle|~\begin{aligned} &\varepsilon>0\implies\exists b_{0},\dots,b_{D},b_{0}^{\prime},\dots,b_{D}^{\prime},c_{0},\dots,c_{D},c_{0}^{\prime},\dots,c_{D}^{\prime}\in R:\\
&X+\varepsilon=\left(\sum_{i=0}^{D}b_{i}X^{i}\right)^{2}+\left(\sum_{i=0}^{D}b_{i}^{\prime}X^{i}\right)^{2}+\\
&\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\left(\left(\sum_{i=0}^{D}c_{i}X^{i}\right)^{2}+\left(\sum_{i=0}^{D}c_{i}^{\prime}X^{i}\right)^{2}\right)X^{3}(1-X)\end{aligned}\right\}

As in the proof of
4.5
, one shows that
S_{D}
is for each
D\in\mathbb{N}

an
\mathbb{R}
-semialgebraic class. Set
\mathscr{E}:=\{S_{D}\mid D\in\mathbb{N}\}
. We claim
that the answer to the above question is no. Assume it would be yes.
Then
\set_{\mathbb{R}}(S_{D})=\mathbb{R}
for some
D\in\mathbb{N}
and thus

\bigcup\mathscr{E}=\mathscr{R}_{1}
by
4.2
.
Choose a non-Archimedean real closed extension field
R
of
\mathbb{R}
and
an
\varepsilon>0
which is infinitesimal in
R
. Then there are

p_{1},p_{2},q_{1},q_{2}\in R[X]
satisfying
(*)
. It suffices to show that all
coefficients of these four polynomials are finite in
R
[
\to

4.7
] since
then

X=\st(X+\varepsilon)=(\st(p_{1}))^{2}+(\st(p_{2}))^{2}+((\st(q_{1}))^{2}+(\st(q_{2}))^{2})X^{3}(1-X)

in contradiction to
3.7
. It therefore suffices to show that
the coefficient
c
of biggest absolute value among all coefficients
of the four polynomials is finite. Assume it were infinite. Then
\frac{1}{c}
would
be infinitesimal and

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=\st\left(\frac{X+\varepsilon}{c^{2}}\right)=\st\left(\left(\frac{p_{1}}{c}\right)^{2}+\left(\frac{p_{2}}{c}\right)^{2}+\left(\left(\frac{q_{1}}{c}\right)^{2}+\left(\frac{q_{2}}{c}\right)^{2}\right)X^{3}(1-X)\right)

\displaystyle=\Big(\underbrace{\st\left(\frac{p_{1}}{c}\right)}_{\widetilde{p}_{1}}\Big)^{2}+\Big(\underbrace{\st\left(\frac{p_{2}}{c}\right)}_{\widetilde{p}_{2}}\Big)^{2}+\Big(\Big(\underbrace{\st\left(\frac{q_{1}}{c}\right)}_{\widetilde{q}_{1}}\Big)^{2}+\Big(\underbrace{\st\left(\frac{q_{2}}{c}\right)}_{\widetilde{q}_{2}}\Big)^{2}\Big)X^{3}(1-X).

It follows that
\widetilde{p}_{1}=\widetilde{p}_{2}=\widetilde{q}_{1}=\widetilde{q}_{2}=0

on
(0,1)
and thus
\widetilde{p}_{1}=\widetilde{p}_{2}=\widetilde{q}_{1}=\widetilde{q}_{2}=0
, contradicting the choice of
c

\lightning
.

Remark 4.9
.

Completely analogous to
4.5
, one can prove the existence
of degree bounds for the real Stellensätze
7.5
,
7.6

and
7.7

in the case
K=R
.

Chapter 6
Semialgebraic geometry

Throughout this chapter, we let
R
be a real closed field and
K
a subfield of

R
. Moreover,
\mathscr{S}_{n}
denotes for each
n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
the Boolean algebra
of all
K
-semialgebraic subsets of
R^{n}
[
\to

9.3
,
8.3
].

1
Semialgebraic sets and functions

Reminder 1.1
.

[
\to

8.6
,
8.4
(a)]
Every
K
-semialgebraic
subset of
R^{n}
is of the form

\bigcup_{i=1}^{k}\left\{x\in R^{n}\mid f_{i}(x)=0,g_{i1}(x)>0,\dots,g_{im}(x)>0\right\}

for some
k,m\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
,
f_{i},g_{ij}\in K[X_{1},\dots,X_{n}]
.

Reminder 1.2
.

[
\to

8.17
]
For all
n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
and
S\in\mathscr{S}_{n+1}
,

\{x\in R^{n}\mid\exists y\in R:(x,y)\in S\},\{x\in R^{n}\mid\forall y\in R:(x,y)\in S\}\in\mathscr{S}_{n}.

Definition 1.3
.

Let
m,n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
and
A\subseteq R^{m}
. A map
f\colon A\to R^{n}

is called
K
-semialgebraic
if its graph

\Gamma_{f}:=\{(x,y)\in A\times R^{n}\mid y=f(x)\}\subseteq R^{m+n}

is
K
-semialgebraic. We
say “semialgebraic” for “
R
-semialgebraic”.

Remark 1.4
.

The domains of
K
-semialgebraic functions are

K
-semialgebraic. Indeed, if
A\subseteq R^{m}
and
f\colon A\to R^{n}

is
K
-semialgebraic, then by
1.2
also

\{x\in R^{m}\mid\exists y\in R^{n}:(x,y)\in\Gamma_{f}\}=A

is
K
-semialgebraic.

Definition 1.5
.

We equip
R
with the
order topology
which is generated
[
\to

1.2
(b)] by the intervals
(a,b)_{R}
with
a,b\in R

[
\to

4.15
(b)]. Moreover, we endow
R^{n}
with the corresponding
product topology [
\to

1.5
(b)] which is generated
according to
1.4
by the sets
\prod_{i=1}^{n}(a_{i},b_{i})_{R}
with

a_{i},b_{i}\in R
.

Remark 1.6
.

For
R=\mathbb{R}
, the topology introduced in
1.4
on
R^{n}=\mathbb{R}^{n}
is obviously
the usual Euclidean topology on
\mathbb{R}^{n}
.

Exercise 1.7
.

Let
m,n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
,
A\subseteq R^{m}
and
f\colon A\to R^{n}
a map.
Then
f
is continuous [
\to

1.3
,
1.5
(a)] if and only
if

\forall x\in A:\forall\varepsilon\in R_{>0}:\exists\delta\in R_{>0}:\forall y\in A:(\|x-y\|_{\infty}<\delta\implies\|f(x)-f(y)\|_{\infty}<\varepsilon)

where

\|x\|_{\infty}:=\begin{cases}0&\text{if $k=0$}\\
\max\{|x_{1}|,\dots,|x_{k}|\}&\text{if $k>0$}\end{cases}

for
x\in R^{k}
.

Proposition 1.8
.

The maps

\displaystyle R^{2}\to R,\

\displaystyle(a,b)\mapsto a+b,

\displaystyle R^{2}\to R,\

\displaystyle(a,b)\mapsto ab,

\displaystyle R\setminus\{0\}\to R,\

\displaystyle a\mapsto a^{-1},

\displaystyle R\to R,\

\displaystyle a\mapsto|a|\qquad\emph{\text{[$\to$ \ref{introabssgn}]}},

\displaystyle R_{\geq 0}\to R,\

\displaystyle a\mapsto\sqrt{a}\qquad\emph{\text{[$\to$ \ref{notremsqrt}]}}

are
\mathbb{Q}
-semialgebraic and continuous.

Proof.

It is clear that these maps are
\mathbb{Q}
-semialgebraic. Because of the real
quantifier elimination
8.17
, the class of all real closed fields for which the
claim holds is semialgebraic [
\to

8.3
]. Since the claim is known to
hold for
R=\mathbb{R}
, it holds also for all real closed fields
[
\to

8.5
].
∎

Corollary 1.9
.

Polynomial maps
R^{m}\to R^{n}
are continuous.

Corollary 1.10
.

R^{n}\to R,\ x\mapsto\|x\|:=\|x\|_{2}:=\sqrt{x_{1}^{2}+\ldots+x_{n}^{2}}
is continuous.

Remark 1.11
.

Because of
1.10
and
1.7
, there is to every

\varepsilon\in R_{>0}
some
\delta\in R_{>0}
such that

\forall x\in R^{n}:(\|x\|_{\infty}<\delta\implies\|x\|<\varepsilon)
.
On the other hand,
\|x\|_{\infty}\leq\|x\|
for all
x\in R^{n}
.
It follows that the topology on
R^{n}
is also generated by the open balls

\{x\in R^{n}\mid\|x-y\|<\varepsilon\}
(
y\in R^{n},\varepsilon>0
) and that
1.7

holds also with
\|.\|
instead of
\|.\|_{\infty}
.

Remark 1.12
.

(a)

By
1.9
,
R^{n}
is obviously endowed with the
initial topology with respect to all maps
R^{n}\to R,\ x\mapsto p(x)

(p\in R[\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.6817pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.7584pt{}}}$\hss}}}X]
) [
\to

1.4
].

(b)

Because of (a), the topology on
R^{n}
is obviously generated by the sets

\{x\in R^{n}\mid p(x)>0\}\qquad(p\in R[\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.6817pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.7584pt{}}}$\hss}}}X]).

(c)

Viewing
R^{n}
in virtue of the injective map

R^{n}\to\sper R[\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.6817pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.7584pt{}}}$\hss}}}X],\ x\mapsto P_{x}=\{f\in R[\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.6817pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.7584pt{}}}$\hss}}}X]\mid f(x)\geq 0\}

as a subset of
\sper R[\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.6817pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.7584pt{}}}$\hss}}}X]
, the topology on
R^{n}
is due to (b) induced
by the spectral topology [
\to

2.1
] on
\sper R[\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.6817pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.7584pt{}}}$\hss}}}X]
.

Theorem 1.13
.

(a)

If
A\subseteq R^{m}
and
f\colon A\to R^{n}
is
K
-semialgebraic, then

f(B)\in\mathscr{S}_{n}
for all
B\in\mathscr{S}_{m}
with
B\subseteq A

and
f^{-1}(C)\in\mathscr{S}_{m}
for all
C\in\mathscr{S}_{n}
.

(b)

If
A\subseteq R^{\ell}
,
B\subseteq R^{m}
,
f\colon A\to B
and

g\colon B\to R^{n}
are
K
-semialgebraic, then
g\circ f\colon A\to R^{n}

is again
K
-semialgebraic.

(c)

If
A\in\mathscr{S}_{n}
, then the
K
-semialgebraic functions

A\to R
form a subring of the ring
R^{A}
of all functions
A\to R
.

Proof.

(a)

Let
A\subseteq R^{m}
and
f\colon A\to R^{n}
be
K
-semialgebraic. By

1.2
, with
\Gamma_{f}
also

f(B)=\{y\in R^{n}\mid\exists x\in R^{m}:(x\in B\mathbin{\&}(x,y)\in\Gamma_{f})\}
is
for all
B\in\mathscr{S}_{m}
with

B\subseteq A

K
-semialgebraic, and

f^{-1}(C)=\{x\in R^{m}\mid\exists y\in R^{n}:(y\in C\mathbin{\&}(x,y)\in\Gamma_{f})\}
is for all

C\in\mathscr{S}_{n}
also
K
-semialgebraic.

(b)

Suppose
A\subseteq R^{\ell}
,
B\subseteq R^{m}
and
f\colon A\to B

as well as
g\colon B\to R^{n}
are
K
-semialgebraic.
Then
\Gamma_{f}\in\mathscr{S}_{\ell+m}
and
\Gamma_{g}\in\mathscr{S}_{m+n}
and thus

\Gamma_{g\circ f}=\{(x,z)\in A\times R^{n}\mid\exists y\in R^{m}:((x,y)\in\Gamma_{f}\mathbin{\&}(y,z)\in\Gamma_{g})\}\in\mathscr{S}_{\ell+n}.

Hence
g\circ f
is
K
-semialgebraic.

(c)

If
A\in\mathscr{S}_{n}
and
f_{1},f_{2}\colon A\to R
are
K
-semialgebraic,
then also

A\to R^{2},\ x\mapsto(f_{1}(x),f_{2}(x))

is
K
-semialgebraic. Now apply

1.8
and (b).

∎

Example 1.14
.

If
R
is a non-Archimedean (real closed) extension of
\mathbb{R}
,
then
[0,1]_{R}
is not compact
[
\to

1.14
]. Indeed, if
\varepsilon\in\mathfrak{m}_{R}
[
\to

4.7
]
with
\varepsilon>0
, then

[0,1]_{R}\subseteq\bigcup_{a\in[0,1]_{R}}(a-\varepsilon,a+\varepsilon)_{R},

but there is no
N\in\mathbb{N}
and
a_{1},\dots,a_{N}\in[0,1]_{R}
with

[0,1]_{R}\subseteq\bigcup_{k=1}^{N}(a_{k}-\varepsilon,a_{k}+\varepsilon)_{R}

(for otherwise
[0,1]_{\mathbb{R}}=\st([0,1]_{R})\subseteq\{\st(a_{1}),\dots,\st(a_{N})\}\ \lightning
).

Definition 1.15
.

Let
A\subseteq R^{n}
. We call
A

bounded
if there is

b\in R
with
\|x\|\leq b
for all
x\in A
[
\to

1.10
]. Moreover,
A
is called

K
-semialgebraically compact
if
A\in\mathscr{S}_{n}
and
A

is bounded and closed. We simply say “semialgebraically compact” instead
of “
R
-semialgebraically compact”.

Remark 1.16
.

From analysis, one knows for
R=\mathbb{R}
: A
K
-semialgebraic set

A\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}
is compact if and only if it is
K
-semialgebraically compact.

Proposition 1.17
.

Let
A\in\mathscr{S}_{n}
. Then the following are equivalent:

(a)

A
is bounded.

(b)

\exists b\in R:\forall x\in A:\|x\|\leq b

(c)

\exists b\in R:\forall x\in A:\|x\|_{\infty}\leq b

(d)

\exists b\in K:\forall x\in A:\|x\|\leq b

(e)

\exists b\in K:\forall x\in A:\|x\|_{\infty}\leq b

Proof.

WLOG
A\neq\emptyset
. We have
(a)
\overset{\ref{dfsacompact}}{\iff}
(b)
\overset{\ref{inf22}}{\iff}
(c)
\Longleftarrow
(e)
\Longleftarrow
(d).
It remains to show (b)
\implies
(d). Suppose therefore that (b) holds.
The set

S:=\{\|x\|\mid x\in A\}\subseteq R_{\geq 0}

is
K
-semialgebraic [
\to

1.2
].
Hence
S
can be defined by finitely many polynomials [
\to

8.6
] with coefficients in
K
and
by Lemma
5.3
(a) we find some
b\in K_{>1}
such that each of these polynomials
has constant sign on the interval
(b,\infty)_{R}
. Then either
(b,\infty)_{R}\cap S=\emptyset
or

(b,\infty)_{R}\subseteq S
. But the latter is impossible due to (b). Hence
\forall x\in A:\|x\|\leq b
.
∎

Theorem 1.18
.

Let
A\subseteq R^{m}
and suppose
f\colon A\to R^{n}
is

K
-semialgebraic and continuous. Then for every
K
-semialgebraically compact
set
B\subseteq A
, the set
f(B)
is also
K
-semialgebraically compact.

Proof.

If
B\in\mathscr{S}_{m}
with
B\subseteq A
, then
f(B)\in\mathscr{S}_{n}
by

1.13
(a) since
f
is
K
-semialgebraic. For the rest of the claim we
can suppose that
K=R
. We fix a “complexity bound”
N\in\mathbb{N}
and fix

m,n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
but no longer fix
A
and
f
. By
1.1
, it suffices to show
the following:

(*)
For all
f_{1},\dots,f_{N},g_{11},g_{12},\dots,g_{NN}\in R[X_{1},\dots,X_{m},Y_{1},\dots,Y_{n}]_{N}
and

\widetilde{f}_{1},\dots,\widetilde{f}_{N},\widetilde{g}_{11},\widetilde{g}_{12},\dots,\widetilde{g}_{NN}\in R[X_{1},\dots,X_{m}]_{N}
, if we set

\displaystyle\Gamma

\displaystyle:=\bigcup_{i=1}^{N}\{(x,y)\in R^{m}\times R^{n}\mid f_{i}(x,y)=0,g_{i1}(x,y)>0,\dots,g_{iN}(x,y)>0\},

\displaystyle A

\displaystyle:=\{x\in R^{m}\mid\exists y\in R^{n}:(x,y)\in\Gamma\}\text{ and}

\displaystyle B

\displaystyle:=\bigcup_{i=1}^{N}\{x\in R^{m}\mid\widetilde{f}_{i}(x)=0,\widetilde{g}_{i1}(x)>0,\ldots,\widetilde{g}_{iN}(x)>0\},

then

•

\Gamma
is not the graph of a continuous function from
A
to
R^{n}
or

•

B
is not a subset of
A
or

•

B
is not closed in
R^{m}
or

•

B
is not bounded in
R^{m}
or

•

\{y\in R^{n}\mid\exists x\in R^{m}:(x\in B\mathbin{\&}(x,y)\in\Gamma)\}
is closed and
bounded in
R^{n}
.

We now in addition no longer fix
R
. One can easily figure out why the class
of all real closed fields
R
for which
(*)
holds is semialgebraic. For this aim,
one applies many times the real quantifier elimination
8.17
, for example for
introducing the finitely many coefficients of the
f_{i},g_{ij},\widetilde{f}_{i},\widetilde{g}_{ij}
by universal quantifiers. By
8.5
,
(*)
now holds
either for all or for no real closed field
R
. Therefore it is enough to show

(*)
for
R=\mathbb{R}
. But we know this from analysis due to
1.16
.
∎

Exercise 1.19
.

(a)

The
\begin{Bmatrix}\text{open}\\
\text{closed}\end{Bmatrix}
semialgebraic subsets of
R
are exactly the
finite unions of pairwise disjoint sets of the form

\begin{Bmatrix}(-\infty,\infty)_{R},(-\infty,a)_{R},(a,\infty)_{R}\text{ and }(a,b)_{R}\\
(-\infty,\infty)_{R},(-\infty,a]_{R},[a,\infty)_{R}\text{ and }[a,b]_{R}\end{Bmatrix}

with
a,b\in R
.

(b)

The semialgebraically compact
subsets of
R
are exactly the finite unions of pairwise disjoint
sets of the form
[a,b]_{R}
with
a,b\in R
.

2
The Łojasiewicz inequality

Proposition 2.1
.

Let
a\in K
and suppose

h\colon(a,\infty)_{R}\to R
is
K
-semialgebraic. Then there is

b\in K\cap[a,\infty)_{R}
and
N\in\mathbb{N}
such that

|h(x)|\leq x^{N}
for all
x\in(b,\infty)_{R}
.

Proof.

Using
1.1
, we write

\Gamma_{h}=\bigcup_{i=1}^{k}\{(x,y)\in R^{2}\mid f_{i}(x,y)=0,g_{i1}(x,y)>0,\ldots,g_{im}(x,y)>0\}

with
k,m\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
and
f_{i},g_{ij}\in K[X,Y]
where we suppose each of the
k

sets contributing to this union to be nonempty. We must have
k>0
and

\deg_{Y}f_{i}>0
for all
i\in\{1,\dots,k\}

(for otherwise there would be
x,c,d\in R
with
c<d
and
\{x\}\times(c,d)_{R}\subseteq\Gamma_{h}
which is impossible since
\Gamma_{h}
is the graph of a
function). Write
\prod_{i=1}^{k}f_{i}=\sum_{i=0}^{d}p_{i}Y^{i}
with

d>0
,
p_{0},\ldots,p_{d}\in K[X]
and
p_{d}\neq 0
.
By rescaling one of the
f_{i}
if necessary, we can suppose that the leading coefficient of
p_{d}
is
greater than
1
. Choose
c\in K\cap[a,\infty)_{R}
such
that
p_{d}>1
on
(c,\infty)_{R}
[
\to

5.3
(a)]. Because of

\sum_{i=0}^{d}p_{i}(x)h(x)^{i}=0
and
p_{d}(x)\neq 0
for all
x\in(c,\infty)_{R}
, we
have

|h(x)|\leq\max\left\{1,\frac{|p_{0}(x)|+\ldots+|p_{d-1}(x)|}{|p_{d}(x)|}\right\}\leq 1+|p_{0}(x)|+\ldots+|p_{d-1}(x)|

for all
x\in(c,\infty)_{R}
[
\to

5.3
(a)].
Now the existence of
b
is easy to see.
∎

Theorem 2.2

(Łojasiewicz inequality)
.

Let
n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
and
suppose
A\subseteq R^{n}
is
K
-semialgebraically compact
and
f,g\colon A\to R
are continuous
K
-semialgebraic functions satisfying

\forall x\in A:(f(x)=0\implies g(x)=0).

Then there is
N\in\mathbb{N}
and
C\in K_{\geq 0}

such that

\forall x\in A:|g(x)|^{N}\leq C|f(x)|.

Proof.

With
A
also
A_{t}:=\{x\in A\mid|g(x)|=\frac{1}{t}\}
is
K
-semialgebraically compact
for each
t\in R_{>0}
. Set
I:=\{t\in R_{>0}\mid A_{t}\neq\emptyset\}
. For each

t\in I
,

f_{t}:=\min\{|f(x)|\mid x\in A_{t}\}

exists by
1.18
and
1.19
(b).
Apparently, we have to show that there exist

N\in\mathbb{N}
and
C\in K_{\geq 0}
such that

\forall t\in I:\left(\frac{1}{t}\right)^{N}\leq Cf_{t}
.
By hypothesis, we have
f_{t}>0
for all
t\in I
. Furthermore,

R_{>0}\to R,\ t\mapsto\begin{cases}0&\text{if $t\notin I$}\\
\frac{1}{f_{t}}&\text{if $t\in I$}\end{cases}

is
K
-semialgebraic. Thus, by
2.1
there are
b\in K_{>0}
and

N\in\mathbb{N}
such that

(*)\qquad\frac{1}{f_{t}}\leq t^{N}

for all
t\in I\cap(b,\infty)_{R}
. Since

B:=\left\{x\in A\mid|g(x)|\geq\frac{1}{b}\right\}=\bigcup_{t\in I\cap(0,b]_{R}}A_{t}

is
K
-semialgebraically compact, we can choose according to
1.18
and
1.17

some
C\in K_{\geq 1}
satisfying

\frac{|g(x)|^{N}}{|f(x)|}\leq C

for all
x\in B
(note that
f(x)\neq 0
for all
x\in B
).
We deduce

(**)\qquad\frac{1}{f_{t}}\leq Ct^{N}

for all
t\in I\cap(0,b]_{R}
. Together with
(*)
, we obtain
(**)
even
for all
t\in I
as desired.
∎

Lemma 2.3
.

(“shrinking map”, in German: “Schränkungstranformation”)

Let
n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
,

B:=\{x\in R^{n}\mid\|x\|<1\}
and
S:=\{x\in R^{n}\mid\|x\|=1\}
.
The maps

\displaystyle\varphi\colon R^{n}\to B,\

\displaystyle x\mapsto\frac{x}{\sqrt{1+\|x\|^{2}}}\qquad\text{and}

\displaystyle\psi\colon B\to R^{n},\

\displaystyle y\mapsto\frac{y}{\sqrt{1-\|y\|^{2}}}

are
\mathbb{Q}
-semialgebraic, continuous and inverse to each other. For all

A\in\mathscr{S}_{n}
, we have

\text{$A$ closed}\iff\text{$\varphi(A)\cup S$ is $K$-semialgebraically compact.}

Proof.

From
1.8
, the
\mathbb{Q}
-semialgebraicity and the continuity are clear.
For all
x\in R^{n}
, we have

\psi(\varphi(x))=\frac{\frac{x}{\sqrt{1+\|x\|^{2}}}}{\sqrt{1-\frac{\|x\|^{2}}{1+\|x\|^{2}}}}=\frac{\frac{x}{\sqrt{1+\|x\|^{2}}}}{\frac{1}{\sqrt{1+\|x\|^{2}}}}=x.

For all
y\in B
, we have

\varphi(\psi(y))=\frac{\frac{y}{\sqrt{1-\|y\|^{2}}}}{\sqrt{1+\frac{\|y\|^{2}}{1-\|y\|^{2}}}}=\frac{\frac{y}{\sqrt{1-\|y\|^{2}}}}{\sqrt{\frac{1}{1-\|y\|^{2}}}}=y.

Now let
A\in\mathscr{S}_{n}
. To show:

A
closed
\iff

\varphi(A)\cup S
closed.

“
\Longleftarrow
” Suppose
\varphi(A)\cup S
is closed. Then

\varphi(A)=(\varphi(A)\cup S)\cap B
is closed in
B
(with respect to the
topology induced from
R^{n}
) and thus also
A=\varphi^{-1}(\varphi(A))
in
R^{n}
.

“
\Longrightarrow
” Let
A
be closed. Then
\varphi(A)=\psi^{-1}(A)
is closed in
B

and hence
\varphi(A)=C\cap B
for some closed set
C\subseteq R^{n}
.
WLOG
C\subseteq B\cup S
(otherwise replace
C
by
C\cap(B\cup S)
).
WLOG
S\subseteq C
(otherwise replace
C
by
C\cup S
).
Now
\varphi(A)\cup S\subseteq C\subseteq(C\cap B)\cup(C\cap S)=\varphi(A)\cup S
. Hence
\varphi(A)\cup S=C
is closed.
∎

Corollary 2.4
.

Let
n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
and suppose that
A\subseteq R^{n}
is
closed and

f,g\colon A\to R
are continuous
K
-semialgebraic functions satisfying

\forall x\in A:(f(x)=0\implies g(x)=0).

Then there are
N,k\in\mathbb{N}
and

C\in K_{\geq 0}
such that

\forall x\in A:|g(x)|^{N}\leq C(1+\|x\|^{2})^{k}|f(x)|.

Proof.

By
1.4
,
A
is
K
-semialgebraic.
If
A
is bounded, then
A
is
K
-semialgebraically compact and
the claim follows (with
k:=1
) from the
Łojasiewicz inequality
2.2
. Now suppose that
A
is unbounded.
Since
\{\|x\|\mid x\in A\}\subseteq R
is
K
-semialgebraic, there is then some

a\in K
such that
(a,\infty)_{R}\subseteq\{\|x\|\mid x\in A\}
. The functions

\displaystyle\mathring{f}\colon(a,\infty)_{R}\to R,\

\displaystyle t\mapsto\max\{|f(x)|\mid x\in A,\|x\|=t\}\qquad\text{and}

\displaystyle\mathring{g}\colon(a,\infty)_{R}\to R,\

\displaystyle t\mapsto\max\{|g(x)|\mid x\in A,\|x\|=t\}

are semialgebraic. By
2.1
, there are
b\in K\cap[a,\infty)_{R}
with
b\geq 1

and
\ell\in\mathbb{N}
such that
\mathring{f}(t)\leq(1+t^{2})^{\ell}
and
\mathring{g}(t)\leq(1+t^{2})^{\ell}
for all
t\in(b,\infty)_{R}\subseteq R_{\geq 1}
. Now consider the continuous
K
-semialgebraic functions

f_{0}\colon A\to R,\ x\mapsto\frac{f(x)}{(1+\|x\|^{2})^{\ell+1}}\qquad\text{and}\qquad g_{0}\colon A\to R,\ x\mapsto\frac{g(x)}{(1+\|x\|^{2})^{\ell+1}}.

We have
\forall x\in A:(f_{0}(x)=0\implies g_{0}(x)=0)
and obviously it is enough
to show that there are
N\in\mathbb{N}
and
C\in K_{\geq 0}
such that

\forall x\in A:|g_{0}(x)|^{N}\leq C|f_{0}(x)|
(set then

k:=\max\{1,(N-1)(\ell+1)\}
). The advantage of
f_{0}
and
g_{0}
over
f
and
g

is that there is for all
\varepsilon\in R_{>0}
a semialgebraically compact set

B\subseteq A
such that
|f_{0}(x)|<\varepsilon
and
|g_{0}(x)|<\varepsilon
for all

x\in A\setminus B
. With the notation of Lemma
2.3
, the
K
-semialgebraic
functions

\displaystyle\widetilde{f}\colon\varphi(A)\cup S\to R,\

\displaystyle y\mapsto\begin{cases}0&\text{if $y\in S$}\\
f_{0}(\psi(y))&\text{if $y\in\varphi(A)$}\end{cases}\qquad\text{and}

\displaystyle\widetilde{g}\colon\varphi(A)\cup S\to R,\

\displaystyle y\mapsto\begin{cases}0&\text{if $y\in S$}\\
g_{0}(\psi(y))&\text{if $y\in\varphi(A)$}\end{cases}

are continuous.
For example, for
\widetilde{f}
one sees this as follows:
Since
f_{0}\circ\psi|_{\varphi(A)}
is continuous and
\varphi(A)=(\varphi(A)\cup S)\cap B

is open in
\varphi(A)\cup S
, it suffices to show by
1.7
and

1.11
that

\forall y_{0}\in S:\forall\varepsilon\in R_{>0}:\exists\delta\in R_{>0}:\forall y\in\varphi(A):(\|y_{0}-y\|<\delta\implies|f_{0}(\psi(y))|<\varepsilon).

To this end, let
y_{0}\in S
and
\varepsilon\in R_{>0}
. Choose a semialgebraically
compact set
B\subseteq A
with
|f_{0}(x)|<\varepsilon
for all
x\in A\setminus B
.
Then
\varphi(B)
is semialgebraically compact by
1.18
and
consequently
S\cup\varphi(A\setminus B)=(S\cup\varphi(A))\setminus\varphi(B)

is open in
\varphi(A)\cup S
. Thus there is
\delta\in R_{>0}
with

\{y\in\varphi(A)\cup S\mid\|y_{0}-y\|<\delta\}\subseteq S\cup\varphi(A\setminus B)
, i.e.,

\{y\in\varphi(A)\mid\|y_{0}-y\|<\delta\}\subseteq\varphi(A\setminus B).

Now let

y\in\varphi(A)
with
\|y_{0}-y\|<\delta
. Then
y\in\varphi(A\setminus B)
and
thus
\psi(y)\in A\setminus B
. Hence
|f_{0}(\psi(y))|<\varepsilon
. This shows the
continuity of
\widetilde{f}
. For all
y\in\varphi(A)
, we have obviously

\widetilde{f}(y)=0\implies f_{0}(\psi(y))=0\implies g_{0}(\psi(y))=0\implies\widetilde{g}(y)=0.

Altogether,
\forall y\in\varphi(A)\cup S:(\widetilde{f}(y)=0\implies\widetilde{g}(y)=0)
.
Since
\varphi(A)\cup S
is
K
-semialgebraically compact by
2.3
,
we get from the Łojasiewicz inequality
2.2

N\in\mathbb{N}
and

C\in R_{\geq 0}
with
\forall y\in\varphi(A)\cup S:|\widetilde{g}(y)|^{N}\leq C|\widetilde{f}(y)|
. In particular, we obtain

\forall y\in\varphi(A):|g_{0}(\psi(y))|^{N}\leq C|f_{0}(\psi(y))|
which means
\forall x\in A:|g_{0}(x)|^{N}\leq C|f_{0}(x)|
as desired.
∎

3
The finiteness theorem for semialgebraic sets

Definition 3.1
.

Let
n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
. A subset
S
of
R^{n}
is called

K
-basic
\begin{Bmatrix}\text{open}\\
\text{closed}\end{Bmatrix}
if there are
m\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
and

g_{1},\dots,g_{m}\in K[\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.6817pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.7584pt{}}}$\hss}}}X]
satisfying

S=\{x\in R^{n}\mid g_{1}(x)\begin{Bmatrix}>\\
\geq\end{Bmatrix}0,\dots,g_{m}(x)\begin{Bmatrix}>\\
\geq\end{Bmatrix}0\}
.

Remark 3.2
.

Every
K
-basic
\begin{Bmatrix}\text{open}\\
\text{closed}\end{Bmatrix}
subset of
R^{n}
is
K
-semialgebraic
and
\begin{Bmatrix}\text{open}\\
\text{closed}\end{Bmatrix}
in
R^{n}
.

Theorem 3.3

(Finiteness theorem for semialgebraic sets)
.

Let
n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
and
S\in\mathscr{S}_{n}

\begin{Bmatrix}\text{open}\\
\text{closed}\end{Bmatrix}
. Then
S
is a finite
union of
K
-basic
\begin{Bmatrix}\text{open}\\
\text{closed}\end{Bmatrix}
subsets of
R^{n}
.

Proof.

S
is a finite union of
K
-basic open subsets of
R^{n}

\displaystyle\iff

S
is a finite union of finite intersections of sets of the form

\{x\in R^{n}\mid g(x)>0\}

(
g\in K[\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.6817pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.7584pt{}}}$\hss}}}X]
)

\displaystyle\iff

\complement S
is a finite intersection of finite unions of sets of the form

\{x\in R^{n}\mid g(x)\geq 0\}

(
g\in K[\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.6817pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.7584pt{}}}$\hss}}}X]
)

\displaystyle\overset{\ref{unionsection}}{\iff}

\complement S
is a finite union of finite intersections of sets of the form

\{x\in R^{n}\mid g(x)\geq 0\}

(
g\in K[\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.6817pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.7584pt{}}}$\hss}}}X]
)

\displaystyle\iff

\complement S
is a finite union of
K
-basic closed subsets of
R^{n}
.

It is thus enough to show the claim for open
S
. Write

S=\bigcup_{i=1}^{\ell}\{x\in R^{n}\mid f_{i}(x)=0,g_{i1}(x)>0,\ldots,g_{im}(x)>0\}

according to
1.1
with
\ell,m\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
,
f_{i},g_{ij}\in K[\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.6817pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.7584pt{}}}$\hss}}}X]
. Fix

i\in\{1,\dots,\ell\}
. It is enough to find a
K
-basic open set
U\subseteq R^{n}

such that

\{x\in R^{n}\mid f_{i}(x)=0,g_{i1}(x)>0,\ldots,g_{im}(x)>0\}\subseteq U\subseteq S.

Consider the closed set
A:=R^{n}\setminus S\in\mathscr{S}_{n}

and the continuous
K
-semialgebraic functions

\displaystyle f\colon A\to R,

\displaystyle\ x\mapsto(f_{i}(x))^{2}\qquad\text{and}

\displaystyle g\colon A\to R,

\displaystyle\ x\mapsto\prod_{j=1}^{m}(|g_{ij}(x)|+g_{ij}(x)).

We have
\forall x\in A:(f(x)=0\implies g(x)=0)
.
By
2.4
, there thus exist

N,k\in\mathbb{N}
and
C\in K_{\geq 0}
such that

\forall x\in A:|g(x)|^{N}\leq C(1+\|x\|^{2})^{k}f(x)
. For all
x\in A
satisfying

g_{i1}(x)>0,\ldots,g_{im}(x)>0
, we thus have

(2^{m}\prod_{j=1}^{m}g_{ij}(x))^{N}\leq C(1+\sum_{j=1}^{n}x_{j}^{2})^{k}f_{i}(x)^{2}
.
Set

U:=\left\{x\in R^{n}\mid C\left(1+\sum_{j=1}^{n}x_{j}^{2}\right)^{k}f_{i}(x)^{2}<\left(2^{m}\prod_{j=1}^{m}g_{ij}(x)\right)^{N},g_{i1}(x)>0,\dots,g_{im}(x)>0\right\}.

Then
U\cap A=\emptyset
and
\{x\in R^{n}\mid f_{i}(x)=0,g_{i1}(x)>0,\ldots,g_{im}(x)>0\}\subseteq U\subseteq S
.
∎

Example 3.4
.

The “slashed square”
S:=(-1,1)_{R}^{2}\setminus([0,1]_{R}\times\{0\})
is

K
-semialgebraic and open. By
3.3
, it is thus a finite union
of
K
-basic open subsets of
R^{2}
. Indeed,

\displaystyle S=

\displaystyle\{(x,y)\in R^{2}\mid-1<x<1,-(y+1)y^{2}(y-1)>0\}\cup

\displaystyle\left\{(x,y)\in R^{2}\mid\left(x+\frac{1}{2}\right)^{2}+y^{2}<\left(\frac{1}{2}\right)^{2}\right\}

is a union of two
K
-basic open sets. However,
S
is not
K
-basic open.
To show this, we assume

S=\{(x,y)\in R^{2}\mid g_{1}(x,y)>0,\ldots,g_{m}(x,y)>0\}

with
m\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
,
g_{i}\in K[X,Y]
.
For continuity reasons, we have
g_{i}(x,0)\geq 0
for all
x\in[0,1]_{R}
and

i\in\{1,\ldots,m\}
. Because of
([0,1]_{R}\times\{0\})\cap S=\emptyset
, we
have thus
[0,1]_{R}=\bigcup_{i=1}^{m}\{x\in[0,1]_{R}\mid g_{i}(x,0)=0\}
.
WLOG
\#\{x\in[0,1]_{R}\mid g_{1}(x,0)=0\}=\infty
. Then
g_{1}(X,0)=0

and consequently
(R\times\{0\})\cap S=\emptyset
in contradiction to

(-1,0)_{R}\times\{0\}\subseteq S
.

Theorem 3.5

(Abstract version of the finiteness theorem for semialgebraic sets)
.

Let
R|K
be algebraic, i.e.,
R
be the real closure of
(K,K\cap R^{2})
.
Let
n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
and write
A:=K[\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.6817pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.7584pt{}}}$\hss}}}X]
and
T:=\sum K_{\geq 0}A^{2}

so that we are in the setting described before
6.3
.
Denote by

\fatten\colon\mathscr{S}_{n}\to\mathscr{C}:=\mathscr{C}_{(A,T)}

again the fattening
[
\to

6.4
,
3.1
]
.
Let
S\in\mathscr{S}_{n}
.
Then

S\begin{Bmatrix}\text{open}\\
\text{closed}\end{Bmatrix}\text{in $R^{n}$}\iff\fatten(S)\begin{Bmatrix}\text{open}\\
\text{closed}\end{Bmatrix}\text{in $\sper(A,T)$}.

Proof.

It is enough to show:
S
open
\iff

\fatten(S)
open.

“
\Longleftarrow
” By definition of the spectral topology
[
\to

2.1
],
\fatten(S)
is a union of sets of the form

\{P\in\sper(A,T)\mid\widehat{g}_{1}(P)>0,\ldots,\widehat{g}_{m}(P)>0\}

(
m\in\mathbb{N}_{0},g_{1},\dots,g_{m}\in A
). By
2.3
and

1.21
,
\fatten(S)
is quasicompact [
\to

1.14
]
with respect to the constructible topology [
\to

2.1
]. Hence

\fatten(S)
is a finite union of sets of the described form, i.e.,

(**)\qquad\fatten(S)=\bigcup_{i=1}^{k}\{P\in\sper(A,T)\mid\widehat{g}_{i1}(P)>0,\ldots,\widehat{g}_{im}(P)>0\}

with
k,m\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
,
g_{ij}\in A
. It follows by
6.4
that

(*)\qquad S=\bigcup_{i=1}^{k}\{x\in R^{n}\mid g_{i1}(x)>0,\ldots,g_{im}(x)>0\}.

In particular,
S
is open.

“
\Longrightarrow
”
By the finiteness theorem for semialgebraic sets
3.3
,
we can find
k,m\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
and
g_{ij}\in A
such that
(*)
holds.
It follows that
(**)
holds.
In particular,
\fatten(S)
is open.
∎

Remark 3.6
.

Suppose we are in the situation of Theorem
3.5
.

(a)

From the very definition of the slimming
\slim

6.4
, one sees immediately that it is (unlike in general the fattening!)
compatible even with arbitrary unions instead of just finite ones: If
(C_{i})_{i\in I}
is a family of constructible subsets of
\sper(A,T)
whose union
\bigcup_{i\in I}C_{i}
is
again constructible, then

\slim\left(\bigcup_{i\in I}C_{i}\right)=\bigcup_{i\in I}\slim\left(C_{i}\right).

(b)

From (a) it is clear that the proof of the easy direction “
\Longleftarrow
” of Theorem
3.5
could be considerably simplified
by ignoring the quasicompactness of
\fatten(S)
and instead replacing
(*)
and
(**)
by similar conditions with a possibly infinite union instead of a finite one.

(c)

In the special case
R=K
, the easy direction “
\Longleftarrow
” of Theorem
3.5
follows also simply from
1.12
(c) which says that

S=\slim(\fatten(S))=\{x\in R^{n}\mid P_{x}\in\fatten(S)\}

is open.

(d)

If one had already
3.9
(c) available, the easy direction “
\Longleftarrow
” of Theorem
3.5
would follow exactly as in the preceding item (c) also in the general case. However, our proof of
3.9
(c) will use Corollary
3.8
and therefore Theorem
3.5
.

Remark 3.7
.

The description of
3.5
as an abstract version of
3.3

is motivated by the fact that one can easily retrieve the latter from
the first: Note first that one can reduce in
3.3
to the case
where
R|K
is algebraic by using the transfer between
R
and

\overline{(K,K\cap R_{\geq 0})}
[
\to

9.5
].
For this, one has to argue that this transfer preserves openness which can be
accomplished by real quantifier elimination
8.17
. Thus let now
R|K
be
algebraic,
n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
and
S\in\mathscr{S}_{n}
open (by the first part of the
proof of Theorem
3.3
, it suffices to treat the case of open sets).
We have to show that
S
is a finite union of
K
-basic open subsets of
R^{n}
.
As seen in the easy part “
\Longleftarrow
” of the proof of
3.5
,
for this purpose, it suffices to show that
\fatten(S)
is open. This follows from
the difficult part “
\Longrightarrow
” of
3.5
.

Corollary 3.8

(Strengthening of
3.8
)
.

Let
R|K
be algebraic, i.e.,

R
be the real closure of
(K,K\cap R_{\geq 0})
. Let
n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}

and write
A:=K[\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.6817pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.7584pt{}}}$\hss}}}X]
and
T:=\sum K_{\geq 0}A^{2}
. Then
