\sper R[\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.6817pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.7584pt{}}}$\hss}}}X]\to\sper(A,T),\ P\mapsto P\cap A

is a homeomorphism with respect to both, the spectral as well as the constructible
topology on both sides.

Proof.

The map is continuous with respect to both topologies by

2.7
and bijective by
3.8
. According to the definition of
a homeomorphism
2.2
and the definition of both topologies in

2.1
, it suffices to show that for all
C\in\mathscr{C}_{R[\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.6817pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.6817pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to3.55522pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to2.53944pt{}}}$\hss}}}X]}
we have

\{P\cap A\mid P\in C\}\in\mathscr{C}_{(A,T)}
and that this latter set is open
in
\sper(A,T)
whenever
C
is open in
\sper R[\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.6817pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.7584pt{}}}$\hss}}}X]
. For this purpose, let

C\in\mathscr{C}_{R[\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.6817pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.6817pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to3.55522pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to2.53944pt{}}}$\hss}}}X]}
. The slimming
\{x\in R^{n}\mid P_{x}\in C\}

[
\to

6.4
] of
C
is then a semialgebraic subset of
R^{n}

and thus even
K
-semialgebraic by
3.7
since
R|K
is algebraic.
By
1.1
, we thus find
k,m\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
and
f_{i},g_{ij}\in K[\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.6817pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.7584pt{}}}$\hss}}}X]
such that

\{x\in R^{n}\mid P_{x}\in C\}=\bigcup_{i=1}^{k}\{x\in R^{n}\mid f_{i}(x)=0,g_{i1}(x)>0,\ldots,g_{im}(x)>0\},

where one can even choose
f_{1}=\ldots=f_{k}=0
by the finiteness theorem for
semialgebraic sets
3.3
in the case where
C
is open. Fattening this,
we obtain

C=\bigcup_{i=1}^{k}\{P\in\sper R[\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.6817pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.7584pt{}}}$\hss}}}X]\mid\widehat{f}_{i}(P)=0,\widehat{g}_{i1}(P)>0,\ldots,\widehat{g}_{im}(P)>0\}

and therefore [
\to

2.7
]

\{P\cap A\mid P\in C\}=\bigcup_{i=1}^{k}\{P\in\sper(A,T)\mid\widehat{f}_{i}(P)=0,\widehat{g}_{i1}(P)>0,\ldots,\widehat{g}_{im}(P)>0\}\\
\in\mathscr{C}_{(A,T)}.

If
C
is open, then so is
\{P\cap A\mid P\in C\}
because of the choice of

f_{i}=0
.
∎

Remark 3.9
.

In the situation of
3.5
, one can now generalize
1.12
as follows:

(a)

R^{n}
is equipped with the
initial topology with respect to all maps
R^{n}\to R,\ x\mapsto p(x)

(p\in A
).

(b)

The topology on
R^{n}
is generated by the sets

\{x\in R^{n}\mid p(x)>0\}
(
p\in A
).

(c)

Viewing
R^{n}
in virtue of the injective map [
\to

6.3
]

R^{n}\to\sper A,\ x\mapsto P_{x}=\{f\in A\mid f(x)\geq 0\}

as a subset of
\sper A
, the topology on
R^{n}
is induced
by the spectral topology on
\sper A
[
\to

3.8
].

Indeed, (a) is again obvious.
Unlike in
3.5
, (b) does not immediately follow from (a) anymore.
Instead one first proves (c) by using the corresponding item from
1.12
together with Corollary
3.8
.
Finally, one easily deduces (b) from (c).

Chapter 7
Convex sets in vector spaces

In this chapter,
K
denotes always a subfield of
\mathbb{R}
equipped with the order
and the subspace topology [
\to

1.5
(a)] induced by
\mathbb{R}

unless otherwise specified.

1
The isolation theorem for cones

Definition 1.1
.

Let
V
be a
K
-vector space. A subset
C\subseteq V
is called a

(convex) cone
(in
V
) if
0\in C
,
C+C\subseteq C

and
K_{\geq 0}C\subseteq C
[
\to

1.18
]. A cone
C\subseteq V
is called

proper
if
C\neq V
.

Example 1.2
.

Let
T
be a preorder [
\to

2.1
] of
K[\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.6817pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.7584pt{}}}$\hss}}}X]
with

K_{\geq 0}\subseteq T
. Then
T
is a cone. Moreover,
T
is proper as a preorder
[
\to

2.5
] if and only if
T
is proper as a cone.

Proposition 1.3
.

Let
V
be a
K
-vector space
and
C\subseteq V
. Then the following are equivalent:

(a)

C
is a cone.

(b)

C
is convex
[
\to

4.1
]
,
C\neq\emptyset
and

K_{\geq 0}C\subseteq C
.

Proof.

(a)
\implies
(b)
is trivial.

(b)
\implies
(a)
Suppose that (b) holds.
From
C\neq\emptyset
and
0C\subseteq C
, we get
0\in C
.
To show:
C+C\subseteq C
. Let
x,y\in C
. Then
\frac{x}{2}+\frac{y}{2}\in C

and thus
x+y=2\left(\frac{x}{2}+\frac{y}{2}\right)\in C
.
∎

Definition 1.4
.

Let
C
be a cone in the
K
-vector space
V
and
u\in V
.
Then
u
is called a
unit
for
C
(in
V
) if for every
x\in V
there is some

N\in\mathbb{N}
with
Nu+x\in C
.

Example 1.5
.

[
\to

1.2
]
Let
T
be a preorder of
K[\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.6817pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.7584pt{}}}$\hss}}}X]
with
K_{\geq 0}\subseteq T
. Then
T
is
Archimedean [
\to

1.2
(a)] if and only if
1
is a unit for
T
.

Proposition 1.6
.

Let
C
be a cone on the
K
-vector space
V
and
u\in V
. Then the following
are equivalent:

(a)

u
is a unit for
C
.

(b)

V=C-\mathbb{N}u

(c)

V=C-K_{\geq 0}u

(d)

u\in C
and
V=C+\mathbb{Z}u

(e)

u\in C
and
V=C+Ku

(f)

\forall x\in V:\exists\varepsilon\in K_{>0}:u+\varepsilon x\in C

Proof.

(a)
\implies
(b)
\implies
(c)
is clear.

(c)
\implies
(d)
Suppose that (c) holds.
Then
u\in C-K_{\geq 0}u
and thus

(1+\lambda)u\in C
for some
\lambda\in K_{\geq 0}
and so
u\in C
. Fix now
x\in V
.
To show:
x\in C+\mathbb{Z}u
. Choose
\lambda\in K_{\geq 0}
with
x\in C-\lambda u
.
Choose
N\in\mathbb{N}
with
\lambda\leq N
. Then
(N-\lambda)u\in C
and hence

x=(x-(N-\lambda)u)+(N-\lambda)u\in(C-\lambda u-(N-\lambda)u)+C\\
\subseteq C-Nu\subseteq C-\mathbb{N}u\subseteq C+\mathbb{Z}u.

(d)
\implies
(e)
is trivial.

(e)
\implies
(f)
Suppose that (e) holds and let

x\in V
. Choose
\lambda\in K
such that
x\in C-\lambda u
. If
\lambda\leq 0
,
then
x\in C
and consequently
u+\varepsilon x=u+x\in C+C\subseteq C
with
\varepsilon:=1
.
If
\lambda>0
, then set
\varepsilon:=\frac{1}{\lambda}>0
. Then
u+\varepsilon x\in\varepsilon C\subseteq C
.

(f)
\implies
(a)
Suppose that (f) holds and let
x\in V
.
To show:
\exists N\in\mathbb{N}:Nu+x\in C
. Choose
\varepsilon\in K_{>0}
with
u+\varepsilon x\in C
.
Choose
N\in\mathbb{N}
with
\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\leq N
. From (f), it follows also that
u\in C

and hence
(N-\frac{1}{\varepsilon})u\in C
. Now
Nu+x=(N-\frac{1}{\varepsilon})u+\frac{1}{\varepsilon}u+x\in C+\frac{1}{\varepsilon}(u+\varepsilon x)\subseteq C+\frac{1}{\varepsilon}C\subseteq C+C\subseteq C
.
∎

Corollary 1.7
.

Let
u
be a unit for the cone
C
in the
K
-vector space

V
. Then
u\in C
and
V=C-C
.

Remark 1.8
.

The units for a cone in
K^{n}
are exactly its interior points
[
\to

2.5
,
1.6
(f)].

Definition 1.9
.

Let
V
be a
K
-vector space,
C\subseteq V
and
u\in V
. A
state

of
(V,C,u)
is a
K
-linear function
\varphi\colon V\to\mathbb{R}
satisfying

\varphi(C)\subseteq\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
and
\varphi(u)=1
. We refer to the set

S(V,C,u)\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{V}
of all states of
(V,C,u)
as the
state space

of
(V,C,u)
.

Example 1.10
.

Set
K:=\mathbb{R}
,
V:=\mathbb{R}[X]
,
C:=P_{\infty}\in\sper\mathbb{R}[X]
. Then the cone
C
does
not possess a unit in
V
and we have
S(V,C,u)=\emptyset
for all
u\in V
.
Indeed, let
u\in V
. Choose
d\in\mathbb{N}
with
d>\deg u
. Then

u-\varepsilon X^{d}\notin C
for all
\varepsilon>0
. By
1.6
(f),
u
is thus not a unit for

C
. Assume
\varphi\in S(V,C,u)
. Then
\varepsilon\varphi(X^{d})-1=\varphi(\varepsilon X^{d}-u)\in\varphi(C)\subseteq\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
for all
\varepsilon>0

\lightning
.

Example 1.11
.

Set
K:=\mathbb{Q}
,
V:=\mathbb{Q}^{2}
,
C:=\{(x,y)\in\mathbb{Q}^{2}\mid y\geq\sqrt{2}x\}
. All elements of

C
except
0
are units for
C
[
\to

1.8
]. There is no

\varphi\in V^{*}\setminus\{0\}
satisfying
\varphi(C)\subseteq\mathbb{Q}_{\geq 0}
but for each

u\in C\setminus\{0\}
, we have
\#S(V,C,u)=1
.

Lemma 1.12
.

Let
u
be a unit for a proper cone
C
in the
K
-vector space
V
. Then

\varrho\colon V\to\mathbb{R},\ x\mapsto\sup\{\lambda\in K\mid x-\lambda u\in C\}

is well-defined and we have
\varrho(x)+\varrho(y)\leq\varrho(x+y)
as well as

\varrho(\lambda x)=\lambda\varrho(x)
for all
x,y\in V
and
\lambda\in K_{\geq 0}
.

Proof.

Let
x,y\in V
and
\lambda\in K_{\geq 0}
. For the well-definedness of
\varrho
, we have to
show that
I:=\{\lambda\in K\mid x-\lambda u\in C\}
is nonempty and bounded from above
[
\to

1.9
,
1.16
]. Since
u
is a unit for
C
, we
have
I\neq\emptyset
and furthermore there is
N\in\mathbb{N}
such that
-x+Nu\in C
.
Then
\lambda<N+1
for all
\lambda\in I
since otherwise, if
\lambda\in I
satisfied
\lambda\geq N+1
,
then

\displaystyle-u

\displaystyle=Nu-(N+1)u=(-x+Nu)+x-(N+1)u

\displaystyle\in C+x-\lambda u+(\lambda-(N+1))u

\displaystyle\subseteq C+C+K_{\geq 0}u\subseteq C.

But now
-u\notin C
for otherwise
C\overset{\text{\ref{unitchar}(b)}}{=}V
.
Now choose sequences
(\lambda_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
and
(\mu_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
in
K

such that
x-\lambda_{n}u,y-\mu_{n}u\in C
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
and

\lim_{n\to\infty}\lambda_{n}=\varrho(x)
as well as
\lim_{n\to\infty}\mu_{n}=\varrho(y)
.
Then we have
(x+y)-(\lambda_{n}+\mu_{n})u\in C+C\subseteq C
and thus

\lambda_{n}+\mu_{n}\leq\varrho(x+y)
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
. It follows that

\varrho(x)+\varrho(y)=\left(\lim_{n\to\infty}\lambda_{n}\right)+\left(\lim_{n\to\infty}\mu_{n}\right)=\lim_{n\to\infty}(\lambda_{n}+\mu_{n})\leq\varrho(x+y).

Moreover,
\lambda x-\lambda\lambda_{n}u\in\lambda C\subseteq C
and thus
\lambda\lambda_{n}\leq\varrho(\lambda x)
for
all
n\in\mathbb{N}
. It follows that
\lambda\varrho(x)=\lambda\lim_{n\to\infty}\lambda_{n}=\lim_{n\to\infty}\lambda\lambda_{n}\leq\varrho(\lambda x)
and analogously
\frac{1}{\lambda}\varrho(\lambda x)\leq\varrho\left(\frac{1}{\lambda}(\lambda x)\right)

if
\lambda\neq 0
, i.e.,
\lambda\varrho(x)=\varrho(\lambda x)
.
∎

Theorem 1.13

(Isolation theorem for cones)
.

Let
u
be a unit for the proper cone
C

in the
K
-vector space
V
. Then
S(V,C,u)\neq\emptyset
.

Proof.

Since the union of a nonempty chain of cones in
V
is again a cone in
V
,
we can use Zorn’s lemma to enlarge
C
to a cone of
V
that is maximal with
respect to the property of not containing
-u
. WLOG suppose that
C
has
already this maximality property.

Claim 1:

C\cup-C=V

Explanation.
Let
x\in V
with
x\notin-C
. To show:
x\in C
.
Due to the maximality of
C
it is enough to show that the cone

C+K_{\geq 0}x
does not contain
-u
. But if we had
-u=y+\lambda x

for some
y\in C
and
\lambda\in K_{\geq 0}
, then
\lambda>0
and

x=\frac{1}{\lambda}(-u-y)\in-C

\lightning
.

Consider for each
x\in V
, the sets

I_{x}:=\{\lambda\in K\mid x-\lambda u\in C\}\text{ and }J_{x}:=\{\lambda\in K\mid x-\lambda u\in-C\}.

Claim 2:

\forall x\in V:\forall\lambda\in I_{x}:\forall\mu\in J_{x}:\lambda\leq\mu

Explanation.
Let
x\in V
,
\lambda\in I_{x}
and
\mu\in J_{x}
. Then

x-\lambda u\in C
and
\mu u-x\in C
. Thus,

(\mu-\lambda)u=(\mu u-x)+(x-\lambda u)\in C+C\subseteq C
. If we had
\mu<\lambda
, then
we had
-u\in C\ \lightning
.

Consider now
\varphi\colon V\to\mathbb{R},\ x\mapsto\sup I_{x}
[
\to

1.12
].

Claim 3:

-\varphi(x)=\sup\{\lambda\in K\mid x-\lambda(-u)\in-C\}
for all
x\in V

Explanation.
Let
x\in V
. From
I_{x}\cup J_{x}\overset{\text{Claim 1}}{=}K
and Claim 2, we get

\varphi(x)=\sup I_{x}=\inf J_{x}

and hence

-\varphi(x)=-\inf J_{x}=\sup\{-\lambda\mid\lambda\in K,x-\lambda u\in-C\}=\sup\{\lambda\in K\mid x+\lambda u\in-C\}.

From
1.12
, we obtain
\varphi(x)+\varphi(y)\leq\varphi(x+y)
and
\varphi(\lambda x)=\lambda\varphi(x)

for all
x,y\in V
and
\lambda\in K_{\geq 0}
. Since
-u
is a unit for the proper cone

-C
,
1.12
and Claim 3 yield also
-\varphi(x)-\varphi(y)\leq-\varphi(x+y)
for all

x,y\in V
. It follows that

\varphi(x)+\varphi(y)\leq\varphi(x+y)\leq\varphi(x)+\varphi(y)

and
therefore
\varphi(x)+\varphi(y)=\varphi(x+y)
for all
x,y\in V
. In particular,

\varphi(x)+\varphi(-x)=\varphi(0)=0
and hence
\varphi(-x)=-\varphi(x)
for all
x\in V
from which
we deduce

\varphi((-\lambda)x)=\varphi(-\lambda x)=-\varphi(\lambda x)=-\lambda\varphi(x)=(-\lambda)\varphi(x)

for all
x\in V
and
\lambda\in K_{\geq 0}
. Altogether,
\varphi(\lambda x)=\lambda\varphi(x)
for all

x\in V
and
\lambda\in K_{\geq 0}\cup K_{\leq 0}=K
, i.e.,
\varphi
is
K
-linear. Obviously,

\varphi(C)\subseteq\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
and
\varphi(u)=1
. Therefore
\varphi\in S(V,C,u)
.
∎

Lemma 1.14
.

Let
C
be a cone in the
K
-vector space
V
and
x\in V
. Then

x\in C\iff x\in C-K_{\geq 0}x.

Proof.

“
\Longrightarrow
” is trivial.

“
\Longleftarrow
” Let
x\in C-K_{\geq 0}x
, for instance
x=y-\lambda x
with

y\in C
and
\lambda\in K_{\geq 0}
. Then

x=\frac{1}{1+\lambda}y\in C.

∎

Corollary 1.15
.

Suppose
u
is a unit for the cone
C
in the
K
-vector space
V

and
x\in V
. If
\varphi(x)>0
for all
\varphi\in S(V,C,u)
, then
x\in C
.

Proof.

Suppose
x\notin C
. To show:
\exists\varphi\in S(V,C,u):\varphi(x)\leq 0
.
By
1.14
, the cone
C-K_{\geq 0}x
is proper. Since
u
is a unit for
C
,
it is of course also a unit for
C-K_{\geq 0}x
. By the isolation theorem

1.13
, there is
\varphi\in S(V,C-K_{\geq 0}x,u)
. We have
\varphi\in S(V,C,u)
and

\varphi(x)\leq 0
.
∎

Exercise 1.16
.

[
\to

1.9
]
Let
V
be a
K
-vector space,
C\subseteq V

and
u\in V
. We equip the
\mathbb{R}
-vector space
\mathbb{R}^{V}
of all functions from
V
to

\mathbb{R}
with the product topology [
\to

1.5
(b)]. Then

S(V,C,u)
is a closed convex subset of
\mathbb{R}^{V}
which we equip with the
subspace topology [
\to

1.5
(a)]. Using
1.4
, one shows that this topology is
at the same time also the initial topology with
respect to the functions

S(V,C,u)\to\mathbb{R},\ \varphi\mapsto\varphi(x)\qquad(x\in V).

Theorem 1.17
.

Let
u
be a unit for the cone
C
in the
K
-vector space
V
.
Then the state space
S(V,C,u)
is compact
[
\to

1.14
]
.

Proof.

Choose for each
x\in V
an
N_{x}\in\mathbb{N}
such that

\pm x+N_{x}u\in C
. Then we have for all
\varphi\in S(V,C,u)
and
x\in V

that
\pm\varphi(x)+N_{x}=\varphi(\pm x+N_{x}u)\geq 0
and thus

\varphi(x)\in[-N_{x},N_{x}].

Thus
S(V,C,u)\subseteq\prod_{x\in V}[-N_{x},N_{x}]
. From analysis
(cf.
1.16
) and Tikhonov’s theorem
1.18
,

\prod_{x\in V}[-N_{x},N_{x}]
is compact with respect to the product topology.
But the product topology on
\prod_{x\in V}[-N_{x},N_{x}]
is induced
by the topology of
\mathbb{R}^{V}
[
\to

1.6
]. By
1.16
,
S(V,C,u)
is thus closed
in the compact space
\prod_{x\in V}[-N_{x},N_{x}]
and hence is compact itself
[
\to

1.21
].
∎

Exercise 1.18
.

Let
M
and
N
be topological spaces and
f\colon M\to N

be continuous. If
M
is quasicompact [
\to

1.14
], then so is
f(M)

[
\to

1.21
]

Corollary 1.19
.

Let
M
be a nonempty quasicompact topological space and
f\colon M\to\mathbb{R}

be continuous. Then
f
takes on a minimum and a maximum, i.e., there are

x,y\in M
with

f(x)\leq f(z)\leq f(y)

for all
z\in M
.

Proof.

f(M)
is compact by
1.18
. Hence
f(M)
is nonempty,
bounded and closed. From the first two properties, it follows that
\inf f(M),\sup f(M)\in\mathbb{R}
exist [
\to

1.9
(c),
1.16
].
The last property yields
\inf f(M)=\min f(M)
and
\sup f(M)=\max f(M)
.
∎

Theorem 1.20

(Strengthening of
1.15
)
.

[
\to

2.2
]

Let
u
be a unit for the cone
C
in the
K
-vector space
V
and
x\in V
.
Then the following are equivalent:

(a)

\forall\varphi\in S(V,C,u):\varphi(x)>0

(b)

\exists N\in\mathbb{N}:x\in\frac{1}{N}u+C

(c)

x
is a unit for
C
.

Proof.

(b)
\implies
(a)
is trivial.

(a)
\implies
(b)
Suppose that (a) holds.
If
S(V,C,u)=\emptyset
, then
C=V
by
1.13
and we can choose

N\in\mathbb{N}
arbitrarily. Suppose therefore that
S(V,C,u)\neq\emptyset
. Then the
continuous function
S(V,C,u)\to\mathbb{R},\ \varphi\mapsto\varphi(x)
takes on
by
1.17
and
1.19

a minimum
\mu
for which
\mu>0
holds by (a). Choose
N\in\mathbb{N}
such that

\frac{1}{N}<\mu
. Then
\varphi\left(x-\frac{1}{N}u\right)=\varphi(x)-\frac{1}{N}\geq\mu-\frac{1}{N}>0

for all
\varphi\in S(V,C,u)
. Now
1.15
yields that
x-\frac{1}{N}u\in C
.

(b)
\implies
(c)
Suppose that (b) holds and let
y\in V
. To show:
\exists N\in\mathbb{N}:Nx+y\in C
.
Choose
N^{\prime},N^{\prime\prime}\in\mathbb{N}
with
x\in\frac{1}{N^{\prime}}u+C
and
N^{\prime\prime}u+y\in C
.
Setting
N:=N^{\prime}N^{\prime\prime}
, we obtain

Nx+y\in N^{\prime\prime}N^{\prime}\left(\frac{1}{N^{\prime}}u+C\right)+y\subseteq N^{\prime\prime}(u+C)+y\subseteq N^{\prime\prime}u+y+C\subseteq C+C\subseteq C
.

(c)
\implies
(a)
Suppose that (c) holds and let
\varphi\in S(V,C,u)
.
To show:
\varphi(x)>0
. Choose
N\in\mathbb{N}
with
Nx-u\in C
. Then

N\varphi(x)-1=\varphi(Nx-u)\geq 0
and thus
\varphi(x)\geq\frac{1}{N}>0
for all

\varphi\in S(V,C,u)
.
∎

2
Separating convex sets in topological vector spaces

Definition 2.1
.

A
K
-vector space
V
together with a topology on
V
[
\to

1.2
(a)] is called a
topological
K
-vector space

if
V\times V\to V,(x,y)\mapsto x+y
and

K\times V\to V,\ (\lambda,x)\mapsto\lambda x
are continuous and
\{0\}
is a closed
set in
V
.

Example 2.2
.

(a)

If
I
is a set, then
K^{I}
(endowed with the product topology
[
\to

1.5
(b)]) is a topological
K
-vector space.

(b)

A
K
-vector space
V
together with the discrete topology on
V
is a
topological vector space if and only if
V=\{0\}
. Indeed, if
y\in V\setminus\{0\}
,
then

\{(\lambda,x)\in K\times V\mid\lambda x=y\}=\{(\lambda,\lambda^{-1}y)\mid\lambda\in K^{\times}\}

is not open in
K\times V
.

(c)

From analysis, one knows that every normed
\mathbb{R}
-vector space,
in particular every
\mathbb{R}
-vector space with scalar product, is a topological

\mathbb{R}
-vector space.

Lemma 2.3
.

Let
V
be a
K
-vector space and
A\subseteq V
be convex.
If
0\notin A\neq\emptyset
, then
A
generates a proper convex cone, i.e.,

\sum_{x\in A}K_{\geq 0}x\neq V
.

Proof.

Suppose that
A\neq\emptyset
and
\sum_{x\in A}K_{\geq 0}x=V
. We show

0\in A
. Choose
y\in A
and write
-y=\sum_{i=1}^{m}\lambda_{i}x_{i}
with
\lambda_{1},\dots,\lambda_{m}\in K_{\geq 0}
and
x_{1},\dots,x_{m}\in A
. Setting
\mu:=1+\sum_{i=1}^{m}\lambda_{i}>0
, we have then
0=\frac{1}{\mu}y+\sum_{i=1}^{m}\frac{\lambda_{i}}{\mu}x_{i}\in A

since
\frac{1}{\mu}+\sum_{i=1}^{m}\frac{\lambda_{i}}{\mu}=\frac{\mu}{\mu}=1
.
∎

Lemma 2.4
.

[
\to

1.8
]
Let
V
be a topological
K
-vector space,
C\subseteq V
a convex cone and

u\in C^{\circ}
[
\to

2.5
]. Then
u
is a unit for
C

[
\to

1.4
].

Proof.

We show
\forall x\in V:\exists\varepsilon\in K_{>0}:u+\varepsilon x\in C
[
\to

1.6
(f)].
For this aim, fix
x\in V
. From Definition
2.1
, it follows that

K\to V,\lambda\mapsto u+\lambda x
is continuous. Choose an open set
A\subseteq V

such that
u\in A\subseteq C
. Then
\{\lambda\in K\mid u+\lambda x\in A\}
is open and
contains
0
. In particular, there is
\varepsilon\in K_{>0}
such that
u+\varepsilon x\in A\subseteq C
.
∎

Example 2.5
.

Consider the
\mathbb{R}
-vector space
V:=C([0,1],\mathbb{R})
of all
continuous real valued functions on the interval
[0,1]\subseteq\mathbb{R}

together with the scalar product defined by

\langle f,g\rangle:=\int_{0}^{1}f(x)g(x)dx\qquad(f,g\in V).

By
2.2
(c), this is a topological vector space. The constant function

u\colon[0,1]\to\mathbb{R},\ x\mapsto 1
is a unit for the cone

C:=C([0,1],\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0})
of all functions nonnegative on
[0,1]
by

1.19
(since
[0,1]
is compact by
1.16
).
But
u
does not lie in
C^{\circ}
since for every
\varepsilon>0
there is some

f\in V
with
\|u-f\|=\sqrt{\int_{0}^{1}(u(x)-f(x))^{2}dx}<\varepsilon
and
f\notin C
.

Remark 2.6
.

From Definition
2.1
, it follows that for every topological

K
-vector space
V
the maps

V\to V,\ x\mapsto\lambda x+y
(
\lambda\in K^{\times},y\in V)
are homeomorphisms
[
\to

2.2
].

Lemma 2.7
.

Suppose
V
is a topological
K
-vector space
and
\varphi\colon V\to\mathbb{R}
is
K
-linear. Then the following are equivalent:

(a)

\varphi
is continuous.

(b)

\varphi^{-1}(\mathbb{R}_{>0})
is open.

(c)

\varphi^{-1}(\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0})
is closed.

Proof.

(b)
\iff
(c)
follows from

\varphi^{-1}(\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0})=-\varphi^{-1}(\mathbb{R}_{\leq 0})=-(V\setminus\varphi^{-1}(\mathbb{R}_{>0}))
since

V\to V,\ x\mapsto-x
is a homeomorphism by
2.6
.

(a)
\implies
(b)
is trivial.

(b)
\implies
(a)
WLOG
\varphi\neq 0
. WLOG choose
u\in V
in
such a way that
\varphi(u)=1
(otherwise scale
\varphi
). Suppose that (b) holds.
Then the set
\varphi^{-1}(\mathbb{R}_{>a})=au+\varphi^{-1}(\mathbb{R}_{>0})
is open
and hence

\varphi^{-1}(\mathbb{R}_{<-a})=-\varphi^{-1}(\mathbb{R}_{>a})
is open for all
a\in K

[
\to

2.6
].
So the set
\varphi^{-1}((a,b)_{\mathbb{R}})=\varphi^{-1}(\mathbb{R}_{>a})\cap\varphi^{-1}(\mathbb{R}_{<b})
is open
for all
a,b\in K
. Since every open subset of
\mathbb{R}
is a union of
intervals
(a,b)_{\mathbb{R}}
with
a,b\in K
, the continuity of
\varphi
follows.
∎

Lemma 2.8
.

Let
V
be a topological
K
-vector space and
\varphi\colon V\to\mathbb{R}
be
K
-linear
map. Then
\varphi
is continuous if and only if

\varphi^{-1}(\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0})
has an interior point.

Proof.

WLOG
\varphi\neq 0
.
If
\varphi
is continuous, then
\varphi^{-1}(\mathbb{R}_{>0})
is open and because of
\varphi\neq 0

nonempty. Conversely, let
u
be an interior point of
\varphi^{-1}(\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0})
.
By
2.7
, it is enough to show that
\varphi^{-1}(\mathbb{R}_{>0})
is open.
For this, consider
x\in\varphi^{-1}(\mathbb{R}_{>0})
. We have to show that there is an
open set
A\subseteq V
such that
x\in A\subseteq\varphi^{-1}(\mathbb{R}_{>0})
.
Choose an open
set
B\subseteq V
with
u\in B\subseteq\varphi^{-1}(\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0})
.
Choose
\lambda\in K_{>0}
such that
\lambda\varphi(u)<\varphi(x)
.
Then
A:=x+\lambda(B-u)
is open by

2.6
, and we have
x=x+\lambda(u-u)\in A
and

\varphi(A)=\varphi(x)+\lambda(\varphi(B)-\varphi(u))\subseteq\varphi(x)+\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}-\lambda\varphi(u)\subseteq\mathbb{R}_{>0}.

∎

Example 2.9
.

Let
V:=C([0,1],\mathbb{R})
be the topological
K
-vector space from
2.5

and
x\in[0,1]
. Then
V\to\mathbb{R},\ f\mapsto f(x)
is not continuous.

Theorem 2.10

(Separation theorem for topological vector spaces)
.

Let
A
and
B
be convex sets in the topological
K
-vector space
V

with
A^{\circ}\neq\emptyset\neq B
and
A\cap B=\emptyset
. Then there is
a continuous
K
-linear function
\varphi\colon V\to\mathbb{R}
with
\varphi\neq 0
and

\varphi(x)\leq\varphi(y)
for all
x\in A
and
y\in B
.

Proof.

Since
A
is convex, also
-A
is convex and thus the Minkowski sum

B-A=B+(-A)
[
\to

4.5
] is also convex.
By hypothesis, we have
0\notin B-A\neq\emptyset
, for which reason there is according to
2.3
a proper
cone
C\subseteq V
such that
B-A\subseteq C
.
Due to
A^{\circ}\neq\emptyset
and
B\neq\emptyset
,
2.6

yields
(B-A)^{\circ}\neq\emptyset
and thus
C^{\circ}\neq\emptyset
. Choose

u\in C^{\circ}
. By
2.4
,
u
is a unit for
C
. By the isolation theorem

1.13
, there exists a state
\varphi
of
(V,C,u)
. Because of
\varphi(u)=1
,
we have
\varphi\neq 0
and because of
\varphi(B-A)\subseteq\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
, we have

\varphi(x)\leq\varphi(y)
for all
x\in A
and
y\in B
.
Finally,
\varphi
is continuous by
2.8
since
u
is an interior point
of
C
and a fortiori of
\varphi^{-1}(\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0})
.
∎

Corollary 2.11
.

Let
A
and
B
be convex sets in the topological

K
-vector space
V
satisfying
A\cap B=\emptyset
. Suppose
A
is open.
Then there is a continuous
K
-linear function
\varphi\colon V\to\mathbb{R}

and an
r\in\mathbb{R}
such that
\varphi(x)<r\leq\varphi(y)
for all
x\in A
and
y\in B
.

Proof.

If
A=\emptyset
or
B=\emptyset
then we can set
\varphi:=0\in V^{*}
and choose
r\in\mathbb{R}
arbitrarily since
the statement
\forall x\in A:\forall y\in B:\varphi(x)<r\leq\varphi(y)
is empty. WLOG
A\neq\emptyset
and

B\neq\emptyset
.
Choose by
2.10
a continuous
K
-linear function
\varphi\colon V\to\mathbb{R}

with
\varphi\neq 0
and
\varphi(x)\leq\varphi(y)
for all
x\in A
and
y\in B
. The set

\{\varphi(x)\mid x\in A\}\subseteq\mathbb{R}
is nonempty because of
A\neq\emptyset

and bounded from above because of
B\neq\emptyset
. It thus possesses a
supremum
r\in\mathbb{R}
. We have
\varphi(x)\leq r\leq\varphi(y)
for all
x\in A
and
y\in B
.
Let
x\in A
. It remains to show that
\varphi(x)<r
. For this purpose, choose

z\in V
such that
\varphi(z)>0
. The function
K\to V,\ \lambda\mapsto x+\lambda z
is
continuous and together with
0
, a whole neighborhood of
0
lies in the
preimage of
A
under this function. In particular, there is an
\varepsilon\in K_{>0}

such that
x+\varepsilon z\in A
. Then
\varphi(x)<\varphi(x)+\varepsilon\varphi(z)=\varphi(x+\varepsilon z)\leq r
.
∎

Lemma 2.12
.

Let
V
be a topological
K
-vector space,
A\subseteq V
be convex,

x\in A^{\circ}
,
y\in A
and
\lambda\in K
with
0<\lambda\leq 1
. Then

\lambda x+(1-\lambda)y\in A^{\circ}
.

Proof.

Choose an open neighborhood
B
of
x
with
B\subseteq A
.
Setting
z:=\lambda x+(1-\lambda)y
,
C:=z+\lambda(B-x)
is by
2.6

an open neighborhood of
z
. It is enough to show
C\subseteq A
.
To this end, let
c\in C
. Because of
B=x+\frac{1}{\lambda}(C-z)
, we have then

b:=x+\frac{1}{\lambda}(c-z)\in B\subseteq A
. Consequently,

c=\lambda(b-x)+z=\lambda b-\lambda x+\lambda x+(1-\lambda)y=\lambda b+(1-\lambda)y\in A
.
∎

Proposition 2.13
.

Suppose
V
is a topological
K
-vector space and
A\subseteq V
is convex.
Then both
A^{\circ}
and
\overline{A}
are convex.

Proof.

It follows immediately from Lemma
2.12
that

A^{\circ}
is convex. In order to show that
\overline{A}
is convex,
fix
x,y\in\overline{A}
and
\lambda\in[0,1]_{K}
. To show:

z:=\lambda x+(1-\lambda)y\in\overline{A}
. Let
B
be a neighborhood of
z
in
V
.
To show:
B\cap A\neq\emptyset
. Since

V\times V\to V,\ (x^{\prime},y^{\prime})\mapsto\lambda x^{\prime}+(1-\lambda)y^{\prime}

is continuous, there are neighborhoods
C
of
x
and

D
of
y
in
V
such that

\lambda C+(1-\lambda)D\subseteq B.

Due to

x,y\in\overline{A}
, we find
x_{0}\in C\cap A
and
y_{0}\in D\cap A
.
Then

z_{0}:=\lambda x_{0}+(1-\lambda)y_{0}\in B\cap A.

∎

Definition 2.14
.

Let
V
be a
K
-vector space and
A\subseteq V
a set. Then
A
is called

balanced
if
\lambda x\in A
for all
x\in A
and
\lambda\in K
with
|\lambda|\leq 1
.

Proposition 2.15
.

Suppose
V
be a topological
K
-vector space
and
B
is a neighborhood of
0
in
V
.
Then there is a balanced open neighborhood
A
of
0
in
V
with

A\subseteq B
.

Proof.

WLOG
B
is open [
\to

1.9
]. Since the scalar multiplication is continuous by
2.1
, there is an
\varepsilon\in K_{>0}
and an open
neighborhood
C
of
0
in
V
such that

\forall\lambda\in(-\varepsilon,\varepsilon)_{K}:\forall x\in C:\lambda x\in B.

By
2.6
, each
\lambda C
with
\lambda\in K^{\times}
is open. Thus
also
A:=\bigcup_{\lambda\in(-\varepsilon,\varepsilon)_{K}\setminus\{0\}}\lambda C\subseteq B
is open.
Moreover, we have
0\in A
and
A
is obviously balanced.
∎

Exercise 2.16
.

In a Hausdorff space [
\to

1.14
], every compact subset
[
\to

1.21
] is closed.

Definition 2.17
.

Let
V
be a
K
-vector space. We call a topology on
V
making
V
into
a topological vector space [
\to

2.1
] a
vector space topology

on
V
.

Remark 2.18
.

Up to now the condition
\overline{\{0\}}=\{0\}
from Definition
2.1
has
been used nowhere. From now on, we will however need it. We will show that
each finite-dimensional
\mathbb{R}
-vector space carries exactly one vector space
topology which would be false without the condition
\overline{\{0\}}=\{0\}

since otherwise the trivial topology [
\to

1.2
(e)] would also be a
vector space topology.

Proposition 2.19
.

Every topological
K
-vector space is a Hausdorff space.

Proof.

Let
V
be a topological
K
-vector space [
\to

2.1
] and let
x,y\in V

with
x\neq y
. Set
z:=x-y\neq 0
. By Definition
2.1
,
\{0\}
and thus
by
2.6
also
\{z\}
is closed. Hence
V\setminus\{z\}
is an open
neighborhood of
0
. Since
V\times V\to V,\ (v,w)\mapsto v-w
is continuous by

2.1
, there is a neighborhood
U
of
0
such that

U-U\subseteq V\setminus\{z\}
. Then
(x+U)\cap(y+U)=\emptyset
for otherwise
there would be
u,v\in U
with
x+u=y+v
from which it would follow

z=x-y=v-u\in U-U

\lightning
.
∎

Proposition 2.20
.

Let
V
be a finite-dimensional
\mathbb{R}
-vector space. Then there is
exactly one vector space topology
[
\to

2.17
]
on
V
.

Proof.

Choose a basis
v_{1},\dots,v_{n}
of
V
. Then

f\colon\mathbb{R}^{n}\to V,\ x\mapsto\sum_{i=1}^{n}x_{i}v_{i}
is a vector space isomorphism.
With
\mathbb{R}^{n}
[
\to

2.2
] also
V
possesses therefore
a vector space topology. This shows existence. For uniqueness, endow now

V
with any vector space topology. We show that
f
is a homeomorphism.
By
2.1
,
f
is certainly continuous. It is enough to show that images of
open sets under
f
are again open. For this purpose, it suffices to show that for
all open balls in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
the image of their center is an interior point of their image
because if
A\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}
is open then every point in
f(A)
is the image of
the center of an open ball contained in
A
. Due to
2.6
, it
suffices to consider the ball
B:=\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\mid\|x\|<1\}
around the origin of
radius
1
. In order to show that
0\in(f(B))^{\circ}
, we take the sphere

S:=\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\mid\|x\|=1\}
. By
1.16
,
S
is compact and hence so is
by
1.18
and
2.19
also
f(S)
.
According to
2.16
,
f(S)
is thus closed in
V
. Hence
V\setminus f(S)

is a neighborhood of
0
in
V
. By
2.15
, there is a balanced open
neighborhood
A
of
0
in
V
with
A\subseteq V\setminus f(S)
, i.e.,

A\cap f(S)=\emptyset
. Since
f
is continuous,
f^{-1}(A)
is an open
neighborhood of
0
in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
. Due to the linearity of
f
, with
A
also

f^{-1}(A)
is balanced according to Definition
2.14
. Since

f^{-1}(A)
is disjoint to
S
, it follows that
f^{-1}(A)\subseteq B
and thus

A\subseteq f(B)
. Hence
0\in(f(B))^{\circ}
as desired.
∎

3
Convex sets in locally convex vector spaces

Definition 3.1
.

A
locally convex
K
-vector space
is a topological

K
-vector space [
\to

2.1
] in which for every
x\in V
each
neighborhood of
x
contains a convex neighborhood of
x
.

Remark 3.2
.

Because of
2.6
, one can restrict oneself in
3.1

to
x=0
.

Example 3.3
.

[
\to

2.2
]

(a)

If
I
is a set, then
K^{I}
is a locally convex
K
-vector space.

(b)

If a
K
-vector space
V
is endowed with the initial topology
[
\to

1.4
] with respect
to a family
(f_{i})_{i\in I}
of
K
-linear functions
f_{i}\colon V\to\mathbb{R}
in such a way
that to each
x\in V\setminus\{0\}
there is some
i\in I
with
f_{i}(x)\neq 0
,
then
V
is a locally convex
K
-vector space.

(c)

Every normed
\mathbb{R}
-vector space
V
, in particular every
\mathbb{R}
-vector space
with scalar product, is a locally convex
\mathbb{R}
-vector space since

\|\lambda x+(1-\lambda)y\|\leq\lambda\|x\|+(1-\lambda)\|y\|<\lambda\varepsilon+(1-\lambda)\varepsilon=\varepsilon

for all
\varepsilon>0
,
x,y\in V
satisfying
\|x\|,\|y\|<\varepsilon
(“balls are convex”) and
\lambda\in[0,1]_{\mathbb{R}}
.

Lemma 3.4
.

Suppose
V
is a topological
K
-vector space,
A\subseteq V
is closed and

C\subseteq V
is compact. Then
A+C
is closed.

Proof.

Let
x\in V\setminus(A+C)
. We have to show that there is a
neighborhood
U
of the origin satisfying
(x+U)\cap(A+C)=\emptyset
.

Claim:

For each
y\in C
, there exists a neighborhood
U_{y}
of the origin such that

(x+U_{y})\cap(y+U_{y}+A)=\emptyset.

Explanation.
Let
y\in C
. Then

V\times V\to V,\ (x^{\prime},y^{\prime})\mapsto x-y+x^{\prime}-y^{\prime}
is continuous and
(0,0)
lies in the
preimage of the open set
V\setminus A
since

x-y\notin A
(otherwise we would have
x\in A+y\subseteq A+C
). Hence there is a
neighborhood
U_{y}
with

x-y+U_{y}-U_{y}\subseteq V\setminus A,

i.e.,
(x+U_{y}-y-U_{y})\cap A=\emptyset
.

By compactness of
C
, there is a finite subset
D\subseteq C
such that

C\subseteq\bigcup_{y\in D}(y+U_{y})
. Now
U:=\bigcap_{y\in D}U_{y}
is a
neighborhood of the origin. In order to show that

(x+U)\cap(A+C)=\emptyset,

it is enough to prove that
(x+U)\cap(A+y+U_{y})=\emptyset
for all
y\in D
.
For this purpose, it suffices to show that

(x+U_{y})\cap(y+U_{y}+A)=\emptyset
for all
y\in D
. But this holds even for all

y\in C
by the above claim.
∎

Theorem 3.5

(Separation theorem for locally convex vector spaces)
.

[
\to

2.10
,
2.11
]

Let
A
and
C
be convex sets in the locally convex
K
-vector space

V
with
A\cap C=\emptyset
. Let
A
be closed and
C
be compact. Then
there is a continuous
K
-linear function
\varphi\colon V\to\mathbb{R}
and

r,s\in\mathbb{R}
with
\varphi(x)\leq r<s\leq\varphi(y)
for all
x\in A
and
y\in C
.

Proof.

If
A=\emptyset
or
C=\emptyset
, we can take
\varphi:=0\in V^{*}
and choose
r,s\in\mathbb{R}
arbitrary since the statement

\forall x\in A:\forall y\in C:\varphi(x)\leq r<s\leq\varphi(y)
is empty. WLOG
A\neq\emptyset
and
C\neq\emptyset
. We have that

B:=C-A
is by
3.4
closed and by hypothesis we have

0\notin B
. Since
V
is locally convex, there is in view of
2.13

a convex open set
D\subseteq V
with
0\in D
and
D\cap B=\emptyset
.
Since
B
is also convex, there is by Corollary
2.11
a
continuous
K
-linear function
\varphi\colon V\to\mathbb{R}
and an
\varepsilon\in\mathbb{R}
such that

\varphi(x)<\varepsilon\leq\varphi(y)
for all
x\in D
and
y\in B
. In particular,
\varepsilon>\varphi(0)=0

and
\varphi(x)+\varepsilon\leq\varphi(y)
for all
x\in A
and
y\in C
. Because of

A\neq\emptyset\neq C
,
r:=\sup\{\varphi(x)\mid x\in A\}\in\mathbb{R}
and

s:=\inf\{\varphi(y)\mid y\in C\}\in\mathbb{R}
exist. Moreover, we have
r+\varepsilon\leq s
, i.e.,

r<s
.
∎

Definition 3.6
.

Let
V
be a
K
-vector space and
A\subseteq V
be convex. Then a convex set
F\subseteq A
is called a
face
of
A
if for all
x,y\in A

with
\frac{x+y}{2}\in F
, we have also
x,y\in F
.

Proposition 3.7
.

Suppose
V
is a
K
-vector space,
A\subseteq V
is convex and

x\in A
. Then
x
is an extreme point of
A

[
\to

4.1
]

if and only if
\{x\}
is a face of
A
.

Proof.

\displaystyle x\text{ is an extreme point of }A

\displaystyle\overset{\ref{dfconv}}{\iff}

\displaystyle\nexists y,z\in A:\left(y\neq z\mathbin{\&}x=\frac{y+z}{2}\right)

\displaystyle\iff

\displaystyle\forall y,z\in A:\left(x=\frac{y+z}{2}\implies y=z\right)

\displaystyle\iff

\displaystyle\forall y,z\in A:\left(x=\frac{y+z}{2}\implies y=z=x\right)

\displaystyle\iff

\displaystyle\forall y,z\in A:\left(\frac{y+z}{2}\in\{x\}\implies y,z\in\{x\}\right)

∎

Proposition 3.8
.

[
\to

4.2
]

Suppose
V
is a
K
-vector space,
A\subseteq V
is convex,
F\subseteq A

is convex and
\lambda\in(0,1)_{K}
. Then the following are equivalent:

(a)

F
is a face of
A

(b)

\forall x,y\in A:(\lambda x+(1-\lambda)y\in F\implies x,y\in F)

Proof.

(b)
\implies
(a)
is an easy exercise.

(a)
\implies
(b)
Assume that
F
is a face of
A
but there are

x,y\in A
such that

\lambda x+(1-\lambda)y\in F

and WLOG
(otherwise permute
x
and
y
and replace
\lambda
by
1-\lambda
)

x\notin F
. If
\lambda<\frac{1}{2}
, one then can replace

(x,\lambda)
by
(x^{\prime},\lambda^{\prime})
where
x^{\prime}:=\frac{x+y}{2}
and
\lambda^{\prime}:=2\lambda\in(0,1)_{K}
because
we then have
x^{\prime}\in A\setminus F
(since
A
is convex and
F
is a face of
A
),

\lambda^{\prime}\in(0,1)_{K}
and

\lambda^{\prime}x^{\prime}+(1-\lambda^{\prime})y=2\lambda\frac{x+y}{2}+(1-2\lambda)y=\lambda x+(1-\lambda)y\in F.

By iterating this in case of need finitely many times, one can suppose

\lambda\geq\frac{1}{2}
. Then

z:=x+2((\lambda x+(1-\lambda)y)-x)=(2\lambda-1)x+2(1-\lambda)y\in A

since

2\lambda-1\geq 0
,
2(1-\lambda)\geq 0
and
(2\lambda-1)+2(1-\lambda)=1
.
Now

\frac{x+z}{2}=x+(\lambda x+(1-\lambda)y)-x=\lambda x+(1-\lambda)y\in F

and thus

x,z\in F
since
F
is a face of
A

\lightning
.
∎

Example 3.9
.

(a)

If
V
is a
K
-vector space and
A\subseteq V
is convex, then both

\emptyset
and
A
are faces of
A
. We call these the
trivial

faces of
A
.

(b)

The faces of
[0,1]^{2}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{2}
are

\emptyset
,
\{(0,0)\}
,
\{(0,1)\}
,
\{(1,0)\}
,
\{(1,1)\}
,
\{0\}\times[0,1]
,

\{1\}\times[0,1]
,
[0,1]\times\{0\}
,
[0,1]\times\{1\}
,
[0,1]^{2}
.

(c)

The faces of
B:=\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{2}\mid\|x\|\leq 1\}
are
\emptyset
,

\{x\}
(
x\in B\setminus B^{\circ}
) and
B
.

(d)

\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{2}\mid\|x\|<1\}
has only the trivial faces.

Definition and Proposition 3.10
.

Let
V
be a
K
-vector space and suppose
A\subseteq V
is convex.
We call
F
an
exposed face
of
A
if there is a
K
-linear function

\varphi\colon V\to\mathbb{R}
such that

F=\{x\in A\mid\forall y\in A:\varphi(x)\leq\varphi(y)\}.

Every exposed face of
A
is a face of
A
.

Proof.

Let
F
be an exposed face of
A
. To show:
F
is a face of
A
.
It is easy to show that
F
is convex.
Choose a
K
-linear
\varphi\colon V\to\mathbb{R}
such that

F=\{x\in A\mid\forall y\in A:\varphi(x)\leq\varphi(y)\}
.
Let
x,y\in A
such that
\frac{x+y}{2}\in F
. To show:
x,y\in F
.
It is obviously enough to show that
\varphi(x)=\varphi\left(\frac{x+y}{2}\right)=\varphi(y)
.
We have that

\varphi(x)+\varphi(y)=\varphi\left(\frac{x+y}{2}\right)+\varphi\left(\frac{x+y}{2}\right)\overset{x,y\in A}{\underset{\frac{x+y}{2}\in F}{\leq}}\varphi(x)+\varphi(y)

where the inequality would be strict if one of
\varphi(x)
and
\varphi(y)
were different
from
\varphi\left(\frac{x+y}{2}\right)
.
∎

Example 3.11
.

[
\to

3.9
]

(a)

If
V
is a
K
-vector space and
A\subseteq V
is convex, then
A
is an
exposed face of
A
while
\emptyset
might be exposed
[
\to

3.9
(d)] or non-exposed [
3.9
(c)].

(b)

All faces of
[0,1]^{2}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{2}
are exposed except
\emptyset
.

(c)

All faces of
\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{2}\mid\|x\|\leq 1\}
are exposed except
\emptyset
.

(d)

All faces of
\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{2}\mid\|x\|<1\}
are exposed.

(e)

((-\infty,0]\times[0,\infty))\cup\{(x,y)\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{2}\mid y\geq x^{2}\}
has exactly
one nonexposed face, namely
\{0\}
.

Proposition 3.12
.

Suppose
V
is a
K
-vector space,
A\subseteq V
is convex,
F
is a face of
A

and
G\subseteq F
. Then the following holds:

\text{$G$ is a face of $F$}\iff\text{$G$ is a face of $A$}.

Proof.

“
\Longrightarrow
” Let
G
be a face of
F
and let
x,y\in A
with

\frac{x+y}{2}\in G
. To show:
x,y\in G
. Because of
\frac{x+y}{2}\in G\subseteq F
,
we have
x,y\in F
. Since
G
is a face of
F
, it follows that
x,y\in G
.

“
\Longleftarrow
” Let
G
be a face of
A
and let
x,y\in F
with

\frac{x+y}{2}\in G
. Because of
x,y\in F\subseteq A
, we then have
x,y\in G
.
∎

Remark 3.13
.

Every intersection of faces (except possible
\bigcap\emptyset:=V
) of a convex set in a
K
-vector space
V
is obviously
again a face of this convex set.

Lemma 3.14
.

Let
C\neq\emptyset
be a compact convex
subset of a locally convex
K
-vector
space
V
. Then
C
possesses an extreme point.

Proof.

Every intersection of a nonempty chain of closed nonempty faces of
C
is
again a closed nonempty face of
C
. Indeed, if the intersection were empty,
then a finite subintersection would be empty by the compactness of
C

[
\to

1.14
] which is impossible since we dealt with a chain. By
Zorn’s lemma there is thus a minimal closed nonempty face
F
of
C
.
Being a closed subset of a compact set,
F
is compact itself [
\to

1.21
]. By

3.7
, it suffices to show that
\#F=1
. Due to

F\neq\emptyset
, it suffices to exclude
\#F\geq 2
. Assume
x,y\in F
such that

x\neq y
. By
3.5
, there is a continuous
K
-linear function

\varphi\colon V\to\mathbb{R}
such that
\varphi(x)<\varphi(y)
. Then

G:=\{v\in F\mid\forall w\in F:\varphi(v)\leq\varphi(w)\}

is nonempty by
1.19

because
F
is compact and nonempty and
\varphi
is continuous.
According to
3.10
,
G
is an (exposed) face of
F
.
Hence
G
is a face of
C
by
3.12
. From the continuity of
\varphi
,
we deduce that

G=F\cap\bigcap_{w\in F}\varphi^{-1}((-\infty,\varphi(w)])

is closed.
Moreover,
y\notin G
since
\varphi(y)\not\leq\varphi(x)
. Therefore
G
is a closed
nonempty face of
C
that is properly contained in
F
, contradicting the
minimality of
F
.
∎

Notation 3.15
.

Let
A
be a convex set in a
K
-vector space
V
. Then we write

\extr A

for the set of extreme points of
A
.

Theorem 3.16
.

[
\to

1.19
]

Suppose
C
is a nonempty compact convex subset of a locally convex

K
-vector space
V
and
\varphi\colon V\to\mathbb{R}
is a continuous
K
-linear function.
Then
\varphi
attains on
C
a minimum and a maximum in an extreme point of
C
.
In other words, there are
x,y\in\extr C
such that

\varphi(x)\leq\varphi(z)\leq\varphi(y)

for all
z\in C
.

Proof.

Since one could replace
\varphi
by
-\varphi
, we show only the existence of

x\in\extr C
such that
\varphi(x)\leq\varphi(z)
for all
z\in C
. By
1.19
,
there is
y\in C
such that
\varphi(y)\leq\varphi(z)
for all
z\in C
, i.e., the exposed face
[
\to

3.10
]

F:=\{y\in C\mid\forall z\in C:\varphi(y)\leq\varphi(z)\}

of
C
is nonempty. Since
\varphi
is continuous,

F=C\cap\bigcap_{z\in C}\varphi^{-1}((-\infty,\varphi(z)]_{\mathbb{R}})

is a closed subset of the
compact set
C
and hence compact itself. By Lemma
3.14
,

F
possesses an extreme point
x
which is by
3.12
and

3.7
also an extreme point of
C
.
∎

Corollary 3.17

(Krein–Milman theorem)
.

Suppose
C
is a compact convex subset of a locally convex
K
-vector space

V
. Then
C
is the closure of the convex hull of the set of its extreme points,
i.e.,

C=\overline{\conv(\extr C)}.

Proof.

“
\supseteq
” From
\extr C\subseteq C
and the convexity of
C
, we get

\conv(\extr C)\subseteq C
. Being a compact subset of a Hausdorff space
[
\to

2.19
],
C
is closed [
\to

2.16
] which entails even

\overline{\conv(\extr C)}\subseteq C
.

“
\subseteq
” WLOG
C\neq\emptyset
.

A:=\overline{\conv(\extr C)}
is closed, nonempty by Lemma

3.14
and convex by
2.13
. We show

V\setminus A\subseteq V\setminus C
. Let
x\in V\setminus A
. By the
separation theorem for locally convex vector spaces
3.5
,
there is a continuous
K
-linear function
\varphi\colon V\to\mathbb{R}
such that

\varphi(y)<\varphi(x)
for all
y\in A
. By
3.16
, there is

y\in\extr C\subseteq A
satisfying
\varphi(z)\leq\varphi(y)
for all
z\in C
.
It follows that
\varphi(z)\leq\varphi(y)<\varphi(x)
for all
z\in C
. Therefore
x\notin C
.
∎

Definition 3.18
.

Let
V
be a
K
-vector space,
C\subseteq V
and
u\in V
.
We call an extreme point [
\to

4.1
] of the state space
S(V,C,u)

[
\to

1.9
,
1.16
] a
pure state
of
(V,C,u)
.

Theorem 3.19

(Strengthening of
1.20
)
.

Suppose
u
is a unit for the cone
C
in the
K
-vector space
V
and
let
x\in V
. Then the following are equivalent:

(a)

\forall\varphi\in\extr S(V,C,u):\varphi(x)>0

(b)

\forall\varphi\in S(V,C,u):\varphi(x)>0

(c)

\exists N\in\mathbb{N}:x\in\frac{1}{N}u+C

(d)

x
is a unit for
C
.

Proof.

(b)
\iff
(c)
\iff
(d)
is
1.20
.

(b)
\implies
(a)
is trivial.

(a)
\implies
(b)
WLOG
S(V,C,u)\neq\emptyset
. It suffices to
show that the function

S(V,C,u)\to\mathbb{R},\ \varphi\mapsto\varphi(x)

attains a minimum in an
extreme point of
S(V,C,u)
. But this follows from
3.16
because

S(V,C,u)
is a nonempty compact [
\to

1.17
] convex
[
\to

1.16
] subset of the locally convex [
\to

2.2
(a)]

\mathbb{R}
-vector space
\mathbb{R}^{V}
and

\mathbb{R}^{V}\to\mathbb{R},\ \varphi\mapsto\varphi(x)

is continuous
[
\to

1.5
(b)].
∎

Corollary 3.20

(Strengthening of
1.15
)
.

Suppose
u
is a unit for the cone
C
in the
K
-vector space
V
and
let
x\in V
. If
\varphi(x)>0
for all pure states
\varphi
of
(V,C,u)
, then

x\in C
.

4
Convex sets in finite-dimensional vector spaces

Lemma 4.1
.

Let
C
be a cone in a finite-dimensional
K
-vector space
V
.
Then
U:=C-C
is a subspace of
V
and
C
possesses
in
U

a unit [
\to

1.4
].

Proof.

On the basis of Definition
1.1
, it is easy to see that
U
is a subspace of

V
. Choose a basis
u_{1},\dots,u_{m}
of
U
and write
u_{i}=v_{i}-w_{i}
with

v_{i},w_{i}\in C
for
i\in\{1,\dots,m\}
. We show that

u:=\sum_{i=1}^{m}v_{i}+\sum_{i=1}^{m}w_{i}\in C
is a unit for
C

in
U
.
For this purpose, fix
v\in U
. To show:
\exists N\in\mathbb{N}:Nu+v\in C
. Write

v=\sum_{i=1}^{m}\lambda_{i}u_{i}
with
\lambda_{i}\in K
for
i\in\{1,\dots,m\}
. Choose

N\in\mathbb{N}
with
N\geq|\lambda_{i}|
for
i\in\{1,\dots,m\}
. Then

Nu+v=\sum_{i=1}^{m}\underbrace{(N+\lambda_{i})}_{\geq 0}v_{i}+\sum_{i=1}^{m}\underbrace{(N-\lambda_{i})}_{\geq 0}w_{i}\in C.

∎

Theorem 4.2

(Finite-dimensional isolation theorem)
.

[
\to

1.13
]

Let
C
be a proper cone in the finite-dimensional
K
-vector space
V
.
Then there is a
K
-linear function
\varphi\colon V\to\mathbb{R}
with
\varphi\neq 0
and

\varphi(C)\subseteq\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
.

Proof.

U:=C-C
is by
4.1
a subspace of
V
.

Case 1:

C=U

Then
U
is a proper subspace of
V
and by linear algebra it is easy to see that
there is some
\varphi\in V^{*}\setminus\{0\}
such that
\varphi(U)=\{0\}
.

Case 2:

C\neq U

By
4.1
, there exists a unit
u
for
C
in
U
. The isolation theorem

1.13
provides us with some
\varphi_{0}\in S(U,C,u)
. Extend
\varphi_{0}

by linear algebra to a
K
-linear function
\varphi\colon V\to\mathbb{R}
.
∎

Remark 4.3
.

Example
1.10
shows that one cannot omit the hypothesis

\dim V<\infty
in
4.1
and
4.2
.

Theorem 4.4

(Separation theorem for finite-dimensional vector spaces)
.

[
\to

2.10
]

Let
A
and
B
be convex sets in the finite-dimensional
K
-vector space
V

such that
A\neq\emptyset\neq B
and
A\cap B=\emptyset
. Then there is a

K
-linear function
\varphi\colon V\to\mathbb{R}
such that
\varphi\neq 0
and

\varphi(x)\leq\varphi(y)
for all
x\in A
and
y\in B
.

Proof.

Completely analogous to the proof of
2.10
.
∎

Definition 4.5
.

[
\to

4.1
]
Let
V
be a
K
-vector space and
A\subseteq V
. Then
A
is called an

affine subspace of
V
if
\forall x,y\in A:\forall\lambda\in K:\lambda x+(1-\lambda)y\in A
.
The smallest affine subspace of
V
containing
A
is obviously

\aff A:=\left\{\sum_{i=1}^{m}\lambda_{i}x_{i}\mid m\in\mathbb{N},\lambda_{i}\in K,x_{i}\in A,\sum_{i=1}^{m}\lambda_{i}=1\right\},

called the affine subspace generated by
A
or the
affine hull
of
A
.

Definition and Proposition 4.6
.

Let
V
be a
K
-vector space. Then for each
A\subseteq V
,
the following are equivalent:

(a)

A
is a nonempty affine subspace of
V
.

(b)

There is an
x\in V
and a subspace
U
of
V
such that
A=x+U
.

If these conditions are met, then
U
as in
(b)

is uniquely determined and is called
the
direction
of
A
. Then
\dim A:=\dim U\in\mathbb{N}_{0}\cup\{\infty\}
is the

dimension
of
A
. We set
\dim\emptyset:=-1
.

Proof.

(b)
\implies
(a)
is easy.

(a)
\implies
(b)
Suppose that (a) holds. Choose
x\in A
. Set

U:=A-x
. To show:
U+U\subseteq U
and
KU\subseteq U
. Let
u,v\in U

and
\lambda\in K
. To show:
u+v\in U
and
\lambda u\in U
. Choose
a,b\in A
such that

u=a-x
and
v=b-x
. Then
u+v=(1a+1b+(-1)x)-x\in(\aff A)-x=A-x=U
and

\lambda u=(\lambda a+(1-\lambda)x)-x\in(\aff A)-x=A-x=U
.

Uniqueness claim
Whenever
x,y\in V
and
U
and
W
are
subspaces of
V
satisfying
x+U=y+W
, then
x-y\in W
and thus

U=(y-x)+W=W
.
∎

Definition 4.7
.

Let
V
be a
K
-vector space and
A\subseteq V
be convex. Then

\dim A:=\dim\aff A\in\{-1\}\cup\mathbb{N}_{0}\cup\{\infty\}

is the
dimension
of
A
.

Proposition 4.8
.

[
\to

3.8
]

Suppose that
V
is a
K
-vector space,
A\subseteq V
is convex and

F
is a face of
A
. Let
m\in\mathbb{N}
,
x_{1},\dots,x_{m}\in A
and

\lambda_{1},\dots,\lambda_{m}\in K_{>0}
such that
\sum_{i=1}^{m}\lambda_{i}=1
and

\sum_{i=1}^{m}\lambda_{i}x_{i}\in F
. Then
x_{1},\dots,x_{m}\in F
.

Proof.

WLOG
m\geq 2
. Let
i\in\{1,\dots,m\}
. To show:
x_{i}\in F
.
WLOG
i=1
. We have
0<\lambda_{1}<1
and
y:=\sum_{i=2}^{m}\frac{\lambda_{i}}{1-\lambda_{1}}x_{i}\in A
since
\sum_{i=2}^{m}\frac{\lambda_{i}}{1-\lambda_{1}}=\frac{1-\lambda_{1}}{1-\lambda_{1}}=1
.
From
\sum_{i=1}^{m}\lambda_{i}x_{i}=\lambda_{1}x_{1}+(1-\lambda_{1})y
it follows thus by

3.8
that
x_{1},y\in F
.
∎

Proposition 4.9
.

Suppose
V
is a
K
-vector space,
A\subseteq V
is convex and
F
is a
face of
A
. Then
F=(\aff F)\cap A
.

Proof.

“
\subseteq
” is trivial.

“
\supseteq
” Let
x\in(\aff F)\cap A
. To show:
x\in F
. Write

x=\sum_{i=1}^{m}\lambda_{i}y_{i}-\sum_{j=1}^{n}\mu_{j}z_{j}
with
m,n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
,

\lambda_{i},\mu_{j}\in K_{>0}
,
y_{i},z_{j}\in F
and

\sum_{i=1}^{m}\lambda_{i}-\sum_{j=1}^{n}\mu_{j}=1
. Setting
\lambda:=\sum_{i=1}^{m}\lambda_{i}
and

\mu:=\sum_{j=1}^{n}\mu_{j}
, it follows that

\frac{1}{1+\mu}x+\sum_{j=1}^{n}\frac{\mu_{j}}{1+\mu}z_{j}=\sum_{i=1}^{m}\frac{\lambda_{i}}{\lambda}y_{i}\in F

and thus
x\in F
by
4.8
.
∎

Proposition 4.10
.

Let
V
be a finite-dimensional
K
-vector space.

(a)

If
A
and
B
are affine subspaces of
V
with
A\subseteq B
, then

A=B\iff\dim A=\dim B.

(b)

If
F
and
G
are faces of the convex set
A\subseteq V
with

F\subseteq G
, then

F=G\iff\dim F=\dim G.

Proof.

(a) follows from
4.6
by linear algebra and (b) follows hereof by

4.7
and
4.9
.
∎

Remark 4.11
.

Suppose
V
is a topological
K
-vector space,
K^{\prime}
is a subfield of
K
and
V^{\prime}

a
K^{\prime}
-vector subspace of the
K^{\prime}
-vector space
V
. Then
V
induces on

V^{\prime}
a vector space topology. This is easy to see since
V\times V
induces
on
V^{\prime}\times V^{\prime}
the product topology of the induced topologies and

K\times V
induces on
K^{\prime}\times V^{\prime}
the product topology of the induced topologies.

Definition and Proposition 4.12
.

Let
A
be a convex set in the topological
K
-vector space
V
. The interior of

A
in the topological space
\aff A
(endowed with the topology induced by
V
)
is called the
relative interior
of
A
, denoted by

\relint A
. This is a convex set.

Proof.

WLOG
A\neq\emptyset
. Write
\aff A=x+U
for some
x\in V
and some
subspace
U
of
V
[
\to

4.6
]. WLOG
x=0
[
\to

2.6
].
WLOG
U=V
[
\to

4.11
]. Then

\relint A=A^{\circ}
is convex by
2.13
.
∎

Remark 4.13
.

Let
V
be a finite-dimensional
K
-vector space. Choose a basis
v_{1},\dots,v_{n}

of
V
. Then
f\colon K^{n}\to V,\ x\mapsto\sum_{i=1}^{n}x_{i}v_{i}
is a vector space
isomorphism that is continuous with respect to every vector space topology of

V
[
\to

2.17
] and that is a homeomorphism with respect to exactly
one vector space topology of
V
[
\to

2.2
(a)]. Consequently, there
is a finest [
\to

1.2
(c)] vector space topology on
V

(cf. also
2.20
). With respect to this topology on
V
, we have for all

A\subseteq V
that

\text{$A$ open in $V$}\iff\text{$f^{-1}(A)$ open in $K^{n}$},

independently of the choice of the basis
v_{1},\dots,v_{n}
. It is easy to see that

K^{n}
carries the initial topology with respect to all

\begin{Bmatrix}\text{linear forms on $K^{n}$}\\
\text{$K$-linear functions $K^{n}\to\mathbb{R}$}\end{Bmatrix}
.
The finest vector space topology on
V
is therefore also the initial topology
[
\to

1.4
] with respect to all

\begin{Bmatrix}\text{linear forms on $V$}\\
\text{$K$-linear functions $V\to\mathbb{R}$}\end{Bmatrix}
.
If
U
is a subspace of
V
, then the finest vector space topology of
V
induces
on
U
again the finest vector space topology because one can extend every
linear form on
U
to one on
V
.

Example 4.14
.

Since
\mathbb{R}
is a topological
\mathbb{R}
-vector space and thus also a topological

\mathbb{Q}
-vector space, also
\mathbb{Q}+\sqrt{2}\mathbb{Q}\subseteq\mathbb{R}
is a topological

\mathbb{Q}
-vector space with respect to the induced topology [
\to

4.11
].
One sees easily that

\mathbb{Q}+\sqrt{2}\mathbb{Q}\to\mathbb{Q},\ x+\sqrt{2}y\mapsto x\qquad(x,y\in\mathbb{Q})

is not continuous.

Lemma 4.15
.

Let
A\subseteq K^{n}
be convex. Then

A^{\circ}=\emptyset\implies\aff A\neq K^{n}
.

Proof.

Suppose that
\aff A=K^{n}
. We show that
A^{\circ}\neq\emptyset
. Denote by

e_{1},\dots,e_{n}
the standard basis of
K^{n}
and set
e_{0}:=0\in K^{n}
.
Write
e_{i}=\sum_{j=1}^{m}\lambda_{ij}x_{ij}
with
m\in\mathbb{N}
,
\lambda_{ij}\in K
,

x_{ij}\in A
and
\sum_{j=1}^{m}\lambda_{ij}=1
for
i\in\{0,\dots,n\}
.
We show that

x:=\sum_{i=0}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{m}\frac{1}{m(n+1)}x_{ij}\in A^{\circ}.

Since
A
is convex, we have
x\in A
and it suffices to show that for each

i\in\{1,\dots,n\}
, there is an
\varepsilon\in K_{>0}
such that
x\pm\varepsilon e_{i}\in A

(cf. also
1.8
). For this purpose, fix
i\in\{1,\dots,n\}
.
From
e_{i}=e_{i}-0=e_{i}-e_{0}=\sum_{j=1}^{m}\lambda_{ij}x_{ij}+\sum_{j=1}^{m}(-\lambda_{0j})x_{0j}

and
\sum_{j=1}^{m}\lambda_{ij}-\sum_{j=1}^{m}\lambda_{0j}=1-1=0
, the existence of such an
\varepsilon>0
easily follows.
∎

Theorem 4.16
.

Suppose
V
is a finite-dimensional topological

K
-vector space that is equipped
with the finest vector space topology
[
\to

4.13
]

and
A\subseteq V
is convex. Then

A\subseteq\overline{\relint A}
.

Proof.

WLOG
A\neq\emptyset
. Write
\aff A=x+U
for some
x\in V
and some
subspace
U
of
V
. Obviously,
\aff(A-x)\overset{\ref{affinehull}}{=}(\aff A)-x=U
,
\relint(A-x)\overset{\ref{transhomeo}}{=}(\relint A)-x
and

\overline{\relint(A-x)}=\overline{\relint A}-x
.
Replacing
A
by
A-x
, we can thus suppose that
\aff A=U
.
Using the last remark from
4.13
,
we can therefore suppose that

\aff A=V
(otherwise replace
V
by
U
).
According to
4.13
,
we can reduce to the case where
V=K^{n}
(with the product topology).
We have to show
A\subseteq\overline{A^{\circ}}
. Choose
y\in A^{\circ}
with Lemma
4.15
.
Let
x\in A
. To show:
x\in\overline{A^{\circ}}
. By
2.12
, we have

(1-\lambda)x+\lambda y\in A^{\circ}
for all
\lambda\in(0,1]_{K}
. Obviously, we have

x\in\overline{\{(1-\lambda)x+\lambda y\mid\lambda\in(0,1]_{K}\}}\subseteq\overline{A^{\circ}}
.
∎

Theorem 4.17
.

Let
V
be a finite-dimensional
K
-vector space that is equipped
with the finest vector space topology
[
\to

4.13
]
.
Let
A\subseteq V

be convex and
x\in A\setminus\relint A
. Then there is an exposed face
F
of

A
satisfying
\dim F<\dim A
and
x\in F
.

Proof.

Similarly to the proof of
4.16
, we reduce again
to the case
\aff A=V
. Note that
A^{\circ}
is convex
[
\to

4.12
] and nonempty [
\to

4.16
]. The separation
theorem
4.4
yields a
K
-linear function
\varphi\colon V\to\mathbb{R}

with
\varphi\neq 0
and
\varphi(x)\leq\varphi(y)
for all
y\in A^{\circ}
.
Since
\varphi
is continuous [
\to

4.13
], the set

\varphi^{-1}([\varphi(x),\infty)_{\mathbb{R}})
is closed and contains with

A^{\circ}
also
\overline{A^{\circ}}
and hence by
4.16
also

A
, i.e.,
\varphi(x)\leq\varphi(y)
for all
y\in A
.
In other words,
x
is an element of the exposed face
[
\to

3.10
]
F:=\{z\in A\mid\forall y\in A:\varphi(z)\leq\varphi(y)\}
of
A
.
By
4.10
, it is enough to show
F\neq A
. If we had

F=A
, we would have
\varphi|_{A}=\varphi(x)
and hence by linearity of
\varphi

also
\varphi=\varphi|_{\aff A}=\varphi(x)
, i.e.,
\varphi=0

\lightning
.
∎

Remark 4.18
.

If we use topological notions in a finite-dimensional
\mathbb{R}
-vector space
V
, then
we tacitly furnish
V
with its unique vector space topology [
\to

2.20
]
which is the initial topology with respect to the family of all linear forms on
V

[
\to

4.13
].

Theorem 4.19

(Minkowski’s theorem)
.

[
\to

4.4
,
3.17
]

Let
V
be a finite-dimensional
\mathbb{R}
-vector space. Let

A\subseteq V
be convex and compact. Then

A=\conv(\extr A).

Proof.

Since
A
is closed [
\to

2.16
], all faces of
A
are also closed
[
\to

4.9
,
4.6
,
2.6
] and therefore compact
[
\to

1.21
]. By induction, we can thus
assume that
F=\conv(\extr F)
for all faces
F
of
A
that satisfy

\dim F<\dim A
.

“
\supseteq
” is trivial.

“
\subseteq
” Let
x\in A
. To show:
x\in\conv(\extr A)
. WLOG
x\notin\extr A
.
Choose
y,z\in A
with
y\neq z
and
x\in\conv\{y,z\}
. Because of the
assumptions on
A
, WLOG
y,z\in A\setminus\relint A
. By
4.17
,
there are (exposed) faces
F
and
G
of
A
such that
\dim F<\dim A
,

\dim G<\dim A
,
y\in F
and
z\in G
.
From
3.7
and
3.12
, we get
\extr F\subseteq\extr A

and
\extr G\subseteq\extr A
. Consequently,

y\in F=\conv(\extr F)\subseteq\conv(\extr A)
and

z\in G=\conv(\extr G)\subseteq\conv(\extr A)
where the equalities follow from the induction hypothesis.
Finally,
x\in\conv\{y,z\}\subseteq\conv(\extr A)
.
∎

Theorem 4.20
.

Let
(K,\leq)
be an arbitrary ordered field, let
V
be a
K
-vector space
with
n:=\dim V<\infty
. Suppose that

E\subseteq V
is a finite set that generates
V
and
x\in V
.
Then exactly one of the following conditions occurs:

(a)

x
is a nonnegative linear combination of elements of
E
that form a basis of
V
.

(b)

There is some
\ell\in V^{*}
with
\ell(E)\subseteq K_{\geq 0}
and
\ell(x)<0

and a linearly independent set
F\subseteq E\cap\ker\ell
with
\#F=n-1
.

Proof.

It is easy to see that (a) and (b) cannot occur both at the same time. Indeed, from
(a) it follows that
x\in\sum_{v\in E}K_{\geq 0}v
which is not compatible with (b)
because if
\ell\in V^{*}
with
\ell(E)\subseteq K_{\geq 0}
, then

\ell(x)\in\ell\left(\sum_{v\in E}K_{\geq 0}v\right)\subseteq K_{\geq 0}
.

We choose an order
\leq
on
E
[
\to

1.1
] and a basis

B\subseteq E
of
V
. We show that the following algorithm always terminates:

(1)

Write
x=\sum_{v\in B}\lambda_{v}v
with
\lambda_{v}\in K
for all
v\in B
.

(2)

If
\lambda_{v}\geq 0
for all
v\in B
, then stop since (a) occurs.

(3)

u:=\min\{v\in B\mid\lambda_{v}<0\}

(4)

Define
\ell\in V^{*}
by
\ell(u)=1
and
\ell(v)=0
for all

v\in B\setminus\{u\}
(so that
\ell(x)=\lambda_{u}<0
).

(5)

If
\ell(E)\subseteq K_{\geq 0}
, then stop since (b) occurs.

(6)

w:=\min\{v\in E\mid\ell(v)<0\}

(7)

Replace
B
by the new basis
(B\setminus\{u\})\cup\{w\}
and
go to (1).

Observe first of all that in Step (7) the set

(B\setminus\{u\})\cup\{w\}
is again a basis
since
B
is one. Indeed,
w
does not lie in the subspace generated by

B\setminus\{u\}
since
\ell
vanishes according to its choice in (4) on this
subspace while it does not vanish on
w
by the choice of
w
in (6).

To show that this algorithm terminates, we assume that this is not the case. Let
then denote by
(B_{k},u_{k},w_{k},\ell_{k})
the value of
(B,u,w,\ell)
after Step (6)
in the
k
-th iteration of the loop. We first argue that the existence of

s,t\in\mathbb{N}
with

(*)\qquad u_{t}\leq u_{s}=w_{t}\text{ and }\{v\in B_{s}\mid v>u_{s}\}=\{v\in B_{t}\mid v>u_{s}\}

causes a contradiction. For this purpose, let
x=\sum_{v\in B_{s}}\lambda_{v}v
with

\lambda_{v}\in K
for all
v\in B_{s}
be the representation of
x
from the
s
-th
iteration of the loop. We will apply
\ell_{t}
to this representation of
x
.
For that matter, observe the following:

•

For all
v\in B_{s}
with
v<u_{s}=w_{t}
, we have by the assignment to
u_{s}

in (3) that
\lambda_{v}\geq 0
.

•

For all
v\in E\supseteq B_{s}
with
v<u_{s}=w_{t}
, we have by the
assignment to
w_{t}
in (6) that
\ell_{t}(v)\geq 0
.

•

\lambda_{u_{s}}<0
according to (3)

•

\ell_{t}(u_{s})=\ell_{t}(w_{t})<0
according to (6)

•

For all
v\in B_{s}
with
v>u_{s}=w_{t}
, we have
\ell_{t}(v)=0
since for these

v
we have by
(*)
that
v\in B_{t}\setminus\{u_{t}\}

(using that
u_{t}\leq u_{s}
) and thus
\ell_{t}(v)=0
by (4).

It thus follows that

0\overset{(4)}{>}\ell_{t}(x)=\underbrace{\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}v\in B_{s}\\
v<u_{s}\end{subarray}}\underbrace{\lambda_{v}}_{\geq 0}\underbrace{\ell_{t}(v)}_{\geq 0}}_{\geq 0}+\underbrace{\underbrace{\lambda_{u_{s}}}_{<0}\underbrace{\ell_{t}(u_{s})}_{<0}}_{>0}+\underbrace{\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}v\in B_{s}\\
v>u_{s}\end{subarray}}\lambda_{v}\underbrace{\ell_{t}(v)}_{=0}}_{=0}>0

which is the desired contradiction.

Finally, we show the existence of
s,t\in\mathbb{N}
with
(*)
. For clarity, we first
generalize the algorithm by looking at the following more abstract version of it:

Suppose
E
is a finite set,
\leq
an order on
E
and
B
a subset of
E
.

(1’)

Choose
u\in B
.

(2’)

Choose
w\in E\setminus B
.

(3’)

Replace
B
by
(B\setminus\{u\})\cup\{w\}
and go to (1’).

Denote by
(B_{k},u_{k},w_{k})
the value of
(B,u,w)
after Step (2’)
in the
k
-th iteration of the algorithm. We show the existence of
s,t\in\mathbb{N}

satisfying
(*)
. Since
E
is finite, the power set of
E
is also finite.
Consequently, there are
p,q\in\mathbb{N}
such that
p<q
and
B_{p}=B_{q}
.
Because of (3’), it then obviously holds that
\{u_{s}\mid p\leq s<q\}=\{w_{t}\mid p\leq t<q\}
. Set
v_{0}:=\max\{u_{s}\mid p\leq s<q\}=\max\{w_{t}\mid p\leq t<q\}
. Then

\{v\in B_{s}\mid v>v_{0}\}=\{v\in B_{t}\mid v>v_{0}\}

for all
s,t\in\{p,\dots,q-1\}
. Choose
s,t\in\{p,\dots,q-1\}
with
u_{s}=v_{0}=w_{t}

(note that
s<t
or
t<s
but certainly not
s=t
). Now

(*)
holds.
∎

Corollary 4.21
.

[
\to

3.2
]

Let
(K,\leq)
be an arbitrary ordered field. Let
m,n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
,

f,\ell_{1},\dots,\ell_{m}\in K[X_{1},\dots,X_{n}]
be linear forms

[
\to

6.1
(a)]
and set

S:=\{x\in K^{n}\mid\ell_{1}(x)\geq 0,\ldots,\ell_{m}(x)\geq 0\}.

Then the following are
equivalent:

(a)

f\geq 0
on
S

(b)

f\in K_{\geq 0}\ell_{1}+\ldots+K_{\geq 0}\ell_{m}

(c)

There are
i_{1},\dots,i_{s}\in\{1,\dots,m\}
such that
\ell_{i_{1}},\dots,\ell_{i_{s}}
are linearly independent and

f\in K_{\geq 0}\ell_{i_{1}}+\ldots+K_{\geq 0}\ell_{i_{s}}.

Proof.

(c)
\implies
(b)
\implies
(a)
is trivial.

(a)
\implies
(c)
Suppose that (a) holds.

Claim:

f\in V:=K\ell_{1}+\ldots+K\ell_{m}

Explanation.
Assume
f\notin V
. Then there is some

\varphi\in(KX_{1}+\ldots+KX_{n})^{*}
such that
\varphi(\ell_{1})=\ldots=\varphi(\ell_{m})=0

and
\varphi(f)=-1
. Set
x:=(\varphi(X_{1}),\ldots,\varphi(X_{n}))\in K^{n}
. Then

\ell_{i}(x)=\varphi(\ell_{i})=0
for all
i\in\{1,\dots,m\}
. Hence
x\in S
and

f(x)=\varphi(f)=-1<0
.
\lightning

Now apply
4.20
to
V
and

E:=\{\ell_{1},\dots,\ell_{m}\}
(taking account of the claim). Then it suffices to show
that for all
\varphi\in V^{*}
with
\varphi(E)\subseteq K_{\geq 0}
also
\varphi(f)\geq 0
holds.
Thus let
\varphi\in V^{*}
with
\varphi(E)\subseteq K_{\geq 0}
. Choose

\psi\in(KX_{1}+\ldots+KX_{n})^{*}
with
\psi|_{V}=\varphi
.
Set
x:=(\psi(X_{1}),\ldots,\psi(X_{n}))\in K^{n}
. Then

\ell_{i}(x)=\psi(\ell_{i})=\varphi(\ell_{i})\geq 0
for all
i\in\{1,\dots,m\}
and thus

x\in S
and
\varphi(f)=\psi(f)=f(x)\geq 0
.
∎

Corollary 4.22

(Linear Nichtnegativstellensatz)
.

[
\to

3.5
,
7.7
]

Let
(K,\leq)
be an arbitrary ordered field.
Let
m,n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
,
f,\ell_{1},\dots,\ell_{m}\in K[X_{1},\dots,X_{n}]_{1}

[
\to

5.1
]
and suppose

S:=\{x\in K^{n}\mid\ell_{1}(x)\geq 0,\ldots,\ell_{m}(x)\geq 0\}\neq\emptyset.

Then the following are equivalent:

(a)

f\geq 0
on
S

(b)

f\in K_{\geq 0}+K_{\geq 0}\ell_{1}+\ldots+K_{\geq 0}\ell_{m}

(c)

There are
i_{1},\dots,i_{s}\in\{0,\dots,m\}
such that
\ell_{i_{1}},\ldots,\ell_{i_{s}}

are linearly independent and

f\in K_{\geq 0}\ell_{i_{1}}+\ldots+K_{\geq 0}\ell_{i_{s}}

where
\ell_{0}:=1
.

Proof.

(c)
\implies
(b)
\implies
(a)
is trivial.

(a)
\implies
(c)
Suppose that (a) holds. Due to

4.21
, it suffices to show that

f^{*}\geq 0
on

S^{*}:=\{x=(x_{0},\dots,x_{n})\in K^{n+1}\mid x_{0}\geq 0,\ell_{1}^{*}(x)\geq 0,\ldots,\ell_{m}^{*}(x)\geq 0\}
[
\to

2.1
(c)(d),
2.2
(e)].
To this end, let
(x_{0},\dots,x_{n})\in S^{*}
.

Case 1:

x_{0}>0

Then
\left(1,\frac{x_{1}}{x_{0}},\dots,\frac{x_{n}}{x_{0}}\right)=\frac{1}{x_{0}}(x_{0},\dots,x_{n})\in S^{*}
and hence
\left(\frac{x_{1}}{x_{0}},\ldots,\frac{x_{n}}{x_{0}}\right)\in S
.
From (a), it follows that

f^{*}\left(1,\frac{x_{1}}{x_{0}},\dots,\frac{x_{n}}{x_{0}}\right)=f\left(\frac{x_{1}}{x_{0}},\dots,\frac{x_{n}}{x_{0}}\right)\geq 0

and hence also

f^{*}(x_{0},\ldots,x_{n})=x_{0}f^{*}\left(1,\frac{x_{1}}{x_{0}},\dots,\frac{x_{n}}{x_{0}}\right)\geq 0

Case 2:

x_{0}=0

Then
(\lf(\ell_{i}))(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})\overset{\text{\ref{homdehom}(a)}}{=}\ell_{i}^{*}(x_{0},\dots,x_{n})\geq 0
and therefore

(\lf(\ell_{i}))(\lambda x_{1},\dots,\lambda x_{n})\geq 0

for all
i\in\{1,\dots,m\}
and

\lambda\in K_{\geq 0}
. Because of
S\neq\emptyset
, we can choose

(y_{1},\dots,y_{n})\in S
. Then
\ell_{i}(y_{1}+\lambda x_{1},\dots,y_{n}+\lambda x_{n})\geq 0

for all
\lambda\in K_{\geq 0}
and
i\in\{1,\dots,m\}
.
Due to (a), we have thus

f(y_{1}+\lambda x_{1},\dots,y_{n}+\lambda x_{n})\geq 0
for all
\lambda\in K_{\geq 0}
.
It follows that
(\lf(f))(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})\geq 0
. Hence

f^{*}(x_{0},\dots,x_{n})\overset{\text{\ref{homdehom}(a)}}{=}(\lf(f))(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})\geq 0.

∎

Definition 4.23
.

Let
V
be a
K
-vector space and
C\subseteq V
a cone
[
\to

1.1
].

(a)

The sets of the form
K_{\geq 0}x
with
x\in C\setminus\{0\}
are called
the
rays
of
C
.

(b)

Rays of
C
that are at the same time faces [
\to

3.6
] of
C
are
called
extreme rays
of
C
.

(c)

A set
B\subseteq C\setminus\{0\}
is called a
base
of
C
,
if for each
x\in C\setminus\{0\}
there is exactly one
\lambda\in K_{>0}
such that

\lambda x\in B
, i.e., if every ray of
C
hits the set
B
in exactly one point.

Proposition 4.24
.

Suppose
V
is a
K
-vector space and
C\subseteq V
is a cone with convex
base
B
. Then for all
x\in V
,

\text{$K_{\geq 0}x$ is an extreme ray of $C$}\iff\exists\lambda\in K_{>0}:\lambda x\in\extr B.

Proof.

Let
x\in V
.

“
\Longrightarrow
” Let
K_{\geq 0}x
be an extreme ray of
C
. Then it follows
that
x\in C\setminus\{0\}
. Hence there is exactly one
\lambda\in K_{>0}
such that

\lambda x\in B
. We claim
\lambda x\in\extr B
. For this purpose, consider
y,z\in B
with

\frac{y+z}{2}=\lambda x
. To show:
y=z=\lambda x
. From
y,z\in C
and

\frac{y+z}{2}\in K_{\geq 0}x
, we deduce
y,z\in K_{\geq 0}x
. Due to
y,z\in B
and

0\notin B
, we get
y,z\in K_{>0}x
. Again from
y,z\in B
and the uniqueness of

\lambda
, we get
y=\lambda x=z
.

“
\Longleftarrow
” WLOG let
x\in\extr B
. To show:
K_{\geq 0}x
is an extreme
ray of
C
. Since
x\in B\subseteq C\setminus\{0\}
,
K_{\geq 0}x
is a ray of
C
.
Let
y,z\in C
with
\frac{y+z}{2}\in K_{\geq 0}x
. To show:
y,z\in K_{\geq 0}x
.
WLOG
y\neq 0
and
z\neq 0
. If we had
y+z=0
, then one could easily show

0\in B\ \lightning
.
WLOG
y+z=x
. Choose
\mu,\nu\in K_{>0}
such that
\mu y,\nu z\in B
.
Then

x=y+z=(\mu^{-1}+\nu^{-1})\underbrace{\left(\frac{\mu^{-1}}{\mu^{-1}+\nu^{-1}}(\mu y)+\frac{\nu^{-1}}{\mu^{-1}+\nu^{-1}}(\nu z)\right)}_{\in B}

and thus
\mu^{-1}+\nu^{-1}=1
. Since

x=\mu^{-1}(\mu y)+\nu^{-1}(\nu z)
,
\mu y,\nu z\in B
and
x\in\extr B
,
we have
\mu y=x=\nu y
.
∎

Theorem 4.25
.

[
\to

4.19
]

Every convex cone with compact
[
\to

4.18
]
convex base in a
finite-dimensional
\mathbb{R}
-vector space is the sum of its extreme rays.

Proof.

Suppose
V
is a finite-dimensional
\mathbb{R}
-vector space and

C\subseteq V
is a convex cone with compact convex base
B
. Let
x\in C
.
To show:
x
is a sum of elements of extreme rays of
C
. WLOG
x\in B
.
By Minkowski’s theorem
4.19
, we have
x\in\conv(\extr B)
, say

x=\sum_{i=1}^{m}\lambda_{i}x_{i}
with
m\in\mathbb{N}
,
\lambda_{1},\dots,\lambda_{m}\in K_{\geq 0}
,

\lambda_{1}+\ldots+\lambda_{m}=1
and
x_{i}\in\extr B
. According to
4.24
,

K_{\geq 0}x_{i}
is for all
i\in\{1,\dots,m\}
an extreme ray of
C
.
∎

Proposition 4.26
.

Every convex cone with compact
[
\to

4.18
]

base in a finite-dimensional
\mathbb{R}
-vector space is closed.

Proof.

Suppose
V
is a finite-dimensional
\mathbb{R}
-vector space and

C\subseteq V
is a convex cone with compact base
B
. By
Tikhonov’s theorem
1.18
, also
[0,1]_{\mathbb{R}}\times B
is compact.
From
1.18
together with the continuity of the
scalar multiplication, we obtain
that

A:=\{\lambda x\mid\lambda\in[0,1]_{\mathbb{R}},x\in B\}

is again compact. WLOG
V=\mathbb{R}^{n}
by
2.20
. WLOG
B\neq\emptyset
.
Set

d:=\min\{\|y\|\mid y\in B\}>0.

In order to show that
C
is closed,
we now let
x\in V\setminus C
. WLOG
\|x\|<\frac{d}{2}
[
\to

2.6
].
Since
A
is closed by
2.16
, there is an
\varepsilon>0
such that

\{y\in V\mid\|x-y\|<\varepsilon\}\cap A=\emptyset
. From
0\in A
, we get

\varepsilon\leq\|x\|<\frac{d}{2}
. Then
\{y\in V\mid\|x-y\|<\varepsilon\}\cap C=\emptyset

for if
y\in C\setminus A
, then there is
\lambda\in K
with
0<\lambda<1
and

\lambda y\in B
and it follows that
\|y\|=\frac{1}{\lambda}\|\lambda y\|\geq\frac{1}{\lambda}d>d
which is
incompatible with
\|x-y\|<\frac{d}{2}
(which would imply contrarily

\|y\|\leq\|y-x\|+\|x\|<\frac{d}{2}+\frac{d}{2}=d
). This shows that
C
is closed.
∎

5
Application to ternary quartics

A
ternary quartic
is a
4
-form (also called quartic form [
\to

3.4
])
in
3
variables.

Lemma 5.1
.

Let
(K,\leq)
be an ordered field and
G\in SK^{m\times m}
.
Then
G
is psd [
\to

3.1
] if and
only if
x^{T}Gx\geq 0
for all
x\in(K^{\times})^{m}
.

Proof.

Suppose
x^{T}Gx\geq 0
for all
x\in(K^{\times})^{m}
. Let
z\in K^{m}
. We
have to show that

z^{T}Gz\geq 0.

Choose
y\in(K^{\times})^{m}
arbitrary. Then
z+\lambda y\in(K^{\times})^{m}
and
therefore

z^{T}Gz+2\lambda y^{T}Gz+\lambda^{2}y^{T}Gy=(z+\lambda y)^{T}G(z+\lambda y)\geq 0

for
all but finitely many
\lambda\in K
. For example, by
5.3
(b) applied to
the polynomial

z^{T}Gz+2y^{T}GzT+y^{T}GyT^{2}\in K[T],

it follows that
z^{T}Gz\geq 0
.
∎

Lemma 5.2
.

Let
K
be a Euclidean field and
f\in K[X,Y,Z]
a
4
-form. Suppose that there
are linearly independent
v_{1},v_{2},v_{3}\in K^{3}
such that

f(v_{1})=f(v_{2})=f(v_{3})=0
. Then the following are equivalent:

(a)

f
is psd [
\to

3.1
(a)]

(b)

f\in\sum K[X,Y,Z]^{2}

(c)

f
is a sum of
3
squares of quadratic forms in
K[X,Y,Z]
.

Proof.

Denote by
e_{1},e_{2},e_{3}
the standard basis of
K^{3}
. Set

A:=\begin{pmatrix}v_{1}&v_{2}&v_{3}\end{pmatrix}\in\GL_{3}(K)
and
g:=f\left(A\left(\begin{smallmatrix}X\\
Y\\
Z\end{smallmatrix}\right)\right)\in K[X,Y,Z]
. Then
g
is a
4
-form
satisfying
g(e_{1})=g(e_{2})=g(e_{3})=0
. Since
A
defines a permutation
(even a vector space isomorphism)

K^{3}\to K^{3},\ \left(\begin{smallmatrix}x\\
y\\
z\end{smallmatrix}\right)\mapsto A\left(\begin{smallmatrix}x\\
y\\
z\end{smallmatrix}\right)

on
K^{3}
, we have that

\text{$f$ is psd}\iff\text{$g$ is psd}.

Since
A
induces on the other hand a ring automorphism

K[X,Y,Z]\to K[X,Y,Z],\ h\mapsto h\left(A\left(\begin{smallmatrix}X\\
Y\\
Z\end{smallmatrix}\right)\right),

we obtain

f\in\sum K[X,Y,Z]^{2}\iff g\in\sum K[X,Y,Z]^{2}.

Since this ring automorphism permutes the quadratic forms
in
K[X,Y,Z]
, we have that

\text{(c)}\iff\text{$g$ is a sum of $3$ squares of quadratic forms}.

Replacing
f
by
g
, we can henceforth suppose that
v_{1}=e_{1}
,

v_{2}=e_{2}
and
v_{3}=e_{3}
.

(c)
\implies
(b)
\implies
(a)
is trivial.

(a)
\implies
(c)
It is easy to see that each polynomial
g\in K[T]

with
g\geq 0
on
K
and
g(0)=0
lies in the ideal
(T^{2})

[
\to

5.3
(b)].
Suppose now that
(a)
holds. The vanishing at
0
and the nonnegativity
of the polynomials

f(1,T,0),\ f(1,0,T),\ f(T,1,0),\ f(0,1,T),\ f(0,T,1),\ f(T,0,1)\in K[T]

therefore forces the coefficients of

X^{4},\quad X^{3}Y,\quad X^{3}Z,\quad Y^{4},\quad Y^{3}X,\quad Y^{3}Z,\quad Z^{4},\quad Z^{3}X,\quad Z^{3}Y

in
f
to vanish. For example, the first polynomial forces
the coefficients of
X^{4}
and
X^{3}Y
to vanish, and
the second one the coefficients of again
X^{4}
and of
X^{3}Z
.
It follows that

\displaystyle N(f)

\displaystyle\subseteq\conv\{(2,2,0),\xcancel{(2,1,1)},(2,0,2),(0,2,2),\xcancel{(1,2,1)},\xcancel{(1,1,2)}\}\text{, i.e.,}

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}N(f)

\displaystyle\subseteq\conv\{(1,1,0),(1,0,1),(0,1,1)\}\quad\text{and thus}

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}N(f)\cap\mathbb{N}_{0}^{3}

\displaystyle\subseteq\{(1,1,0),(1,0,1),(0,1,1)\}.

By the Gram matrix method
6.1
, we have to show that there is a

psd
matrix
G\in SK^{3\times 3}
satisfying

(*)\qquad f=\begin{pmatrix}XY&XZ&YZ\end{pmatrix}G\begin{pmatrix}XY\\
XZ\\
YZ\end{pmatrix}.

Since every monomial occurring in
f
is a product of two entries
of
\begin{pmatrix}XY&XZ&YZ\end{pmatrix}
, there is certainly a

G\in SK^{3\times 3}
satisfying
(*)
(actually one sees easily that there is a

unique
such
G
which does however not play an immediate role).
But from
(*)
it follows

automatically
that
G
is
psd
since
f
is psd.
In order to see this, let
v\in K^{3}
. We have to show that

v^{T}Gv\geq 0
. Using
5.1
, one reduces to the case

v\in(K^{\times})^{3}
. Then set
\lambda:=v_{1}v_{2}v_{3}

and
x:=\frac{1}{v_{3}}
,
y:=\frac{1}{v_{2}}
and
z:=\frac{1}{v_{1}}
.
Now
v=\lambda\begin{pmatrix}xy\\
xz\\
yz\end{pmatrix}
and therefore

v^{T}Gv=\lambda^{2}\begin{pmatrix}xy&xz&yz\end{pmatrix}G\begin{pmatrix}xy\\
xz\\
yz\end{pmatrix}\overset{(*)}{=}\lambda^{2}f(x,y,z)\geq 0.

∎

Lemma 5.3
.

Let
K
be a Euclidean field and
f\in K[X,Y,Z]
a
4
-form.
Suppose there are linearly independent
v_{1},v_{2},v_{3}\in K^{3}
satisfying

f(v_{1}+Tv_{2})\in(T^{3})
and
f(v_{3})=0
. Then the following are equivalent:

(a)

f
is psd

(b)

f\in\sum K[X,Y,Z]^{2}

(c)

f
is a sum of
3
squares of quadratic forms in
K[X,Y,Z]
.

Proof.

Almost exactly as in the proof of
5.2
, one sees that one can suppose
WLOG
v_{1}=e_{1}
,
v_{2}=e_{2}
and
v_{3}=e_{3}
.

(c)
\implies
(b)
\implies
(a)
is again trivial.

(a)
\implies
(c)
One sees easily that a polynomial

g\in K[T]
with
g\geq 0
on
K
and
g\in(T^{2k-1})
lies in
(T^{2k})

for
k\in\mathbb{N}
. Suppose now that (a) holds. By considering the polynomials

f(1,T,0),\ f(1,0,T),\ f(0,T,1),\ f(T,0,1)\in K[T],

one sees easily that the coefficients of

X^{4},\quad X^{3}Y,\quad X^{2}Y^{2},\quad XY^{3},\quad X^{3}Z,\quad Z^{4},\quad Z^{3}Y,\quad Z^{3}X

in
f
must vanish. More precisely, the first polynomial is responsible for the
first four of these coefficients, the second for the coefficients of
X^{4}
(again)
and
X^{3}Z
, the third for the coefficients of
Z^{4}
and
Z^{3}Y
, and the last
for the coefficients of
Z^{4}
(again) and
Z^{3}X
.
It follows that

\displaystyle N(f)

\displaystyle\subseteq\conv\{(2,0,2),(2,1,1),\xcancel{(1,1,2)},\xcancel{(1,2,1)},(0,2,2),\xcancel{(0,3,1)},(0,4,0)\}\text{, i.e.,}

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}N(f)

\displaystyle\subseteq\conv\left\{(1,0,1),\left(1,\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{2}\right),(0,1,1),(0,2,0)\right\}\quad\text{and thus}

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}N(f)\cap\mathbb{N}_{0}^{3}

\displaystyle\subseteq\{(1,0,1),(0,1,1),(0,2,0)\}.

By the Gram matrix method
6.1
, we have to show that there is a

psd
matrix
G\in SK^{3\times 3}
satisfying

(*)\qquad f=\begin{pmatrix}XZ&YZ&Y^{2}\end{pmatrix}G\begin{pmatrix}XZ\\
YZ\\
Y^{2}\end{pmatrix}.

If the monomial
X^{2}YZ
actually appeared in
f
, we would now run into
a big problem that we did not have in the proof of
5.2
because
this monomial is not a product of two entries of

\begin{pmatrix}XZ&YZ&Y^{2}\end{pmatrix}
.
But this coefficient vanishes as one easily shows since for all
y\in K
, the leading
coefficient of
f(X,y,1)\in K[X]
is nonnegative since this polynomial
is nonnegative on
K
.
As in the proof of
5.2
, one sees again that there exists

G\in SK^{3\times 3}
satisfying
(*)
(one could again see easily
that
G
is unique). From
(*)
it follows
automatically
that
G
is psd
since
f
is psd. To see this, let
v\in K^{3}
. To show:
v^{T}Gv\geq 0
.
Using
5.1
, one reduces to the case

v\in K\times(K^{\times})^{2}
. Then set
\lambda:=v_{2}^{2}v_{3}
and

x:=\frac{v_{1}}{v_{2}^{2}}
,
y:=\frac{1}{v_{2}}
,
z:=\frac{1}{v_{3}}
.
Now
v=\lambda\begin{pmatrix}xz\\
yz\\
y^{2}\end{pmatrix}
and therefore

v^{T}Gv=\lambda^{2}\begin{pmatrix}xz&yz&y^{2}\end{pmatrix}G\begin{pmatrix}xz\\
yz\\
y^{2}\end{pmatrix}\overset{(*)}{=}\lambda^{2}f(x,y,z)\geq 0.

∎

Lemma 5.4
.

Let
K
be a Euclidean field and
f\in K[X,Y,Z]
a
4
-form.
Suppose there are linearly independent
v_{1},v_{2}\in K^{3}
satisfying

f(v_{1}+Tv_{2})\in(T^{3})
and
f(v_{2})=0
. Then the following are equivalent:

(a)

f
is psd

(b)

f\in\sum K[X,Y,Z]^{2}

(c)

f
is a sum of
3
squares of quadratic forms in
K[X,Y,Z]
.

Proof.

One can again suppose WLOG
v_{1}=e_{1}
and
v_{2}=e_{2}
.

(c)
\implies
(b)
\implies
(a)
is again trivial.

(a)
\implies
(c)
One uses again that a polynomial

g\in K[T]
with
g\geq 0
on
K
and
g\in(T^{2k-1})
lies in
(T^{2k})

for
k\in\mathbb{N}
. Suppose now that (a) holds. By considering the polynomials

f(1,T,0),\ f(1,0,T),\ f(T,1,0),\ f(0,1,T)\in K[T],

one sees easily that the coefficients of

X^{4},\quad X^{3}Y,\quad X^{2}Y^{2},\quad XY^{3},\quad X^{3}Z,\quad Y^{4},\quad Y^{3}Z

in
f
must vanish. More precisely, the first polynomial is responsible for the
first four of these coefficients, the second for the coefficients of
X^{4}
(again)
and
X^{3}Z
, the third for the coefficients of
Y^{4}
and
XY^{3}
(again),
and the last for the coefficients of
Y^{4}
(again) and
Y^{3}Z
.
It follows that

\displaystyle N(f)

\displaystyle\subseteq\conv\{(2,0,2),(2,1,1),(1,2,1),(0,2,2),\xcancel{(0,1,3)},(0,0,4),\xcancel{(1,0,3)},\xcancel{(1,1,2)}\}\text{, i.e.,}

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}N(f)

\displaystyle\subseteq\conv\left\{(1,0,1),\left(1,\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{2}\right),\left(\frac{1}{2},1,\frac{1}{2}\right),(0,1,1),(0,0,2)\right\}\quad\text{and thus}

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}N(f)\cap\mathbb{N}_{0}^{3}\subseteq\{(1,0,1),(0,1,1),(0,0,2)\}.

By the Gram matrix method
6.1
, we have to show that there is a

psd
matrix
G\in SK^{3\times 3}
satisfying

(*)\qquad f=\begin{pmatrix}XZ&YZ&Z^{2}\end{pmatrix}G\begin{pmatrix}XZ\\
YZ\\
Z^{2}\end{pmatrix}.

If one of the monomials
X^{2}YZ
and
XY^{2}Z

actually appeared in
f
, we would have trouble since
these monomials are not a product of two entries of

\begin{pmatrix}XZ&YZ&Z^{2}\end{pmatrix}
.
But these coefficients vanish
as one easily shows since for all
x,y\in K
, the leading
coefficients of
f(X,y,1)\in K[X]
and
f(x,Y,1)\in K[Y]
are nonnegative since these
polynomials are nonnegative on
K
.
One sees again that there exists

G\in SK^{3\times 3}
satisfying
(*)
(one could again see easily
that
G
is unique). From
(*)
it follows
automatically
that
G
is psd
since
f
is psd. To see this, let
v\in K^{3}
. To show:
v^{T}Gv\geq 0
.
Using
5.1
, one reduces to the case

v\in K\times(K^{\times})^{2}
. Then set
\lambda:=v_{2}^{2}v_{3}
and

x:=\frac{v_{1}}{v_{2}v_{3}}
,
y:=\frac{1}{v_{3}}
,
z:=\frac{1}{v_{2}}
.
Now
v=\lambda\begin{pmatrix}xz\\
yz\\
z^{2}\end{pmatrix}
and therefore

v^{T}Gv=\lambda^{2}\begin{pmatrix}xz&yz&z^{2}\end{pmatrix}G\begin{pmatrix}xz\\
yz\\
z^{2}\end{pmatrix}\overset{(*)}{=}\lambda^{2}f(x,y,z)\geq 0.

∎

Lemma 5.5
.

Let
f\in\mathbb{R}[X,Y,Z]
be a psd
4
-form that is not a sum of
3
squares of quadratic forms in

\mathbb{R}[X,Y,Z]
and that has two linearly independent zeros in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
. Then there is a linear form

\ell\in\mathbb{R}[X,Y,Z]\setminus\{0\}
such that
f-\ell^{4}
is psd.

Proof.

By Lemma
5.2
, the zeros of
f
span a two-dimensional subspace of
\mathbb{R}^{3}
.
By a change of coordinates, we can thus achieve that
f(e_{2})=f(e_{3})=0
and

f>0\text{ on }\mathbb{R}^{\times}\times\mathbb{R}^{2}.

We now claim that there is some
\varepsilon\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
such that
f
-\varepsilon X^{4}
is psd. By homogeneity, it suffices to find
\varepsilon>0
such that
f-\varepsilon X^{4}\geq 0
holds
on the compact set

[-1,1]_{\mathbb{R}}^{3}\setminus(-1,1)_{\mathbb{R}}^{3}.

For this purpose, it is enough show that for each two-dimensional face
F
of the polytope

[-1,1]^{3}
(i.e., for each side of the cube
[-1,1]^{3}
) there is some
\varepsilon>0

such that
f-\varepsilon X^{4}\geq 0
on
F
. On the two sides
\{-1\}\times[-1,1]^{2}
and

\{1\}\times[-1,1]^{2}
,
f
is positive so that the existence of such an
\varepsilon
for them follows
from
1.19
. After a further change of coordinates, it suffices to consider from the
remaining four sides just
[-1,1]^{2}\times\{1\}
. Consider therefore

\widetilde{f}:=f(X,Y,1)\in\mathbb{R}[X,Y]
[
\to

2.1
(d)]. From Lemma
5.4
, we deduce

\frac{\partial^{2}\widetilde{f}}{\partial Y^{2}}(0,y)>0

for all
y\in\mathbb{R}
that satisfy
\widetilde{f}(0,y)=0

(apply
5.4
to
f
,
v_{1}:=(0,y,1)
and
v_{2}:=(0,1,0)
, taking into account that

\frac{\partial\widetilde{f}}{\partial Y}(0,y)=0
due to
\widetilde{f}\geq 0
on
\mathbb{R}^{2}
).
In the same way, Lemma
5.3
implies that for each
y\in\mathbb{R}
satisfying

\widetilde{f}(0,y)=0
all other directional derivatives of
\widetilde{f}
in
(0,y)
are also positive.
Altogether,
