\widetilde{f}
has thus only zeros in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
at which the second derivative
(i.e., the Hessian) is
pd
(recall that all zeros of
\widetilde{f}
lie on the
y
-axis).
From analysis we know that each zero of the nonnegative polynomial
\widetilde{f}

(in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
, or equivalently
\{0\}\times\mathbb{R}
since all zeros lie on the
y
-axis) is an isolated

global
minimizer. Therefore

\{(x,y)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}\mid\widetilde{f}(x,y)=0\}=\{(0,y_{1}),\dots,(0,y_{m})\}

for some
m\in\mathbb{N}
and
y_{1},\dots,y_{m}\in\mathbb{R}
(one of the
y_{i}
is
0
).
Since
-X^{4}
as well as its first and second derivative vanishes on the
y
-axis
(since
\frac{\partial X^{4}}{\partial X}=4X^{3}
,
\frac{\partial X^{4}}{\partial Y}=0
,

\frac{\partial^{2}X^{4}}{\partial X^{2}}=12X^{2}
,
\frac{\partial^{2}X^{4}}{\partial X\partial Y}=0

and
\frac{\partial^{2}X^{4}}{\partial Y^{2}}=0
), every
(0,y_{i})
is a zero and an isolated

local
minimizer of
\widetilde{f}-X^{4}
. Choose for each
i\in\{1,\dots,m\}
an open
neighborhood
U_{i}
of
(0,y_{i})
such that
\widetilde{f}-X^{4}>0
on
U_{i}\setminus\{(0,y_{i})\}
.
Then of course also
\widetilde{f}-\varepsilon X^{4}>0
on
U_{i}\setminus\{(0,y_{i})\}

for all
\varepsilon\leq 1
and
i\in\{1,\dots,m\}
. Since
\widetilde{f}
is positive on the compact set

[-1,1]^{2}\setminus(U_{1}\cup\dots\cup U_{m})
, there is an
\varepsilon\in(0,1)_{\mathbb{R}}
such that

\widetilde{f}-\varepsilon X^{4}>0
on
[-1,1]^{2}\setminus(U_{1}\cup\dots\cup U_{m})
. Altogether,

\widetilde{f}-\varepsilon X^{4}>0
on
[-1,1]^{2}\setminus\{(0,y_{1}),\dots,(0,y_{m})\}
and

\widetilde{f}-\varepsilon X^{4}=0
on
\{(0,y_{1}),\dots,(0,y_{m})\}
.
∎

Lemma 5.6
.

Suppose
f
lies on an extreme ray [
\to

4.23
(b)] of the cone
P
of
the psd
4
-forms in
\mathbb{R}[X,Y,Z]
. Then there are linearly independent
v_{1},v_{2}\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
such
that
f(v_{1})=f(v_{2})=0
.

Proof.

If
f
were pd, then the forms
f\pm\varepsilon X^{4}
would be psd for some
\varepsilon>0

(choose
\varepsilon
for instance as the minimum of
f
on the compact unit sphere of
\mathbb{R}^{3}
) and
because of
f=\frac{1}{2}(f-\varepsilon X^{4})+\frac{1}{2}(f+\varepsilon X^{4})
it would follow that

f+\varepsilon X^{4}\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}f
and thus
f\in\mathbb{R}X^{4}

\lightning
. Hence
f
has at least one
zero
v_{1}\in\mathbb{R}^{3}\setminus\{0\}
. After a change of coordinates, we can without loss of generality
achieve
v_{1}=e_{1}
. Since
(0,0)
is a local (even a global) minimizer of
f(1,Y,Z)\in\mathbb{R}[Y,Z]
,
we know from analysis that
\frac{\partial f}{\partial Y}(1,0,0)=\frac{\partial f}{\partial Z}(1,0,0)=0
.
It follows that there are a quadratic form
a\in\mathbb{R}[Y,Z]
, a cubic form
b\in\mathbb{R}[Y,Z]
and a quartic form
c\in\mathbb{R}[Y,Z]
such that

f=aX^{2}+bX+c.

The quadratic form
a
is positive semidefinite since either
a(y,z)=0
or
a(y,z)
is the leading coefficient of

f(X,y,z)\in\mathbb{R}[X]
which is nonnegative on
\mathbb{R}
.
We have to show that there
exists
v_{2}\in\mathbb{R}\times(\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus\{0\})
such that
f(v_{2})=0
. We make a case distinction
by
\rk(a)
[
\to

6.1
(h)].

Case 1:

\rk(a)=0

Then
a=0
and thus
b(y,z)=0
for all
(y,z)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
from which
b=0
follows by
2.3
.
If
f=c\in\mathbb{R}[Y,Z]
was pd, then
c\pm\varepsilon Y^{4}\in\mathbb{R}[Y,Z]
would be psd for some
\varepsilon>0

and it would follow that
c+\varepsilon Y^{4}\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}c
and thus
c\in\mathbb{R}Y^{4}

\lightning
.
Now choose
(y,z)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus\{0\}
such that
c(y,z)=0
and set
v_{2}:=(0,y,z)
. Then
f(v_{2})=c(y,z)=0
and
v_{1}
and
v_{2}
are linearly independent.

Case 2:

\rk(a)=1

By a coordinate change in the
y
-
z
-plane WLOG
a=Y^{2}
. Then
b(0,z)=0
for all
z\in\mathbb{R}

and hence
b(0,Z)=0
, i.e.,
b=Yb^{\prime}
for some
b^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}[Y,Z]
. It follows that

f=X^{2}Y^{2}+b^{\prime}XY+c=\left(XY+\frac{b^{\prime}}{2}\right)^{2}+\left(c-\frac{b^{\prime 2}}{4}\right)
.
For all
(y,z)\in\mathbb{R}^{\times}\times\mathbb{R}
,
we find some
x\in\mathbb{R}
satisfying
xy+\frac{b^{\prime}(y,z)}{2}=0
from which

c(y,z)-\frac{b^{\prime}(y,z)^{2}}{4}=f(x,y,z)\geq 0
follows. Hence
c-\frac{b^{\prime 2}}{4}\in P
.
Aside from that, we have of course
(XY+\frac{b^{\prime}}{2})^{2}\in P
. Since
f
lies on an extreme
ray of
P
, it follows that
(XY+\frac{b^{\prime}}{2})^{2}\in\mathbb{R}f
(and
c-\frac{b^{\prime 2}}{4}\in\mathbb{R}f
). Now choose

(y,z)\in\mathbb{R}^{\times}\times\mathbb{R}
arbitrary and with it
x\in\mathbb{R}
such that

xy+\frac{b^{\prime}(y,z)}{2}=0
. Then
f(x,y,z)=0
.

Case 3:

\rk(a)=2

By a coordinate change in the
y
-
z
-plane WLOG
a=Y^{2}+Z^{2}
. Since
f
is psd, also
the
6
-form
4ac-b^{2}\in\mathbb{R}[Y,Z]
is psd. We have to show that there is

(y,z)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus\{0\}
such that there exists
x\in\mathbb{R}
satisfying

a(y,z)x^{2}+b(y,z)x+c(y,z)=0
. Because of
a(y,z)\neq 0
for all
(y,z)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus\{0\}
,
this is equivalent to the existence of
(y,z)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus\{0\}
with
(b^{2}-4ac)(y,z)\geq 0
,
i.e.,
(4ac-b^{2})(y,z)=0
(since
4ac-b^{2}
is psd). We have thus to show that
4ac-b^{2}

is not pd. Aiming for a contradiction, assume that
4ac-b^{2}
is pd. Then also the

6
-forms
4a(c\pm\varepsilon Y^{4})-b^{2}
are psd for some
\varepsilon>0
(choose for example

4\varepsilon
as the minimum of
4ac-b^{2}
on the compact unit sphere of
\mathbb{R}^{2}
and
take into account that
a=Y^{2}+Z^{2}
). It follows that
f\pm\varepsilon Y^{4}\in P
.
From
f=\frac{1}{2}(f+\varepsilon Y^{4})+\frac{1}{2}(f-\varepsilon Y^{4})
, we obtain
f+\varepsilon Y^{4}\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}f

and thus
f\in\mathbb{R}Y^{4}

\lightning
.
∎

Lemma 5.7
.

Let
d,n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
and let
V
be the
\mathbb{R}
-vector space of all
2d
-forms in
\mathbb{R}[\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.6817pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.7584pt{}}}$\hss}}}X]=\mathbb{R}[X_{1},\dots,X_{n}]
and
P\subseteq V
be the cone of all psd forms in
V
. Then
P

is a closed cone with compact convex base [
\to

4.23
(c)].

Proof.

As an intersection of closed sets,
P=\bigcap_{x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}}\{p\in V\mid p(x)\geq 0\}
is closed.
By
2.3
,

\|p\|:=\sum_{x_{1}=-d}^{d}\ldots\sum_{x_{n}=-d}^{d}|p(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})|\qquad(p\in V)

defines a norm on
V
. Then

B:=\{p\in P\mid\|p\|=1\}=\left\{p\in V\mid\sum_{x_{1}=-d}^{d}\ldots\sum_{x_{n}=-d}^{d}p(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})=1\right\}

is a compact convex base of
P
.
∎

Lemma 5.8
.

Let
V
denote the
\mathbb{R}
-vector space of all
4
-forms in
\mathbb{R}[X,Y,Z]
and
P\subseteq V

the cone of all psd forms in
V
. Suppose that
f
lies on an extreme ray of
P
. Then
f
is
a square of a quadratic form.

Proof.

It is enough to show that
f
is a
sum of
squares of quadratic forms for if

f=\sum_{i=1}^{m}q_{i}^{2}\neq 0
with
2
-forms
q_{i}\in\mathbb{R}[X,Y,Z]
, then

f=\frac{1}{2}\underbrace{2q_{1}^{2}}_{\in P}+\frac{1}{2}\underbrace{2\sum_{i=2}^{m}q_{i}^{2}}_{\in P}

and thus
q_{1}^{2}\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}f
. If there is a linear form
\ell\in\mathbb{R}[X,Y,Z]\setminus\{0\}
such that

f-\ell^{4}
is psd, then
\ell^{4}\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}f
and
f=(c\ell^{2})^{2}
for some
c\in\mathbb{R}^{\times}
so that
we are done. From now on therefore suppose that such a linear form does not exist.
From the Lemmata
5.5
and
5.6
, it follows now that
f
is a
sum of
3
squares of
2
-forms in
\mathbb{R}[X,Y,Z]
.
∎

Theorem 5.9
.

Let
R
be a real closed field and
f\in R[X,Y,Z]
a
4
-form. Then the following are equivalent:

(a)

f
is psd.

(b)

f\in\sum R[X,Y,Z]^{2}

(c)

f
is a sum of squares of quadratic forms in
R[X,Y,Z]
.

Proof.

(c)
\implies
(b)
\implies
(a)
is trivial.

(a)
\implies
(c)
follows for
R=\mathbb{R}
from
5.8
together with
the conic version
4.25
of Minkowski’s theorem and
5.7
. Using the Gram matrix method

6.1
(or
4.20
), one sees that the class of all real closed fields
R
for which
(a)
\implies
(c) holds for all
4
-forms
f\in R[X,Y,Z]
, is semialgebraic. By
8.5
, every
real closed field belongs to this class. In short, the statement follows thus from the case
R=\mathbb{R}

by the Tarski principle
8.19
.
∎

Corollary 5.10

(dehomogenized version of
5.9
)
.

Let
R
be a real closed field and
f\in R[X,Y]_{4}
. Then

\text{$f\geq 0$ on $R^{2}$}\iff f\in\sum R[X,Y]^{2}.

Proof.

“
\Longleftarrow
” is trivial.

“
\Longrightarrow
” Suppose
f\geq 0
on
R^{2}
. WLOG
f\notin R
. Then
\deg f=2

or
\deg f=4
by
2.4
(b). For
\deg f=2
, the claim follows from
3.5
.
Suppose therefore
\deg f=4
. Then
f^{*}:=Z^{4}f\left(\frac{X}{Z},\frac{Y}{Z}\right)\in R[X,Y,Z]

is the homogenization of
f
with respect to
Z
[
\to

2.1
(c)] and
f^{*}
is psd
by
2.6
(a). Now
5.9
yields
f^{*}\in\sum R[X,Y,Z]^{2}
. By
dehomogenization [
\to

2.1
(d),
2.2
], it follows that
f\in\sum R[X,Y]^{2}
.
∎

Remark 5.11
.

A posteriori, we see now that in the situation of Lemma
5.6
, there actually exist even infinitely many pairwise linearly independent zeros of
f
. This follows from

5.8
. Indeed, if
f=q^{2}
with a
2
-form
q\in\mathbb{R}[X,Y,Z]
,
then WLOG
\sg q\geq 0
(otherwise replace
q
by
-q
) and thus
\sg q\in\{0,1,2,3\}
.

If
\sg q=3
, then
q
and thus
f
is positive definite which is of course impossible by
5.6
.

If
\sg q=2
, then after a linear change of coordinates we have WLOG
f=X^{2}+Y^{2}
which contradicts again
5.6
since any zero of
q
and hence of
f

in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
lies in
\{(0,0)\}\times\mathbb{R}
.

If
\sg q=1
, then WLOG
q\in\{X^{2},X^{2}+Y^{2}-Z^{2}\}
.
If
q=X^{2}
, then
for example the
(0,y,1)
where
y\in\mathbb{R}
are pairwise linearly
independent zeros of
q
and therefore also of
f
. If
q=X^{2}+Y^{2}-Z^{2}
, then the
(x,1,\sqrt{x^{2}+1})
where
x\in\mathbb{R}

are pairwise linearly independent zeros of
q
and therefore of
f
. Indeed even the projections of these vectors onto their first two components are already linearly independent as
we have already seen.

If
\sg q=0
, then WLOG
q\in\{0,X^{2}-Y^{2}\}
. The case
q=0
is trivial. In the case
q=X^{2}-Y^{2}

for example the
(x,x,1)
where
x\in\mathbb{R}
are pairwise linearly
independent zeros of
q
and therefore also of
f
.
Again even the projections of these vectors onto their second and third components are already linearly independent.

Remark 5.12
.

We will neither use nor prove the following:

(a)

In 1888, Hilbert showed a strengthening of
5.9

(“sum of
three
squares” instead of “sum of squares”, cf. also

5.2
,
5.3
,
5.4
and
5.5
)
[
Hil
]
.
A very long and tedious elementary proof for this has been given by Scheiderer and
Pfister in 2012
[
PS
]
..

(b)

Scheiderer showed in 2016 that

X^{4}+XY^{3}+Y^{4}-3X^{2}YZ-4XY^{2}Z+2X^{2}Z^{2}+XZ^{3}+YZ^{3}+Z^{4}

is psd but does not belong to
\sum\mathbb{Q}[X,Y,Z]^{2}

[
S2
]
. In the same year,
Henrion, Naldi, Safey El Din gave an elementary proof for this
[
HNS
]
.

Chapter 8
Nonnegative polynomials with zeros

Throughout this chapter,
K
denotes again always a subfield of
\mathbb{R}

with the induced order. Moreover, we let
A
always be a commutative ring (e.g.,

A=K[X_{1},\dots,X_{n}]
).

1
Modules over semirings

Definition 1.1
.

Let
T\subseteq A
. Then we call
T
a
semiring
of
A
if
\{0,1\}\subseteq T
,

T+T\subseteq T
and
TT\subseteq T
[
\to

2.1
]. If
T
is a semiring of
A
,
then
M\subseteq A
is called a
T
-module
of
A
if
0\in M
,
M+M\subseteq M

and
TM\subseteq M
.

Remark 1.2
.

(a)

\text{$T$ is a preorder of $A$}\iff(\text{$T$ is a semiring of $A$}\mathbin{\&}A^{2}\subseteq T)

(b)

If
T
is a semiring of
A
, then
T-T
is a subring of
A
.

(c)

If
T
is a semiring of
A
and
M
a
T
-module of
A
, then

M-M
is a
(T-T)
-module of
A
.

(d)

If
T
is a semiring of
A
, then
T
is a
T
-module of
A
.

Definition 1.3
.

Let
T
be a semiring of
A
and
M
a
T
-module of
A
.
Then
M
is called
Archimedean
(in
A
) if

\forall a\in A:\exists N\in\mathbb{N}:N+a\in M
[
\to

1.2
(a)].

Remark 1.4
.

Due to
1.2
(d), the notion of an Archimedean semiring is also defined by

1.3
. Because of
1.2
(a), this generalizes the notion of an
Archimedean preorder of
A
[
\to

1.2
(a)].

Definition 1.5
.

[
\to

3.1
]
Let
T
be a semiring of
A
,
M
a
T
-module of
A
and
u\in A
. Then

B_{(A,M,u)}:=\{a\in A\mid\exists N\in\mathbb{N}:Nu\pm a\in M\}

the set of with respect to
M
by
u

arithmetically bounded
elements of
A
.
If
u=1
, then we write
B_{(A,M)}:=B_{(A,M,u)}
and omit the specification “by
u
”.

Proposition 1.6
.

Suppose
T
is a semiring of
A
,
M_{1}
and
M_{2}
are
T
-modules of
A
,

u_{1}\in M_{1}
and
u_{2}\in M_{2}
. Then
\sum M_{1}M_{2}
is also a
T
-module of
A

and we have

B_{(A,M_{1},u_{1})}B_{(A,M_{2},u_{2})}\subseteq B_{(A,\sum M_{1}M_{2},u_{1}u_{2})}.

Proof.

Let
a_{i}\in B_{(A,M_{i},u_{i})}
, say
Nu_{i}\pm a_{i}\in M_{i}
for
i\in\{1,2\}
with
N\in\mathbb{N}
.
Then (cf. the proof of
3.1
)

3N^{2}u_{1}u_{2}\pm a_{1}a_{2}=(Nu_{1}+a_{1})(Nu_{2}\pm a_{2})+Nu_{2}(Nu_{1}-a_{1})+Nu_{1}(Nu_{2}\mp a_{2}).

∎

Corollary 1.7
.

Let
T
be a semiring of
A
,
M
a
T
-module of
A
,
u\in T
and
v\in M
.
Then
B_{(A,T,u)}B_{(A,M,v)}\subseteq B_{(A,M,uv)}
.

Proof.

Apply
1.6
to
M_{1}:=T
,
M_{2}:=M
,
u_{1}:=u
,

u_{2}:=v
and observe
\sum M_{1}M_{2}=\sum TM=M
.
∎

Corollary 1.8
.

[
\to

3.1
]

Let
T
be a semiring of
A
. Then
B_{(A,T)}
is a subring of
A
.
Moreover, if
M
a
T
-module of
A
and
u\in M
, then
B_{(A,M,u)}

is a
B_{(A,T)}
-module of
A
.

Remark 1.9
.

[
\to

1.3
,
3.3
]
If
T\subseteq A
is a semiring and
M\subseteq A
a
T
-module with
1\in M
, then
M
is Archimedean if
and only if
B_{(A,M)}=A
.

Theorem 1.10
.

[
\to

3.4
]

Let
n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
and
T\subseteq K[\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.6817pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.7584pt{}}}$\hss}}}X]
a semiring with
K_{\geq 0}\subseteq T
.
Then the following are equivalent:

(a)

T
is Archimedean.

(b)

\exists N\in\mathbb{N}:\forall i\in\{1,\dots,n\}:N\pm X_{i}\in T

(c)

\exists m\in\mathbb{N}:\exists\ell_{1},\dots,\ell_{m}\in T\cap K[\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.6817pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.7584pt{}}}$\hss}}}X]_{1}:\exists N\in\mathbb{N}:\\
\emptyset\neq\{x\in K^{n}\mid\ell_{1}(x)\geq 0,\dots,\ell_{m}(x)\geq 0\}\subseteq[-N,N]_{K}^{n}

Proof.

Write
A:=K[\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.6817pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.7584pt{}}}$\hss}}}X]
. From
K_{\geq 0}\subseteq T
, it follows that

K\subseteq B_{(A,T)}
. Hence we have
B_{(A,T)}=A\iff X_{1},\dots,X_{n}\in B_{(A,T)}
which
shows (a)
\iff
(b). The implication (b)
\implies
(c) is trivial and (c)
\implies
(b) is an easy
consequence of the linear Nichtnegativstellensatz
4.22
.
∎

Lemma 1.11
.

[
\to

3.2
]
Suppose that
\frac{1}{2}\in A
(i.e.,
2\in A^{\times}
), let
M\subseteq A
be a

(\sum A^{2})
-module with
1\in M
and let
a\in A
. Then

a^{2}\in B_{(A,M)}\iff a\in B_{(A,M)}.

Proof.

“
\Longrightarrow
” If
N\in\mathbb{N}
with
(N-1)-a^{2}\in M
, then

N\pm a=(N-1)-a^{2}+\left(\frac{1}{2}\pm a\right)^{2}+3\left(\frac{1}{2}\right)^{2}\in M

(exactly as in the proof of
3.2
).

“
\Longleftarrow
” If
N\in\mathbb{N}
with
(2N-1)\pm a\in M
, then

N^{2}(2N-1)-a^{2}=2\left(\frac{1}{2}\right)^{2}((N-a)^{2}(2N-1+a)+(N+a)^{2}(2N-1-a))\in M.

∎

Proposition 1.12
.

Suppose
\frac{1}{2}\in A
,
T\subseteq A
is a preorder and
M\subseteq A

is a
T
-module with
1\in M
. Then
B_{(A,M)}
is a subring of
A
and
B_{(A,M,u)}
a

B_{(A,M)}
-module of
A
for each
u\in T
.

Proof.

It obviously suffices to show
B_{(A,M)}B_{(A,M,u)}\subseteq B_{(A,M,u)}
for all
u\in T

(since this means
B_{(A,M)}B_{(A,M)}\subseteq B_{(A,M)}
for
u=1
).
If
a\in B_{(A,M)}
, then we have

a=\left(\frac{1}{2}\right)^{2}((a+1)^{2}-(a-1)^{2})

and
because of
1\in M
also
a+1,a-1\in B_{(A,M)}
. Therefore it is enough to show

a^{2}B_{(A,M,u)}\subseteq B_{(A,M,u)}
for all
a\in B_{(A,M)}
and
u\in T
. For this purpose,
fix
a\in B_{(A,M)}
,
u\in T
and
b\in B_{(A,M,u)}
. To show:
a^{2}b\in B_{(A,M,u)}
.
From
1.11
, we get
a^{2}\in B_{(A,M)}
. Choose
N\in\mathbb{N}
such that

N-a^{2},Nu\pm b\in M
. Due to
a^{2},u\in T
, we get now
Nu-ua^{2},Nua^{2}\pm a^{2}b\in M
.
Consequently,

N^{2}u\pm a^{2}b=(N^{2}u-Nua^{2})+(Nua^{2}\pm a^{2}b)\in M+M\subseteq M.

∎

Theorem 1.13
.

[
\to

3.4
,
1.10
]

Suppose
n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
and
M\subseteq K[\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.6817pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.7584pt{}}}$\hss}}}X]
is a
(\sum K_{\geq 0}K[\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.6817pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.7584pt{}}}$\hss}}}X]^{2})
-module with

1\in M
. Then the following are equivalent:

(a)

M
is Archimedean.

(b)

\exists N\in\mathbb{N}:N-\sum_{i=1}^{n}X_{i}^{2}\in M

(c)

\exists N\in\mathbb{N}:\forall i\in\{1,\dots,n\}:N\pm X_{i}\in M

(d)

\exists m\in\mathbb{N}:\exists\ell_{1},\dots,\ell_{m}\in M\cap K[\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.6817pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.7584pt{}}}$\hss}}}X]_{1}:\exists N\in\mathbb{N}:\\
\emptyset\neq\{x\in K^{n}\mid\ell_{1}(x)\geq 0,\dots,\ell_{m}(x)\geq 0\}\subseteq[-N,N]_{K}^{n}

Proof.

(a)
\implies
(b)
is trivial.

(b)
\implies
(c)
If (b) holds, then
N-X_{i}^{2}\in M
and thus
X_{i}^{2}\in B_{(K[\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.6817pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.6817pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to3.55522pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to2.53944pt{}}}$\hss}}}X],M)}

for all
i\in\{1,\dots,n\}
. Apply now
1.11
.

(c)
\implies
(d)
is trivial and
(d)
\implies
(c)
follows again from the linear
Nichtnegativstellensatz
4.22
.

(c)
\implies
(a)
follows from
1.12
.
∎

2
Pure states on rings and ideals

In this section, we always suppose that the field
K
is a subring of
A
. In particular,

\mathbb{Q}\subseteq A
and
A
is a
K
-vector space.

Remark 2.1
.

Under the just made mild hypothesis
\mathbb{Q}\subseteq A
, one can reformulate the
abstract Archimedean Positivstellensatz
1.3

as follows:

For arbitrary
A
and
K
as above,
let
T
be an Archimedean preorder of
A
such that
K_{\geq 0}\subseteq T
and
a\in A
.
Then the following are equivalent:

(a)

\varphi(a)>0
for all
K
-linear ring homomorphisms
\varphi\colon A\to\mathbb{R}
with

\varphi(T)\subseteq\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
.

(b)

\exists N\in\mathbb{N}:a\in\frac{1}{N}+T

To see this, first note that in (a),
one can omit the
K
-linearity of
\varphi
since it just means that
\varphi|_{K}=\id_{K}

which follows from
1.15
by
K_{\geq 0}\subseteq T
since the identity is the

only
embedding of ordered fields from
K
to
\mathbb{R}
(cf. the proof of
2.1
).
But then the theorem becomes
strongest for
K=\mathbb{Q}
and we can thus assume
K=\mathbb{Q}
which makes
redundant the hypothesis
K_{\geq 0}\subseteq T
since for all
m,n\in\mathbb{N}
, we have

\frac{m}{n}=mn\left(\frac{1}{n}\right)^{2}\in\sum A^{2}\subseteq T
.
This last fact also shows (b)
\iff
(b’) where we denote by (a’) and (b’) the
corresponding conditions from

1.3
, namely:

(a’)

\widehat{a}>0
on
\sper(A,T)

(b’)

\exists N\in\mathbb{N}:Na\in 1+T

It remains to show that (a)
\iff
(a’). To this end,
it suffices by
1.4
(d) to show that (a’) is equivalent to

(a”)

\widehat{a}(Q)>0
for all maximal elements
Q
of
\sper(A,T)
.

It is clear that (a’)
\implies
(a”). To show (a”)
\implies
(a’), suppose that (a”) holds and let

P\in\sper(A,T)
. To show:
\widehat{a}(P)>0
. Using
2.3
or

2.5
, we find a maximal element
Q
of
\sper(A,T)
such that

P\subseteq Q
. By
2.4
, we have
Q=P\cup\supp(Q)
. Due to (a”), we
have
a\in Q\setminus-Q
, i.e.,
a\in Q\setminus\supp(Q)\subseteq P
,
and because of
a\notin-P
(for otherwise
a\in-Q
) it follows that
a\in P\setminus-P
,
i.e.,
\widehat{a}(P)>0
. This shows (a’)
\iff
(a”). These
arguments were implicitly present already in the proof of

2.2
.

Remark 2.2
.

Suppose
T
is a semiring of
A
with
K_{\geq 0}\subseteq T
and
M
a
T
-module of
A
.
Then
M
is a cone in the
K
-vector space
A
and we have:

\text{$M$ is Archimedean [$\to$ \ref{defarchsemiring}]}\iff\text{$1$ is a unit for $M$ [$\to$ \ref{defunit}]}

Motivation 2.3
.

If
T
is an Archimedean preorder of
A
with
K_{\geq 0}\subseteq T
, then the Archimedean
Positivstellensatz
1.3
in the version of

2.1
amounts to the equivalence of

\exists N\in\mathbb{N}:a\in\frac{1}{N}+T

with

(*)\qquad\varphi(a)>0\text{ for all ($K$-linear) ring homomorphisms }\varphi\colon A\to\mathbb{R}\text{ with $\varphi(T)\subseteq\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}$}

while
3.19
, paying attention to
2.2
,
tells that the same condition is equivalent to

(**)\qquad\varphi(a)>0\text{ for all pure states }\varphi\text{ of }(A,T,1).

The following imprecise questions arise:

(a)

What do pure states “on rings” have to do with ring homomorphisms?

(b)

Can the Archimedean Positivstellensatz be generalized from preorders to semirings
or even to modules over semirings?

(c)

If
(*)
holds only with “
\geq
” instead of “
>
”, then
\exists N\in\mathbb{N}:a\in\frac{1}{N}+T

can of course not hold anymore but one would still want to prove that
a\in T
.
In this case, is it possible to find an ideal
I\subseteq A

(e.g., the kernel of a ring homomorphism
\varphi
from
(*)
with
\varphi(a)=0
) such that

I\cap T
possesses in the
K
-vector space
I
a unit
u
in such a way that

a\in I
and
(**)
holds for
(I,I\cap T,u)
instead of
(A,T,1)
?
Then one could apply
3.19
or
3.20

in order to finally still show that
a\in T
(even
a\in\frac{1}{N}u+(I\cap T)
).

(d)

What can one say about pure states “on ideals”? This question generalizes (a) and is
motivated by (c).

Reminder 2.4
.

For
z\in\mathbb{C}
and
k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
, the binomial coefficient

\binom{z}{k}:=\prod_{i=1}^{k}\frac{z-i+1}{i}

is declared. From analysis, one knows that

\sqrt{1+t}=(1+t)^{\frac{1}{2}}=\sum_{i=0}^{\infty}\binom{\frac{1}{2}}{i}t^{i}

for all
t\in\mathbb{R}
with
|t|<1
.

Lemma 2.5
.

For all
k\in\mathbb{N}
, the coefficients of

p_{k}:=\left(\sum_{i=0}^{k}\binom{\frac{1}{2}}{i}(-T)^{i}\right)^{2}-(1-T)\in\mathbb{Q}[T]

are nonnegative.

Proof.

In the ring
\mathbb{Q}[[T]]
of formal power series, we have because of
2.4

and the identity theorem for power series from analysis that

\left(\sum_{i=0}^{\infty}\binom{\frac{1}{2}}{i}(-T)^{i}\right)^{2}=1-T.

Now let
k\in\mathbb{N}
be fixed. For
i\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
with
i\leq k
, the coefficient of
T^{i}
in
p_{k}

obviously equals the coefficient of
T^{i}
in

\left(\sum_{i=0}^{\infty}\binom{\frac{1}{2}}{i}(-T)^{i}\right)^{2}-(1-T)

which is zero. The binomial coefficient
\binom{\frac{1}{2}}{i}
is positive for
i\in\{0,1,3,5,\dots\}

and negative for
i\in\{2,4,6,\dots\}
. The only positive coefficient of

\sum_{i=0}^{k}\binom{\frac{1}{2}}{i}(-T)^{i}

is thus the constant term. Hence,
for
i\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
with
i>k
, the coefficient of
T^{i}
in
p_{k}
is thus a sum of products of two
nonpositive reals and therefore nonnegative.
∎

Lemma 2.6
.

Suppose
I
is an ideal of
A
,
T
is a preorder of
A
with
K_{\geq 0}\subseteq T
,

M\subseteq I
is a
T
-module of
A
,
u
is a unit for
M
in
I
,
a\in T
and

(1-2a)u\in M
. Then [
\to

1.9
]

S(I,M,u)\subseteq S(I,(1-a)M,u).

Proof.

Let
\varphi\in S(I,M,u)
. To show:
\varphi((1-a)M)\subseteq\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
. Let
b\in M
.
To show:

\varphi((1-a)b)\geq 0.

WLOG
u-b\in M

(otherwise choose
N\in\mathbb{N}
with
Nu-b\in M
and replace
b
by
\frac{1}{N}b\in M
).
We show
\varphi((1-a)b)>-\varepsilon
for all
\varepsilon>0
. To this end, let
\varepsilon>0
. It is enough to show
that there is a
k\in\mathbb{N}
satisfying

\varphi((1-a)b)>\varphi\left(\left(\sum_{i=0}^{k}\binom{\frac{1}{2}}{i}(-a)^{i}\right)^{2}b\right)-\varepsilon

since
A^{2}M\subseteq TM\subseteq M\subseteq\varphi^{-1}(\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0})
. Because of
a\in T
,
we have

(1-(2a)^{i})u=\sum_{j=0}^{i-1}((2a)^{j}-(2a)^{j+1})u=\sum_{j=0}^{i-1}(2a)^{j}(1-2a)u\in M

for all
i\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
, i.e.,

(\square)\qquad\left(\frac{1}{2^{i}}-a^{i}\right)u\in M

for all
i\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
. By
2.4
, we can choose
k\in\mathbb{N}
such that

\left(\sum_{i=0}^{k}\binom{\frac{1}{2}}{i}\left(-\frac{1}{2}\right)^{i}\right)^{2}<\left(1-\frac{1}{2}\right)+\varepsilon,

i.e.,
p_{k}\left(\frac{1}{2}\right)<\varepsilon
with
p_{k}
as in Lemma
2.5
.
We show that
\varphi(p_{k}(a)b)<\varepsilon
which is exactly our claim. Since
p_{k}(a)\in T
holds by Lemma
2.5
,
it is enough to show that
\varphi(p_{k}(a)u)<\varepsilon
since

\varphi(p_{k}(a)b)\leq\varphi(p_{k}(a)u)
holds due to

p_{k}(a)(u-b)\in M
. But we have

\varphi(p_{k}(a)u)\leq\varphi(p_{k}\left(\frac{1}{2}\right)u)=p_{k}\left(\frac{1}{2}\right)\varphi(u)=p_{k}\left(\frac{1}{2}\right)<\varepsilon

due to
(p_{k}\left(\frac{1}{2}\right)-p_{k}(a))u\in M

(use
2.5
and
(\square)
).
∎

Theorem 2.7

(Burgdorf, Scheiderer, Schweighofer
[
BSS
]
)
.

[
\to

2.3
(d)]

Suppose that
I
is an ideal of
A
,
T
is a preorder or an Archimedean semiring of
A
,

K_{\geq 0}\subseteq T
,
M\subseteq I
is a
T
-module of
A
,
u
is a unit for
M
in

I
and
\varphi
is a pure state of
(I,M,u)
. Then

(*)\qquad\varphi(ab)=\varphi(au)\varphi(b)

for all
a\in A
and
b\in I
.

Proof.

Due to
T-T=A
[
\to

2.3
,
1.3
] it suffices to show
(*)
for all

a\in T
and
b\in I
. If
T
is
\begin{Bmatrix}\text{an Archimedean semiring}\\
\text{a preorder}\end{Bmatrix}
, then one can
here suppose by scaling
a
that
\begin{Bmatrix}1-a\in T\\
u-2au\in M\end{Bmatrix}
and thus because of

\begin{Bmatrix}\text{$TM\subseteq M$}\\
\text{\text{Lemma \ref{magiclemma}}}\end{Bmatrix}
that

S(I,M,u)\subseteq S(I,(1-a)M,u).

Moreover, we can suppose that
\varphi(au)<1
. Fix therefore
a\in T
with

S(I,M,u)\subseteq S(I,(1-a)M,u)
and
\varphi(au)<1
. We have to show
(*)
for all
b\in I
.

Case 1:

\varphi(au)=0

Then we have to show that
\varphi(ab)=0
for all
b\in I
. For this purpose, fix
b\in I
. Choose

N\in\mathbb{N}
such that
Nu\pm b\in M
. Then
Nau\pm ab\in TM\subseteq M
and therefore

|\varphi(ab)|\leq N\varphi(au)=0
. Hence
\varphi(ab)=0
.

Case 2:

\varphi(au)\neq 0

Then
\varphi(au)>0
because of
au\in TM\subseteq M
. Furthermore, we have

\varphi((1-a)u)>0
since
\varphi(au)<1=\varphi(u)
. For each
c\in A
with
\varphi(cu)>0
and

\varphi\in S(I,cM,u)
,

\varphi_{c}\colon I\to\mathbb{R},\ b\mapsto\frac{\varphi(cb)}{\varphi(cu)}

is a state of
(I,M,u)
. In particular,
\varphi_{a},\varphi_{1-a}\in S(I,M,u)
. Because of

\varphi=\varphi(au)\varphi_{a}+\varphi((1-a)u)\varphi_{1-a},

\varphi(au)>0
,
\varphi((1-a)u)>0
and

\varphi(au)+\varphi((1-a)u)=\varphi(u)=1
, we have by
4.2
or

3.8
that
\varphi=\varphi_{a}
(and
\varphi=\varphi_{1-a}
).
∎

Corollary 2.8
.

[
\to

2.3
(a)]

Let
T
be an Archimedean semiring of
A
such
that
K_{\geq 0}\subseteq T
and
M
a
T
-module of
A
with
1\in M
. Then every pure state
of
(A,M,1)
is a ring homomorphism.

Corollary 2.9
.

[
\to

2.3
(a)]

Let
M
be an Archimedean
\left(\sum K_{\geq 0}A^{2}\right)
-module of
A
.
Then every pure state of
(A,M,1)
is a ring homomorphism.

Corollary 2.10

(Becker, Schwartz
[
BS
]
, first generalization of the abstract Archimedean
Positivstellensatz
1.3

in the version of
2.1
)
.

[
\to

2.3
(b)]

Let
T
be an Archimedean semiring of
A
with
K_{\geq 0}\subseteq T
,
M
a
T
-module
of
A
with
1\in M
and
a\in A
. Then the following are equivalent:

(a)

\varphi(a)>0
for all (
K
-linear) ring homomorphisms
\varphi\colon A\to\mathbb{R}
with

\varphi(M)\subseteq\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
.

(b)

\exists N\in\mathbb{N}:a\in\frac{1}{N}+M

Proof.

3.19
,
2.2
,
2.8

∎

Corollary 2.11

(Jacobi
[
Jac
]
, second generalization of the abstract Archimedean
Positivstellensatz
1.3

in the version of
2.1
)
.

[
\to

2.3
(b)]

Let
M
be an Archimedean
\left(\sum K_{\geq 0}A^{2}\right)
-module of

. Then (a) and (b)
from
2.10
are equivalent.

Remark 2.12
.

Using Lemma
2.1
, one gets for the polynomial ring

K[\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.6817pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.7584pt{}}}$\hss}}}X]
concrete geometric versions of
2.10
and

2.11
which are completely analogous to
2.2

(first and second generalization of the Archimedean Positivstellensatz). Instead of
stating them, we give immediately concrete examples.

Example 2.13
.

[
\to

2.10
]
Let
\ell_{1},\dots,\ell_{m}\in\mathbb{R}[\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.6817pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.7584pt{}}}$\hss}}}X]_{1}
such that

\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\mid\ell_{1}(x)\geq 0,\dots,\ell_{m}(x)\geq 0\}

is nonempty and compact. Moreover, let
g_{1},\dots,g_{\ell}\in\mathbb{R}[\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.6817pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.7584pt{}}}$\hss}}}X]
and set

S:=\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\mid\ell_{1}(x)\geq 0,\dots,\ell_{m}(x)\geq 0,g_{1}(x)\geq 0,\dots,g_{\ell}(x)\geq 0\}.

Then for each
f\in\mathbb{R}[\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.6817pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.7584pt{}}}$\hss}}}X]
with
f>0
on
S
, we have

f\in\sum_{i=0}^{\ell}\sum_{\alpha\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{m}}\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\ell_{1}^{\alpha_{1}}\dotsm\ell_{m}^{\alpha_{m}}g_{i}=:M

where
g_{0}:=1
. This is because
M
is a
T
-module with
1\in M
for the
semiring

T:=\sum_{\alpha\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{m}}\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\ell_{1}^{\alpha_{1}}\dotsm\ell_{m}^{\alpha_{m}}

which is
Archimedean by
1.10
(c).

Example 2.14

(Putinar)
.

[
\to

2.11
]
Let
R\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
and let
g_{1},\dots,g_{m}\in\mathbb{R}[\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.6817pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.7584pt{}}}$\hss}}}X]
. Set

S:=\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\mid g_{1}(x)\geq 0,\dots,g_{m}(x)\geq 0,\|x\|\leq R\}.

Then for every
f\in\mathbb{R}[\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.6817pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.7584pt{}}}$\hss}}}X]
with
f>0
on
S
, we have

f\in\sum_{i=0}^{m+1}\sum\mathbb{R}[\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.6817pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.7584pt{}}}$\hss}}}X]^{2}g_{i}

with
g_{0}:=1
and
g_{m+1}:=R^{2}-\sum_{i=1}^{n}X_{i}^{2}
[
\to

1.13
(b)].

Example 2.15

(Pólya
[
Pól
]
)
.

[
\to

2.10
]
Let
k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
and suppose
f\in\mathbb{R}[\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.6817pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.7584pt{}}}$\hss}}}X]
a
k
-form such that
f(x)>0
for all

x\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{n}\setminus\{0\}
. Then there is some
N\in\mathbb{N}
such that

(X_{1}+\dots+X_{n})^{N}f\in\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\alpha\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{n}\\
|\alpha|=N+k\end{subarray}}\mathbb{R}_{>0}\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.6817pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.7584pt{}}}$\hss}}}X^{\alpha}.

This can be shown as follows: We have
f>0
on

\Delta:=\{x\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{n}\mid x_{1}+\ldots+x_{n}=1\}
. By
2.10
,
we obtain analogously to
2.13
that

f-\varepsilon\in\sum_{\alpha\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{n+2}}\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}X_{1}^{\alpha_{1}}\dotsm X_{n}^{\alpha_{n}}(1-(X_{1}+\dots+X_{n}))^{\alpha_{n+1}}(X_{1}+\dots+X_{n}-1)^{\alpha_{n+2}}.

By substituting
X_{i}\mapsto\frac{X_{i}}{X_{1}+\dots+X_{n}}
and clearing denominators, one gets the
claim due to homogeneity of
f
.

3
Dichotomy of pure states on ideals

In this section, we let
K
again be a subring of
A
so that we consider
A
also
as a
K
-vector space.

Proposition 3.1
.

Let
I
be a ideal of
A
and
u\in I
. Let
\varphi\in S(I,\emptyset,u)

[
\to

1.9
]
.
Then the following are equivalent:

(a)

\forall a\in A:\forall b\in I:\varphi(ab)=\varphi(au)\varphi(b)

[
\to

2.7
(*)
]

(b)

There is a ring homomorphism
\Phi\colon A\to\mathbb{R}
such that

(**)\qquad\forall a\in A:\forall b\in I:\varphi(ab)=\Phi(a)\varphi(b).

In Condition (b),
\Phi
is uniquely determined since
(**)
implies

\Phi(a)=\varphi(au)
for all
a\in A
and we call
\Phi
the ring homomorphism
belonging
to
or
associated to

\varphi
(on
A
). Note that
\Phi
does not depend on
u
for if
v\in I
with
\varphi(v)=1
then

(**)
of course also implies
\Phi(a)=\varphi(av)
. Exactly one of the following alternatives occurs:

(1)

\Phi(u)\neq 0
and
\forall b\in I:\varphi(b)=\frac{\Phi(b)}{\Phi(u)}

(2)

\Phi|_{I}=0

Proof.

(a)
\implies
(b)
If (a) holds, then

\Phi\colon A\to\mathbb{R},\ a\mapsto\varphi(au)
is a ring homomorphism since

\Phi(a)\Phi(b)=\varphi(au)\varphi(bu)\overset{(*)}{=}\varphi(abu)=\Phi(ab)
holds for all
a,b\in A
.

(b)
\implies
(a)
is clear.

Because of
u\in I
it is clear that (1) and (2) exclude
each other. If
\varphi(u^{2})\neq 0
, then (1) occurs since
(*)
implies

\varphi(bu)=\varphi(u^{2})\varphi(b)
for all
b\in I
. If
\varphi(u^{2})=0
, then

\varphi(bu)=\varphi(u^{2})\varphi(b)=0\varphi(b)=0
for all
b\in I
.
∎

Theorem 3.2

(Dichotomy)
.

Under the hypotheses of
2.7
, exactly one of the following cases occurs:

(1)

\varphi
is the restriction of a scaled ring homomorphism: There is a ring homomorphism

\Phi\colon A\to\mathbb{R}
such that
\Phi(u)\neq 0
and
\varphi=\frac{1}{\Phi(u)}\Phi|_{I}
.

(2)

There is a ring homomorphism
\Phi\colon A\to\mathbb{R}
with
\Phi|_{I}=0
such that

(**)
from
3.1
(b) holds.

We have
\text{(1)}\iff\varphi(u^{2})\neq 0
and
\text{(2)}\iff\varphi(u^{2})=0
.
In both
(1)
and
(2)
,
\Phi
is uniquely determined, namely it is the ring homomorphism
that according to
3.1
belongs to
\varphi
. We have
\Phi(T)\subseteq\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
.
If
u\in T
, then additionally
\Phi(M)\subseteq\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
.

Proof.

Easy with
2.7
and
3.1
.
∎

Corollary 3.3
.

Let
M
be a
\left(\sum K_{\geq 0}A^{2}\right)
-module of
A
with
1\in M
. If
M
has a unit in

A
, then
M
is Archimedean.

Proof.

Let
u
be a unit for
M
in
A
. By
3.19
, it is enough to show
that
\varphi(1)>0
for all
\varphi\in\extr S(A,M,u)
. Now let
\varphi
be a pure state of

(A,M,u)
with the associated ring homomorphism
\Phi\colon A\to\mathbb{R}
. Due to

\Phi(1)=1\neq 0
, in the Dichotomy
3.2

only case (1) can occur, i.e.,
\Phi(u)\neq 0
and
\varphi=\frac{1}{\Phi(u)}\Phi
.
Because of
\Phi(u)=\varphi(u^{2})=\varphi(u^{2}\cdot 1)\in\varphi(M)\subseteq\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
, we have

\Phi(u)>0
. It follows that
\varphi(1)>0
.
∎

Example 3.4
.

Consider the semiring
T:=\sum_{\alpha,\beta,\gamma\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}K_{\geq 0}X^{\alpha}Y^{\beta}(1-X-Y)^{\gamma}

of
K[X,Y]
and

S:=\{(x,y)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}\mid\forall p\in T:p(x,y)\geq 0\}=\{(x,y)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}\mid x\geq 0,y\geq 0,x+y\leq 1\}.

Since
S
is bounded and

X,Y,1-X-Y
are linear,
T
is Archimedean by
1.10
(c).
Consider the ideal
I:=(X,Y)
and the
T
-module
M:=T\cap I
of
K[X,Y]
.
Then
u:=X+Y
is a unit for
M
in
I
because
B_{(K[X,Y],T)}\overset{\ref{archmoduleb}}{=}K[X,Y]

and thus by
1.8

B_{(K[X,Y],M,u)}
is an ideal of

K[X,Y]
that contains
X
,
Y
and thus

I
since
u\pm X,u\pm Y\in M
. The ring homomorphisms

\Phi\colon K[X,Y]\to\mathbb{R}

satisfying
\Phi(T)\subseteq\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
are obviously exactly the evaluations

\ev_{x}
in points
x\in S
(compare Lemma
2.1
). Now let
\varphi
be a pure state
of
(I,M,u)
. By the Dichotomy
3.2
, exactly one of the following cases occurs:

(1)

There is some
(x,y)\in S\setminus\{(0,0)\}
with

\varphi(p)=\frac{p(x,y)}{x+y}
for all
p\in I
.

(2)

\varphi(pX+qY)=\varphi(pX)+\varphi(qY)=p(0,0)\varphi(X)+q(0,0)\varphi(Y)
for all
p,q\in K[X,Y]
.

In Case (2), one can set
\lambda_{1}:=\varphi(X)\geq 0
and
\lambda_{2}:=\varphi(Y)\geq 0
and one obtains

\lambda_{1}+\lambda_{2}=\varphi(X+Y)=\varphi(u)=1
as well as
\varphi=\lambda_{1}\varphi_{1}+\lambda_{2}\varphi_{2}
with

\varphi_{1}\colon I\to\mathbb{R},\ p\mapsto\frac{\partial p}{\partial X}(0,0)
and

\varphi_{2}\colon I\to\mathbb{R},\ p\mapsto\frac{\partial p}{\partial Y}(0,0)
. Since every polynomial
in
M
vanishes in the origin and is nonnegative on
S
, we obtain

\varphi_{1},\varphi_{2}\in S(I,M,u)
. Because of
\varphi\in\extr S(I,M,u)
, in Case (2)
we have
\varphi=\varphi_{1}
or
\varphi=\varphi_{2}
. Using
3.19
, we now
obtain: If
f\in K[X,Y]
with
f>0
on
S\setminus\{0\}
,
f(0)=0
,

\frac{\partial f}{\partial X}(0)>0
and
\frac{\partial f}{\partial Y}(0)>0
, then
f\in T
.

Example 3.5
.

Let
T
and
S
be as in Example
3.4
. Consider the ideal
I:=(X)
and the

T
-module
M:=T\cap I
of
K[X,Y]
. Then
u:=X
is a unit for
M
in
I
since

B_{(K[X,Y],M,u)}
is an ideal of
K[X,Y]
by
1.8
that contains
X
and thus
I

because
u\pm X\in M
. Let
\varphi
be a pure state of
(I,M,u)
. By the Dichotomy

3.2
, exactly one of the following cases occurs:

(1)

There is some
(x,y)\in S\setminus(\{0\}\times\mathbb{R})
with
\varphi(p)=\frac{p(x,y)}{x}
for all

p\in I
.

(2)

There is some
y\in[0,1]
such that
\varphi(pX)=p(0,y)\varphi(X)=p(0,y)\varphi(u)=p(0,y)
for
alle
p\in K[X,Y]
.

In Case (2), there is obviously a
y\in[0,1]
such that
\varphi(p)=\frac{\partial p}{\partial X}(0,y)

for all
p\in I
. Observe that each
f\in K[X,Y]
with
f=0
on
S\cap(\{0\}\times\mathbb{R})

satisfies
f(0,Y)=0
and thus
f\in I
. Now
3.19
yields:
If
f\in K[X,Y]
with
f>0
on
S\setminus(\{0\}\times\mathbb{R})
,
f=0
on

S\cap(\{0\}\times\mathbb{R})
and
\frac{\partial f}{\partial X}(0,y)>0
for all
y\in[0,1]
, then

f\in T
. At first glance, it might irritate that one would have to check here that

\frac{\partial f}{\partial X}(0,1)>0
. However, note that for
y=1
and in fact for every

y\in\mathbb{R}
,
\frac{\partial f}{\partial X}(0,y)
is the derivative of
f
in
every
direction

(1,z)
with
z\in\mathbb{R}
since
\frac{\partial f}{\partial Y}(0,y)=0
.

Example 3.6
.

Let
T
and
S
again be as in
3.4
and
3.5
. Consider the ideal

I:=(X^{2},XY)
and the
T
-module
M:=T\cap I
of
K[X,Y]
. Then
u:=X^{2}+XY
is a
unit for
M
in
I
since
u\pm X^{2},u\pm XY\in M
. Let
\varphi
be a pure state of

(I,M,u)
. By the Dichotomy
3.2
, exactly one of the following cases occurs:

(1)

There is some
(x,y)\in S\setminus(\{0\}\times\mathbb{R})
with

\varphi(p)=\frac{p(x,y)}{x(x+y)}
for all
p\in I
.

(2)

There is some
y\in[0,1]
such that
\varphi(pX^{2}+qXY)=p(0,y)\varphi(X^{2})+q(0,y)\varphi(XY)

for all
p,q\in K[X,Y]
.

Suppose now that (2) holds and fix
y\in[0,1]
accordingly. Consider

\lambda_{1}:=\varphi(X^{2})\geq 0
,
\lambda_{2}:=\varphi(XY)\geq 0
. Then
\lambda_{1}+\lambda_{2}=\varphi(u)=1
.

Consider first the case
y>0
. From
0=\varphi(YX^{2}-X(XY))=\lambda_{1}y-\lambda_{2}0=\lambda_{1}y
we
get
\lambda_{1}=0
. Then

\frac{1}{y}\frac{\partial(pX^{2}+qXY)}{\partial X}(0,y)=\frac{1}{y}q(0,y)y=q(0,y)=\lambda_{1}p(0,y)+\lambda_{2}q(0,y)=\varphi(pX^{2}+qXY)

for all
p,q\in K[X,Y]
.
Hence
\varphi=\varphi_{y}
with

\varphi_{y}\colon I\to\mathbb{R},\ p\mapsto\frac{1}{y}\frac{\partial p}{\partial X}(0,y).

Consider now the case
y=0
. Then

\frac{1}{2}\frac{\partial^{2}(pX^{2}+qXY)}{\partial X^{2}}(0,0)=p(0,0)=p(0,y)
and

\frac{\partial^{2}(pX^{2}+qXY)}{\partial X\partial Y}(0,0)=q(0,0)=q(0,y)

for all
p,q\in K[X,Y]
. Hence
\varphi=\lambda_{1}\psi_{1}+\lambda_{2}\psi_{2}
with

\psi_{1}\colon I\to\mathbb{R},\ p\mapsto\frac{1}{2}\frac{\partial^{2}p}{\partial X^{2}}(0,0)\qquad\text{and}\qquad\psi_{2}\colon I\to\mathbb{R},\ p\mapsto\frac{\partial^{2}p}{\partial X\partial Y}(0,0).

Before we give a summary, we observe that

I=\left\{f\in K[X,Y]\mid f=0\text{ on }S\cap(\{0\}\times\mathbb{R}),\frac{\partial f}{\partial X}(0)=0\right\}

where “
\subseteq
” is clear since the right hand side forms obviously an ideal and
“
\supseteq
” can be seen as follows:
If
f\in K[X,Y]
with
f=0
on
S\cap(\{0\}\times\mathbb{R})
, then
f(0,Y)=0
and thus
f\in(X)
.
If
f=Xg\in K[X,Y]
with
\frac{\partial f}{\partial X}(0)=0
, then
g(0)=0
, hence

g\in(X,Y)
and consequently
f\in(X^{2},XY)
. Taking into account that each polynomial in
M

is nonnegative on
S
, one obtains
\varphi_{y}\in S(I,M,u)
for all
y\in(0,1]_{\mathbb{R}}
and

\psi_{1},\psi_{2}\in S(I,M,u)
. The above considerations therefore yield

\extr S(I,M,u)\subseteq\{\varphi_{y}\mid y\in(0,1]_{\mathbb{R}}\}\cup\{\psi_{1},\psi_{2}\}

from which one obtains with

3.19
: If
f\in K[X,Y]
with

•

f>0
on
S\setminus(\{0\}\times\mathbb{R})
,

•

f=0
on
S\cap(\{0\}\times\mathbb{R})
,

•

\frac{\partial f}{\partial X}(0,y)>0
for
y\in(0,1]_{\mathbb{R}}
,

•

\frac{\partial f}{\partial X}(0,0)=0
,

•

\frac{\partial^{2}f}{\partial X^{2}}(0,0)>0
and

•

\frac{\partial^{2}f}{\partial X\partial Y}(0,0)>0
,

then
f\in T
.

4
A local-global-principle

Proposition 4.1
.

Let
T
be a semiring of
A
with
K_{\geq 0}\subseteq T
,
M
a
T
-module of

A
,
n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
and
a_{1},\dots,a_{n}\in A
. Set
I:=(a_{1},\dots,a_{n})
. Moreover, let
u
be a
unit for
\begin{Bmatrix}T\\
M\end{Bmatrix}
in
A
and suppose
a_{1},\dots,a_{n}\in\begin{Bmatrix}M\\
T\end{Bmatrix}
. Then

u(a_{1}+\ldots+a_{n})
is a unit for
M\cap I
in
I
.

Proof.

Let
b\in I
and set
v:=u(a_{1}+\dots+a_{n})
. To show:
\exists N\in\mathbb{N}:Nv+b\in M\cap I
.
Write
b=\sum_{i=1}^{n}c_{i}a_{i}
with
c_{1},\dots,c_{n}\in A
. Choose
N\in\mathbb{N}
such that

Nu\pm c_{i}\in\begin{Bmatrix}T\\
M\end{Bmatrix}
for
i\in\{1,\dots,n\}
. Then
Nv\pm b=\sum_{i=1}^{n}(Nua_{i}\pm c_{i}a_{i})=\sum_{i=1}^{n}(Nu\pm c_{i})a_{i}\in M
.
∎

Theorem 4.2

(Burgdorf, Scheiderer, Schweighofer
[
BSS
]
)
.

Let
T
be an Archimedean semiring of
A
with
K_{\geq 0}\subseteq T
and
M
a
T
-module
of
A
. Let
a\in A
such that there is for each maximal ideal
\mathfrak{m}
of
A
some

t\in T\setminus\mathfrak{m}
with
ta\in M
. Then
a\in M
.

Proof (simplified by Leonhard Nenno).

The ideal

I:=(\{t\in T\mid ta\in M\})

is not contained in any maximal ideal
\mathfrak{m}
of
A
for otherwise we find by our hypothesis some
s\in T\setminus\mathfrak{m}
with

sa\in M
which entails the contradiction
s\in I\subseteq\mathfrak{m}
. It follows that
1\in I
, i.e., we can choose
m\in\mathbb{N}
and
t_{1},\dots,t_{m}\in T
and

d_{1},\dots,d_{m}\in A
with
t_{1}a,\dots,t_{m}a\in M
such that

1=d_{1}t_{1}+\ldots+d_{m}t_{m}.

Multiplying with
a
, it follows that

a\in(at_{1},\ldots,at_{m})=:J.

By the (first version of)
4.1
,
u:=at_{1}+\ldots+at_{m}=at

with
t:=t_{1}+\ldots+t_{m}\in T
is a unit for
M\cap J
in
J
. To show that
a\in M
, we will now
apply
3.20
. So let
\varphi
be a pure state of
(J,J\cap M,u)
. To show:

\varphi(a)>0
. Denote by
\Phi
the ring homomorphism that belongs to
\varphi
according to
2.7
and

3.1
. We have
\Phi(T)\subseteq\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
[
\to

3.2
].
Now

1=\varphi(u)=\varphi(at)=\varphi(ta)=\underbrace{\Phi(t)}_{\geq 0}\varphi(a).

Thus
\varphi(a)>0
.
∎

Chapter 9
Pure states and nonnegative polynomials over real closed fields

1
Pure states and polynomials over real closed fields

Throughout this section, we let
R
be a real closed extension field of
\mathbb{R}
, we set

\mathscr{O}:=\mathscr{O}_{R}
,
\mathfrak{m}:=\mathfrak{m}_{R}
and we make extensive use of the standard part maps

\mathscr{O}\to\mathbb{R},\ a\mapsto\st(a)
,
\mathscr{O}[\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.6817pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.7584pt{}}}$\hss}}}X]\to\mathbb{R}[\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.6817pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.7584pt{}}}$\hss}}}X],\ p\mapsto\st(p)
[
\to

4.7
] and

\mathscr{O}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}^{n},\ x\mapsto\st(x):=(\st(x_{1}),\ldots,\st(x_{n}))
which are
surjective ring homomorphisms.

Definition 1.1
.

[
\to

2.1
,
1.2
(a)]
Let
A
be a commutative ring and
M\subseteq A
. Then
M
is called a
quadratic module
of
A
if

M
is a
\sum A^{2}
-module of
A
containing
1
[
\to

1.1
], or in other words, if

\{0,1\}\subseteq M
,
M+M\subseteq M
and
A^{2}M\subseteq M
.
We call a quadratic module
M
of
A

Archimedean
if

B_{(A,M)}=A
[
\to

3.1
,
3.3
,
1.5
].

Proposition 1.2
.

[
\to

1.13
]

Suppose
n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
and
M
is a quadratic module of
\mathscr{O}[\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.6817pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.7584pt{}}}$\hss}}}X]
. Then the following are equivalent:

(a)

M
is Archimedean.

(b)

\exists N\in\mathbb{N}:N-\sum_{i=1}^{n}X_{i}^{2}\in M

(c)

\exists N\in\mathbb{N}:\forall i\in\{1,\dots,n\}:N\pm X_{i}\in M

Proof.

(a)
\implies
(b)
is trivial.

(b)
\implies
(c)
If (b) holds, then
N-X_{i}^{2}\in M
and thus
X_{i}^{2}\in B_{(\mathscr{O}[\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.6817pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.6817pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to3.55522pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to2.53944pt{}}}$\hss}}}X],M)}

for all
i\in\{1,\dots,n\}
. Apply now
1.11
.

(c)
\implies
(a)
follows from
1.12
since
\mathscr{O}\subseteq B_{(\mathscr{O}[\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.6817pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.6817pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to3.55522pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to2.53944pt{}}}$\hss}}}X],M)}
.
∎

Remark 1.3
.

In contrast to
1.13
(d), one cannot add

\exists m\in\mathbb{N}:\exists\ell_{1},\dots,\ell_{m}\in M\cap\mathscr{O}[\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.6817pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.7584pt{}}}$\hss}}}X]_{1}:\exists N\in\mathbb{N}:\\
\emptyset\neq\{x\in R^{n}\mid\ell_{1}(x)\geq 0,\dots,\ell_{m}(x)\geq 0\}\subseteq[-N,N]_{R}^{n}

as another equivalent condition
in
1.2
. Indeed, choose
R
non-Archimedean [
\to

4.4
] and
\varepsilon\in\mathfrak{m}\setminus\{0\}
.
Then
\emptyset\neq\{0\}=\{x\in R\mid\varepsilon x\geq 0,-\varepsilon x\geq 0\}\subseteq[-1,1]_{R}
but
the quadratic module

\sum\mathscr{O}[X]^{2}+\sum\mathscr{O}[X]^{2}\varepsilon X+\sum\mathscr{O}[X]^{2}(-\varepsilon X)\overset{\ref{diffsquare}}{=}\sum\mathscr{O}[X]^{2}+\mathscr{O}[X]\varepsilon X

generated by
\varepsilon X
and
-\varepsilon X

in
\mathscr{O}[X]
is not Archimedean for if we had
N\in\mathbb{N}
with

N-X^{2}\in\sum\mathscr{O}[X]^{2}+\mathscr{O}[X]\varepsilon X,

then taking standard parts would yield

N-X^{2}\in\sum\mathbb{R}[X]^{2}
which contradicts
2.4
(b).

Definition 1.4
.

[
\to

2.1
]
For every
x\in\mathscr{O}^{n}
, we define the ring homomorphism

\ev_{x}\colon\mathscr{O}[\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.6817pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.7584pt{}}}$\hss}}}X]\to\mathscr{O},\ p\mapsto p(x)

and set
I_{x}:=\ker\ev_{x}
.

Proposition 1.5
.

Let
x\in\mathscr{O}^{n}
. Then
I_{x}=(X_{1}-x_{1},\dots,X_{n}-x_{n})
.

Proof.

It is trivial that
J:=(X_{1}-x_{1},\dots,X_{n}-x_{n})\subseteq I_{x}
.
Conversely,
p\equiv_{J}p(x)=0
for all

p\in I_{x}
. This shows the converse inclusion
I_{x}\subseteq J
.
∎

Notation 1.6
.

Suppose
A
is a commutative ring and
I
is an ideal of
A
.
As it is customary in commutative algebra, we will in the following often denote by

I^{2}
the product of the ideal
I
with itself which in our suggestive notation
[
\to

1.18
] would be written
\sum II
. From the context,
the reader should be able to avoid misinterpreting

I^{2}
as what it would mean in this suggestive notation, namely

\{a^{2}\mid a\in I\}
. The same applies to
I^{3}
and so on.
Another source of confusion could be that, we will often use the notation

\mathfrak{m}^{n}
to denote the Cartesian power

\underbrace{\mathfrak{m}\times\ldots\times\mathfrak{m}}_{\text{$n$ times}}.

Lemma 1.7
.

Suppose
x,y\in\mathscr{O}^{n}
with
\st(x)\neq\st(y)
. Then
I_{x}
and
I_{y}
are coprime, i.e.,

1\in I_{x}+I_{y}
.

Proof.

WLOG
x_{1}-y_{1}\notin\mathfrak{m}
. Then
x_{1}-y_{1}\in\mathscr{O}^{\times}
and

1=\frac{x_{1}-X_{1}}{x_{1}-y_{1}}+\frac{X_{1}-y_{1}}{x_{1}-y_{1}}\in I_{x}+I_{y}.

∎

Lemma 1.8
.

Let
M
be an Archimedean quadratic module of
\mathscr{O}[\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.6817pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.7584pt{}}}$\hss}}}X]
and
x\in\mathscr{O}^{n}
. Then

u_{x}:=(X_{1}-x_{1})^{2}+\ldots+(X_{n}-x_{n})^{2}

is a unit for
M\cap I_{x}^{2}
in the real vector space
I_{x}^{2}
[
\to

1.4
].

Proof.

Using the ring automorphism

\mathscr{O}[\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.6817pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.7584pt{}}}$\hss}}}X]\to\mathscr{O}[\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.6817pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.7584pt{}}}$\hss}}}X],\ p\mapsto p(X_{1}-x_{1},\ldots,X_{n}-x_{n}),

which is also an isomorphism of real vector spaces, we can reduce to the case
x=0
.
Since
u_{x}\in I_{0}^{2}
, it suffices to show that
I_{0}^{2}\subseteq B_{(\mathscr{O}[\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.6817pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.6817pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to3.55522pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to2.53944pt{}}}$\hss}}}X],M,u_{x})}
.
Since
M
is Archimedean,
1.12
yields that

B_{(\mathscr{O}[\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.6817pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.6817pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to3.55522pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to2.53944pt{}}}$\hss}}}X],M,u_{x})}
is an
\mathscr{O}[\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.6817pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.7584pt{}}}$\hss}}}X]
-module of
\mathscr{O}[\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.6817pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.7584pt{}}}$\hss}}}X]
[
\to

1.1
], i.e., an ideal of

\mathscr{O}[\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.6817pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.7584pt{}}}$\hss}}}X]
. Because of

I_{0}^{2}=(X_{i}X_{j}\mid i,j\in\{1,\dots,n\}),

it suffices therefore to show that
X_{i}X_{j}\in B_{(\mathscr{O}[\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.6817pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.6817pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to3.55522pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to2.53944pt{}}}$\hss}}}X],M,u_{x})}
for all
i,j\in\{1,\dots,n\}
.
Thus fix
i,j\in\{1,\dots,n\}
. Then
\frac{1}{2}(X_{i}^{2}+X_{j}^{2})\pm X_{i}X_{j}=\frac{1}{2}(X_{i}\pm X_{j})^{2}\in M

and thus
\frac{1}{2}u_{x}\pm X_{i}X_{j}\in M
. Since
u_{x}\in M
, this implies

u_{x}\pm X_{i}X_{j}\in M
.
∎

Notation 1.9
.

We use the symbols
\nabla
and
\hess
to denote the gradient and the Hessian of a real-valued function of
n
real variables, respectively. For a
polynomial

p\in\mathbb{R}[\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.6817pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.7584pt{}}}$\hss}}}X]
, we understand its
gradient
\nabla p
as a column vector from
\mathbb{R}[\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.6817pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.7584pt{}}}$\hss}}}X]^{n}
, i.e., as a vector of polynomials. Similarly, its Hessian

\hess p
is a symmetric matrix polynomial of size
n
, i.e., a symmetric matrix from
\mathbb{R}[\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.6817pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.7584pt{}}}$\hss}}}X]^{n\times n}
.
Using formal partial derivatives, we more generally define
\nabla p\in R[\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.6817pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.7584pt{}}}$\hss}}}X]^{n}
and

\hess p\in R[\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.6817pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.7584pt{}}}$\hss}}}X]^{n\times n}
even for
p\in R[\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.6817pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.7584pt{}}}$\hss}}}X]
.

Lemma 1.10
.

Let
x\in\mathscr{O}^{n}
and
\varphi\in S(I_{x}^{2},\sum\mathscr{O}[\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.6817pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.7584pt{}}}$\hss}}}X]^{2}\cap I_{x}^{2},u_{x})
[
\to

1.9
] such that

\varphi|_{I_{x}^{3}}=0
. Then there exist

v_{1},\dots,v_{n}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
such that

\sum_{i=1}^{n}v_{i}^{T}v_{i}=1
and

\varphi(p)=\frac{1}{2}\st\left(\sum_{i=1}^{n}v_{i}^{T}(\hess p)(x)v_{i}\right)

for all
p\in I_{x}^{2}
.

Proof.

As in the proof of Lemma
1.8
, one easily reduces to the case
x=0
.

Claim 1:

\varphi(au_{x})=0
for all
a\in\mathfrak{m}
.

Explanation.
Let
a\in\mathfrak{m}
. WLOG
a\geq 0
. Then
a\in\mathscr{O}\cap R_{\geq 0}=\mathscr{O}^{2}
and thus

au_{x}\in\sum\mathscr{O}[\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.6817pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.7584pt{}}}$\hss}}}X]^{2}\cap I_{0}^{2}
. This shows
\varphi(au_{x})\geq 0
. It remains to show that
\varphi(au_{x})\leq\frac{1}{N}
for all

N\in\mathbb{N}
. For this purpose, fix
N\in\mathbb{N}
. Then
\frac{1}{N}-a\in\mathscr{O}\cap R_{\geq 0}=\mathscr{O}^{2}
and thus

\left(\frac{1}{N}-a\right)u\in\sum\mathscr{O}[\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.6817pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.7584pt{}}}$\hss}}}X]^{2}\cap I_{0}^{2}
. It follows that
\varphi\left(\left(\frac{1}{N}-a\right)u_{x}\right)\geq 0
, i.e.,

\varphi(au_{x})\leq\frac{1}{N}
.

Claim 2:

\varphi(aX_{i}^{2})=0
for all
a\in\mathfrak{m}
and
i\in\{1,\dots,n\}
.

Explanation.
Let
a\in\mathfrak{m}
. WLOG
a\geq 0
and thus
a\in\mathscr{O}^{2}
.
Then

\sum_{i=1}^{n}\underbrace{\varphi(\overbrace{aX_{i}^{2}}^{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptstyle\in\mathscr{O}[\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to3.55522pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to3.55522pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to2.48865pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to1.7776pt{}}}$\hss}}}X]^{2}\cap I_{0}^{2}$\hss}})}_{\geq 0}=\varphi(au_{x})\overset{\text{Claim 1}}{=}0.

Claim 3:

\varphi(aX_{i}X_{j})=0
for all
a\in\mathfrak{m}
and
i,j\in\{1,\dots,n\}
.

Explanation.
Fix
i,j\in\{1,\dots,n\}
and
a\in\mathfrak{m}
. If
i=j
, then we are done by Claim 2. So suppose

i\neq j
. WLOG
a\geq 0
and thus
a\in\mathscr{O}^{2}
.
Then

a(X_{i}^{2}+X_{j}^{2}\pm 2X_{i}X_{j})=a(X_{i}\pm X_{j})^{2}\in\mathscr{O}[\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.6817pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.7584pt{}}}$\hss}}}X]^{2}\cap I_{0}^{2}

and thus

\pm 2\varphi(aX_{i}X_{j})\underset{\text{Claim 2}}{=}\varphi(aX_{i}^{2})+\varphi(aX_{j}^{2})\pm 2\varphi(aX_{i}X_{j})\geq 0
.

Claim 4:

\varphi(p)=\frac{1}{2}\st\left(\tr\left((\hess p)(0)A\right)\right)
for all
p\in I_{0}^{2}
where

A:=\begin{pmatrix}\varphi(X_{1}X_{1})&\dots&\varphi(X_{1}X_{n})\\
\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
\varphi(X_{n}X_{1})&\dots&\varphi(X_{n}X_{n})\end{pmatrix}.

Explanation.
Let
p\in I_{0}^{2}
. By
\varphi|_{I_{0}^{3}}=0
, we can reduce to the case

p=aX_{i}X_{j}
with
i,j\in\{1,\dots,n\}
and
a\in\mathscr{O}
.
Using Claim 3, we can assume
a=1
. Comparing both sides,
yields the result.

Claim 5:

A
is psd [
\to

3.1
(b)].

Explanation.
If
w\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
, then
w^{T}Aw=\varphi((w_{1}X_{1}+\ldots+w_{n}X_{n})^{2})\geq 0
since

(w_{1}X_{1}+\ldots+w_{n}X_{n})^{2}\in\mathbb{R}[\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.6817pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.7584pt{}}}$\hss}}}X]^{2}\cap I_{0}^{2}\subseteq\sum\mathscr{O}[\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.6817pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.7584pt{}}}$\hss}}}X]^{2}\cap I_{0}^{2}.

By Claim 5 and
3.3
(c), we can choose
B\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
such that
A=B^{T}B
. Denote by
v_{i}
the
i
-th
row of
B
for
i\in\{1,\dots,n\}
. Then by Claim 4, we get

\varphi(p)=\frac{1}{2}\st(\tr((\hess p)(0)A))=\frac{1}{2}\st(\tr((\hess p)(0)B^{T}B))\\
=\frac{1}{2}\st(\tr(B(\hess p)(0)B^{T}))=\frac{1}{2}\st\left(\sum_{i=1}^{n}v_{i}^{T}(\hess p)(0)v_{i}\right)

for all
p\in I_{0}^{2}
.
In particular, we obtain
1=\varphi(u_{0})=\sum_{i=1}^{n}v_{i}^{T}v_{i}
.
∎

Lemma 1.11
.

Let
\Phi\colon\mathscr{O}[\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.6817pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.7584pt{}}}$\hss}}}X]\to\mathbb{R}
be a ring homomorphism. Then there is some
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
such that

\Phi(p)=\st(p(x))
for all
p\in\mathscr{O}[\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.6817pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.7584pt{}}}$\hss}}}X]
.

Proof.

By
1.15
, we have
\Phi|_{\mathbb{R}}=\id_{\mathbb{R}}
.
It is easy to see that
\Phi|_{\mathfrak{m}}=0
.
Indeed, for each

N\in\mathbb{N}
and
a\in\mathfrak{m}
, we have
\frac{1}{N}\pm a\in R_{\geq 0}\cap\mathscr{O}=\mathscr{O}^{2}

and therefore

\frac{1}{N}\pm\Phi(a)\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
.
Finally set

x:=(\Phi(X_{1}),\dots,\Phi(X_{n}))\in\mathbb{R}^{n}

and use that
\Phi|_{\mathbb{R}}=\id_{\mathbb{R}}
,
\Phi|_{\mathfrak{m}}=0
and that
\Phi
is a ring homomorphism.
∎

Theorem 1.12
.

[
\to

3.2
]

Let
M
be an Archimedean quadratic module of
\mathscr{O}[\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.6817pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.7584pt{}}}$\hss}}}X]
and set

S:=\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\mid\forall p\in M:\st(p(x))\geq 0\}.

Moreover, suppose
k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
and let
x_{1},\ldots,x_{k}\in\mathscr{O}^{n}
satisfy
\st(x_{i})\neq\st(x_{j})
for

i,j\in\{1,\dots,k\}
with
i\neq j
. Then

u:=u_{x_{1}}\dotsm u_{x_{k}}
is a unit for the cone
M\cap I
in the real vector space

I:=I_{x_{1}}^{2}\dotsm I_{x_{k}}^{2}=I_{x_{1}}^{2}\cap\ldots\cap I_{x_{k}}^{2}

and for all pure states
\varphi
of
(I,M\cap I,u)

exactly one of the following cases occurs:

(1)

There is an
x\in S\setminus\{\st(x_{1}),\dots,\st(x_{k})\}
such that

\varphi(p)=\st\left(\frac{p(x)}{u(x)}\right)

for all
p\in I
.

(2)

There is an
i\in\{1,\dots,k\}
and
v_{1},\dots,v_{n}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
such that

\sum_{\ell=1}^{n}v_{\ell}^{T}v_{\ell}=1
and

\varphi(p)=\st\left(\frac{\sum_{\ell=1}^{n}v_{\ell}^{T}(\hess p)(x_{i})v_{\ell}}{2\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}j=1\\
j\neq i\end{subarray}}^{k}u_{x_{j}}(x_{i})}\right)

for all
p\in I
.

Proof.

The Chinese remainder theorem from commutative algebra shows that

I=I_{x_{1}}^{2}\dotsm I_{x_{k}}^{2}=I_{x_{1}}^{2}\cap\ldots\cap I_{x_{k}}^{2}

since
I_{x_{i}}
and
I_{x_{j}}
and thus also
I_{x_{i}}^{2}
and
I_{x_{j}}^{2}
are coprime for all

i,j\in\{1,\dots,k\}
with
i\neq j
.
By
1.8
,
u_{x_{i}}
is a unit for
M\cap I_{x_{i}}^{2}
in
I_{x_{i}}^{2}

for each
i\in\{1,\dots,k\}
. To show that
u
is a unit
for the cone
M\cap I
in the real vector space
I
, it suffices to find for all

a_{1},b_{1}\in I_{x_{1}},\dots,a_{k},b_{k}\in I_{x_{k}}
an
N\in\mathbb{N}
such that

Nu+ab\in M
where we set
a:=a_{1}\dotsm a_{k}
and
b:=b_{1}\dotsm b_{k}
.
Because of
Nu+ab=(Nu-\frac{1}{2}a^{2}-\frac{1}{2}b^{2})+\frac{1}{2}(a+b)^{2}
, it is enough to find
N\in\mathbb{N}
with

Nu-a^{2}\in M
and
Nu-b^{2}\in M
. By symmetry, it suffices to find
N\in\mathbb{N}
with

Nu-a^{2}\in M
. Choose
N_{i}\in\mathbb{N}
with
N_{i}u_{x_{i}}-a_{i}^{2}\in M
for
i\in\{1,\ldots,k\}
. We now claim that

N:=N_{1}\dotsm N_{k}
does the job. Indeed, the reader shows easily by induction that actually

N_{1}\dotsm N_{i}u_{x_{1}}\dotsm u_{x_{i}}-a_{1}^{2}\dotsm a_{i}^{2}\in M

for
i\in\{1,\dots,k\}
. Now let
\varphi
be a pure state of
(I,M\cap I,u)
.
Denote by
\Phi\colon\mathscr{O}[\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.6817pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.7584pt{}}}$\hss}}}X]\to\mathbb{R}
the ring homomorphism belonging to

\varphi
, i.e.,

(*)\qquad\varphi(pq)=\Phi(p)\varphi(q)

for all
p\in\mathscr{O}[\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.6817pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.7584pt{}}}$\hss}}}X]
and
q\in I
[
\to

3.1
,
3.2
].
By Lemma
1.11
, we can choose
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
such that

\Phi(p)=\st(p(x))

for all
p\in\mathscr{O}[\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.6817pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.7584pt{}}}$\hss}}}X]
.
Since
u\in I\cap\sum\mathscr{O}[\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.6817pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.7584pt{}}}$\hss}}}X]^{2}
, we have

\st(p(x))=\Phi(p)=\Phi(p)\varphi(u)\overset{(*)}{=}\varphi(pu)=\varphi(up)\overset{up\in M}{\in}\varphi(M)\subseteq\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}

for all
p\in M
which means
x\in S
.

Now first suppose that Case (1) in the Dichotomy
3.2
occurs.
We show that
x
satisfies (1). Note that
\Phi(u)\neq 0
by
3.2
. This means
\st(u_{x_{i}}(x))\neq 0

and therefore
\st(x)\neq\st(x_{i})
for all
i\in\{1,\dots,k\}
. The rest follows from
3.2
.

Now suppose that Case (2) in the Dichotomy
3.2
occurs. We show that then (2) holds.
First note that
\prod_{i=1}^{k}\Phi(u_{x_{i}})=\Phi(u)=0
because
u\in I
and
\Phi|_{I}=0
. Choose
i\in\{1,\dots,k\}
such that
\st(u_{x_{i}}(x))=\Phi(u_{x_{i}})=0
.
Then
x=\st(x_{i})
. Define

\psi\colon I_{x_{i}}^{2}\to\mathbb{R},\ p\mapsto\varphi\left(p\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}j=1\\
j\neq i\end{subarray}}^{k}u_{x_{j}}\right).

Since
u_{x_{j}}\in\sum\mathscr{O}[\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.6817pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.7584pt{}}}$\hss}}}X]^{2}\cap I_{x_{j}}^{2}
for all
j\in\{1,\dots,k\}
, it follows that

\psi\in S(I_{x_{i}}^{2},M\cap I_{x_{i}}^{2},u_{x_{i}})
. If
p\in I_{x_{i}}
and
q\in I_{x_{i}}^{2}
, then

\psi(pq)=\varphi\left(pq\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}j=1\\
j\neq i\end{subarray}}^{k}u_{x_{j}}\right)\overset{(*)}{=}\Phi(p)\varphi\left(q\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}j=1\\
j\neq i\end{subarray}}^{k}u_{x_{j}}\right)=0

since
\Phi(p)=\st(p(x))=(\st(p))(x)=(\st(p))(\st(x_{i}))=\st(p(x_{i}))=\st(0)=0
.
It follows that
\psi|_{I_{x_{i}}^{3}}=0
.
We can thus apply Lemma
1.10
to
\psi
and obtain

v_{1},\dots,v_{n}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
such that

\sum_{\ell=1}^{n}v_{\ell}^{T}v_{\ell}=1
and

\psi(p)=\frac{1}{2}\st\left(\sum_{\ell=1}^{n}v_{\ell}^{T}(\hess p)(x_{i})v_{\ell}\right)

for all
p\in I_{x_{i}}^{2}
.
Because of
\st(x_{i})\neq\st(x_{j})
for
j\in\{1,\dots,k\}\setminus\{i\}
, we have

c:=\Phi\left(\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}j=1\\
j\neq i\end{subarray}}^{k}u_{x_{j}}\right)=\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}j=1\\
j\neq i\end{subarray}}^{k}\Phi(u_{x_{j}})=\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}j=1\\
j\neq i\end{subarray}}^{k}(\st(u_{x_{j}}))(\st(x_{i}))\neq 0.

Hence we obtain

c\varphi(p)\overset{(*)}{=}\psi(p)

for all
p\in I
.

It only remains to show that (1) and (2) cannot occur both at the same time. If (1) holds, then
we have obviously
\varphi(u^{2})\neq 0
. If (2) holds, then
\varphi(u^{2})=0
since
\hess(u^{2})(x_{i})=0
for
all
i\in\{1,\dots,k\}
as one easily shows.
∎

Lemma 1.13
.

For all
x\in\mathscr{O}^{n}
, we have

I_{x}^{2}=\left\{p\in\mathscr{O}[\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.6817pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.7584pt{}}}$\hss}}}X]\mid p(x)=0,\nabla p(x)=0\right\}.

Proof.

For
x=0
it is easy. One reduces the general case to the case
x=0
as in the proof of

1.8
.
∎

Theorem 1.14
.

Let
M
be an Archimedean quadratic module of
\mathscr{O}[\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.6817pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.7584pt{}}}$\hss}}}X]
and set

S:=\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\mid\forall p\in M:\st(p(x))\geq 0\}.

Moreover, suppose
k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
and let
x_{1},\ldots,x_{k}\in\mathscr{O}^{n}
have pairwise distinct standard parts. Let

f\in\bigcap_{i=1}^{k}I_{x_{i}}^{2}

such that

\st(f(x))>0

for all
x\in S\setminus\{\st(x_{1}),\ldots,\st(x_{k})\}
and

\st(v^{T}(\hess f)(x_{i})v)>0

for all
i\in\{1,\dots,k\}
and
v\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus\{0\}
.
Then
f\in M
.

Proof.

Define
I
and
u
as in Theorem
1.12
so that
f\in I
.
We will apply
3.20
to the real vector space
I
, the cone
M\cap I
in
I
and
the unit
u
for
M\cap I
. From Theorem
1.12
, we see indeed easily that
\varphi(f)>0
for all

\varphi\in\extr S(I,M\cap I,u)
.
∎

Corollary 1.15
.

Let
M
be an Archimedean quadratic module of
\mathscr{O}[\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.6817pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.7584pt{}}}$\hss}}}X]
and set

S:=\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\mid\forall p\in M:\st(p(x))\geq 0\}.

Moreover, let
k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
and
x_{1},\ldots,x_{k}\in\mathscr{O}^{n}
such that their standard parts are pairwise distinct and lie in the interior of
S
.
Let

f\in\bigcap_{i=1}^{k}I_{x_{i}}^{2}.

Set again
u:=u_{x_{1}}\dotsm u_{x_{k}}\in\mathscr{O}[\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.6817pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.7584pt{}}}$\hss}}}X]
.
Suppose there is
\varepsilon\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
such
that

f\geq\varepsilon u\text{ on }S.

Then
f\in M
.

Proof.

By
1.14
, we have to show:

(a)

\forall x\in S\setminus\{\st(x_{1}),\ldots,\st(x_{k})\}:\st(f(x))>0

(b)

\forall i\in\{1,\dots,k\}:\forall v\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus\{0\}:\st(v^{T}(\hess f)(x_{i})v)>0

It is easy to show (a). To show (b), fix
i\in\{1,\ldots,k\}
.
Because of
f-\varepsilon u\geq 0
on
S
and

(f-\varepsilon u)(x_{i})=f(x_{i})-\varepsilon u(x_{i})=0-0=0,

\st(x_{i})
is a local minimum of
\st(f-\varepsilon u)\in\mathbb{R}[\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.6817pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.7584pt{}}}$\hss}}}X]
on
\mathbb{R}^{n}
. From
elementary analysis, we know therefore that
(\hess\st(f-\varepsilon u))(\st(x_{i}))
is psd.
Because of
u_{x_{i}}(x_{i})=0
and
\nabla u_{x_{i}}(x_{i})=0
, we get

(\hess u)(x_{i})=\left(\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}j=1\\
j\neq i\end{subarray}}^{k}u_{x_{j}}(x_{i})\right)(\hess u_{x_{i}})(x_{i})=2\left(\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}j=1\\
j\neq i\end{subarray}}^{k}u_{x_{j}}(x_{i})\right)I_{n}.

Therefore

\st(v^{T}(\hess f)(x_{i})v)\geq\varepsilon\st(v^{T}(\hess u)(x_{i})v)=2\varepsilon v^{T}v\st\left(\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}j=1\\
j\neq i\end{subarray}}^{k}u_{x_{j}}(x_{i})\right)>0

for all
v\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus\{0\}
.
∎

Corollary 1.16
.

Let
n,m\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
and suppose
g_{1},\ldots,g_{m}\in\mathbb{R}[\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.6817pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.7584pt{}}}$\hss}}}X]
generate an Archimedean quadratic module
in
\mathbb{R}[\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.6817pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.7584pt{}}}$\hss}}}X]

[
\to

1.13
]
. Set

S:=\{x\in R^{n}\mid g_{1}(x)\geq 0,\ldots,g_{m}(x)\geq 0\}.

Moreover, let
k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
and
x_{1},\ldots,x_{k}\in\mathscr{O}^{n}
and
\varepsilon\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
such that
the sets
x_{1}+\varepsilon B,\ldots,x_{k}+\varepsilon B
are pairwise disjoint and all contained in
S

where
[
\to

1.10
]

B:=\{x\in R^{n}\mid\|x\|_{2}<1\}\subseteq\mathscr{O}^{n}.

Set once more
u:=u_{x_{1}}\dotsm u_{x_{k}}\in\mathscr{O}[\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.6817pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.7584pt{}}}$\hss}}}X]
.
Let
f\in\mathscr{O}[\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.6817pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.7584pt{}}}$\hss}}}X]
such that
f\geq\varepsilon u
on
S
and

f(x_{1})=\ldots=f(x_{k})=0.

Then
f
lies in the quadratic module generated by
g_{1},\ldots,g_{m}
in
\mathscr{O}[\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.6817pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.7584pt{}}}$\hss}}}X]
.

Proof.

The quadratic module
M
generated by
g_{1},\ldots,g_{m}
in
\mathscr{O}[\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.6817pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.7584pt{}}}$\hss}}}X]
is clearly also Archimedean [
\to
9.1.2].
Moreover, it is easy to see that
\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\mid\forall p\in M:\st(p(x))\geq 0\}=S\cap\mathbb{R}^{n}
.
Hence
f\in M
follows from
1.15
once we show that

\nabla f(x_{1})=\ldots=\nabla f(x_{k})=0.

Choose
d\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
with
f\in\mathbb{R}[\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.6817pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.7584pt{}}}$\hss}}}X]_{d}
.
Since
f\geq\varepsilon u\geq 0
on
S
and thus
f\geq 0
on
x_{i}+\varepsilon B
for all
i\in\{1,\ldots,k\}
, it suffices
to prove the following for
R^{\prime}=R
: If
p\in R^{\prime}[\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.6817pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.7584pt{}}}$\hss}}}X]_{d}
,
x\in R^{\prime n}
,
\delta\in R^{\prime}_{>0}
such that
p\geq 0
on

x+\delta B^{\prime}
where
B^{\prime}:=\{x\in R^{\prime n}\mid\|x\|_{2}<1\}
and

p(x)=0
, then
\nabla p(x)=0
. To see this, we employ the Tarski principle [
\to

8.19
]:
The class
of all
R^{\prime}\in\mathscr{R}
[
\to

8.3
] such that this holds true for all
p\in R^{\prime}[\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.6817pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.7584pt{}}}$\hss}}}X]_{d}
is
obviously a
0
-ary semialgebraic class by real quantifier elimination.
By elementary analysis,
\mathbb{R}
is an element of this class.
We conclude thus by
8.5
.
∎

2
Degree bounds and quadratic modules

Definition 2.1
.

Let
d,m\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
,
g_{1},\dots,g_{m}\in\mathbb{R}[\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.6817pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.7584pt{}}}$\hss}}}X]
and set
g_{0}:=1\in\mathbb{R}[\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.6817pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.7584pt{}}}$\hss}}}X]
. For
i\in\{0,\dots,m\}
, set

r_{i}:=\frac{d-\deg g_{i}}{2}
if
g_{i}\neq 0
and
r_{i}:=-\infty
if
g_{i}=0
.
Then we denote by
M(g_{1},\dots,g_{m})
the quadratic module generated by

g_{1},\dots,g_{m}
in
\mathbb{R}[\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.6817pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.7584pt{}}}$\hss}}}X]
.
Moreover, we define the
d
-truncated quadratic module

M_{d}(g_{1},\dots,g_{m})

associated to
g_{1},\dots,g_{m}
by

M_{d}(g_{1},\dots,g_{m}):=\left\{\sum_{i=0}^{m}\sum_{j}p_{ij}^{2}g_{i}\mid p_{ij}\in\mathbb{R}[\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.6817pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.7584pt{}}}$\hss}}}X]_{r_{i}}\right\}\subseteq M(g_{1},\dots,g_{m})\cap\mathbb{R}[\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.6817pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.7584pt{}}}$\hss}}}X]_{d}.

Remark 2.2
.

Let
m\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
and
g_{1},\dots,g_{m}\in\mathbb{R}[\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.6817pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.7584pt{}}}$\hss}}}X]
. Set again
g_{0}:=1\in\mathbb{R}[\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.6817pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.7584pt{}}}$\hss}}}X]
.

(a)

M(g_{1},\dots,g_{m})=\bigcup_{d\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}M_{d}(g_{1},\dots,g_{m})

(b)

For all
d\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
,

M_{d}(g_{1},\dots,g_{m})=\sum_{i=0}^{m}\left(\left(\sum\mathbb{R}[\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.6817pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.7584pt{}}}$\hss}}}X]^{2}g_{i}\right)\cap\mathbb{R}[\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.6817pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.7584pt{}}}$\hss}}}X]_{d}\right)

by
2.4
(b).

(c)

In general, the inclusion
M_{d}(g_{1},\dots,g_{m})\subseteq M(g_{1},\dots,g_{m})\cap\mathbb{R}[\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.6817pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.7584pt{}}}$\hss}}}X]_{d}
is proper
as
4.8
shows.
In fact, the validity of Schmüdgen’s and Putinar’s Positivstellensätze
3.5
and

2.14
strongly relies on this.

Theorem 2.3

(Putinar’s Positivstellensatz with zeros and degree bounds)
.

Let
n,m\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
and
g_{1},\ldots,g_{m}\in\mathbb{R}[\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.6817pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.7584pt{}}}$\hss}}}X]
such that
M(g_{1},\ldots,g_{m})
is Archimedean. Set

\displaystyle B

\displaystyle:=\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\mid\|x\|<1\}\qquad\text{and}

\displaystyle S

\displaystyle:=\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\mid g_{1}(x)\geq 0,\ldots,g_{m}(x)\geq 0\}.

Moreover, let
k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
,
N\in\mathbb{N}
and
\varepsilon\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
.
Then there exists

d\in\mathbb{N}_{0}

such that for all
f\in\mathbb{R}[\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.6817pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.7584pt{}}}$\hss}}}X]_{N}
with all coefficients in
[-N,N]_{\mathbb{R}}

and
\#\{x\in S\mid f(x)=0\}=k
, we have: Denoting by
x_{1},\dots,x_{k}
the distinct zeros of
f
on
S
,
if the sets
x_{1}+\varepsilon B,\ldots,x_{k}+\varepsilon B
are pairwise disjoint and contained in
S
and if we have

f\geq\varepsilon u
on
S

where
