u:=u_{x_{1}}\dotsm u_{x_{k}}\in\mathbb{R}[\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.6817pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.7584pt{}}}$\hss}}}X]

[
\to

1.8
]
, then

f\in M_{d}(g_{1},\dots,g_{m}).

Proof.

(cf. the proof of Theorem
4.5
)
Set
\nu:=\dim\mathbb{R}[\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.6817pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.7584pt{}}}$\hss}}}X]_{N}
. For each
d\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
, the class
S_{d}
of
all pairs
(R,a)
where
R
is a real closed extension field of
\mathbb{R}

and
a\in R^{\nu}
such that the following holds
is obviously a
\nu
-ary
\mathbb{R}
-semialgebraic class [
\to

8.3
]:
If
a\in[-N,N]_{R}^{\nu}
and if

a
is the vector of coefficients (in a certain fixed order) of a polynomial
f\in R[\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.6817pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.7584pt{}}}$\hss}}}X]_{N}
with
exactly
k
zeros
x_{1},\dots,x_{k}
on
S^{\prime}:=\{x\in R^{n}\mid g_{1}(x)\geq 0,\dots,g_{m}(x)\geq 0\}
, then
at least one of the following conditions (a), (b) and (c) is fulfilled:

(a)

The sets
x_{1}+\varepsilon B^{\prime},\ldots,x_{k}+\varepsilon B^{\prime}
are not pairwise disjoint or not all contained in
S^{\prime}
where

B^{\prime}:=\{x\in R^{n}\mid\|x\|_{2}<1\}
.

(b)

f\geq\varepsilon u
on
S^{\prime}
is violated where
u:=u_{x_{1}}\dotsm u_{x_{k}}\in R[\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.6817pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.7584pt{}}}$\hss}}}X]
.

(c)

f
is a sum of
d
elements from
R[\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.6817pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.7584pt{}}}$\hss}}}X]
where each term in the sum is of degree at most
d

and is of the form
p^{2}g_{i}
with
p\in R[\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.6817pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.7584pt{}}}$\hss}}}X]
and
i\in\{0,\dots,m\}
where
g_{0}:=1\in R[\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.6817pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.7584pt{}}}$\hss}}}X]
.

Set
\mathscr{E}:=\{S_{d}\mid d\in\mathbb{N}_{0}\}
and
observe that
\forall d_{1},d_{2}\in\mathbb{N}_{0}:\exists d_{3}\in\mathbb{N}_{0}:S_{d_{1}}\cup S_{d_{2}}\subseteq S_{d_{3}}
(take
d_{3}:=\max\{d_{1},d_{2}\}
). By
1.16
,
we have
\bigcup\mathscr{E}=\mathscr{R}_{\nu}
. Now

4.2
yields
S_{d}=\mathscr{R}_{\nu}
for some
d\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
.
∎

Corollary 2.4

(Putinar’s Positivstellensatz with degree bounds
[
Pre
,
NS
,
Kri’
]
)
.

[
\to

2.14
]

Let
n,m\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
and
g_{1},\ldots,g_{m}\in\mathbb{R}[\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.6817pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.7584pt{}}}$\hss}}}X]
such that
M(g_{1},\ldots,g_{m})
is Archimedean. Set

S:=\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\mid g_{1}(x)\geq 0,\ldots,g_{m}(x)\geq 0\}.

Moreover, let
N\in\mathbb{N}
and
\varepsilon\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
.
Then there exists

d\in\mathbb{N}_{0}

such that for all
f\in\mathbb{R}[\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.6817pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.7584pt{}}}$\hss}}}X]_{N}
with all coefficients in
[-N,N]_{\mathbb{R}}

and with
f\geq\varepsilon
on
S
, we have

f\in M_{d}(g_{1},\dots,g_{m}).

Proposition 2.5
.

Suppose
S\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}
is compact,
x_{1},\dots,x_{k}\in S^{\circ}
are pairwise distinct,

u:=u_{x_{1}}\dotsm u_{x_{k}}\in\mathbb{R}[\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.6817pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.7584pt{}}}$\hss}}}X]

[
\to

1.8
]
and
f\in\mathbb{R}[\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.6817pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.7584pt{}}}$\hss}}}X]
with
f(x_{1})=\ldots=f(x_{k})=0
.
Then the following are equivalent:

(a)

f>0
on
S\setminus\{x_{1},\ldots,x_{k}\}
and
\hess f(x_{1}),\dots,\hess f(x_{k})
are pd.

(b)

There is some
\varepsilon\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
such that
f\geq\varepsilon u
on
S
.

Proof.

(b)
\implies
(a)
is easy to show (cf. the proof of
1.15
).

(a)
\implies
(b)
It is easy to show that one can WLOG
assume that
S=\mathop{\dot{\bigcup}}_{i=1}^{k}(x_{i}+\varepsilon B)
for some
\varepsilon>0
where
B
is the closed unit ball
in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
. Then one finds easily an Archimedean quadratic module
M
of
\mathbb{R}[\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.6817pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.7584pt{}}}$\hss}}}X]
such that

S=\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\mid\forall p\in M:p(x)\geq 0\}.

A strengthened version of Theorem
1.14
now yields
f-\varepsilon u\in M
for some
\varepsilon\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}

and thus
f-\varepsilon u\geq 0
on
S
. One gets this strengthened version of Theorem
1.14

by applying (a)
\implies
(c) from
3.19
instead of

3.20
in its proof. Alternatively, we leave it as an exercise to the reader to
give a direct proof using only basic multivariate analysis.
∎

Corollary 2.6

(Putinar’s Positivstellensatz with zeros
[
S1
]
)
.

Let
g_{1},\ldots,g_{m}\in\mathbb{R}[\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.6817pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.7584pt{}}}$\hss}}}X]
such that
M(g_{1},\ldots,g_{m})
is Archimedean. Set

S:=\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\mid g_{1}(x)\geq 0,\ldots,g_{m}(x)\geq 0\}.

Moreover, suppose
k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
and
x_{1},\ldots,x_{k}\in S^{\circ}
are pairwise distinct.
Let
f\in\mathbb{R}[\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.6817pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.7584pt{}}}$\hss}}}X]
such that
f(x_{1})=\ldots=f(x_{k})=0
,
f>0
on
S\setminus\{x_{1},\ldots,x_{k}\}
and

\hess f(x_{1}),\dots,\hess f(x_{k})
are pd.
Then

f\in M(g_{1},\ldots,g_{m}).

Proof.

This follows from
2.3
by Proposition
2.5
.
∎

Remark 2.7
.

Because of Proposition
2.5
, Theorem
2.3
is really a
quantitative version of Corollary
2.6
.

Remark 2.8
.

(a)

In Condition (c) from the proof of Theorem
2.3
, we speak of
“a sum of
d
elements” instead of “a sum of elements” (which would in general
be strictly weaker).
Our motivation to do this was that this is the easiest way to make sure that we can formulate
(c) in a “semialgebraic way”. A second motivation could have been to formulate
Theorem
2.3
in stronger way, namely by letting
d
be a bound
not only on the degree of the quadratic module representation but also on the number of terms in it.
This second motivation is however not interesting because we get also from the Gram matrix method

6.1
a bound on this number of terms (a priori bigger than
d
but after readjusting
d
we can
again assume it to be
d
). We could have used the Gram matrix method already to see that
“a sum of elements” (instead of “a sum of
d
elements”) can also be expressed semialgebraically.

(b)

We could strengthen condition (c) from the proof of
Theorem
2.3
, by writing “with
p\in R[\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.6817pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.7584pt{}}}$\hss}}}X]
all of whose coefficients lie
in
[-d,d]_{R}
” instead of just “with
p\in R[\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.6817pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.7584pt{}}}$\hss}}}X]
”. Then
\bigcup\mathscr{E}=\mathscr{R}_{\nu}
would
still hold since Corollary
1.16
states that

f
lies in the quadratic module generated by
g_{1},\ldots,g_{m}
even in
\mathscr{O}[\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.6817pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.7584pt{}}}$\hss}}}X]
not just in
\mathbb{R}[\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.6817pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.7584pt{}}}$\hss}}}X]
.
This would lead to a real strengthening of Theorem
2.3
, namely we
could ensure that
d
is a bound
not only on the degree of the quadratic module representation but also on the size of the coefficients
in it. However, we do currently not know of any application of this and therefore renounced to carry this
out.

Chapter 10
Linearizing systems of polynomial inequalities

1
The Lasserre hierachy for a system of polynomial inequalities

By a
system of polynomial inequalities
,
we understand a (finite) system of (real non-strict) polynomial inequalities in several variables, i.e., a condition of the form

g_{1}(x)\geq 0,\ldots,g_{m}(x)\geq 0\qquad(x\in\mathbb{R}^{n})

where
m,n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
and
\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.10278pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.10278pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to3.32196pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to2.94585pt{}}}$\hss}}}g=(g_{1},\ldots,g_{m})\in\mathbb{R}[\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.6817pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.7584pt{}}}$\hss}}}X]^{m}
. Its sets of solutions are exactly the basic closed semialgebraic subsets of

\mathbb{R}^{n}
[
\to

3.1
]. We now introduce notation for these.

Definition 1.1
.

For
\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.10278pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.10278pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to3.32196pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to2.94585pt{}}}$\hss}}}g=(g_{1},\ldots,g_{m})\in\mathbb{R}[\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.6817pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.7584pt{}}}$\hss}}}X]^{m}
, we consider the basic closed semialgebraic set [
\to

3.1
]

S(\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.10278pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.10278pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to3.32196pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to2.94585pt{}}}$\hss}}}g):=\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\mid g_{1}(x)\geq 0,\ldots,g_{m}(x)\geq 0\}.

One can easily imagine that many “horribly complicated” computational problems can be easily translated into the problem of solving
systems of polynomial inequalities. It is outside of the scope of these lecture notes to make this statement more formal. Consequently,
it would be no surprise if it were in general very hard to deal with polynomial inequalities algorithmically. The proof of real quantifier elimination
[
\to
Theorem
8.17
, §
8
]
(by means of which one can for example decide whether a given system of polynomial inequalities is solvable) can be read as an algorithm
of horrible complexity which is basically useless for practical purposes.
We try to identify cases where efficient algorithms for dealing with systems of polynomial inequalities might possibly exist.
Our approach will be to relate systems of polynomial inequalities to
linear matrix inequalities
. Before we introduce the latter, we need
more notation.

Definition 1.2
.

[
\to

3.1
(b)]
Let
A\in S\mathbb{R}^{k\times k}
. We write

A\begin{Bmatrix}\succeq\\
\succ\end{Bmatrix}0
to express that

A
is
\begin{Bmatrix}\text{psd}\\
\text{pd}\end{Bmatrix}
, i.e.,
A
is symmetric and
x^{T}Mx\begin{Bmatrix}\geq\\
>\end{Bmatrix}0
for all
x\in\begin{Bmatrix}\mathbb{R}^{k}\\
\mathbb{R}^{k}\setminus\{0\}\end{Bmatrix}
.
If
B\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times k}
is another matrix, we write
A\begin{Bmatrix}\succeq\\
\succ\end{Bmatrix}B
or

B\begin{Bmatrix}\preceq\\
\prec\end{Bmatrix}A
to express that

A-B\begin{Bmatrix}\succeq\\
\succ\end{Bmatrix}0
.
We say that
A
is

\begin{Bmatrix}\text{negative semidefinite (nsd)}\\
\text{negative definite (nd)}\end{Bmatrix}
if
A\preceq 0
.

By a
linear matrix inequality (LMI)
,
we understand a condition of the form

A_{0}+x_{1}A_{1}+\ldots+x_{n}A_{n}\succeq 0\qquad(x\in\mathbb{R}^{n})

where
n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
,
k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
and
A_{0},\ldots,A_{n}\in S\mathbb{R}^{k\times k}
. An equivalent way of writing this is by saying it is of the form

\begin{pmatrix}\ell_{11}(x)&\ldots&\ell_{1k}(x)\\
\vdots&&\vdots\\
\ell_{k1}(x)&\ldots&\ell_{kk}(x)\end{pmatrix}\qquad(x\in\mathbb{R}^{n})

where each
\ell_{ij}\in\mathbb{R}[\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.6817pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.7584pt{}}}$\hss}}}X]_{1}
is a linear polynomial [
\to

3.4
] and
\ell_{ij}=\ell_{ji}
for all
i,j\in\{1,\ldots,k\}
.
We speak of a
diagonal
LMI if, in the above, each
A_{i}
is diagonal or, equivalently,
\ell_{ij}=0
for all
i
different from
j
.
A diagonal LMI obviously just corresponds to a finite system of (non-strict) linear inequalities. Solution sets of LMIs are called

spectrahedra
. They generalize the solution sets of diagonal LMIs that are called
polyhedra
.

Example 1.3
.

Consider the LMIs

\begin{pmatrix}1+x_{1}&x_{2}\\
x_{2}&1-x_{1}\end{pmatrix}\succeq 0\qquad(x_{1},x_{2}\in\mathbb{R})

and

\begin{pmatrix}1&x_{1}&x_{2}\\
x_{1}&1&0\\
x_{2}&0&1\end{pmatrix}\succeq 0\qquad(x_{1},x_{2}\in\mathbb{R}).

By
3.3
(f), we have that the first LMI is equivalent to

1\geq 0\mathbin{\&}1+x_{1}\geq 0\mathbin{\&}1-x_{1}\geq 0\mathbin{\&}(1+x_{1})(1-x_{1})-x_{2}^{2}\geq 0\qquad(x_{1},x_{2}\in\mathbb{R})

and the second one to

1\geq 0\mathbin{\&}1-x_{1}^{2}\geq 0\mathbin{\&}1-x_{2}^{2}\geq 0\mathbin{\&}1-x_{1}^{2}-x_{2}^{2}\geq 0\qquad(x_{1},x_{2}\in\mathbb{R}).

Hence both are actually equivalent to

x_{1}^{2}+x_{2}^{2}\leq 1\qquad(x_{1},x_{2}\in\mathbb{R})

so that they define the closed unit disk in the plane.

There are very efficient algorithms to deal with finite systems of linear inequalities (for example, to decide whether such a system is
solvable). We catched already a glimpse of this in the algorithmic proof of
4.20
. Finite systems of linear inequalities
correspond to diagonal LMIs. As a matter of fact which goes beyond our scope, there are still very efficient algorithms to deal with
arbitrary LMIs. In fact, solving a system of linear inequalities in such a way that a given linear objective function is maximized or minimized is the
problem of
linear programming
(LP). LP is ubiquitous in science and engineering and there are extremely efficient
LP solvers. The more general problem of solving an LMI in such a way that a given linear objective function is maximized or minimized is the
problem of
semidefinite programming
(SDP). SDP is still in its infancy but gets more and more appreciated by many people
within mathematics and its applications. For SDP there are still very efficient solvers although they cannot yet compete with LP solvers.

Example 1.4
.

Consider the following toy system of polynomial inequalities:

(*)\qquad\quad 1-x_{1}+x_{2}\geq 0,\quad 1-x_{1}^{4}-x_{2}^{4}\geq 0\qquad(x_{1},x_{2}\in\mathbb{R}).

The terms
x_{1}^{4}
and
x_{2}^{4}
make that it is not a system of linear inequalities.
A first idea which is in general way too naive is to simply replace them by
new unknowns
y_{1}
and
y_{2}
and consider

(**)\qquad 1-x_{1}+x_{2}\geq 0,\quad 1-y_{1}-y_{2}\geq 0\qquad(x_{1},x_{2},y_{1},y_{2}\in\mathbb{R}).

Every solution of
(*)
clearly gives rise to a solution of
(**)
by setting
y_{1}:=x_{1}^{4}
and
y_{2}:=x_{2}^{4}
. This implies that the projection

\{(x_{1},x_{2})\in\mathbb{R}^{2}\mid\exists y_{1},y_{2}:(x_{1},x_{2},y_{1},y_{2})\text{ solves }(**)\}

of the set of solutions of
(**)
onto
x
-space contains the set of solutions of
(*)
. The converse is however obviously not
true and this does not come as a surprise since one lost too much information about
(*)
.

The idea is now to add a whole bunch of redundant inequalities to
(*)
before replacing all nonlinear terms by new variables.
For example, we can simply add for each
a,b,c,d,e,f\in\mathbb{R}
the inequality

(***)\qquad(a+bx_{1}+cx_{2}+dx_{1}^{2}+ex_{1}x_{2}+fx_{2}^{2})^{2}\geq 0.

We could now expand these inequalities into
a^{2}+2abx_{1}+\ldots+f^{2}x_{2}^{4}\geq 0

and replace not only the term
x_{1}^{4}
and
x_{2}^{4}
by
y_{1}
and
y_{2}
as before but also all other nonlinear terms
by new variables
y_{3}
,
y_{4}
and so on. This would lead to an infinite family of linear inequalities parametrized by
a,b,c,\ldots\in\mathbb{R}
.
The hope is that inequalities that were redundant before the linearization, could be valuable after the linearization.

One of the problems is
that one does in general not know how to deal algorithmically with
infinite
systems of linear inequalities. Happily, there is a way of
turning the infinite system parametrized by
a,b,c,\dots
into one single LMI. In order to get rid of the parameters, one first separates the
parameters
a,b,c,\ldots
from the monomial expressions
1,x_{1},x_{2},x_{1}^{2},\ldots
by the following trick: Rewrite
(***)
as a (matrix) product of
row and column vectors as follows:

\begin{pmatrix}a&b&c&d&e&f\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}1\\
x_{1}\\
x_{2}\\
x_{1}^{2}\\
x_{1}x_{2}\\
x_{2}^{2}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}1&x_{1}&x_{2}&x_{1}^{2}&x_{1}x_{2}&x_{2}^{2}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}a\\
b\\
c\\
d\\
e\\
f\end{pmatrix}\geq 0.

Multiplying the two interior vectors gives a symmetric matrix of monomials of a certain structure. More precisely it transform our condition
to

\begin{pmatrix}a&b&c&d&e&f\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}1&x_{1}&x_{2}&x_{1}^{2}&x_{1}x_{2}&x_{2}^{2}\\
x_{1}&x_{1}^{2}&x_{1}x_{2}&x_{1}^{3}&x_{1}^{2}x_{2}&x_{1}x_{2}^{2}\\
x_{2}&x_{1}x_{2}&x_{2}^{2}&x_{1}^{2}x_{2}&x_{1}x_{2}^{2}&x_{2}^{3}\\
x_{1}^{2}&x_{1}^{3}&x_{1}^{2}x_{2}&x_{1}^{4}&x_{1}^{3}x_{2}&x_{1}^{2}x_{2}^{2}\\
x_{1}x_{2}&x_{1}^{2}x_{2}&x_{1}x_{2}^{2}&x_{1}^{3}x_{2}&x_{1}^{2}x_{2}^{2}&x_{1}x_{2}^{3}\\
x_{2}^{2}&x_{1}x_{2}^{2}&x_{2}^{3}&x_{1}^{2}x_{2}^{2}&x_{1}x_{2}^{3}&x_{2}^{4}\\
\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}a\\
b\\
c\\
d\\
e\\
f\end{pmatrix}\geq 0.

If we now linearize again, we get again an infinite family of linear inequalities, namely the same as we would have had got before
by simply expanding and linearizing
(***)
:

\begin{pmatrix}a&b&c&d&e&f\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}1&x_{1}&x_{2}&y_{3}&y_{4}&y_{5}\\
x_{1}&y_{3}&y_{4}&y_{6}&y_{7}&y_{8}\\
x_{2}&y_{4}&y_{5}&y_{7}&y_{8}&y_{9}\\
y_{3}&y_{6}&y_{7}&y_{1}&y_{11}&y_{12}\\
y_{4}&y_{7}&y_{8}&y_{11}&y_{12}&y_{10}\\
y_{5}&y_{8}&y_{9}&y_{12}&y_{10}&y_{2}\\
\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}a\\
b\\
c\\
d\\
e\\
f\end{pmatrix}\geq 0.

The big advantage is now however that we can get rid of the parameters
a,b,c,\ldots
by
passing over to a linear matrix inequality. Namely, the above is by
3.1
(b) valid for all
a,b,c,\ldots\in\mathbb{R}
if and only if
the LMI

\begin{pmatrix}1&x_{1}&x_{2}&y_{3}&y_{4}&y_{5}\\
x_{1}&y_{3}&y_{4}&y_{6}&y_{7}&y_{8}\\
x_{2}&y_{4}&y_{5}&y_{7}&y_{8}&y_{9}\\
y_{3}&y_{6}&y_{7}&y_{1}&y_{11}&y_{12}\\
y_{4}&y_{7}&y_{8}&y_{11}&y_{12}&y_{10}\\
y_{5}&y_{8}&y_{9}&y_{12}&y_{10}&y_{2}\\
\end{pmatrix}\succeq 0\qquad(x_{1},x_{2},y_{1},y_{2},\ldots\in\mathbb{R})

holds.
But there are other redundant inequalities that one can add before the linearization. For example, one could multiply
the inequality
1-x_{1}+x_{2}\geq 0
from the original system
(*)
by a square of a general polynomial of some degree. Because
we do not want to introduce to many new variables
y_{i}
in the end (and intuitively we think that each variable
y_{i}
should
ideally appear many times in the final LMIs so that there is not too much freedom), we decide to multiply this time just with a general
linear polynomial. So we add for each
a,b,c\in\mathbb{R}
the inequality

(***\,*)\qquad(a+bx_{1}+cx_{2})^{2}(1-x_{1}+x_{2})\geq 0.

We could again expand this and linearize (re-using some of the
y_{i}
from before of course) to get another infinite family of linear
inequalities. But we again apply our trick that will lead to an LMI by doing this in the following equivalent way:
Rewrite
(***\,*)
as

\begin{pmatrix}a&b&c\end{pmatrix}(1-x_{1}+x_{2})\begin{pmatrix}1\\
x_{1}\\
x_{2}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}1&x_{1}&x_{2}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}a\\
b\\
c\end{pmatrix}\geq 0

where the second (invisible) multiplication dot out of the four is a scalar product and the others are matrix products of row and column vectors. Linearizing

(1-x_{1}+x_{2})\begin{pmatrix}1\\
x_{1}\\
x_{2}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}1&x_{1}&x_{2}\end{pmatrix}=(1-x_{1}+x_{2})\begin{pmatrix}1&x_{1}&x_{2}\\
x_{1}&x_{1}^{2}&x_{1}x_{2}\\
x_{2}&x_{1}x_{2}&x_{2}^{2}\end{pmatrix}\\
=\begin{pmatrix}1-x_{1}+x_{2}&x_{1}-x_{1}^{2}+x_{1}x_{2}&x_{2}-x_{1}x_{2}+x_{2}^{2}\\
x_{1}-x_{1}^{2}+x_{1}x_{2}&x_{1}^{2}-x_{1}^{3}+x_{1}^{2}x_{2}&x_{1}x_{2}-x_{1}^{2}x_{2}+x_{1}x_{2}^{2}\\
x_{2}-x_{1}x_{2}+x_{2}^{2}&x_{1}x_{2}-x_{1}^{2}x_{2}+x_{1}x_{2}^{2}&x_{2}^{2}-x_{1}x_{2}^{2}+x_{2}^{3}\end{pmatrix}

we obtain the LMI

\begin{pmatrix}-x_{1}+x_{2}+1&x_{1}-y_{3}+y_{4}&x_{2}-y_{4}+y_{5}\\
x_{1}-y_{3}+y_{4}&y_{3}-y_{6}+y_{7}&y_{4}-y_{7}+y_{8}\\
x_{2}-y_{4}+y_{5}&y_{4}-y_{7}+y_{8}&y_{5}-y_{8}+y_{9}\end{pmatrix}\succeq 0\qquad(x_{1},x_{2},y_{1},y_{2},\ldots\in\mathbb{R}).

Finally, we could also add redundant inequalities stemming from the inequality
1-x_{1}^{4}-x_{2}^{4}
. So we could multiply it
by a square of a general polynomial of some degree. Again, to economize additional variables
y_{i}
, we decide to take degree zero
here which amounts to linearize the inequality
1-x_{1}^{4}-x_{2}^{4}
just like this without further ado. This leads to the
the linear inequality

1-y_{1}-y_{2}\geq 0\qquad(y_{1},y_{2}\in\mathbb{R})

that we had already in
(**)
and which can be seen as an LMI of size
1
.
All in all, we consider now the following system of three
LMIs of sizes
6
,
3
and
1
(which could easily be written as a single LMI of size
10
by forming a block diagonal matrix)

(\Box)\qquad\begin{aligned} \begin{pmatrix}1&x_{1}&x_{2}&y_{3}&y_{4}&y_{5}\\
x_{1}&y_{3}&y_{4}&y_{6}&y_{7}&y_{8}\\
x_{2}&y_{4}&y_{5}&y_{7}&y_{8}&y_{9}\\
y_{3}&y_{6}&y_{7}&y_{1}&y_{11}&y_{12}\\
y_{4}&y_{7}&y_{8}&y_{11}&y_{12}&y_{10}\\
y_{5}&y_{8}&y_{9}&y_{12}&y_{10}&y_{2}\\
\end{pmatrix}&\succeq 0\\
\begin{pmatrix}-x_{1}+x_{2}+1&x_{1}-y_{3}+y_{4}&x_{2}-y_{4}+y_{5}\\
x_{1}-y_{3}+y_{4}&y_{3}-y_{6}+y_{7}&y_{4}-y_{7}+y_{8}\\
x_{2}-y_{4}+y_{5}&y_{4}-y_{7}+y_{8}&y_{5}-y_{8}+y_{9}\end{pmatrix}&\succeq 0\\
1-y_{1}-y_{2}&\geq 0.\end{aligned}\qquad(x_{1},x_{2},y_{1},y_{2},\ldots\in\mathbb{R}).

It is clear that the projection

\{(x_{1},x_{2})\in\mathbb{R}^{2}\mid\exists y_{1},\ldots,y_{12}:(x_{1},x_{2},y_{1},\ldots,y_{12})\text{ solves }(\Box)\}

of the set of solutions of
(\Box)
onto
x
-space contains the set of solutions of the original system
(*)
.
Each solution of
(*)
can be made into a solution of
(\Box)
by assigning to each
y_{i}
the value of the nonlinear
monomial expression that has been replaced by
y_{i}
. In this sense
(\Box)
is a so-called
relaxation
of
(*)
.

Conversely, it is not clear in what extent a solution of
(\Box)
might perhaps
give rise to a solution of
(*)
. A first step to investigate this, is to find a more schematic way of describing the solution set of

(\Box)
. This time we will hide the parameters
a,b,c,...
not in an LMI but in a truncated quadratic module [
\to

2.1
].
Note that, before we came up
with the idea of writing it as an LMI, we had produced simply certain infinite systems of linear inequalities in the
x_{1},x_{2},y_{1},y_{2},\ldots
.
If we take, this time really redundantly, also sums of these linear inequalities, we observe that we effectively have added
the linearizations of the inequalities

p(x)\geq 0\qquad(x\in\mathbb{R}^{n})

for each
p\in M_{4}(g_{1},g_{2})\in\mathbb{R}[X_{1},X_{2}]_{4}

where
g_{1}:=1-X_{1}+X_{2}\in\mathbb{R}[X_{1},X_{2}]
and
g_{2}:=1-X_{1}^{4}-X_{2}^{4}\in\mathbb{R}[X_{1},X_{2}]
. An elegant way of
expressing this is to replace the variables of the linearized system
x_{1},x_{2},y_{1},y_{2},\ldots
by the unknown
values

\varphi(X_{1}),\varphi(X_{2}),\varphi(X_{1}^{4}),\varphi(X_{2}^{4}),\ldots

of some linear function
\varphi\colon\mathbb{R}[X_{1},X_{2}]_{4}\to\mathbb{R}

that satisfies
\varphi(1)=1
. Knowing that a linear function
\varphi\colon\mathbb{R}[X_{1},X_{2}]_{4}\to\mathbb{R}
can be uniquely determined by
arbitrarily prescribed values on the
monomials, we see easily that the solution set of
(\Box)
can be identified with the state space [
\to

1.9
]

S(\mathbb{R}[X_{1},X_{2}]_{4},M_{4}(g_{1},g_{2}),1)\subseteq\mathbb{R}[X_{1},X_{2}]^{*}.

Definition 1.5
.

Let
d\in\mathbb{N}
,
m\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
and
\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.10278pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.10278pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to3.32196pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to2.94585pt{}}}$\hss}}}g\in\mathbb{R}[\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.6817pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.7584pt{}}}$\hss}}}X]^{m}
. Then we call

L_{d}(\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.10278pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.10278pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to3.32196pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to2.94585pt{}}}$\hss}}}g):=S(\mathbb{R}[\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.6817pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.7584pt{}}}$\hss}}}X]_{d},M_{d}(\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.10278pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.10278pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to3.32196pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to2.94585pt{}}}$\hss}}}g),1)\subseteq\mathbb{R}[\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.6817pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.7584pt{}}}$\hss}}}X]_{d}^{*}

the
degree
d
state space

associated to
\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.10278pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.10278pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to3.32196pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to2.94585pt{}}}$\hss}}}g
and

S_{d}(\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.10278pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.10278pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to3.32196pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to2.94585pt{}}}$\hss}}}g):=\{(\varphi(X_{1}),\ldots,\varphi(X_{n}))\mid\varphi\in L_{d}(\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.10278pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.10278pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to3.32196pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to2.94585pt{}}}$\hss}}}g)\}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}

the
degree
d
Lasserre relaxation
associated to
\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.10278pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.10278pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to3.32196pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to2.94585pt{}}}$\hss}}}g
.

Remark 1.6
.

Let
m\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
and
\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.10278pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.10278pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to3.32196pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to2.94585pt{}}}$\hss}}}g\in\mathbb{R}[\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.6817pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.7584pt{}}}$\hss}}}X]^{m}
.

(a)

For each
d\in\mathbb{N}
,
L_{d}(\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.10278pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.10278pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to3.32196pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to2.94585pt{}}}$\hss}}}g)
is obviously a convex subset of the real vector space
\mathbb{R}[\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.6817pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.7584pt{}}}$\hss}}}X]_{d}^{*}
.

(b)

For each
d\in\mathbb{N}
,
S_{d}(\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.10278pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.10278pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to3.32196pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to2.94585pt{}}}$\hss}}}g)
is a convex subset of
\mathbb{R}^{n}
due to (a).

(c)

We have

S(\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.10278pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.10278pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to3.32196pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to2.94585pt{}}}$\hss}}}g)\subseteq S_{d}(\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.10278pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.10278pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to3.32196pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to2.94585pt{}}}$\hss}}}g)

for all
d\in\mathbb{N}
since the evaluation at
x

\mathbb{R}[\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.6817pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.7584pt{}}}$\hss}}}X]_{d}\to\mathbb{R},\ p\mapsto p(x)

is a degree
d
state with
(\varphi(X_{1}),\ldots,\varphi(X_{n}))=x
for each
x\in S(\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.10278pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.10278pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to3.32196pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to2.94585pt{}}}$\hss}}}g)
.

(d)

We even have

\conv S(\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.10278pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.10278pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to3.32196pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to2.94585pt{}}}$\hss}}}g)\subseteq S_{d}(\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.10278pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.10278pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to3.32196pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to2.94585pt{}}}$\hss}}}g)

for all
d\in\mathbb{N}
by (b) and (c).

(e)

We have

S_{d+1}(\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.10278pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.10278pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to3.32196pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to2.94585pt{}}}$\hss}}}g)\subseteq S_{d}(\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.10278pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.10278pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to3.32196pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to2.94585pt{}}}$\hss}}}g)

for all
d\in\mathbb{N}
for if we restrict a degree
d+1
state associated to
\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.10278pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.10278pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to3.32196pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to2.94585pt{}}}$\hss}}}g
to
\mathbb{R}[\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.6817pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.7584pt{}}}$\hss}}}X]_{d}
, we get a degree
d
state associated to
\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.10278pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.10278pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to3.32196pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to2.94585pt{}}}$\hss}}}g
.

(f)

If there is some
i\in\{1,\ldots,m\}
such that
g_{i}=0
or
\deg g_{i}>d
, then
M_{d}(\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.10278pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.10278pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to3.32196pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to2.94585pt{}}}$\hss}}}g)
and therefore
L_{d}(\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.10278pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.10278pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to3.32196pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to2.94585pt{}}}$\hss}}}g)
and
S_{d}(\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.10278pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.10278pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to3.32196pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to2.94585pt{}}}$\hss}}}g)
do
not change if we remove
g_{i}
from
\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.10278pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.10278pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to3.32196pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to2.94585pt{}}}$\hss}}}g
[
\to

2.1
]. In this sense,
g_{i}
is simply ignored by
M_{d}(\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.10278pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.10278pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to3.32196pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to2.94585pt{}}}$\hss}}}g)
,
L_{d}(\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.10278pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.10278pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to3.32196pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to2.94585pt{}}}$\hss}}}g)
and
S_{d}(\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.10278pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.10278pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to3.32196pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to2.94585pt{}}}$\hss}}}g)
.
Therefore one often considers only those
d\in\mathbb{N}
that are greater than or equal to the degree of each
g_{i}
.

(g)

Let
d\in\mathbb{N}
such that
g_{1},\ldots,g_{m}\in\mathbb{R}[\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.6817pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.7584pt{}}}$\hss}}}X]_{d}\setminus\{0\}
and set
g_{0}:=1\in\mathbb{R}[\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.6817pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.7584pt{}}}$\hss}}}X]_{d}
. For
i\in\{0,\dots,m\}
, set

r_{i}:=\lfloor\frac{d-\deg g_{i}}{2}\rfloor
[
\to

2.1
]. Let
1,u_{1},\ldots,u_{N}
be the monomial basis of
\mathbb{R}[\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.6817pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.7584pt{}}}$\hss}}}X]_{d}
.
Then
\{(\varphi(u_{1}),\ldots,\varphi(u_{N}))\mid\varphi\in L_{d}(\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.10278pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.10278pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to3.32196pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to2.94585pt{}}}$\hss}}}g)\}
can be defined by m+1 simultaneous LMIs of sizes
\dim(\mathbb{R}[\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.6817pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.7584pt{}}}$\hss}}}X]_{r_{0}}),\ldots,\dim(\mathbb{R}[\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.6817pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.7584pt{}}}$\hss}}}X]_{r_{m}})
in

N
variables. This can be seen analogously to Example
1.4
.

Lemma 1.7
.

Let
d,m\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
and
\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.10278pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.10278pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to3.32196pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to2.94585pt{}}}$\hss}}}g\in\mathbb{R}[\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.6817pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.7584pt{}}}$\hss}}}X]^{m}
. Suppose
S_{d}(\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.10278pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.10278pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to3.32196pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to2.94585pt{}}}$\hss}}}g)\neq\emptyset
. Then

\overline{M_{d}(\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.10278pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.10278pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to3.32196pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to2.94585pt{}}}$\hss}}}g)}=\{f\in\mathbb{R}[\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.6817pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.7584pt{}}}$\hss}}}X]_{d}\mid\forall\varphi\in L_{d}(\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.10278pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.10278pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to3.32196pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to2.94585pt{}}}$\hss}}}g):\varphi(f)\geq 0\}.

Proof.

The right hand side of the claimed equation equals

\bigcap_{\varphi\in L_{d}(\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to3.32196pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to3.32196pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to2.01035pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to1.43596pt{}}}$\hss}}}g)}\varphi^{-1}(\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0})

and is therefore closed since every
\varphi\in L_{d}(\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.10278pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.10278pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to3.32196pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to2.94585pt{}}}$\hss}}}g)
is linear and therefore continuous [
\to

4.18
].
To prove the inclusion from left to right, it is therefore enough to show that
M_{d}(\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.10278pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.10278pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to3.32196pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to2.94585pt{}}}$\hss}}}g)
is contained in the right hand side, which is trivial.

For the converse inclusion, we let
f\in\mathbb{R}[\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.6817pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.7584pt{}}}$\hss}}}X]_{d}\setminus\overline{M_{d}(\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.10278pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.10278pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to3.32196pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to2.94585pt{}}}$\hss}}}g)}
and we want to find
\varphi\in L_{d}(\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.10278pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.10278pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to3.32196pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to2.94585pt{}}}$\hss}}}g)
with
\varphi(f)<0
.
By
3.5
there are a linear
\varphi_{0}\colon\mathbb{R}[\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.6817pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.7584pt{}}}$\hss}}}X]_{d}\to\mathbb{R}
with
\varphi_{0}(g)<\varphi_{0}(f)
for all
g\in\overline{M_{d}(\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.10278pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.10278pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to3.32196pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to2.94585pt{}}}$\hss}}}g)}
.
Because
M_{d}(\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.10278pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.10278pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to3.32196pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to2.94585pt{}}}$\hss}}}g)
is a cone, it follows easily that
\varphi_{0}(M_{d}(\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.10278pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.10278pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to3.32196pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to2.94585pt{}}}$\hss}}}g))\subseteq\mathbb{R}_{\leq 0}
using a scaling argument. Since

0\in M_{d}(\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.10278pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.10278pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to3.32196pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to2.94585pt{}}}$\hss}}}g)
, we have moreover
0<\varphi_{0}(f)
.
Setting
\varphi_{1}:=-\varphi_{0}
, we have
\varphi_{1}(M_{d}(\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.10278pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.10278pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to3.32196pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to2.94585pt{}}}$\hss}}}g))\subseteq\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
and
\varphi_{1}(f)<0
.
If
\varphi_{1}(1)>0
then we set
\varphi:=\frac{1}{\varphi_{1}(1)}\varphi_{1}\in L_{d}(\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.10278pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.10278pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to3.32196pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to2.94585pt{}}}$\hss}}}g)
and we are done. Suppose therefore
\varphi_{1}(1)=0
.
From
S_{d}(\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.10278pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.10278pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to3.32196pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to2.94585pt{}}}$\hss}}}g)\neq\emptyset
it follows that
L_{d}(\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.10278pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.10278pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to3.32196pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to2.94585pt{}}}$\hss}}}g)\neq\emptyset
. Choose
\psi\in L_{d}(\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.10278pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.10278pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to3.32196pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to2.94585pt{}}}$\hss}}}g)
. Now
\psi+N\varphi_{1}\in L_{d}(\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.10278pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.10278pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to3.32196pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to2.94585pt{}}}$\hss}}}g)
for all
N\in\mathbb{N}
.
Choose
N\in\mathbb{N}
big enough such that
\psi(f)+N\varphi_{1}(f)<0
and set
\varphi:=\psi+N\varphi_{1}
.
∎

Remark 1.8
.

The following trivial fact will be crucial and we will use it tacitly:
For all
f\in\mathbb{R}[\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.6817pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.7584pt{}}}$\hss}}}X]_{1}
and linear
\varphi\colon\mathbb{R}[\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.6817pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.7584pt{}}}$\hss}}}X]_{1}\to\mathbb{R}
with
\varphi(1)=1
, we have

f(\varphi(X_{1}),\ldots,\varphi(X_{n}))=\varphi(f).

Lemma 1.9
.

Let
d\in\mathbb{N}
,
m\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
and
\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.10278pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.10278pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to3.32196pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to2.94585pt{}}}$\hss}}}g\in\mathbb{R}[\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.6817pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.7584pt{}}}$\hss}}}X]^{m}
such that
S_{d}(\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.10278pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.10278pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to3.32196pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to2.94585pt{}}}$\hss}}}g)\neq\emptyset
. Then the following are equivalent:

(a)

S_{d}(\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.10278pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.10278pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to3.32196pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to2.94585pt{}}}$\hss}}}g)\subseteq\overline{\conv S(\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.10278pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.10278pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to3.32196pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to2.94585pt{}}}$\hss}}}g)}

(b)

\forall f\in\mathbb{R}[\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.6817pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.7584pt{}}}$\hss}}}X]_{1}:(f\geq 0\text{ on }S(\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.10278pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.10278pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to3.32196pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to2.94585pt{}}}$\hss}}}g)\implies f\in\overline{M_{d}(\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.10278pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.10278pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to3.32196pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to2.94585pt{}}}$\hss}}}g)})

Proof.

Suppose first that (a) holds. In order to show (b), let
f\in\mathbb{R}[\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.6817pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.7584pt{}}}$\hss}}}X]_{1}
with
f\geq 0
on
S(\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.10278pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.10278pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to3.32196pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to2.94585pt{}}}$\hss}}}g)
. Since
f
is linear it follows that

f\geq 0
on
\conv S(\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.10278pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.10278pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to3.32196pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to2.94585pt{}}}$\hss}}}g)
and by continuity even on its closure.
Then (a) implies that in particular
f\geq 0
on
S_{d}(\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.10278pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.10278pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to3.32196pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to2.94585pt{}}}$\hss}}}g)
. But then

\varphi(f)=f(\varphi(X_{1}),\ldots,\varphi(X_{n}))\geq 0
for all
\varphi\in L_{d}(\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.10278pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.10278pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to3.32196pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to2.94585pt{}}}$\hss}}}g)
. By Lemma
1.7
, this implies
f\in\overline{M_{d}(\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.10278pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.10278pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to3.32196pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to2.94585pt{}}}$\hss}}}g)}
as desired.

Conversely, suppose now that (b) holds and let
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus\overline{\conv S(\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.10278pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.10278pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to3.32196pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to2.94585pt{}}}$\hss}}}g)}
. We show that
x\notin S_{d}(\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.10278pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.10278pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to3.32196pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to2.94585pt{}}}$\hss}}}g)
.
By
3.5
there is a linear form
f_{0}\in\mathbb{R}[\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.6817pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.7584pt{}}}$\hss}}}X]_{1}
and
r\in\mathbb{R}
with
f_{0}(y)\leq r<f_{0}(x)
for all
y\in\overline{\conv S(\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.10278pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.10278pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to3.32196pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to2.94585pt{}}}$\hss}}}g)}
.
Setting
f:=r-f_{0}\in\mathbb{R}[\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.6817pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.7584pt{}}}$\hss}}}X]_{1}
, it follows that
f(y)\geq 0>f(x)
for all
y\in\overline{\conv S(\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.10278pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.10278pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to3.32196pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to2.94585pt{}}}$\hss}}}g)}
. In particular
f\geq 0
on
S(\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.10278pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.10278pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to3.32196pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to2.94585pt{}}}$\hss}}}g)
and therefore

f\in\overline{M_{d}(\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.10278pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.10278pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to3.32196pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to2.94585pt{}}}$\hss}}}g)}
by (b). By Lemma
1.7
, we have now
f(\varphi(X_{1}),\ldots,\varphi(X_{n}))=\varphi(f)\geq 0
for all
\varphi\in L_{d}(\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.10278pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.10278pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to3.32196pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to2.94585pt{}}}$\hss}}}g)
. Hence

f\geq 0
on
S_{d}(\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.10278pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.10278pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to3.32196pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to2.94585pt{}}}$\hss}}}g)
. From
f(x)<0
we now deduce that
x\notin S_{d}(\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.10278pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.10278pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to3.32196pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to2.94585pt{}}}$\hss}}}g)
as desired.
∎

Definition and Proposition 1.10
.

[
\to

4.1
,
1.1
]
Let
V
be a real vector space and
A\subseteq V
. The smallest cone containing
A
is obviously

\cone A:=\left\{\sum_{i=1}^{m}\lambda_{i}x_{i}\mid m\in\mathbb{N}_{0},\lambda_{i}\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0},x_{i}\in A\right\}.

We call it the cone
generated
by
A
or the
conic hull
of
A
.

Remark 1.11
.

Let
V
be real vector space and
A\subseteq V
. Consider the subset
A\times\{1\}
of the real vector space
V\times\mathbb{R}
.
One easily sees that

\conv A=\{x\mid(x,1)\in\cone(A\times\{1\})\}.

Lemma 1.12

(Carathéodory)
.

Let
n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
,
V
an
n
-dimensional real vector space and
A\subseteq V
.

(a)

\forall x\in\cone A:\exists B:(B\subseteq A\mathbin{\&}~\#B\leq n\mathbin{\&}x\in\cone B)

(b)

\forall x\in\conv A:\exists B:(B\subseteq A\mathbin{\&}~\#B\leq n+1\mathbin{\&}x\in\conv B)

Proof.

To prove (a), we may suppose that
A
generates
V
as a vector space since otherwise we can pass over from
V
to the subspace
generated by
A
. Let
x\in\cone A
. Choose a finite set
E\subseteq A
such that
E
generates
V
and

x\in\cone E
. By
4.20
there is a basis

B\subseteq E
of
V
such that
x\in\cone B
or there is some
\ell\in V^{*}
with
\ell(E)\subseteq\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
and
\ell(x)<0
. But the latter
cannot occur since
\ell(E)\subseteq\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
would imply
\ell(x)\in\ell(\cone E)\subseteq\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
. Clearly
\#B=n
since
B
is a basis.

To prove (b), let
x\in\conv A
. By Remark
1.11
, we have
(x,1)\in\cone(A\times\{1\})
. Since any subset of
A\times\{1\}
is of the form
B\times\{1\}
for some
B\subseteq A
,
(a) yields that there is some
B\subseteq A
with
\#B\leq\dim(V\times\mathbb{R})=n+1
and
(x,1)\in\cone(B\times\{1\})
. Again by Remark

1.11
, we get
x\in\conv B
.
∎

Lemma 1.13
.

If
S\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}
is compact, then
\conv S
is compact.

Proof.

Set
\Delta:=\{\lambda\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{n+1}\mid\lambda_{1}+\ldots+\lambda_{n+1}=1\}
. The set

\Delta\times S^{n+1}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n+1+n(n+1)}
is bounded and closed and therefore compact.
By Carathéodory
1.12
(b), we have that
\conv S
is the image of the map

\Delta\times S^{n+1}\to\mathbb{R}^{n},\ (\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{n+1},x_{1},\ldots,x_{n+1})\mapsto\sum_{i=1}^{n+1}\lambda_{i}x_{i}.

But since this map is continuous, its image is compact [
\to

1.18
].
∎

Lemma 1.14
.

Let
V
and
W
finite-dimensional real vector spaces [
\to

4.18
] and

f\colon V\to W

continuous with
f^{-1}(\{0\})=\{0\}
. Suppose there exists
r\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
such that

\forall v\in V:\forall\lambda\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}:f(\lambda x)=\lambda^{r}f(x).

Then
f(V)
is closed in
W
.

Proof.

Make both
V
and
W
into normed vector spaces by fixing arbitrary norms. Let
(x_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
be a sequence in
V
such that

\lim_{k\to\infty}f(x_{k})=:y
exists. We show that
y\in f(V)
. Since
f(0)=0
, we suppose WLOG
y\neq 0
. Then WLOG

f(x_{k})\neq 0
and in particular
x_{k}\neq 0
for all
k\in\mathbb{N}
. Due to
f^{-1}(\{0\})=\{0\}
, we have for the continuous function

h\colon S:=\{x\in V\mid\|x\|=1\}\to\mathbb{R},\ x\mapsto\|f(x)\|

that
h>0
on
S
. Because
S
is compact, there is some
\varepsilon\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
such that
h\geq\varepsilon
on
S
. Now

\|f(x_{k})\|=\|x_{k}\|^{r}\left\|f\left(\frac{x_{k}}{\|x_{k}\|}\right)\right\|\geq\|x_{k}\|^{r}\varepsilon.

The convergent sequence
(f(x_{k}))_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
is of course bounded and thus
(x_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
is now also bounded.
By the Bolzano–Weierstrass theorem, we may WLOG suppose that
(x_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
is convergent with
x:=\lim_{k\to\infty}x_{k}\in V
.
Now

y=\lim_{k\to\infty}f(x_{k})\overset{f\text{ continuous}}{=}f\left(\lim_{k\to\infty}x_{k}\right)=f(x)\in f(V).

∎

Lemma 1.15
.

Let
d,m\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
and
\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.10278pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.10278pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to3.32196pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to2.94585pt{}}}$\hss}}}g\in\mathbb{R}[\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.6817pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.7584pt{}}}$\hss}}}X]^{m}
. Suppose that
S(\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.10278pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.10278pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to3.32196pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to2.94585pt{}}}$\hss}}}g)
has nonempty interior. Then
M_{d}(\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.10278pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.10278pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to3.32196pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to2.94585pt{}}}$\hss}}}g)
is closed.

Proof.

Obviously
M_{d}(\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.10278pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.10278pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to3.32196pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to2.94585pt{}}}$\hss}}}g)
does not change if we discard those
g_{i}
which are either the zero polynomial or whose degree exceeds
d

[
\to

1.6
(f)].
Moreover, discarding some of the
g_{i}
does certainly not take away any points from
S(\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.10278pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.10278pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to3.32196pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to2.94585pt{}}}$\hss}}}g)
.
Therefore we have WLOG
0\leq\deg(g_{i})\leq d
for all
i\in\{1,\dots,m\}
.
Set
g_{0}:=1\in\mathbb{R}[\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.6817pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.7584pt{}}}$\hss}}}X]
. For
i\in\{0,\dots,m\}
, set
r_{i}:=\left\lfloor\frac{d-\deg g_{i}}{2}\right\rfloor
and

C_{i}:=\cone(\{p^{2}\mid p\in\mathbb{R}[\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.6817pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.7584pt{}}}$\hss}}}X]_{r_{i}}\})\subseteq\mathbb{R}[\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.6817pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.7584pt{}}}$\hss}}}X]_{2r_{i}}
.
By
2.1
, we get easily

M_{d}(\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.10278pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.10278pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to3.32196pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to2.94585pt{}}}$\hss}}}g)=\left\{\sum_{i=0}^{m}s_{i}g_{i}\mid s_{0}\in C_{0},\ldots,s_{m}\in C_{m}\right\}\subseteq\mathbb{R}[\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.6817pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.7584pt{}}}$\hss}}}X]_{d}.

Setting
k_{i}:=\dim(\mathbb{R}[\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.6817pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.7584pt{}}}$\hss}}}X]_{2r_{i}})
for
i\in\{0,\dots,m\}
, we get from this easily by Carathéodory
1.12
(a) that

M_{d}(\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.10278pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.10278pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to3.32196pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to2.94585pt{}}}$\hss}}}g)=\left\{\sum_{i=0}^{m}\left(\sum_{j=1}^{k_{i}}p_{ij}^{2}\right)g_{i}~\middle|~p_{ij}\in\mathbb{R}[\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.6817pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.7584pt{}}}$\hss}}}X]_{r_{i}}\right\}\subseteq\mathbb{R}[\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.6817pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.7584pt{}}}$\hss}}}X]_{d}.

In other words,
M_{d}(\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.10278pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.10278pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to3.32196pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to2.94585pt{}}}$\hss}}}g)
is the image of the map

f\colon\prod_{i=0}^{m}\mathbb{R}[\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.6817pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.7584pt{}}}$\hss}}}X]_{r_{i}}^{k_{i}}\to\mathbb{R}[\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.6817pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.7584pt{}}}$\hss}}}X]_{d},\ ((p_{ij})_{j\in\{1,\ldots,k_{i}\}})_{i\in\{0,\ldots,m\}}\mapsto\sum_{i=0}^{m}\sum_{j=1}^{k_{i}}p_{ij}^{2}g_{i}.

This map is quadratically homogeneous, that is
f(\lambda p)=\lambda^{2}f(p)
for all
p
in its domain and all
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
.
If we can prove that
f(p)=0
implies
p=0
for all
p
in the domain of
f
, then we will be done by Lemma
1.14
.
Consider the polynomial
h:=g_{1}\cdots g_{m}\neq 0
. Since
S(\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.10278pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.10278pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to3.32196pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to2.94585pt{}}}$\hss}}}g)
has non-empty interior, Lemma
2.3
implies that

h
does not vanish on the whole of
S(\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.10278pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.10278pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to3.32196pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to2.94585pt{}}}$\hss}}}g)
. Hence

S^{\prime}:=\{x\in S(\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.10278pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.10278pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to3.32196pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to2.94585pt{}}}$\hss}}}g)\mid h(x)\neq 0\}=\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\mid g_{1}(x)>0,\ldots,g_{m}(x)>0\}

is non-empty.
Now if

p:=((p_{ij})_{j\in\{1,\ldots,k_{i}\}})_{i\in\{0,\ldots,m\}}\in\prod_{i=0}^{m}\mathbb{R}[\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.6817pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.7584pt{}}}$\hss}}}X]_{r_{i}}^{k_{i}}

such that
f(p)=0
, then each
p_{ij}
vanishes on
S^{\prime}
so that
p=0
as desired because
S^{\prime}
is open.
∎

Theorem 1.16
.

[
\to

1.9
]
Let
d\in\mathbb{N}
,
m\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
and
\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.10278pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.10278pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to3.32196pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to2.94585pt{}}}$\hss}}}g\in\mathbb{R}[\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.6817pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.7584pt{}}}$\hss}}}X]^{m}
such that
S(\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.10278pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.10278pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to3.32196pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to2.94585pt{}}}$\hss}}}g)
is compact with nonempty interior. Then the following are equivalent:

(a)

S_{d}(\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.10278pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.10278pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to3.32196pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to2.94585pt{}}}$\hss}}}g)=\conv S(\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.10278pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.10278pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to3.32196pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to2.94585pt{}}}$\hss}}}g)

(b)

\forall f\in\mathbb{R}[\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.6817pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.7584pt{}}}$\hss}}}X]_{1}:(f\geq 0\text{ on }S(\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.10278pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.10278pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to3.32196pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to2.94585pt{}}}$\hss}}}g)\implies f\in M_{d}(\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.10278pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.10278pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to3.32196pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to2.94585pt{}}}$\hss}}}g))

Proof.

We have
\emptyset\neq S(\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.10278pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.10278pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to3.32196pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to2.94585pt{}}}$\hss}}}g)^{\circ}\subseteq S(\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.10278pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.10278pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to3.32196pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to2.94585pt{}}}$\hss}}}g)\subseteq S_{d}(\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.10278pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.10278pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to3.32196pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to2.94585pt{}}}$\hss}}}g)
and therefore
S_{d}(\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.10278pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.10278pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to3.32196pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to2.94585pt{}}}$\hss}}}g)\neq\emptyset
.
By the Lemmata
1.13
and
1.15
, we have that
\conv S(\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.10278pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.10278pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to3.32196pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to2.94585pt{}}}$\hss}}}g)
and
M_{d}(\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.10278pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.10278pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to3.32196pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to2.94585pt{}}}$\hss}}}g)
are closed.
Now the theorem follows from Lemma
1.9
.
∎

Corollary 1.17
.

Let
d\in\mathbb{N}
,
m\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
and
\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.10278pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.10278pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to3.32196pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to2.94585pt{}}}$\hss}}}g\in\mathbb{R}[\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.6817pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.7584pt{}}}$\hss}}}X]^{m}
such that
S(\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.10278pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.10278pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to3.32196pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to2.94585pt{}}}$\hss}}}g)
is compact with nonempty interior. Suppose that we are given

F\subseteq\mathbb{R}[\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.6817pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.7584pt{}}}$\hss}}}X]_{1}
such that

\conv S(\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.10278pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.10278pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to3.32196pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to2.94585pt{}}}$\hss}}}g)=\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\mid\forall f\in F:f(x)\geq 0\}.

Then the following are equivalent:

(a)

S_{d}(\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.10278pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.10278pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to3.32196pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to2.94585pt{}}}$\hss}}}g)=\conv S(\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.10278pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.10278pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to3.32196pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to2.94585pt{}}}$\hss}}}g)

(b)

F\subseteq M_{d}(\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.10278pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.10278pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to3.32196pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to2.94585pt{}}}$\hss}}}g)

Proof.

(a)
\implies
(b) follows directly from the corresponding implication in Theorem
1.16
. For the other implication, suppose
that (b) holds and let
x\in S_{d}(\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.10278pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.10278pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to3.32196pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to2.94585pt{}}}$\hss}}}g)
. We claim that
x\in\conv S(\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.10278pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.10278pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to3.32196pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to2.94585pt{}}}$\hss}}}g)
. Choose
\varphi\in L_{d}(\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.10278pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.10278pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to3.32196pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to2.94585pt{}}}$\hss}}}g)
such that
x=(\varphi(X_{1}),\ldots,\varphi(X_{n}))
.
By our hypothesis on
F
, it is enough to show that
f(x)\geq 0
for
all
f\in F
. But if
f\in F
, then
f\in M_{d}(\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.10278pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.10278pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to3.32196pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to2.94585pt{}}}$\hss}}}g)
by (b) and hence
\varphi(f)\geq 0
. Since
f
is linear, we have

f(x)=f(\varphi(X_{1}),\ldots,\varphi(X_{n}))=\varphi(f)\geq 0
as desired.
∎

Example 1.18
.

Taking up our Example
1.4
, we consider again the same system of polynomial inequalities

(*)\qquad\quad 1-x_{1}+x_{2}\geq 0,\quad 1-x_{1}^{4}-x_{2}^{4}\geq 0\qquad(x_{1},x_{2}\in\mathbb{R}).

This has given rise to a system
(\Box)
of three LMIs of sizes
6
,
3
and
1

in
14
unknowns
x_{1},x_{2},y_{1},\ldots y_{12}
. We had asked the question whether

\{(x_{1},x_{2})\in\mathbb{R}^{2}\mid\exists y_{1},\ldots,y_{12}:(x_{1},x_{2},y_{1},\ldots,y_{12})\text{ solves }(\Box)\}

equals the set of solutions of
(*)
. Translating this into our setting, we declare

g_{1}:=1-X_{1}-X_{2}\in\mathbb{R}[X_{1},X_{2}],\qquad g_{2}:=1-X_{1}^{4}-X_{2}^{4}\in\mathbb{R}[X_{1},X_{2}]

and
\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.10278pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.10278pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to3.32196pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to2.94585pt{}}}$\hss}}}g:=(g_{1},g_{2})
. Then
S(\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.10278pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.10278pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to3.32196pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to2.94585pt{}}}$\hss}}}g)
is the set of solutions of
(*)
and the
degree four state space associated to
\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.10278pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.10278pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to3.32196pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to2.94585pt{}}}$\hss}}}g
can be identified with the set of solutions of
(\Box)
.
Moreover, the just mentioned question just asks whether
S(\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.10278pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.10278pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to3.32196pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to2.94585pt{}}}$\hss}}}g)=S_{4}(\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.10278pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.10278pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to3.32196pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to2.94585pt{}}}$\hss}}}g)
. We leave it as an exercise to the reader to show that

S(\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.10278pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.10278pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to3.32196pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to2.94585pt{}}}$\hss}}}g)
is convex and compact with nonempty interior and that

\displaystyle F:=\{f\in\mathbb{R}[\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.6817pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.7584pt{}}}$\hss}}}X]_{1}\mid\exists x\in\mathbb{R}^{2}:(

\displaystyle f(x)=0=g_{2}(x)~\mathbin{\&}

\displaystyle\nabla f(x)=\nabla g_{2}(x))\}\cup\{g_{1}\}\subseteq\mathbb{R}[\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.6817pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.7584pt{}}}$\hss}}}X_{1}]

satisfies
S(\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.10278pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.10278pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to3.32196pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to2.94585pt{}}}$\hss}}}g)=\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\mid\forall f\in F:f(x)\geq 0\}
. By Corollary
1.17
, the question whether
S(\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.10278pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.10278pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to3.32196pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to2.94585pt{}}}$\hss}}}g)=S_{4}(\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.10278pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.10278pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to3.32196pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to2.94585pt{}}}$\hss}}}g)

is now equivalent to
F\subseteq M_{4}(\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.10278pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.10278pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to3.32196pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to2.94585pt{}}}$\hss}}}g)
. Clearly
g_{1}\in M_{4}(\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.10278pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.10278pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to3.32196pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to2.94585pt{}}}$\hss}}}g)
.
Now suppose
f\in F\setminus\{g_{1}\}
. We will then show
that
f\in M_{4}(g_{2})\subseteq M_{4}(\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.10278pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.10278pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to3.32196pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to2.94585pt{}}}$\hss}}}g)
. By
5.10
and
2.4
(b) this
means that there is some
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
such that
f-\lambda g_{2}\geq 0
on
\mathbb{R}^{2}
. We claim that we can take
\lambda:=1
,
i.e.,
f-g_{2}\geq 0
on
\mathbb{R}^{2}
. For this purpose, write
f=a_{1}X_{1}+a_{2}X_{2}+b
with
a_{1},a_{2},b\in\mathbb{R}
.
Choose
x\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
with
f(x)=0=g_{2}(x)
and
\nabla f(x)=\nabla g_{2}(x)
. We have

\displaystyle a_{1}x_{1}+a_{2}x_{2}+b=0=1-x_{1}^{4}-x_{2}^{4}\qquad\text{and}

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}a_{1}\\
a_{2}\end{pmatrix}=\nabla f(x)=\nabla g_{2}(x)=-4\begin{pmatrix}x_{1}^{3}\\
x_{2}^{3}\end{pmatrix}.

It follows that
-4x_{1}^{4}-4x_{2}^{4}+b=0
and hence
b=4(x_{1}^{4}+x_{2}^{4})=4
. Therefore we have to show that

\displaystyle h

\displaystyle:=f-g_{2}=-4x_{1}^{3}X_{1}-4x_{2}^{3}X_{2}+4-1+X_{1}^{4}+X_{2}^{4}

\displaystyle=X_{1}^{4}+X_{2}^{4}-4x_{1}^{3}X_{1}-4x_{2}^{3}X_{2}+3

is nonnegative on the whole of
\mathbb{R}^{2}
. Since the leading form
X_{1}^{4}+X_{2}^{4}
of
h
[
\to

2.1
] is positive definite [
\to

2.1
],
it is easy to show that
h
assumes a minimum on
\mathbb{R}^{2}
. Choose
y\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
such that
h(y)\leq h(z)
for all
z\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
. Then

4\begin{pmatrix}y_{1}^{3}\\
y_{2}^{3}\end{pmatrix}-4\begin{pmatrix}x_{1}^{3}\\
x_{2}^{3}\end{pmatrix}=\nabla h(y)=0.

Using that
\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R},\ t\mapsto t^{3}
is injective, we deduce
x=y
. Hence

0=f(x)-g(x)=h(x)=h(y)\leq h(z)

for all
z\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
as desired.

2
Strict quasiconcavity

Definition 2.1
.

Let
g\in\mathbb{R}[\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.6817pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.7584pt{}}}$\hss}}}X]
. If
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
, then we call
g

strictly concave
at
x
if
(\hess g)(x)\prec 0

and
strictly quasiconcave
at
x
if

((\nabla g)(x))^{T}v=0\implies v^{T}(\hess g)(x)v<0

for all
v\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus\{0\}
. If
S\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}
,
we call
g

strictly (quasi-)concave
on
S
is
g
is

strictly (quasi-)concave
at every point of
S
.

Remark 2.2
.

Let
g\in\mathbb{R}[\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.6817pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.7584pt{}}}$\hss}}}X]
and
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
such that
\nabla g(x)=0
.

(a)

g
is strictly quasiconcave at
x
if and only if

\hess g(x)\prec 0
.

(b)

If
g
is strictly quasiconcave at
x
and
g(x)=0
, then there is a neighborhood
U
of
x
such that

U\cap S(g)=\{x\}
.

Lemma 2.3
.

Let
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
g\in\mathbb{R}[\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.25557pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.6817pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.7584pt{}}}$\hss}}}X]
and
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
such that
g(x)=0
and
\nabla g(x)\neq 0
.
Suppose
v_{1},\dots,v_{n}
form a basis of
\mathbb{R}^{n}
,
U
is an open neighborhood of
0
in

\mathbb{R}^{n-1}
,
\varphi\colon U\to\mathbb{R}
is smooth and satisfies
\varphi(0)=0
as well as

(*)\qquad g(x+\xi_{1}v_{1}+\ldots+\xi_{n-1}v_{n-1}+\varphi(\xi)v_{n})=0
